# The Hindu Sufis of South Asia (Boivin) — takeaways

*Source: Michel Boivin, *The Hindu Sufis of South Asia: Partition, Shrine Culture and the Sindhis in India*,
I.B. Tauris / Bloomsbury Publishing Plc, London and New York; "First published in Great Britain 2019",
`Copyright © Michel Boivin 2019`, `ISBN: 978-1-7883-1531-9` (p. 5). No edition, impression or reprint
line is printed: (not stated). **The series statement contradicts itself and is left unreconciled** —
`Islamic South Asia Series` on the series page (p. 3) against `Series: Library of Islamic South Asia` on
the copyright page (p. 5). 253 pages, transcribed `text_layer` on 14 September 2026, chunked into 13 chunks of ~8000 words; provenance in
out/ocr/boivin_hindu_sufis_south_asia/. Page references are PDF page indices; folio = the printed page
number where the notes print one, and "(p. N, folio not stated)" where they do not. This transcription is
clean by this corpus's standards — 29 `[text-layer?]`, 8 `[OCR?]` and 9 `[illegible]` string occurrences
across 1.09 MB of chunk notes, most of them inside the notes' own statements that a chunk carried none —
with the Sindhi-script page at p. 6 the one substantial damage locus. Consolidated from the 13 chunk
notes on 24 September 2026.
Reviewed: no.*

## What this book is

An academic monograph in social anthropology, written in the first person, on Hindu participation in
the Sufi tradition of Sindh and on what happened to that tradition when its Hindu carriers migrated to
India. Boivin states his question directly: "As an anthropologist, my interest is to understand how, in
the early twenty-first century, a number of people in India claiming to be Hindu still maintain a
tradition in which the Sufi elements – usually associated with Islam – are playing a leading part in
structuring it, if they are not the core of the tradition itself, and furthermore, I am talking about a
tradition with permanent links with Pakistan" (p. 15, folio 2). The book grew from a bibliographic
discovery: "I was attracted by the English title of a small volume published in 1997 by S. L. Gajwani:
Sindhi Sufi Literature in Independent India (Gajwani 1997). … this book offered clear evidence that the
Sindhi Sufi tradition was still well alive among the Hindu Sindhis of India, exactly half a century
after partition. For me, this find was the equivalent of discovering a new world" (p. 14, folio not
stated), and from a 2011 visit to Ulhasnagar made for another project (p. 14, folio not stated). Its
object is declared to be culture and not theology — "The object of this study is the Sufi culture of
Sindh, rather than an essentialized form of Sufism" (p. 17, folio 4) — and its field is named with a
coinage, the "Sindhicate area", drawn from Hodgson's "Islamicate" and "`Persaniate`
[text-layer? Persianate]" (p. 16, folio 3). Its thesis is what Boivin calls the **organic theory**:
"the whole religious system of the Hindu Sindhis was mostly constructed … against this 'or', as well as
against any 'and'… This understanding is what I call the organic theory, since the Sufism as practised
by the Hindu Sindhis is a religious system organized of its own, as a separate and distinct entity"
(p. 29, folio 16), restated in the General conclusion as a term offered in place of syncretism,
hybridity and liminality (p. 197, folio 184). Its keystone claim is a division of labour: "Hinduism was
providing the social framework through which the Hindu Sufis were able to perform rituals for birth,
weddings and deaths, while Sufism was addressing the mystical quest… To some extent, one can state that
Sufism was the inner meaning of Hinduism, which was the outward religion" (p. 198, folio 185). His
stance is anti-nostalgic but not optimistic — "I am not inclined to think things were better 'before'…
Nonetheless, beyond the rational argumentation, it is difficult not to think that the Sufi legacy of
the Hindu Sindhis is vanishing" (p. 34, folio 21) — and explicitly anti-idealising about Sufism itself:
"Sufism does not organically imply tolerance towards other religions. For example, the most active
people working for the conversion of young Hindu girls to Islam are well-known Sufi pirs" (p. 177,
folio 164). The book's stated present is 2017: "if this statement is still relevant in 2017, seventy
years after partition" (p. 204, folio 191); elsewhere it dates itself only as "I am writing these lines
seventy years after partition and migration" (p. 34, folio 21), and **the year of writing is otherwise
(not stated)**.

Its method is avowedly triple. "observation of rituals and interviews with people must obviously be
completed and enmeshed in a work on written sources, because, as we shall see, Sufi poetry plays a
cardinal role in the setting. The written corpus is mostly made of Sufi poetry in Sindhi, most of it
recently published in post-partition India" (p. 15, folio 2). The fieldwork centres on three Hindu Sufi
paths and their founders — Rochaldas, Nimano Faqir and Mulchand Faqir, each treated as a different
Partition scenario (p. 70, folio 57) — with "a number of minor ones" neither named nor counted
(p. 15, folio 2, **(not stated)**); the book itself concedes that "The data collected reflects the
situation of three main Sufi paths among the Hindu Sindhis of India, with a survey of contemporary
Sindh" (p. 198, folio 185). Dated field evidence surfaces only in the Notes: "**Personal communication,
Haridwar, 26 April 2012**" (p. 214, folio 201, Ch. 5 n. 5) and a 2017 email exchange with Manu Bhatia
(p. 215, folio 202, Ch. 5 n. 11), in both cases with **what was established (not stated)**. Manuscript
and textual sources are handled with the same explicitness: the India Office Library's Sindhi
manuscripts, "the majority of which do not go back beyond the eighteenth century" (p. 41, folio 28); an
unpublished Siraiki poem reconstructed from two printed versions, whose "slight differences between
them would argue in favour of an oral transmission" (p. 212, folio 199, Ch. 3 n. 9); hagiographers
"(**sometimes anonymous**)" declared as "**the official tradition of Rochaldas's darbar, hereby the
tradition**" (p. 209, folio 196, Ch. 1 n. 12); a website declared as the source of an "**official
narrative**" (p. 213, folio 200, Ch. 4 n. 1); four doctoral or master's theses (Rana 2014, Matai 2003,
Pagani 2007, Garin 2005) and a documentary, "**Thrust into Heaven** (Schaflechner 2016)" (p. 216,
folio 203, Ch. 7 n. 1). Boivin records his failures as evidence — "**I have been unable to find any
information on this Sufi**" (p. 214, folio 201, Ch. 4 n. 8); "Unfortunately, only oral tradition can
help us to understand and examine the process of authority and legitimacy, and no other trace of the
same has remained, such as might be seen in documents or private archives related to the construction
of the shrines" (p. 77, folio 64); "I was unable to collect any information about the author" (p. 156,
folio 143); "I was not able to pay a visit to these places" (p. 152, folio 139); "I do not know how the
first versions of their shrines appeared since I was not able to find any photograph" (pp. 79–80,
folios 66–67). Two of his own declared conventions govern how any entry may quote him: "As it will be
noted, there is no attempt to provide any academic transliteration" (p. 12, folio not stated) — which
is why his spellings will not match the archive's — and "I am the only one responsible for translations
when the name of the author is given after a quotation. When it is not the name of an author given,
this signifies that I am using another translation, and in this case, this is the name of the
translator which is provided" (p. 12, folio not stated). The images are his own ("All images belong to
the author", p. 10, folio not stated), and he warns that "all of the material presented here is not
wholly new… Some parts have already been published, in English and in French" (p. 11, folio not
stated).

**The edition, exactly as its own pages print it.** Half-title: `The Hindu Sufis of South Asia`, title
alone (p. 2, folio not stated). Title page, over four lines: `The Hindu Sufis of South Asia` /
`Partition, Shrine Culture and` / `the Sindhis in India` / `Michel Boivin` (p. 4, folio not stated),
with **no edition statement: (not stated)**. `I.B. TAURIS`, `Bloomsbury Publishing Plc`, `50 Bedford
Square, London, WC1B 3DP, UK`, `1385 Broadway, New York, NY 10018, USA`; `First published in Great
Britain 2019`; `Copyright © Michel Boivin 2019`; `ISBN: 978-1-7883-1531-9`; `eISBN: 978-1-7883-1956-0`;
`ePDF: 978-1-7883-1957-7`; `Cover design: Adriana Brioso`; `Cover image: Mumbai, 2017. Courtesy of the
Author.`; `Typeset by Deanta Global Publishing Services, Chennai, India` (p. 5, folio not stated).
**No edition number, no impression number, no reprint line: (not stated).** **The series statement does
not agree with itself, and both forms are recorded side by side and not reconciled:** the series page
prints `Islamic South Asia Series`, `Series Editor Ruby Lal, Emory University` (p. 3, folio not stated),
while the copyright page prints `Series: Library of Islamic South Asia` (p. 5, folio not stated). The
copyright page also carries a cross-reference that corroborates the roman foliation — "For legal
purposes the Acknowledgements on p. x constitute an extension of this copyright page" (p. 5, folio not
stated) — matching the contents' `Acknowledgements x` (p. 8, folio not stated). **Reliability.** The
transcription route was `text_layer` across 253 PDF pages in 13 chunks, and by this corpus's standards
it is unusually clean: measured across the book's own 13 Pass-1 notes files (1.09 MB), only 29
`[text-layer?]`, 8 `[OCR?]` and 9 `[illegible]` flags occur in the whole book, so where a flag does
appear it is load-bearing and has been carried onto its word here. The book prints a folio on most
pages and the notes carry it, which is why most references below take the form `(p. N, folio N)`; where
the source gave none, `folio not stated` is printed rather than a folio computed. **The one substantial
damage locus the notes name is the Sindhi-script page at p. 6**, which carries a short Sindhi text and
nothing else, returned by the text layer with the diacritics detached from their letters and the words
broken across lines; it is reproduced verbatim under Doubts, and **whether that page is a dedication,
an epigraph or something else is (not stated)** — it bears no label, no attribution and no translation.

## Shrines in the archive this book informs

### lal-shahbaz-qalandar — Lal Shahbaz Qalandar

The book's names for him: **"Lal Shahbaz Qalandar"**, **"Lal Shahbaz"**, once with a definite article,
"the Lal Shahbaz Qalandar" (p. 192, folio 179), once "the qalandar" (p. 182, folio 169), and — as the
name of the song and, once, as a name for the saint himself — **"Mast qalandar"** (p. 141, folio 128).
His town is "Sehwan Sharif", "Sehwan", and once "the holy city of Sehwan Sharif" (p. 141, folio 128).
Present in chunks 001, 003, 004, 006, 007, 008, 009, 010, 011, 012 and 013.

- **Saint**
  - **No death year, no birth year and no alternative personal name of the "Usman Marwandi" kind is
    given anywhere in the book — (not stated).** Every chunk that carries him records this separately
    (chunks 001, 003, 004, 006, 007, 008, 009, 010, 011, 012, 013).
  - **The one biographical claim in the book, and it is a negative one**: "Despite his fame, Lal
    Shahbaz was not Sindhi since he was born in Persia and his alleged diwan is composed in Persian."
    (p. 213, folio 200, Ch. 4 n. 4) **"alleged" is the book's own hedge. No date of birth, no date of
    arrival: (not stated).**
  - **Silsila — the archive row's `Qalandari` is neither confirmed nor contradicted.** The only
    order-word attached to him in the body is `Qalandariyya shahbaziyya`, and it is attached to the
    path into which Muslims are initiated by a Hindu disciple, not stated as his own affiliation:
    "what is the significance of these designations when – as at Sehwan Sharif, for example – a Hindu
    disciple of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar initiates Muslims to the path (tariqa) of Qalandariyya
    shahbaziyya?" (p. 20, folio 7). Chunk 003 records no silsila for him at all (p. 56, folio 43).
    **Against that**, the Annex 2 chart carries the line "Sehwan Sharif (Qalandariyya)" with
    "Rochaldas" and "Dalpat Sufi" under it (p. 227, folio 214), and the commentary reads "The
    Qalandariyya is not so widespread over it, but in the case of Sindh, it reflects the radiation of
    Lal Shahbaz Qalandar and the pilgrimage centre of Sehwan Sharif (Boivin 2012)." (p. 227, folio 214)
    The chart's cell boundaries have not survived the extraction.
  - **A master named, and hedged**: at the Tando Ahmad Khan entrance there is "a shrine devoted to
    Shiva, another one to Raja Vir and still another one to Shah Sadr, who is said to have been the
    Sufi Master of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in Sindh" (p. 185, folio 172). **"is said to have been" is the
    book's own hedge; Shah Sadr is given no dates, no patronymic and no other location: (not stated).**
  - **A successor named**: "On the maternal side, Qutub Ali Shah was a descendant from Sayyid Ali
    Sarmast, the successor of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar." (p. 56, folio 43) Indexed "Sarmast, Sayyid Ali 43"
    (p. 249, folio 236). **Sayyid Ali Sarmast's dates, tomb and order are all (not stated); he is not
    Sachal Sarmast.**
  - **The Hindu names, in four passages and three spellings.** "In Sehwan Sharif, for example, there is
    only one place of worship but the saint is denominated Lal Shahbaz Qalandar by Muslims and **Raja
    Bharthari** (or **Raja Vir**) by Hindus." (p. 50, folio 37) "In Sehwan Sharif, Lal Shahbaz Qalandar
    is said to be **Raja Barthari**, or Raja Vir." (p. 178, folio 165) "Another referent figure is Lal
    Shahbaz Qalandar, the Sufi saint of Sehwan Sharif who is worshipped by the local Hindus as Raja
    Barthari, a main character of the Nathpanth." (p. 200, folio 187) **The book does not gloss `Raja
    Bharthari` or `Raja Vir`, gives no date for either name and no source for the Hindu naming: (not
    stated).** The index keeps the two spellings apart: "Raja Barthari 165" and "Raja Bharthari 37",
    with "Raja Vir 37, 165, 172" (pp. 247–248, folios 234–235).
  - **A fourth equation, at another site**: "A local follower told me it was a 'Hindu dargah' devoted to
    Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, here worshipped as **Jhulelal**." (p. 184, folio 171) And "While the figure of
    Lal Shahbaz is dominant, insofar as the shrine is a replica of the Sehwan one, he has been given the
    identity markers usually attributed to Jhulelal." (pp. 193–194, folios 180–181)
  - **The Jhulelal question raised and expressly refused**: "It is tempting to think of a connection, or
    even of a fusion, between Lal Shahbaz Qalandar and Jhulelal, but none of the sources allow for us to
    go that far. That said, both are today called Jhulelal." (p. 216, folio 203, Ch. 7 n. 5) **The
    author declines the identification; nothing here should be filed as a fusion.**
  - **Named as a poet whose kalams are sung**: at the first celebration of Mulchand's versi, in 1953,
    "All night long, he sang kalams of Sufi poets of Sindh, such as Bedil, Bekas and also Lal Shahbaz
    Qalandar." (p. 82, folio 69) **No kalam is quoted and no corpus is named: (not stated).**
  - Named in a Hindu Sufi's Persian breviary among "Bayazid Bistami, Junaid, Mansur Hallaj, Shams
    Tabrizi, Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, Attar, Rumi and even Bu Ali Qalandar" (p. 121, folio 108); as the
    subject of the third part of Jethmal Parsram's small book, "the third one on Lal Shahbaz Qalandar"
    (p. 49, folio 36); as a travelling companion in a Dalit hagiography, where the pir of Pithoro
    "quotes the names of the Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, Puran Bhagat and Jhulelal" (p. 192, folio 179 — the
    definite article is as printed); and in the closing praise formula of a daily Indian puja, "The puja
    ends with the jai (praise) formula devoted to **the gods**, including Guru Nanak, Jhulelal as well as
    Mast qalandar, that is, Lal Shahbaz Qalandar." (p. 141, folio 128) **The book here places him among
    "the gods"; the wording is the book's own.**
  - **Named as a symbol in a Hindu Sufi's teaching in India**: "Majnun is the symbol of ishq majuzi
    [text-layer? ishq majazi], while Lal Shahbaz Qalandar is the symbol of ishq haqiqi." (p. 166,
    folio 153) This is Rochaldas speaking, in R. M. Hari's malfuzat; the printed form "majuzi"
    contradicts "ishq majazi" eleven lines above on the same page.
  - **A follower with his own shrine**: "The renunciants (faqirs) of **Bodlo Bahar**, the main follower
    of Lal Shahbaz who has a shrine nearby, come once a month, and they perform a beautiful whirling
    dance in their red skirts." (p. 140, folio 127) Indexed "Bodlo Bahar, renunciants (faqirs) of 127"
    (p. 240, folio 227) — **not glossed and not located: (not stated).**
  - **A second Sehwan lodge claiming descent from him**: "a very similar portrait of Ali is exhibited in
    the Doda Mard Haqani Kafi, a Sufi lodge devoted to an alleged disciple of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in
    Sehwan Sharif, in Sindh" (p. 122, folio 109); indexed "Doda Mard Haqani Kafi 109" (p. 242,
    folio 229). **The disciple is not named, and the book's own hedge is "alleged": (not stated).**

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The shrine named and sited**: "In Sehwan Sharif, where his shrine stands, the dhamal is performed
    every day at sunset." (p. 140, folio 127) The book's terms for it are "Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's shrine
    (mazar)" (p. 75, folio 62), "the darbar of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar" (p. 179, folio 166), "Lal Shahbaz
    Qalandar's darbar" (p. 193, folio 180) and "the dargah" (p. 178, folio 165).
  - **Under government control, dated only to a decade**: "two lineages dominate the system, both being
    Sayyids. The Lakkiyaris are usually accepted as being the head of the system, but the Sabzwaris also
    share the symbolic power since **the shrine itself came under government control in the 1960s**."
    (p. 180, folio 167) **No department is named on that page and no year within the decade is given:
    (not stated).** The **Awqaf** are named three pages later, governing the mehndi's timetable: "No
    timetable is given for the custom of mehndi. It proceeds according to the document of the Awqaf
    manager in relation to the procession of the matamis." (p. 183, folio 170) **The spelling there is
    "Awqaf"; no officer is named and no document is cited: (not stated).**
  - **A lingam reported inside the dargah**: "Many years back, it is said that there was a lingam inside
    the dargah." (p. 178, folio 165) **The book's hedge "it is said" is its own; no date beyond "Many
    years back": (not stated).**
  - **A Hindu faqir's tomb built inside the shrine's own compound, while he was still alive**: "Because
    most of his followers had migrated to India, they arranged a tomb (maqbaro) when Mulchand was still
    alive in a compound located at the **western** side of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's shrine (mazar). The
    place is also known as Mulan Faqir jo asthan." (p. 75, folio 62) The same place is later named
    differently — "the Mulchand Kafi in Sehwan, where there is a tomb or maqbaro" (p. 79, folio 66, with
    an endnote 4 glued to "Sehwan") — and sited differently: "**Mulchand Kafi is situated to the east of
    the darbar of Lal Shahbaz** and it adjoins the kafi of Doda Mard Haqani." (p. 180, folio 167)
    **West at p. 75 (folio 62) and east at p. 180 (folio 167): both recorded, neither reconciled by the
    book.** No date for the tomb's construction beyond "when Mulchand was still alive". Indexed
    "Mulchand kafi, Sehwan 66, 100, 111–14" (p. 246, folio 233) and "Mulan Faqir jo asthan 62" (p. 246,
    folio 233).
  - **A sultan buried beside the shrine**: of Muhammad Shah Tughluq, who arrived in Sindh "In 1351",
    "The sultan died shortly afterwards in Sonda on the banks of the Indus, and he was buried in Sehwan
    Sharif, near Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's shrine." (p. 189, folio 176) **No year is given for the burial
    itself: (not stated).**
  - **The lodge system around it, counted**: "Sehwan Sharif hosts about twenty kafis, or Sufi lodges, of
    which two are Hindu." (p. 193, folio 180) "Two kafis are currently managed by Hindus. The first is
    the kafi of Lal Das and the second is that managed by Ramdas (or Ramchand), which is Mulchand Kafi."
    (p. 180, folio 167) "There is another 'Hindu kafi' whose sajjada nashin also leads a mehndi
    procession." (p. 193, folio 180) **The second Hindu kafi and its sajjada nashin are not named on
    p. 193: (not stated).**
  - **The "Sehwani system", the author's own coinage**: "As could be said of many other Sufi dargahs,
    the Hindus use [text-layer? used] to come to pay homage to Lal Shahbaz Qalandar. But in Sehwan they
    are integrated into the system of the kafis, what I call the Sehwani system." (p. 180, folio 167)
    "The Sehwani system is made up of a number of Sufi lodges, settled all around Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's
    darbar." (p. 180, folio 167) "There are complex relationships of authority and dependence between
    the different holders of the Sufi lodges." (p. 180, folio 167) The index heads it "Sehwan system,
    Hindus and 166–71" (p. 249, folio 236), with "Sehwani panchayat 167", "Sehwani Paro 70", "Sabzwaris
    167", "Khuhnbatis 168–70", "khuhnbo ceremony 168", "Lakkiyyaris 168", "Lakkiyyari, Gul Muhammad
    170", "Lal Das 114, 134, 167–8, 170", "Lal Gul 168", "Lal Kunwar 168–9" (pp. 244–245, 248–249,
    folios 231–232, 235–236). **Folios 166–71 are the single most concentrated run in the index for this
    row.**
  - **The vocabulary of the lodges, in three forms**: "In Sehwan Sharif, the place where the faqirs
    stay, usually known as a **khanaqah**, is named kafi. The origin of the use of this word is unknown,
    but maybe that it is related to the fact that it signifies a place where poetical verses, or kafis,
    are sung." (p. 211, folio 198, Ch. 2 n. 4); "The word kafi is locally used instead of **khanaqa**,
    the Sufi lodge." (p. 216, folio 203, Ch. 7 n. 4); and the glossary, "Kafi: a piece of poetry; in
    Sehwan, the name of the Sufi lodge." (p. 219, folio 206) **The glossary carries no entry for
    `khanqah`/`khanaqah` under any spelling: (not stated).**
  - **Mulchand Kafi as a working Hindu institution**: "Although Hindus are very few in number in Sehwan
    today, it should be noted that Mulchand Kafi works to a certain extent as a place of community
    worship." (p. 180, folio 167) **No number of Hindus is given: (not stated).** "other built
    structures can be found here. One of them is used to store different artefacts that are paraded
    during Lal Shahbaz's mehndi procession." (p. 125, folio 112) "although it does not play a ritual
    role for the Hindu community, although it is used for cremation" (p. 193, folio 180). "Concerning
    the cult of Lal Shahbaz proper, although it is true that the kafi is open to the public mainly on
    the occasion of the urs, certain ceremonies of the cycle of life are always realized there."
    (p. 180, folio 167)
  - **A disciple's dargah raised beside the master's**, of Lal Das: "He himself had disciples who raised
    a small dargah not far from his Master's. His devotees regularly receive his blessing, whether Hindu
    or Muslim." (p. 181, folio 168) **No building date, no dimensions, no custodian named for that small
    dargah: (not stated).**
  - **The shrine replicated in a Hindu village shrine in Sindh — the book's most unexpected shrine
    fact**: "Indeed, the sanctum sanctorum was but a replica of the darbar of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in
    Sehwan Sharif, though settled in a different religious environment." (p. 184, folio 171) "the Hindu
    dargah of Tando Ahmad Khan **hosts Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's tomb**, surrounded by Hindu temples, also
    Sufi astanas, and with many inscriptions referring to an array of religious traditions, including
    the Nanakpanth." (p. 184, folio 171) **The book does not say the tomb is a cenotaph or a
    relic-grave, only that the dargah "hosts" it: (not stated).** The site is read as a graded
    pilgrimage: "It can be framed as a spiritual pilgrimage from Shiva to Lal Shahbaz, who is finally
    merged through the space's agency in Jhulelal." (p. 186, folio 173)
  - **His shrine represented on an Indian altar, as a hinge between two levels**: in the darbar of
    Rochaldas "The transition from the first level to the second level is carried out by a representation
    of the darbar of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar." (p. 133, folio 120)
  - **His presence transplanted to Ulhasnagar as a pole and a red banner**: "The darbar is not decorated
    with any tower or dome, maybe except one considered the pole of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, which is high
    and thus can be seen from far away" (p. 80, folio 67); the alam is "a small square courtyard,
    enclosed by four walls, at the top of which floats the red banner of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar" (p. 81,
    folio 68). Figure 2.3 is captioned "Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's alam in Rochaldas's darbar." (p. 81,
    folio 68; listed at p. 10, folio not stated as `2.3 Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's alam in Rochaldas's
    darbar 68`). **The book does not say how the alam came there, who installed it, or what is done with
    it beyond the urs: (not stated).**
  - **A negative the author states himself**: of Mulchand, "even his relationship with Sehwan Sharif and
    Lal Shahbaz Qalandar is not displayed" (p. 75, folio 62). Set against the alam and the pole at
    Rochaldas's darbar, this is the book's own contrast between two of its three Indian cases, and **it
    is not reconciled.**
  - **Index**: the locator run `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar` at folios **7, 36, 37, 43, 45–7, 58, 62, 67–8,
    67–9, 105, 108, 109, 112, 113, 120, 127–8, 132–4, 153, 165, 167–9, 171–3, 176, 179–80, 187, 198 n.5,
    200 n.4, 203 n.5** (p. 245, folio 232) — **"67–8, 67–9" is printed exactly so, two overlapping runs
    in sequence.** Under `darbars (shrine)`, "Qalandar's 120, 166, 167, 171, 176, 180" (p. 241,
    folio 228); under `dhamal`, "Qalandar's darbar 127" (p. 242, folio 229). **At neither subentry does
    the index write out "Lal Shahbaz".** Under `urs`, "of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar 45, 68, 105, 112, 113"
    (p. 251, folio 238) — the first place in the index where the ʿurs is attached to his name written
    out in full. **No death date, no birth date, no silsila and no shrine description anywhere in the two
    index chunks: (not stated).**

- **Practices & Events**
  - **The ʿurs dates as printed**: "For a few years, two distinct urs were celebrated: Lal Shahbaz's urs
    in the Islamic calendar, **18, 19 and 20 Shaban**, and Rochaldas's versi. One year, the two
    anniversaries fell at the same time. From then on, it was decided to celebrate both at the same
    time, or, that is to say, for the versi of Rochaldas." (pp. 146–147, folios 133–134) **The year in
    which the two fell together is (not stated).** Earlier: "They performed the mehndi on the second
    day of the urs, **the nineteenth day of Shaban**." (p. 58, folio 45) — **a bare Hijri month name,
    with no year and no `AH` marker: (not stated).** And: "The only time in a year when it plays a
    social and religious role is the time of the mehndi procession, on **the 19th Shaban**." (p. 127,
    folio 114) The book defines the term: "The Arabic-origin word urs depicts the mystical marriage
    realized between the Sufi and the God at the time of death. It is in fact the big annual festival
    (melo) that brings together the greatest number of pilgrims and an accompanying fair, a vogue, and
    many cultural events such as contests of stories and Sufi poetry." (p. 81, folio 68) **Whether that
    description is of Sehwan's urs or of the Ulhasnagar one is not disambiguated: (not stated).** The
    glossary: "Urs: the annual fair organized for commemorating the demise of a Sufi saint." (p. 220,
    folio 207) and "Versi: see urs." (p. 220, folio 207).
  - **The three mehndi processions of the ʿurs, by day and leader**: "His alleged son, Lal Gul, performs
    the mehndi during the urs. Before the procession, there is a musical session in a house, with the
    presence of the head of the Lakkiyyaris, Murad Shah. Therefore, there are three mehndi processions
    organized for the urs: **on the 18th of Shaban**, it is the procession led by the Lakiyyaris, **on
    the 19th** it is the one led by Ramchand and **on the 20th** is that of Lal Gul." (p. 181,
    folio 168) The book's hedge on Lal Gul is "alleged son". Compare: "In Sehwan, the mehndi is led on
    the first day by the dominant group of Sayyids, the descendants of the Prophet Muhammad, and on the
    second and third days by Hindus who belong to two distinct families." (p. 147, folio 134) **Neither
    Hindu family is named on that page: (not stated).**
  - **The two Hindu mehndi families — named two different ways.** "The **Navani** family in Sehwan
    Sharif was one of the two Hindu families with the **Kanungos** who used to perform mehndi since
    centuries during the urs of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar since centuries." (p. 58, folio 45 — the duplicated
    "since centuries … since centuries" is the text layer's, or the book's). Against that: Dalpat Sufi
    "was born in Sehwan Sharif in the **Mirani** family, one of the two Hindu families whose members
    performed the mehndi ceremony during the urs of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar. The other Hindu family was
    Mulchand Faqir's one, the Navanis." (p. 118, folio 105) And a third form: "On the second day, it
    left the house of the '**Meranis**' and '**Nevanis**' under the direction of Mulchand Faqir."
    (p. 183, folio 170) **Navani/Nevani, Mirani/Merani and Kanungo: all three spellings and both
    pairings recorded side by side, none reconciled by the book.** The index's only Navani is "Navani,
    Parumal 45" (p. 246, folio 233).
  - **The mehndi duty as an inherited office — two incompatible successions.** Chunk 006's: Mulchand's
    "first successor was Chandmal Chandrani, a local Hindu from Sehwan. But, in 1971, he decided to
    migrate to India, and his son Kanya Lal, who had a vegetable shop in the Shahi Bazar in Sehwan,
    refused to take care of the Mulchand Kafi. Mohan Lal, who was Mulchand's **nephew**, finally took
    care of it. Also, he took on the duty of performing the mehndi during the urs of Lal Shahbaz
    Qalandar." (p. 125, folio 112) "Mohan Lal passed away in 1981 and his son Ramchand began to perform
    the mehndi; he is still in charge. Ramchand is an Amil, but his mother was a Thakur." (p. 125,
    folio 112) Chunk 009's: "Before partition, the first procession was led by Gul Muhammad Lakkiyyari.
    On the second day, it left the house of the 'Meranis' and 'Nevanis' under the direction of Mulchand
    Faqir. Finally, on the third day, it started from the house of the Kanungos. Now, the eldest son of
    Gul Muhammad, Murad Shah, has replaced him to lead the mehndi of the first day. The two groups
    leading the following mehndis have changed. After the death of Mulchand Faqir in 1962, the duty of
    the mehndi was transmitted to his **cousin**, Mohan Lal, who then passed it on to his son, Ramchand,
    in 1981. The Kanungos migrated to India in the same year and the burden fell to Lal Das, a Hindu
    from Kashmir. It is today his son Lal Gul who fulfils this obligation." (p. 183, folio 170) A third
    statement makes Ramchand a different relation again: "the sajjada nashin is Ramchand, Mulchand's
    **nephew**, and he performs the mehndi ritual every year, leading a procession from Mulchand Kafi up
    to Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's darbar." (p. 193, folio 180) **"still in charge" and "today" are undated:
    the year of the author's statement is (not stated).** The index carries "Lal, Kanya 112", "Lal,
    Mohan 112", "Ramchand 112–14", "Chandrani, Chandmal 112" (pp. 240, 245, 247, folios 227, 232, 234).
  - **The third-day duty and its migration, traced through five holders**: "On the third day, the mehndi
    was led by the Kanungos, but in 1962 they decided to emigrate to India and settled in Ulhasnagar,
    where the son of Rochaldas, Hari, asked them to lead the mehndi procession in the darbar. In Sehwan
    Sharif, another Hindu named Lal Das then became mehndibardar." (p. 147, folio 134) "Gandaram
    (d. 2008), the eldest of the Kanungos, inherited the charge upon his father's [text-layer? father’s]
    death in 1948, but in the 1980s he was transferred to Bangalore, far from Ulhasnagar." (p. 147,
    folio 134) "After Gandaram’s demise, it would have been his brother Murlidhar who should have taken
    on the duty. But, he was living in Baroda, and Lachhman, the youngest brother who was himself living
    in Ulhaasnagar [text-layer? Ulhasnagar] was not allowed. Thus, it was the son of Hari, Damodar, who
    led the mehndi from that point on." (p. 147, folio 134) **Why Lachhman "was not allowed", and by
    whom, is (not stated).** **Note the collision with p. 183 (folio 170), which puts the Kanungos'
    migration in 1981, not 1962.**
  - **The mehndi's meaning and its form.** "henna or mehndi symbolizes the marriage between the Sufi and
    God, which is to say that it represents death as their union" (p. 147, folio 134). "The protocol of
    the urs reflects the balance at which the communities involved in the distribution of the charisma
    have arrived. Each procession is organized upon the same pattern. It is composed of singers,
    musicians and dancers. A designated person wears the silk chador, which is deposited on the tomb of
    the qalandar, and places a tray on which the henna (mehndi) is located." (p. 182, folio 169) "The
    procession is the reproduction of the classic procession of marriage. Each time, the procession runs
    through the narrow streets of the town while the 'clients' of each party grow more and more
    numerous. The roofs of the houses are filled with onlookers who do not want to miss this show. In
    any case, the celebration of the wedding will be repeated on each of the three days of the feast.
    The only difference will be that, each time, the officials will be different." (p. 183, folio 170)
    **No attendance figure is given anywhere: (not stated).**
  - **The order of events before the Mulchand Kafi procession, as printed**: "The main event occurring
    in Mulchand Kafi is the procession of the mehndi for the urs of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, which starts
    from the kafi." (p. 126, folio 113) "Before the procession, two main events are arranged. First is
    the dastarbandi of Ramchand. As we saw before, this is the coronation, when the ceremonial turban
    (pag) is put on his head. It is followed by a musical session with musicians and dancers from
    Punjab. The apex of the listening to spiritual music is the singing of the famous song devoted to
    Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, **Dam dama mast qalandar**. The third is the preparation of the mehndi under a
    canopy." (p. 126, folio 113) **The book announces "two main events" and then enumerates a third; the
    inconsistency is the book's own and is reproduced rather than harmonised.** Figure 5.2(b) is
    "Ramchand's dastarbandi before the mehndi procession in Sehwan Sharif, 2011" (p. 149, folio 136).
  - **The Hindu custodian's title, and the duties he does not discharge**: "Finally, Mulchand Faqir is
    known in Sehwan as a **mehndibardar**." (p. 126, folio 113) "His kafi does not work like the other
    kafis in the town. Usually, the sajjada nashin stays for welcoming the murids, and provides them
    advice and amulets (tawiz). Ramchand did not play such a role. And no faqir stays in Mulchand Kafi."
    (p. 126, folio 113) "Only Ramchand's family used to come, but the kafi is closed most of the time."
    (p. 127, folio 114) The office is indexed "mehndibardar 44, 113, 134, 139, 169" (p. 246, folio 233).
  - **A caste given a priestly function at the ʿurs**: "The Khuhnbatis, as saffron specialists, were
    attributed a priestly function in the ceremony of the mystical nuptials of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar."
    (p. 181, folio 168) Their name "comes from the Sindhi word khuhnbo, which means saffron" (p. 181,
    folio 168).
  - **The ʿurs as the moment rank is re-established, under a State protocol**: "For all categories of
    people involved in sharing the charisma, the urs is also a moment of prime importance. It is at this
    moment that the position of each one is reaffirmed. These positions, be they honorary or real, can
    be watched during the implementation of a protocol imposed by the State." (p. 181, folio 168) "This
    protocol, as well as the distribution of tasks between confessions, has not undergone fundamental
    change since the partition, with one exception." (p. 181, folio 168) — the exception being the Lal
    Kunwar custom, under Legends below.
  - **A Hindu sajjada nashin at Sehwan, in a figure caption only**: "Figure 7.1 Asan Gul, a Hindu
    sajjada nashin in Sehwan Sharif." (p. 182, folio 169; listed at p. 10, folio not stated). **Asan Gul
    is named nowhere in the body text and no shrine is attached to him: (not stated).** The caption is
    the strongest custodianship fact in chunk 009 and its least supported.
  - **A lifetime of lamp-lighting service, dated at its end**: "Lal Das was a Hindu from Kashmir who came
    to make the pilgrimage to Sehwan Sharif. He devoted nearly forty years of his life to serving Lal
    Shahbaz Qalandar. He was in charge of lighting the lamps (chiraghi wala) before extinguishing
    himself 'in the smell of sanctity' in 1993." (pp. 180–181, folios 167–168) **His birth year is (not
    stated); "nearly forty years" is the only span given.**
  - **The dhamal at the shrine, timed and described**: "In Sehwan Sharif, where his shrine stands, the
    dhamal is performed **every day at sunset**. It is performed by professional drummers in the main
    courtyard and **hundreds of devotees** start to dance to reach ecstasy. Also, a number of women
    participate, but in a different manner." (p. 140, folio 127) **"hundreds of devotees" is the only
    attendance figure for Sehwan; no exact number: (not stated).** Restated in a note: "The dhamal is an
    ecstatic dance performed every day before the sunset in the precinct of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's
    darbar, in Sehwan Sharif." (p. 210, folio 197, Ch. 1 n. 18) The glossary's shorter form: "Dhamal: a
    Sufi ritual where a dance is performed with drums." (p. 218, folio not stated)
  - **The ʿurs exported to India by a dream instruction, and then merged.** The alam "was added to the
    darbar after Rochaldas had a dream in which Lal Shahbaz Qalandar asked him to celebrate his urs
    every year, as it was celebrated at Sehwan Sharif in Sindh" (p. 81, folio 68). Table 5.1 is headed
    "Table 5.1 Daily programme of Rochaldas’s versi in 2012" (p. 146, folio 133), and the text under it
    reads "urs of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar. In the late 1950s, they were celebrated separately, but now they
    are celebrated together. The programme clearly shows both the death anniversaries of Lal Shahbaz and
    Rochaldas have been harmoniously mixed." (p. 146, folio 133 — the opening words are the tail of a
    sentence that begins in the preceding chunk.) "It seems that the first celebration took place in
    **1959**, two years after the death of Rochaldas, who had left orders." (p. 146, folio 133)
    **Rochaldas's own death year is not printed there, only "two years" before 1959; it is not
    computed: (not stated).**
  - **The Indian dhamal, dated and compared**: at Rochaldas's darbar "the dhamal was adapted: it is a
    simple beating of drums practised in front of the samadhi, which plays the rhythm of the devotional
    song Mast qalandar devoted to Lal Shahbaz" (p. 140, folio 127); "**The ritual has gone on unchanged
    since 1952.**" (p. 141, folio 128) "It starts with the dhamal, the ritual related to Lal Shahbaz
    Qalandar. Nonetheless, the dhamal performed in Ulhasnagar is different. It is reduced to the
    drumming, while in Sehwan, the drumming is played for the ecstatic dance of the devotees." (p. 145,
    folio 132) "The purpose of the ritual is clearly to make the darbar of Rochaldas a territory that
    extends from an original sacred territory which is located in Sindh, the holy city of Sehwan
    Sharif." (p. 141, folio 128)
  - **The Indian mehndi's route and its Hindu close**: "At that time, the mehndi procession went as far
    as the river. There, a puja was performed just as it is in the shrine, ending with Hari Om
    Jhulelal." (p. 147, folio 134) **The river is not named: (not stated).** "The mehndi of Lal Shahbaz
    makes it possible to maintain a direct link through the ritual with the lost territory of Sindh."
    (p. 147, folio 134) Figure 5.2(a): "Damodar's mehndi procession at Rochaldas's darbar, Ulhasnagar,
    2012" (p. 149, folio 136).
  - **The Indian rituals in retreat**: "For more practical reasons, other rituals have been simplified –
    for instance, the mehndi procession at Rochaldas’s darbar, since the mehndibardar left Ulhasnagar."
    (p. 152, folio 139) After the Ayodhya Affair "the main impact was the retreat and/or disappearance
    of major Sufi rituals, especially the dhamal, the alam and the mehndi" (p. 201, folio 188); "The
    mehndi procession is performed inside Rochaldas's darbar, while previously, at the time of the
    Kanungos, it was made from the darbar to the river." (p. 201, folio 188) **"the Kanungos" is not
    glossed or dated there: (not stated).**
  - **A possible further presence, recorded without being claimed**: at Dadi Dhan's versi "his Sakhi
    sabajhal was sung before Dam mast qalandar, in the version authored by Qutub Ali Shah" (p. 149,
    folio 136), and in Mulchand's path "other followers were dancing on the Mast qalandar rhythm"
    (p. 149, folio 136). **The book does not state whom the song names: (not stated).**
  - **Index**: `mehndi (henna) 45, 132, 197 n.16`, with "procession 134, 136, 139, 168–70, 180, 188";
    "**Qalandar, ceremony of 45, 47, 58, 105, 112–14**"; "in Ulhasnagar 136"; "wedding and 131"
    (pp. 245–246, folios 232–233). The `urs` subentry's folios (45, 68, 105, 112, 113) overlap the
    `mehndi` subentry's at **45, 105, 112**; **the index does not state that the mehndi ceremony and the
    ʿurs are the same observance: (not stated).**

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The origin of the Hindu mehndi right, attributed by the book to local tradition**: "According to
    local tradition, Meru and Nao were two brothers who used to take care of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar by
    providing food for him. Very grateful to them, Lal Shahbaz decided they would lead the mehndi ritual
    after his death, when his own urs would be celebrated." (p. 58, folio 45) **The brothers' religion
    is not stated and the link between them and the Navani/Kanungo families is not spelled out: (not
    stated).**
  - **The dhamal's origin, given as tradition and not as the author's claim**: "This ritual is directly
    related to Lal Shahbaz Qalandar because tradition says that, under this name, he practised an
    ecstatic giratory [text-layer? gyratory] dance in which he saw the supreme form of meditation
    (zikr), a position that brought him closer to Jalal al-Din Rumi (d. 1275), the celebrated founder of
    the brotherhood of the whirling dervishes. Through the dhamal, Lal Shahbaz Qalandar reached the
    final stage of absorption into God (fana fi Allah)." (p. 140, folio 127) **"under this name" is not
    glossed; the name it contrasts with is not given: (not stated).**
  - **The Lal Kunwar, "the fiancée of the red" — a woman married annually to the saint's grave, in two
    irreconcilable versions.** Burton's: "In the middle of the nineteenth century, Richard Burton
    mentioned the following custom: 'Every year, a daughter of the Khonbati caste (saffron flower dyers)
    was married to the grave, with music, dance and all the solemnities due to her' (Burton 1851: 211).
    This custom, known as Lal Kunwar (the fiancée of the red), **disappeared during the partition in
    1947**." (p. 181, folio 168) "Burton specifies, however, that the bride was released after a year,
    though she had to remain forever unmarried." (p. 181, folio 168) The rival: "according to other
    sources, the young Lal Kunwar belonged to the caste of Kanungos. The origin of the custom comes from
    the fact that a young woman of the caste had fallen in love with Lal Shahbaz. The latter being a
    renouncer, she threw herself from a balcony at his feet, thus sacrificing her life for him. Lal
    Shahbaz would have been touched by this sacrifice and he would have authorized that, after his
    death, a young virgin of the caste should be married to his grave. The Lal Kunwar lived for a year
    in a room where she was venerated as a devi, a goddess. When she was released, she was forced to
    accept celibacy." (p. 182, folio 169) **The author then refuses it as unevidenced: "Despite a
    relatively thorough investigation, with Pakistani and Indian informants, both Muslim and Hindu, it
    was not possible to obtain evidence of this chain of events." (p. 182, folio 169)**
  - **The saint's handprints in the henna overnight**, attributed with "It is said": "It is said that the
    act of depositing the chador on the grave amounts to putting the fiancée in her wedding clothes. It
    is also said that, the next morning, palms and fingerprints are visible in the henna that was
    deposited the day before. This suggests that, during the night, the qalandar coated his hands with
    henna and is therefore ready to be united to his divine lover." (pp. 182–183, folios 169–170)
  - **A saint appears in a dream and commissions his own ʿurs in a foreign country**: "Shortly before his
    death, Rochaldas had a dream in which Lal Shahbaz Qalandar appeared. He pointed out that although the
    Hindu Sindhis had forgotten him, Lal Shahbaz always watched over them in India. He asked him to
    celebrate his urs, as at Sehwan." (p. 146, folio 133) **No date for the dream: (not stated).**

- **Significance**
  - **One of the book's three case studies**: "this chapter is based on the study of three different
    cases. The first case is that of Sehwan Sharif, a main Sufi pilgrimage centre of Sindh, where the
    darbar of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar is located." (p. 179, folio 166); "Sehwan will be one of the case
    studies in this chapter." (p. 178, folio 165)
  - **The book's type case for a shared shrine under two names**: his is the second of four categories,
    "the place of worship is divided, but where the saint in focus is venerated under two different names
    by the Hindus and the Muslims" (p. 50, folio 37); and, restated, "In other places, there is only a
    tomb, but the Hindus give the saint a distinct identity… In this configuration, there is only one
    annual fair, in which the Hindus play a leading ritual role." (p. 178, folio 165)
  - **One of the three Sufi traditions the Hindu Sufis were affiliated to**: "the Hindu Sufis were
    affiliated to three main Sufi traditions of Sindh: Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in Sehwan Sharif, Daraza and
    Tando Jahaniyya in Hyderabad." (p. 60, folio 47) **He is named here as a tradition, alongside two
    toponyms.**
  - **A saint whose shrine drew a Hindu into Sufism without a local master**: "It is interesting to
    observe that Mulchand was not initiated by a local Sufi Master. He was thus independent from the
    local Sufis, and his only connection with Sufism in Sehwan was directly through Lal Shahbaz
    Qalandar." (p. 59, folio 46) "Mulchand nevertheless performed the mehndi ceremony of Lal Shahbaz
    Qalandar in Sehwan until his death" (p. 59, folio 46) — Mulchand Faqir's dates are printed
    "(1883–1962)" (p. 55, folio 42), **but the book does not say the mehndi ran to 1962 and no such
    inference is made here.** "Until his death in 1962, Mulchand lived as a recluse, probably leaving his
    dwelling place only for performing the mehndi of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar" (p. 71, folio 58 — the hedge
    "probably" is the book's own). His "family was perfectly integrated in the dominant groups of the
    town through the main ritual role they were playing in the mehndi procession during the annual
    festival devoted to Lal Shahbaz Qalandar" (p. 71, folio 58). **That festival's date, month and
    duration are (not stated), and the book does not there call it an urs or a melo.**
  - **A Muslim master's homage visit, the occasion of a Hindu boy's initiation**: "Once, Rakhiyyal Shah
    (1846–1940), a high-level Sufi of the Qadiriyya, came to pay homage to Lal Shahbaz Qalandar."
    (p. 59, folio 46)
  - **A Hindu Sufi who chose to die at Sehwan rather than migrate**: Sobhraj Faqir "was often in company
    with Mulchand Faqir and they were both fond of dhamal, as they used to perform together. Sobhraj
    wanted to stay for the ending of his life in Sehwan Sharif, which he did." (p. 179, folio 166)
  - **Hindus still holding a ritual role under Islamist pressure, and the author's reading of why**: "In
    a Pakistan under the pressure of Islamist extremists, it may seem surprising that Hindus can still
    play a ritual role in the Sufi set-up of Sehwan Sharif. This resilience can be explained primarily by
    the desire to preserve a historic heritage. But beyond this integrative dynamic of Sufism, it must be
    understood that the Hindus do not exercise this function as Hindus, but as Hindu Sufis. This is
    obvious from the fact that the Hindu processions cannot be distinguished from the Muslim ones, and
    furthermore, nothing especially Hindu can be observed in these devotional events. The Hindu
    processions are quite similar to all of the processions that take place in the Sufi mausoleums."
    (p. 183, folio 170)
  - **The 2017 attack, with its date, its ritual moment and its casualty figures**: "While the terrorists
    have targeted Sufi places in Pakistan for years, a huge terrorist attack took place in the darbar of
    Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in Sehwan Sharif on **16 February 2017**. It was **during the dhamal** and
    **ninety people were killed; more than 300 were injured**." (p. 217, folio 204, Ch. 7 n. 6) **No
    perpetrator is named: (not stated).** In the body: "since the Sufi dargahs are now targets of
    Islamist terrorism,6 one can wonder about the future of the Hindu dargah. More pragmatically, it is
    difficult not to think that the Hindu dargah would have been temporarily closed after the terrible
    terrorist attack which occurred in Sehwan Sharif in February of 2017." (p. 186, folio 173) **There
    the attack's target is not named beyond "Sehwan Sharif", no casualty figure is given, and the closure
    is the author's inference, not a reported fact: (not stated).**
  - **His rising fame dated to a political era**: "As Pakistani citizens, the local Lohanas could have
    reacted to the growing fame of the saint of Sehwan Sharif, especially since Bhutto's rule in the
    1970s." (p. 193, folio 180) **"Bhutto" is given no first name: (not stated).**
  - **Sehwan as a town without communal violence — and as a town whose Hindus left**: "In a small town
    such as Sehwan Sharif, Mulchand could have not felt any threat, and therefore, there was no reason
    for him to leave the place where his ancestors had been settled since centuries. Contrary to other
    cities in Northern Sindh – for instance, Sukkur, following the Manzilgah riots – no communal violence
    had ever been registered. Maybe this is due to the spirit of toleration which prevailed in Sehwan"
    (p. 71, folio 58 — "could have not" and "Maybe" are the book's own hedges). **Yet: "the large
    majority of the Hindu population of Sehwan migrated to India" (p. 71, folio 58) — the book states
    both and does not explain the tension.**

### shrine-of-sachal-sarmast — Shrine of Sachal Sarmast

The book's names for him: **"Sachal Sarmast"**, abbreviated by the book itself to **"Sachal"**, once
the bare **"Sarmast"** (p. 220, folio 207), once the inscription **"Sai Sachal"** (p. 152, folio 139);
by birth **"Khwaja Salah ̣al-Din Ḥafiẓ Abd al-Wahhab"** (p. 93, folio 80 — `Salah ̣al-Din` carries a
detached combining dot in the text layer); nicknamed **"Mansur sani"**. His place is "Daraza", "Daraza
Sharif", and the adjectival "Darazi" and "Sakhi Darazi". Present in chunks 001, 002, 003, 004, 005,
006, 007, 008, 009, 010, 011, 012 and 013.

- **Saint**
  - **Two different death years, eight pages apart, and neither is the archive's.** "Sachal Sarmast
    (**1739–1825**) was another main Sufi poet of Sindh" (p. 93, folio 80); and "Sachal Sarmast
    (**1739–1829**), one of the most popular Sindhi poets from Daraza, in the north of Sindh" (p. 101,
    folio 88). **Both are recorded side by side; neither is preferred, and the archive row's `1827` is a
    third value that must not be back-filled from the row.** Every other chunk gives him no dates at all:
    **(not stated)** in chunks 001, 002, 003, 004, 006, 007, 008, 009, 010, 011, 012, 013. The only other
    chronology is relative — Rohal Faqir "was a contemporary of Sachal Sarmast" (p. 107, folio 94) — and
    the epithet "nineteenth-century": "a number of Sufis from nineteenth-century Sindh, such as Sachal,
    Qutub Ali Shah and Bedil" (p. 198, folio 185).
  - **Name and laqab**: "Named Khwaja Salah ̣al-Din Ḥafiẓ Abd al-Wahhab by birth, he was given the laqab
    of Sachal Sarmast, meaning the 'truthful one with an intoxicated head'." (p. 93, folio 80) **The
    archive row's alternative name "Abdul Wahab Faruqi" appears here only as the birth-name element "Abd
    al-Wahhab"; the nisba Faruqi is not attached to him anywhere: (not stated)** — though "Sakhi Qabul
    Muhammad Faruqi, the sajjada nashin of Daraza" carries it (pp. 96–97, folios 83–84).
  - **Family and succession at Daraza**: "He was the son of the sajjada nashin of Daraza, Khwaja Muḥammad
    Ḥafiẓ, also known as Sahiḅ Dino I, and brother of the sajjada nashin Khwaja Abd al-Ḥaqq." (p. 93,
    folio 80) Indexed "Ḥafiẓ, Khwaja Muḥammad 80" (p. 243, folio 230). **No dates of office, no line of
    succession and no appointing authority: (not stated).**
  - **Silsila, as the book names it**: "He thus belonged to the **Darazi silsila** located close to
    Khairpur in Northern Sindh." (p. 93, folio 80) The book's spelling is `silsila` and the order
    `Darazi`. Elsewhere "a follower of the **Faruqi silsilo** in Daraza, in Northern Sindh" (p. 60,
    folio 47); "he was the Master of the **Darazi order**" (p. 60, folio 47); "a 'branch' of the dargah
    of Daraza, which was the headquarters of the **Daraza silsila**" (p. 67, folio 54). **The book uses
    both the Sindhi `silsilo` and the Urdu/Arabic `silsila` and both are reproduced as printed. The
    archive row leaves the silsila column empty; nothing here should be read as Qadiri or any other
    order** — and the book's own note says so: "the Chishtiyya was not widespread in Sindh, while it is
    dominant in Punjab and North India. The sajjada nashin of Daraza alluded to a possible link with the
    Chishtiyya, while he is not explicit regarding the formal affiliation of Daraza with a tariqa. On the
    one hand, he claims that, unlike the Qadiriyya, the masters of Daraza loved and promoted poetry and
    singing (Faruqi 2002: 94). But on the other hand, the genealogy he provides clearly shows an
    allegiance to both the Qadiriyya and the Sohrawardiyya (Faruqi 2002: 120–1)." (p. 217, folio 204)
    **Three candidate affiliations stand side by side and the author reconciles none.** Against them, the
    Annex 2 chart's first line reads "**Darazi (Qadiriyya)**", under which stand "Nimano Faqir Radha
    Kishin Pratap Rajani" (p. 227, folio 214). **The chunk never says Sachal Sarmast founded or led the
    Faruqi/Darazi order: (not stated).** Which of two silsilas is his is also left open: "the Darazi and
    the Jahaniyya silsilas share a main feature: they were staunch wujudi silsilas. Their two major
    poets, Sachal Sarmast and Qutub Ali Shah, frequently mentioned in their kalams Hindu deities" (p. 200,
    folio 187) — **the sentence pairs the two names with the two silsilas without matching them.**
  - **Land and the economy of the lodge**: "Although he had never owned a piece of land, he was in charge
    of looking after the business affairs of the Sufi lodge, the khanaqah." (p. 93, folio 80) **No
    endowment, revenue figure or land grant is described: (not stated).**
  - **His attributes, itemised**: "Sachal Sarmast's attributes were those of a bayragin, a stick with two
    forked ends, an asa, a stick, a tanbura, a stringed instrument, and a kishti, a bowl… The bayragin and
    the kishti are the symbol of renunciation." (p. 93, folio 80) The glossary spells the first word
    differently: "Bairagan: a crooked stick, a kind of a forked stick on which the bairagi that is the
    renouncer places his head when meditating." (p. 218, folio not stated)
  - **Practice**: "Like his Sufi predecessors, he used to play music and sing, and also to perform
    ecstatic dances." (p. 93, folio 80)
  - **Doctrine**: "In his poetry, he was a staunch extoller of the doctrine of wahdat-e wujud – as, for
    example, when he states: 'God is the Unique Manifestation everywhere and all have come from Him.'"
    (p. 93, folio 80) He uses "the Sindhi word hekkhe or hekro" for the 'One' (p. 101, folio 88). `Ana
    al-haqq` "was as we saw before a real refrain in Sachal's work (Allana 1983: 146–7)" (p. 94,
    folio 81); "For many Sufis, like for Sachal among others, it is the final stage of ana al-haqq, or 'I
    am God', the famous phrase uttered by the Sufi from Iraq, Mansur Hallaj." (p. 136, folio 123) "Beyond
    the accessibility of his poetry, Sachal was also a staunch wujudi" (p. 150, folio 137).
  - **The coinage `haqq mawjud`**: "With Sachal Sarmast, the vernacularization process reached a new step
    when he used a new expression, that of haqq mawjud, which is in fact not new in the context of Muslim
    thought." (p. 101, folio 88) "The main user of the expression, if not the creator, was Sachal Sarmast.
    On the other hand, Sachal usually used the expression haqq mawjud coupled with another one: sada
    mawjud, that is, sada (always) omnipresent. In one of his poems, for example, one finds it as a
    refrain. Among Sachal's followers, these formulas are used as salutations. When they write to each
    other, they use the expression ishq mawjud" (pp. 101–102, folios 88–89). "it was the haqq mawjud, a
    core principle in Sachal's poetry, which was mostly continued by Nimano" (p. 109, folio 96).
  - **Hallaj and the nickname**: "Sachal Sarmast, nicknamed **Mansur sani, 'the Second Mansur'**, was a
    staunch admirer and devout follower of Hallaj. In his Sindhi poetry, there are many references to
    him." (p. 102, folio 89) Source-mined: "On the figure of Hallaj in Sindhi Sufi poetry, especially in
    Sachal's verses, see Schimmel 1986: 96–149." (p. 212, folio 199, Ch. 3 n. 15)
  - **Persecution**: "His religious conception attracted the wrath of some religious leaders who tried to
    kill him." (p. 93, folio 80) **The leaders are not named and no date is given: (not stated).**
  - **Languages, and the Hindu register of his verse**: "another main Sufi poet of Sindh who composed
    several works in Sindhi, Siraiki and Persian" (p. 93, folio 80); the glossary adds "Siraiki: a
    language spoken in Northern Sindh and Southern Punjab. Several Sufi poets from Sindh, such as Sarmast
    and Bedil, composed poetry in Siraiki." (p. 220, folio 207 — **the bare "Sarmast" is recorded as
    printed**). "Sachal was a Muslim Sufi who made a lot of references to the Hindu gods" (pp. 94–95,
    folios 81–82); "Among the classical Sufi poets, Sachal is probably the one whose work quotes the most
    numerous Hindu references. For example, regarding scriptures, he mentions the Vedas, named Bed in his
    verses. Furthermore, like most of the other Muslim Sufi poets, he refers to the pothis (pl.
    pothiyyun)" (p. 105, folio 92); "Frequently, he puts side by side the scriptures of the different
    involved religions – namely Islam, Hinduism and Christianity. His purpose is to show that reading the
    scriptures does not suffice for reaching God/s." (p. 105, folio 92) "In his poetry, Sachal mentions
    different Hindu gods, such as Ram, Hanuman (as we saw above), Sita and especially Krishna"; "Other
    characters of Hindu mythology are quoted – for example, Ravan and Goga"; "There are also a number of
    sacred Hindu places, such as Hinglaj, Kasi, or Kashi (Banaras), Sri Lanka and the Ganga" (all p. 106,
    folio 93). "Sachal also refers to this episode of the Ramayana" — Diwali and Ram's return to Ayodhya
    (p. 106, folio 93). His words for Hindu renunciants: "gosain, jogi, nango and udesi, but not varied as
    is the case in Shah Abd al-Latif's poetry" (p. 106, folio 93); and for rituals, "mandir, the Hindu
    temple, the darsan, the ritual of visualization of the God, the jap sahib… and the janeo" (p. 106,
    folio 93). Against Qutub Ali Shah: "in Sachal's poetry, he is named **Krishna or Shyam**… Qutub Ali
    Shah calls him **Kaniyya**" (p. 106, folio 93); "Sachal puts **darsan** while Qutub Ali Shah puts
    **darshan**" (p. 107, folio 94); "Sachal mentions Hanuman while Qutub Ali Shah gives the names of
    Laxman and Sita, Ram and Krishna" (p. 107, folio 94).
  - **His language is the accessible one**: "It is noteworthy that the language he used is very accessible
    – more so, for instance, than Shah Abd al-Latif's." (p. 109, folio 96); "While Shah Latif's poetry can
    be difficult to understand… Sachal and especially Sami are far more accessible" (p. 95, folio 82).
  - **Followers named**: Bedil "was a staunch follower of Sachal Sarmast" (p. 97, folio 84); "Nimano Faqir
    (1880–1963), a Hindu Sufi who was a follower of Sachal Sarmast" (p. 101, folio 88); "He had a large
    number of followers among the Hindu trade castes of Norther [text-layer? Northern] Sindh" (p. 150,
    folio 137).

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The dargah located, twice, at two levels of precision.** "Sachal Sarmast's dargah is located in the
    village of **Daraza, not far from Khairpur, in Sindh**." (p. 150, folio 137) And the glossary:
    "**Daraza**: the name of a town in **Northern Sindh** where Sachal's dargah is." (p. 218, folio not
    stated) **The glossary gives no district: (not stated).** Listed among the "remarkable sanctuaries" of
    the Rohri–Sukkur–Shikarpur triangle as "**the dargah of Sachal Sarmast**" (p. 31, folio 18) — **with
    no location given there at all, not Daraza and not Khairpur: (not stated).**
  - **The only physical description of the shrine in the book, and it is an inscription**: "the
    inscription written on the **main gate of Sachal's dargah is haqq mawjud**." (pp. 101–102, folios
    88–89) **No building date, no architecture, no ʿurs attendance, no visitor number, no Auqaf
    involvement, no endowment: (not stated).**
  - **His tomb located, and a Hindu Sufi's ashes placed beside it**: "if the sanctuaries of the three
    Hindu Sufis discussed here have a samadhi, two of them also have a grave in Pakistan. Mulchand's
    remains were buried in a tomb in Sehwan Sharif. **Nimano Faqir was cremated, but his ashes were
    deposited in a tomb in the dargah compound in Daraza, Pakistan, near that of Sachal Sarmast.**"
    (p. 131, folio 118) **No date for the deposition: (not stated).** A second cremated Hindu woman's
    ashes: "After her death in **1963**, her ashes were buried in Daraza close to Sachal Sarmast."
    (p. 60, folio 47) — **the book says ashes, and says they were buried**; and "Her cremation took place
    on the spot and her ashes were carried to Daraza, where a grave sheltered them." (p. 74, folio 61)
    **The grave's location within Daraza, and who arranged it, are (not stated).**
  - **Custodianship at Daraza, named, numbered and dated.** "the new sajjada nashin of Daraza, **Pir
    Khwaja Abd al-Haqq II (1915–78)** visited the shrine" in 1956 to consecrate Nimano's spiritual
    authority (p. 74, folio 61); earlier, permission to go to India is said to have been granted by "the
    sajjada nashin of Daraza" (p. 72, folio 59) — **unnamed there, and the book does not say whether that
    is the same man: (not stated).** Indexed "al-Haqq II, Khwaja Abd 61" (p. 239, folio not stated). In
    the present day, "**Sakhi Qabul Muhammad Faruqi, the sajjada nashin of Daraza**" (pp. 96–97, folios
    83–84). Nimano's initiator: "his Master (murshid), **Sakhi Qabul Muhammad II (1842–1925)**" (p. 132,
    folio 119; the same dates at p. 135, folio 122, where he is nicknamed "**Sakhi Darazi**"), whom
    another passage calls "the **sixth** murshid of Daraza, Sachal's dargah, Sakhi Qabul Muhammad II
    [numeral as printed] (1842–1925)" (p. 150, folio 137) and who is elsewhere "**Sakhi Qabul Muhammad
    VI**" (p. 152, folio 139) — "This was the case for Nimano Faqir… She was also initiated to the Sufi
    path by Sakhi Qabul Muhammad VI." (p. 152, folio 139) **"II", "sixth" and "VI" are reproduced exactly
    as printed and are not harmonised.** Also named: "**Sakhi Qabul VIII**, who recently passed away"
    (p. 152, folio 139). The glossary: "Sakhi: honorific title literally meaning generous. Sakhi also
    refers to several Sufi masters of Daraza, especially Nimano Faqir's murshid, **Sakhi Qabool Muhammad
    II of Daraza Sharif**." (p. 220, folio 207) Unnamed incumbents: "The sajjada nashin of Daraza has paid
    three visits to the darbar" and "he calls on them to give the date of Sachal's versi" (p. 151,
    folio 138); "Soni has to call, or is called by phone by the sajjada nashin of Daraza, for him to
    inform her of the timing." (p. 143, folio 130); "He became one of the closest followers of the sajjada
    nashin of Daraza at the point when he became his khalifa." (p. 179, folio 166) **None of these is
    named and no date is given: (not stated); and the book never writes "sajjada nashin of Sachal's
    dargah" — the link between the Daraza office and Sachal's tomb is the book's juxtaposition, not its
    own sentence.**
  - **`sakhi` as the hereditary honorific of the shrine's masters**: "In Sindhi, here the word sakhi is a
    laqab of the Masters of the Daraza silsila, in Sindh. It is a kind of honorific title they have been
    given for generations, while sakhi is simultaneously a very commonly used term in Sufi poetry for
    naming the Sufi himself." (p. 78, folio 65) "she wanted to 'spread the good word' of the Sakhi – the
    generic name given to the Sufi masters of Daraza, but designating especially Sachal Sarmast" (p. 72,
    folio 59). The Indian foundation's first name, "the Sakhi Kuthiyya", "would mean something like the
    abode of the generous" and "referred to the original dargah in Sindh, and furthermore to the spiritual
    head" (p. 78, folio 65).
  - **The 'official' portrait hanging at the dargah, and the Indian statue copied from it**: "The core of
    the worship in the Rohani Rah is a statue (murti) of Sachal Sarmast. This murti was obviously carved
    according to the 'official' picture of Sachal, **which hangs on a wall at Daraza**. It is said that
    this portrait was drawn by a Sikh follower of Sachal. Sachal is represented in majesty in a pose that
    could be reminiscent of Guru Nanak’s iconography. In both representations, he sits on a gaddi with a
    club in his right hand, and a musical instrument, slightly different, in his left. The only difference
    between the two is that, in the statue, Sachal has a golden tilak on his forehead." (p. 151,
    folio 138) **The book's hedge "It is said that" is its own; the Sikh follower is not named: (not
    stated).** And the dargah itself reproduced on an Indian altar: "Above Sachal's murti, there is Krishna
    as a flute player, without Radha, and still above this, **the darbar of Sachal Sarmast in Daraza**."
    (p. 152, folio 139)
  - **Index**: the locator run `Sachal Sarmast` at folios **7, 14, 18, 35, 36, 47, 59, 61, 78, 80–2, 84,
    85, 88–9, 91–4, 96, 118, 119, 123, 129, 130, 136–9, 142, 143, 145, 147, 148, 153, 155, 185, 187–8,
    191, 200 n.4** (p. 248, folio 235). Under `dargah(s)`, "of Sachal Sarmast 18" (p. 241, folio 228) —
    folio 18 also carries "Sadh Bela (sanctuary) 18", "Khwaja Khizr (sanctuary) 18" and "imambaras 18"
    (pp. 244, 248, folios 231, 235), so folio 18 appears to be where the book inventories sanctuaries.
    **A second, separate dargah subentry the index does not equate with the first**: under `Daraza`,
    "dargah of 54" (p. 241, folio 228), plus "Faruqi silsilo in 47", "sajjada nashin of 14, 59, 61, 80,
    83–4, 122, 130, 138–9, 166, 204 n.1", "silsila 54, 59, 61, 65, 80, 187, 188, 189, 190, 192, 197 n.2",
    and the whole again at top level as "silsila Daraza 54, 59, 61, 65, 80, 187, 188, 189, 190, 192, 197
    n.2" (pp. 241, 249, folios 228, 236). **The index nowhere states that the dargah of Daraza is Sachal's
    dargah: (not stated).** The `Darazi` cluster is kept apart from `Daraza`: "Darazi — Nimano 122; Sakhi
    122; satsangs 137–9; silsila 80" (p. 241, folio 228), plus "Sakhi Darazi 122" (p. 248, folio 235).
    **No death date, no birth date, no silsila for the man anywhere in the two index chunks: (not
    stated)** — the index gives the Faruqi *silsilo* and the *silsila Daraza* as institutions, never as
    his personal affiliation.

- **Practices & Events**
  - **His ʿurs, with its calendar problem stated by the book**: "Throughout the year, one of the most
    significant events is the urs of Sachal Sarmast. Since it is in the Islamic calendar, **the 14th of
    Ramazan**, the date changes every year, be it in the Christian calendar or in the Hindu one named
    Vikram Samvat (or Sambat)." (p. 143, folio 130) **The book's spelling is "Ramazan"; no year is given:
    (not stated).** In India: "two versis are celebrated over the course of the year: **Sachal's versi on
    the 14th of Ramazan**, and Hari Ram's versi in the beginning of November" (p. 151, folio 138); but on
    the same page, "As a matter of fact, **Sachal's birthday is on Ramazan** in the Islamic calendar, so
    that the date changes every year in the Christian calendar" (p. 151, folio 138). **The same date is
    called a versi and a birthday within one page; both recorded, neither reconciled.** At the Sachal Sain
    darbar, "Two versis are celebrated in the darbar: Sachal’s and Pratap’s." (p. 151, folio 138)
  - **The index splits the same observance across two headwords and two folios**: under `urs`, "of Sachal
    Sarmast 130" (p. 251, folio 238); under `death anniversary`, "Sachal 138" (p. 241, folio 228).
    **ʿUrs at folio 130, death anniversary at folio 138; the index nowhere equates the two.**
  - **The 'Darazi satsangs' in India, with their weekly rite and their attendance counted**: "only a
    weekly ritual event is organized in the Maqbul Manzil, in Ulhasnagar. In this space devoted to Sachal
    Sarmast, a Darazi satsang is organized **every Thursday** under the supervision of Soni Kataria. The
    satsang starts at **7.00 pm and lasts until 9.00**." (p. 142, folio 129) "After the arti, the devotees
    sing Sachal's kalam. Two devotees are musicians: one plays harmonium and the other the tablas. Soni is
    the leading singer, and she knows the verses by heart… **About twenty people attend the satsangs.**"
    (p. 143, folio 130) "In the Maqbul Manzil in Ulhasnagar, for example, Sachal's devotees started the
    satsang with an arti: nothing distinguishes it from any other arti performed in a Hindu temple."
    (p. 204, folio 191) "The Daraza school of Sufism still attracts many Hindu Sindhis of India. Already,
    we mentioned they organized ‘Darazi satsangs’ in Mumbai and Ulhasnagar. They gather to sing Sachal
    Sarmast's kalam." (p. 152, folio 139) "The two darbars devoted to Sachal Sarmast are located in the
    same area of Ulhasnagar, **camp 5**." (p. 151, folio 138) A ritual form named after the place appears
    in the contents as `The 'Darazi satsangs' 137` (p. 8, folio not stated), and the book forward-refers to
    it from p. 109 (folio 96).
  - **His poetry as living liturgy**: "kalams written by Sindhi Sufis like Sachal Sarmast are still sung
    every day" at Nimano's shrine (p. 74, folio 61); "his poetry is sung in all the Sufi darbars of India"
    (p. 109, folio 96); "The repertoire consists mainly of works by Sachal and Nimano. The Sachal jo kalam
    is open closeby [text-layer? close by] Kamla." (p. 142, folio 129); chanted at Daulatram's versi "with
    the chanting of Sufi songs composed by Sachal Sarmast, Shah Abd al-Latif and Qutub Ali Shah" (p. 150,
    folio 137); at Dadi Dhan's versi in 2012, "The most requested author was Sachal Sarmast, then
    Rochaldas" (p. 149, folio 136); "after his death, it was run by Hari Ram, who took the charge to sing
    Sachal’s poetry. After him, it was his daughter Soni who performed the satsang every Thursday evening"
    (p. 151, folio 138); Mira Rajani "used to sing Sachal's poetry during the satsang" (p. 151, folio 138).
  - **His name and picture withdrawn from two Indian altars.** "In the Radha Krishin Lal darbar, the core
    of the altar is a photo of Sakhi Qabul Muhammad VI, instead of a representation of Sachal. But the
    whole picture set-up is dominated by an inscription in Latin alphabet: ‘Sai Sachal’… the most amazing
    feature is the absence of Sachal’s picture, and when I asked Soni why it was Sakhi and not Sachal, she
    answered that Sakhi is the one who looks most like Sachal." (p. 152, folio 139) "One can wonder about
    the absence of a representation of Sachal Sarmast, who was at the origin of the creation of the Sakhi
    Kuthiyya founded by Nimano Faqir in Baroda, and later on Vadodara." (p. 132, folio 119) **This makes
    Sachal the originating figure of an Indian foundation; the book gives no date for that origination:
    (not stated).** "We saw that, in the Nimanal Sangam, the reference to Sachal has even been
    suppressed." (p. 204, folio 191) **The date of the suppression is (not stated).** "For example, in
    Vadodara, Sachal has declined, and now Nimano is at the forefront." (p. 201, folio 188) **No date for
    the decline: (not stated).** And in print: of the 2011 Vadodara edition of Nimano Faqir's kalam,
    "Interestingly, one cannot find Sachal's poetry, while in original Nimano's kalam, he was given a
    large part of the poetry. Guru Nanak's poetry has been kept in this new edition." (p. 172, folio 159)
    **The author gives no reason for the removal: (not stated).**

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **Shah Latif's prophecy over the child Sachal**, given by the book as legend and in reported speech:
    "There is a legend according to which Shah Latif met Sachal Sarmast. When he was about seven, it is
    said that Shah Laṭif came to pay homage to the local murshid. After seeing Sachal, he is said to have
    exclaimed: 'This God-gifted child will one day uncover those divine secrets which I have still kept
    concealed.'" (p. 93, folio 80) **The book does not say who "he" is in "When he was about seven", and
    no year is given: (not stated).**
  - **Sachal survives an assassination attempt**, attributed by the book to legend: "The legend states
    that the swords passed through his body as if it was water." (p. 93, folio 80)

- **Significance**
  - **One of the two Sufi traditions most popular among the Hindus**: "the two main Sufi traditions that
    were very popular among the Hindus were Daraza and Jhok. Jhok, contrary to Daraza, is located in
    Southern Sindh" (p. 60, folio 47); and one of the "three main Sufi traditions of Sindh: Lal Shahbaz
    Qalandar in Sehwan Sharif, Daraza and Tando Jahaniyya in Hyderabad" (p. 60, folio 47). Named first
    among the three seats whose sajjada nashins still hold Indian Hindu Sufi lineages: "They are the
    sajjada nashin of Daraza, of Tando Jahaniya and of Jhok Sharif. At each of them, a great Sufi poet is
    attached: Sachal Sarmast, Qutub Ali Shah and Nasir Faqir (Thapan 2005: 210–11)" (p. 27, folio 14).
    **The one-to-one pairing of the two lists is the obvious reading but the book does not state it; the
    sajjada nashin is not named, no date is given, and the number of Hindu disciples is (not stated).**
  - **The authority for the book's central formula**: "As stated by Dalpat Sufi, or Sachal Sarmast,
    hereafter Sachal, the true Sufi is beyond religion, hence the formula **hindu momin nahyan**, to quote
    Nimano Faqir, one of the Hindu Sufi figures under study (Nimano Faqir 1997: 284), for which
    'neither…nor' is the exact English translation" (p. 20, folio 7). **The apposition "Dalpat Sufi, or
    Sachal Sarmast" is the book's own and is recorded exactly as printed; it is not resolved, and the
    annex table lists `Dalpat Sufi 208` separately (p. 13, folio not stated).**
  - **The exemplary case of a Sindhi Sufi carried by a Hindu path with no institutional link**: "The first
    category is made up of Sindhi Sufis without any relation with the involved path. The exemplary example
    in this context is Sachal." (p. 109, folio 96) "some of them transferred their love for singing
    Sachal’s poetry to India when they migrated in the wake of partition." (p. 150, folio 137) "There are
    also other Sufi poets claiming to be 'Darazi'." (p. 152, folio 139) "But Nimano Faqir's verses still
    embodied the very spirit of Sindhi Sufism, and especially that of the Darazi silsila." (pp. 204–205,
    folios 191–192)
  - **Named as one of the "'three great Sufi teachers'"** — "who are Shah Inayat, Shah Abd al-Latif and
    Sachal Sarmast (Boivin 2016)" (p. 49, folio 36) — and as the second of the "'trinity of the Sindhi
    poets'" (p. 91, folio 78). **Counted first in one anthology**: "First comes Sachal Sarmast, with
    forty-six abstracts from his Sindhi poetry." (p. 158, folio 145); and in Vaswani 1955, "The first
    Muslim Sufi is, unsurprisingly, Shah Abd al-Latif, followed by Rohal, Murad, Sachal and Bedil"
    (p. 156, folio 143); "His book focuses on Shah Latif and Sachal" (p. 155, folio 142). **Counted second
    in a Hindu Sufi's own breviary**: "We also notice only one quotation from Shah Abd al-Latif, and two
    from Sachal." (p. 168, folio 155) **And named as an absence**: "no Sufi from Sindh, including those
    who are much-acclaimed poets in Sindhi, as for instance Sachal Sarmast, is enumerated." (p. 213,
    folio 200, Ch. 4 n. 4)
  - **Quoted inside Hindu Sufi teaching**: "Mulchand also quotes Sachal Sarmast's poetry in his
    explanation" of `om` (Motwani 1969: 40) (p. 168, folio 155); "Rochaldas also quotes verses from Kabir,
    Bedil, Sachal and Ajiz (Hari 1995: 190–9)." (p. 166, folio 153) — a bare name in a list of four.
    "The next step of the initiation ritual shows the importance of Krishna among the Sindhi Sufis,
    including Muslim Sufis such as Sachal and Qutub Ali Shah." (p. 136, folio 123)
  - **Author-only mentions and the scholarly apparatus.** Jethmal Parsram "wrote another book on Sachal
    Sarmast in 1922,8" (p. 48, folio 35) — **the glued `8` is endnote 8, not part of the year: the year is
