    1922; the book's title is (not stated).** "In 1955, she had edited the Sindhi kalam of Sachal Sarmast,
    and was herself the author of a Sindhi kalam with the title of Haqq mawjud." (p. 60, folio 47) "Abd
    al-Karim Sindilo, who, interestingly, had already published a glossary of Sachal Sarmast's poetry
    (Sindilo 1984)" (p. 94, folio 81), listed as "Sindilo, Abd al-Karim, *Lughat-e Sachal*, Khairpur,
    Sachal Chair, Shah Abdul Latif University, 1984" (p. 232, folio 219) — **the only entry in the
    bibliography that names an institutional chair devoted to an archive figure.** "According to his
    website, Ram Panjwani also published a book on Sachal Sarmast's poetry, with the title *Sachal jyun
    kafiyun*." (p. 215, folio 202, Ch. 6 n. 2) Editions: "Sachal Sarmast, *Risalo Sachal Sarmast*, ed.
    Ashman Ali Ansari, Karachi- Hyderabad, Sindhi Adabi Board, 1958"; "Sachal Sarmast, *Sachal jo risalo.
    Siraiki kalam*, ed. Mowlana Muhammad Sadiq Ranipuri, Kandiaro, Roshni Publications, 1997"; "Sachal
    Sarmast, *Sachal Sarmast Jo Kalam*, Delhi, Sindhi Akademy, 1997" (all p. 231, folio 218). A
    government-published study of his school: "Faruqi, Dr. Sakhi Qabool Muhammad, *Study of Mysticism in
    Darazi School of Sufi Thought*, Karachi, Culture and Tourism Department, Government of Sindh, 2002"
    (p. 234, folio 221); and a Hindu faqir taking "Darazi" as his own name, "'Krishin', Faqir Radhakrishin
    Lal Sahib Darazi, *Kalam*, Ulhasnagar, Hari R. Kataria, 1986" (p. 230, folio 217).
  - **A possible further mention, recorded without being claimed**: of Nimano's portrait, "she is
    represented in her middle age with detached hair and with a small portrait of her Master from Daraza,
    this being located at the very centre of her breast" (p. 115, folio 102). **The Master from Daraza is
    not named on that page: (not stated).**

### bhit-bhit-shah — Bhit (Bhit Shah)

The book's names for him: **"Shah Abd al-Latif"**, **"Shah Latif"**, the bare **"Shah"**, once
**"Shah Abd al-Latif Bhittai"** (p. 91, folio 78), once **"Shah Abdul Latif"** (p. 212, folio 199),
twice with a diacritic **"Shah Laṭif"**, once run together in the text layer as **`ShahAbd al-Latif`**
(p. 91, folio 78), once with the space-before-apostrophe artefact **`Shah Abd al-Latif ’s`** (p. 160,
folio 147; also p. 14, folio not stated and p. 48, folio 35), and once **"Shah Ab al-Latif"
[text-layer? Shah Abd al-Latif]** (p. 220, folio 207). His work is **"Shah jo Risalo"**, also "Shah jo
rag" and "Shah's poetry". **"Bhittai", "Bhit" and "Bhit Shah" do not occur at all in chunks 001, 002,
006, 008, 010 or 011.** Present in chunks 001, 002, 003, 004, 005, 006, 008, 009, 010, 011 and 012.

- **Saint**
  - **Death year, printed twice in the same parenthetical form**: "the Sufi poet Shah Abd al-Latif
    (**d. 1752**)" (p. 48, folio 35) and "the Shah jo Risalo authored by Shah Abd al-Latif (**d. 1752**)"
    (p. 155, folio 142). **A bare year, no era marker, no `AH` equivalent.** **Life dates once**: "Shah
    Abd al-Latif Bhittai (**1689–1752**)" (p. 91, folio 78) — **no era marker with them: (not stated).**
    Elsewhere in the book he carries no dates at all: **(not stated)** in chunks 001, 003, 004, 006, 009,
    010, 011, 012.
  - **Genealogy and birth**: "He was the great-grandson of **Shah Abd al-Karim, or Shah Karim**. He was
    born in **Hala** in a Sayyid family." (p. 91, folio 78) **No death place is given anywhere: (not
    stated).**
  - **Religious identity, stated once**: he was "a Muslim, a Sayyid, who was a **wujudi**, preaching
    throughout his poetry the Oneness of God." (p. 178, folio 165) **No silsila is given anywhere in the
    book: (not stated).**
  - **The historical record is thin, and the book says so**: "Many legends were framed about him,
    although it is still difficult to trace his historical character." (p. 91, folio 78) **The book
    recounts none of those legends: (not stated).**
  - **Standing**: "a Sindhi Sufi poet who is acclaimed by all Sindhis as the greatest poet of Sindh and
    the Sindhi language" (p. 91, folio 78); "Shah is the symbol of Sindhi culture for Muslims as well as
    for Hindus, in Pakistan as well as in India." (p. 92, folio 79); "Shah Latif retains a unique place in
    Sindhi poetry and literature, and needless to say, not all of his chapters (surs) are equally
    appreciated by the Hindu Sufis." (pp. 94–95, folios 81–82); "**the king of the Sufi poets of Sindh**"
    (p. 213, folio 200, Ch. 4 n. 4); "the greatest Sufi poet" (p. 108, folio 95). Named as one of the
    "'three great Sufi teachers'" (p. 49, folio 36), and as first of the "'trinity of the Sindhi poets'
    by Ram Panjwani (Panjwani 1987: 29): ShahAbd al-Latif, Sachal Sarmast and Sami" (p. 91, folio 78) —
    the three also called "the 'Great Integrators'", a coinage the book attributes in its text to Motilal
    Jotwani but cites as `(Motwani 1986: 150)`.
  - **Placed in the Bhakti movement by a quoted author, and Boivin objects**: "Unfortunately, there is no
    comprehensive argumentation supplying his claim that 'Shah Abdul Latif (was) a late participant in
    the Bhakti movement' (Jotwani 1996: 121)." (p. 88, folio 75 — the parenthesis "(was)" is the book's
    own). The same claim recurs: "'the late participaters in the Bhakti movement which had a major impact
    in Sindh through the spread of Guru Nanak's teaching' (Motwani 1986: 155)" — "here he spoke especially
    of Shah Latif and Sachal" (p. 91, folio 78). **`participaters` is as printed.**
  - **The jogis, and a pilgrimage itinerary**: "he was very fascinated by the jogis, considering them the
    models of renunciation" (p. 91, folio 78); "He probably travelled with them on a number of Hindu
    pilgrimages, including **Dwarka and Hinglaj**. The technical vocabulary used by Shah Latif to name
    the jogis gives evidence that they were Nathpanthis, and that they were therefore an important
    religious community in eighteenth-century Sindh." (p. 92, folio 79) **The hedge "probably" is the
    book's own; no date and no companion is named for the pilgrimages: (not stated).** "The jogi, a
    vernacular form of yogi, is usually the model of renunciation in Sindhi Sufi poetry, especially in
    Shah Abd al-Latif's work" (p. 120, folio 107); the jogi reference "is fully expressed in Shah Abd
    al-Latif's verses" (p. 105, folio 92).
  - **Doctrine**: "His emphasis on the wahdat-e wujud furthered his resonance among different populations
    because it indicated that distinctions, especially religious, were disposable in the face of unity."
    (p. 92, folio 79) But the technical expression is **not** his: "The expression wahdat-e wujud is not
    included in the lexicon, of course, because it was not used by Shah Abd al-Latif." (p. 100, folio 87)
    The word he did use is `wahdat`, rendered into Sindhi as `hekro`; and "tuhnjo Allah hekro, 'Your God
    is One' – which first appears in Shah Karim's verses and then in Shah Abd al-Latif (Lalwani 1890,
    vol. I: 45)" (p. 100, folio 87).
  - **Influences**: "Among his influences, Jalal al-Din Rumi was so dominant that his poetry is sometimes
    called **the Masnawi of Sindh**." (p. 92, folio 79, endnote 5)
  - **Quoted as a doctrinal authority by a Theosophist, and both claims are Parsram's, not the book's**:
    "He added that Shah Abd al-Latif himself referred to the 'one curved word', namely om. He provided a
    translation of Shah Latif's quotation, according to which om is as light in the darkness, and that
    the Sufi has to keep mim (m) in his mind, and place alif (a) before." (p. 49, folio 36); and "he had
    met a very old Sufi who claimed to be a recipient of their teaching, as Shah Latif had been before
    him" (p. 50, folio 37). Both reported from Parsram 1924.
  - **Quoted for a doctrine of secrecy**: "Rochaldas quotes Shah Abd al-Latif's verses, where the poets
    express that silence is golden, and exposure is harmful, and that love is great but secrecy is
    greater (Hari 1995: 211)." (p. 166, folio 153) The context is the warning that a disciple who exposes
    his ishq haqiqi will share "the destiny… of Mansur Hallaj".
  - **Quoted for a definition of the Sufi, at second hand**: "Panjwani quotes him in defining who is a
    Sufi: ‘A Sufi belongs to no sect. No one can label him. He fights with his own selves and divulges
    nothing. He extends help even to those who are hostile to him’ (Panjwani 1987: 34)." (p. 160,
    folio 147) **The quotation is Panjwani's attribution, not the book's own text of Shah Abd al-Latif.**

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The darbar at Bhit Shah as the book's type case for a shared Sufi tomb — the hardest shrine fact in
    the book for this row**: "The first category is the most common: a sacred place is a Sufi tomb which
    is visited both by Muslims and Hindus. For example, this is the case with **Shah Abd al-Latif's darbar
    in Bhit Shah**. The religious identity of the saint is not challenged: he was a Muslim, a Sayyid, who
    was a wujudi, preaching throughout his poetry the Oneness of God. The Hindus belonging to different
    paths and castes come every day and for the annual urs. They go to the tomb, circumambulating around
    it, touching it, and then attend the **Shah jo rag**, the singing of Shah's poetry by the local
    faqirs." (p. 178, folio 165) **The ʿurs is dated only as "annual"; no month, no day, no attendance, no
    custodian: (not stated).**
  - **The shrine named as a negative, and a custodian asserted but unnamed**: "while Shah Abd al-Latif's
    poetry was highly praised, **his darbar at Bhit Shah is not mentioned as a reference point**, and no
    link has been mentioned between the local sajjada nashin and the faqirs." (pp. 60–61, folios 47–48)
    **A sajjada nashin at Bhit Shah is asserted to exist and is not named: (not stated).**
  - **His tomb as an instance of shared ziyarat, in a sentence the book leaves ambiguous**: "Known as
    ziyarat, both Hindus and Muslims bring textiles to put on the tomb, or they only touch and kiss the
    tomb for obtaining blessings from the buried saint. This is the case of the visit to the dargah by the
    great mystic poet Shah Abd al-Latif." (p. 50, folio 37) **The wording is the book's and is ambiguous
    as printed — it can be read as the dargah *of* the poet or a visit *by* him; recorded as printed, not
    resolved.**
  - **The site named once more, and only inside a publisher's name**: the 1985 *Shah jo risalo* is issued
    by the "**Shah Abdul Latif Bhitshah Cultural Centre Committee**" — the full entry as printed: "Shah
    Abd al-Latif Bhittai, 1985, *Shah jo risalo alias Ganje Latif*, 3 volumes, Revised, Annotated and
    Translated by Muhammad Yakoob Agha, Hyderabad, Shah Abdul Latif Bhitshah Cultural Centre Committee"
    (p. 232, folio 219). **This is the only place in the index chunks where "Bhitshah" is printed.**
  - **No tomb description, no building date, no architecture, no ʿurs date, no custodian's name, no
    Auqaf, no revenue and no visitor figure is given for Bhit Shah anywhere in the book: (not stated).**
  - **Index**: the locator run `al-Latif, Shah Abd` at folios **1, 4, 21, 35–7, 48, 55, 75, 78–80, 81–2,
    87, 92–3, 107, 142, 143, 144, 145, 147, 148, 152, 153, 165, 173, 187, 199 n.13, 200 n.4** (p. 239,
    folio not stated) — the longest run in that chunk for any archive figure. A second run for his book,
    listed separately from the man: `Shah jo risalo (al-Latif)` at folios **1, 4, 21, 34–5, 50, 79, 87,
    142, 157** (p. 249, folio 236); the two runs share folios 1, 4, 21 and 87.

- **Practices & Events**
  - **His poetry sung, and the one place it is not used.** At Bhit Shah, the "Shah jo rag, the singing of
    Shah's poetry by the local faqirs" (p. 178, folio 165); "the bhagat's repertoire always includes Sufi
    poetry – **Shah jo Risalo** – first as well as other Sufi kalams. Nonetheless, it is an oral
    transmission." (p. 170, folio 157) **The book does not on that page say whose work the Shah jo Risalo
    is: (not stated).** Chanted at Daulatram's versi (p. 150, folio 137). **Against that, the book's
    sharpest negative about him**: "it is amazing to observe that, while the poetry of the greatest Sufi
    poet, Shah Abd al-Latif, is sung during satsangs, **there is no ritual use during the different
    ceremonies**. Furthermore, his poetry, which is praised by everyone as being the quintessence of
    Sindhi poetry and literature, **does not provide data for any prayer**" (p. 108, folio 95).
  - **His verses preached from an Indian pulpit, on a dated day**: "On **23 May 1957**, Rochaldas comes to
    Poona to meet him, and Sadhu Vaswani asks him to deliver a sermon. His sermon is a comment on Shah Abd
    al-Latif's verses, completed by quotations from the Jap Sahib. It focuses on the importance of the
    shabd (Hari 1995: 117–23)." (p. 165, folio 152)
  - **On a poster at another shrine's fair, paired with a god**: "In this darbar, during the annual fair
    in **April of 2016**, there was a huge poster staging Shah Abd al-Latif and Jhulelal, with the
    epigram: **Sab ka malik ek hai**." (p. 186, folio 173) The author reads the poster: "I would argue
    this is a renewed version of the wahdat-e wujud, expressed in a more 'modern' language, but with a
    Hindu flavour overall, and bridging the Sindhi figures with the popular Shirdi Sai Baba." (p. 186,
    folio 173)
  - **Counted in the Indian anthologies.** First of the Sindhi Sufis in Vaswani 1955 (p. 156, folio 143);
    focus of Mallik's lectures as "Shah Latif" (p. 155, folio 142); first of the "trinity" in Paru
    Chawla's anthology (p. 161, folio 148); **third** in Panjwani's: "First comes Sachal Sarmast, with
    forty-six abstracts from his Sindhi poetry. Second is Bedil, and third is Shah Abd al-Latif, with
    respectively thirty-eight and thirty-six quotations." (p. 158, folio 145); second most quoted after
    Bedil in Rochaldas's dialogues — "is the second most quoted poet, after Shah Abd al-Latif, and before
    Sachal" (p. 98, folio 85) — but **under-represented in one breviary**: "We also notice **only one
    quotation from Shah Abd al-Latif**, and two from Sachal." (p. 168, folio 155) "The choice of these
    poets instead of the most acclaimed, such as Shah Abd al-Latif, who was much quoted in Rochaldas's
    spiritual dialogues, should mean something." (p. 168, folio 155) **And a notable absence**: "There is
    a notable absence: Shah Abd al-Latif, the king of the Sufi poets of Sindh." (p. 213, folio 200,
    Ch. 4 n. 4)

- **Significance**
  - **The shared ground between Hindu and Muslim Sindhis before partition**: the Sindhi Hindus "shared
    their passion for Sufi poetry with Muslim Sindhis and others, especially through Shah Abd al-Latif 's
    Shah jo Risalo" (p. 14, folio not stated — **the space before the apostrophe is the text layer's**).
  - **The canonization of his corpus, dated and attributed twice.** "In terms of corpus, … has promoted,
    in addition to 'classical' poetry, through the canonization of Shah jo Risalo by Shah Abd al-Latif,
    new sciences, new literary forms and, in short, a new approach to knowledge" (p. 17, folio 4). "In
    Sindh, a printing press was brought to Karachi in the 1850s, but the main event in relation to Sufism
    came with the printing in **1866** of the Shah jo Risalo" (p. 48, folio 35). "The work authored by
    Shah Abdul Latif was 'canonized' by the British **in the middle of the nineteenth century**, and later
    on re-appropriated by the Sindhi intelligentsia. Since the latter wanted all of the Sindhi classes to
    understand Shah properly, they started to publish glossaries and lexicons to explain the difficult
    words and expressions, or to translate them into Sindhi, knowing that most of them were Arabic
    expressions. See Boivin 2014." (p. 212, folio 199, Ch. 3 n. 13) And: "following the objectification of
    Sufism after the canonization of the Shah jo Risalo authored by Shah Abd al-Latif (d. 1752), the Sufi
    poetry was accepted as an epitome of Sindhi culture, and thus of Sindhi identity" (p. 155, folio 142).
  - **The corpus as the last surviving monument, in the author's elegiac close**: "Except for some
    monuments such as the Shah jo Risalo by the Sufi Shah Abd al-Latif, what will be left of the wonderful
    Sufi poetry in Sindhi among the new generations?" (p. 34, folio 21)
  - **The benchmark he sets and the limit he marks**: the Hindu corpus prefers poets "who refer the most
    to Hinduism, not only in mentioning the figure of the jogi as was done by classical Sufi poets such as
    Shah Abd al-Latif, but going further so as to refer directly to Hindu gods, and to the episodes of the
    sacred Hindu scriptures" (p. 109, folio 96); the Darazi and Jahaniyya poets mentioned Hindu deities
    "going further than Shah Abd al-Latif, for whom the jogi, the Nathpanthi renunciant, was already the
    pattern of the mystic path" (p. 200, folio 187); "As was the case with Shah Abd al-Latif, Sachal uses
    different words to name the Hindu renunciants… but not varied as is the case in Shah Abd al-Latif's
    poetry" (p. 106, folio 93).
  - **`Shah jo Risalo` and its contents.** "In '**Sur Sorath**', a much-acclaimed chapter of the poetical
    work the Shah jo Risalo, the minstrel Bijal defies Rao Diach, the king of Junagarh. The power of his
    lyre (chang) is such that the queens start crying and the forts begin to crumble." (p. 92, folio 79)
    "The many terms used by the poet to describe the minstrel, as charan, mirasi, jajik or manghanar,
    reflect not only his own interest in music but also the role played by the musician in the regional
    society. Shah Latif attributes a kind of magic power to him." (p. 92, folio 79) "The figure of the
    'mendicant minstrel', the charan, is dominant throughout Shah Latif's poetry." (p. 92, folio 79)
    "although the chang is the most quoted, one can find reference to others – like the kamach, for
    example." (p. 92, folio 79) The glossary defines the unit: "**Sur**: a Sindhi word with the meaning of
    chapter in Shah Ab al-Latif [text-layer? Shah Abd al-Latif]'s work; it is also the equivalent of rag,
    the musical mode in Indian music." (p. 220, folio 207)
  - **Karbala and a Shia genre, given as an open research question.** "Another neglected issue addressed
    by Shah Latif is the tragedy of Karbala. In the Shah jo Risalo, there is another chapter (sur) of
    special interest: **Sur Kedaro**. The whole chapter is devoted to the martyrdom (shahadat) of the
    third Shia imam, Husayn." (p. 92, folio 79) "A thorough study should be made on the reasons for which
    Shah Latif introduced a Shia topic in his poetry; he was likely influenced by Persia, where the
    marsiyyo genre, the dirge devoted to the martyrdom of Husayn and his family, was booming." (pp. 92–93,
    folios 79–80 — **the hedge "likely" is the book's own**). "Shah Latif's references to the tragedy of
    Karbala distinguish him from his predecessors in classic Sindhi Sufi poetry." (p. 93, folio 80) **A
    priority claim and the structure of Sur Kedaro**: "Shah Latif is the **first Sindhi poet to write a
    marsiyyo in Sindhi**, and after him, the genre was adopted by many other poets." (p. 93, folio 80,
    endnote 6) "His marsiyyo did not strictly follow the format of the Persian dirge; instead, Sur Kedaro
    is divided into four parts." — first, "the coming of Moharram, the month of mourning for the martyrdom
    of Husayn"; second, "the apparently helpless state of imams during the ensuing conflict"; third,
    "their bravery in fighting and how their murder was a part of God's plan"; fourth, "their union with
    God after death" (p. 93, folio 80). "Shah Laṭif's depictions of the different episodes of the battle
    are made very vivid with precise details." (p. 93, folio 80)
  - **The difficulty of his language, used as evidence, and the glossaries it produced.** "While Shah
    Latif's poetry can be difficult to understand, especially in terms of the somewhat ancient vocabulary
    he uses, Sachal and especially Sami are far more accessible. The evidence is given by the fact that
    the technical lexicon of Shah's poetry was the object of a study as soon as **1913** by Mirza Qalich
    Beg, while… similar works were only recently published for Sachal and Sami." (p. 95, folio 82) "more
    than two decades after the first publication of Shah jo Risalo in 1866, Sindhi literati thought it
    necessary to publish Shah's works so that his poetry could be more accessible to the people."
    (p. 100, folio 87) "The first glossary was published in **1890** by **Lilaram Watanmal Lalwani** in a
    book he wrote in English, with the title *The Life, Religion, and Poetry of Shah Latif*. It includes a
    glossary of 'all the difficult words and expressions occurring in Shah jo Risalo'." (p. 100, folio 87)
    "In **1913**, he [Mirza Qalich Beg] published in Sindhi the first book totally devoted to Shah Abd
    al-Latif's technical lexicon." (p. 100, folio 87)
  - **The Hindu literati's biographical turn**: Parsram's "first books authored by Parsram himself were
    nevertheless devoted to Shah Abd al-Latif ’s work, but not to his poetry. He was more interested in
    teasing out the moral values of the folktales (**akhaniyyun**) used by Shah Abd al-Latif. After
    publishing two books on the topic… finally a biography of Shah Abd al-Latif in **1926**." (p. 48,
    folio 35) Of the 1921 *Sufi sagora*: "A portion of the work is also devoted to the life of Shah Abd
    al-Latif, and probably borrowed from Parsram's own publication on the same topic." (p. 48, folio 35)
    "Consequently, in **1945**, thus before migrating to India, he published a book on Shah Abd al-Latif,
    which was **republished in India in 1981** (Panjwani 1981)." (p. 157, folio 144) His place in the
    trinity in Panjwani's own image: "‘He (Shah Abd al-Latif) brings the kettle on the boil, Sachal takes
    the lid off from it, while Sami passes on the beverage to the rank and file of the people so that they
    may be brought to the intense awareness of God even when engaged in the day-to-day affairs of life’
    (Panjwani 1987: 29–30). Shah Abd al-Latif ’s [text-layer? Shah Abd al-Latif’s] poetry was of a
    surpassing beauty in language and thought." (p. 160, folio 147)
  - **A darbar in India tracing its descent to him**: a booklet "clearly indicates he was an 'integrator',
    since his spiritual genealogy goes back to the Sufis of Sindh, including **Shah Latif**, Bedil, Rohal
    Shah and others (Jotwani 1968: 208)" (p. 68, folio 55). **That is the whole of the evidence there: a
    bare short name inside a list.**
  - **Bibliography entries on and by him, exactly as printed**: "Advani, Kalyan, *Shah jo Risalo – risalo
    shah Abdul Latif Bhittai Jo*, Bombay, Smt Liladevi C. Vaswani, 1955" (p. 229, folio not stated);
    "Beg, Mirza Qalich, *Lughat-e Latifi: Shah ji risali mushkil lafzan ji mana*, Hyderabad, Sindhi Adabi
    Board, 1913" (p. 229, folio not stated); "Allana, G., *Four Classical Poets of Sind: Minyoon Shah
    Inat, Shah Abdul Latif, Sachal Sarmast, Sami*, Jamshoro, Institute of Sindhology, University of Sindh,
    1983" (p. 229, folio not stated); "Panjwani, Ram, *Latif*, Bombay, Bharat Jivan Sahita Mandal, 1981
    (1st edn, 1945)" (p. 231, folio 218); "Lalwani, L. W., *The Life, Religion and Poetry of Shah Latif: A
    Greatest Poet of Sind*, Lahore, Sang-e Meel Publications, 1994 [1890]" (p. 236, folio 223 — **the
    square-bracketed 1890 is the book's own**); and Boivin's own "'Shah Abd al-Latif's Risalo and the
    Reshaping of Sufism in Nineteenth-Century Sindh: Imperial Policy and the Impact of Printing in British
    India'… 2014, pp. 99–120" (p. 233, folio 220). **The index carries the Lalwani title separately as
    "*The Life, Religion, and Poetry of Shah Latif* (Lalwani) 87" (p. 245, folio 232) — with a comma the
    bibliography does not print.**

### dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed — Dargah / Roza Sufi Shah Inayat Shaheed

The book's names for the site: **"Jhok Sharif"**, "Jhok", "the dargah of Jhok Sharif", "the Sufi centre
of Jhok Sharif". For the saint: **"Sufi Shah Inayat"**, "Shah Inayat from Jhok Sharif", "Shah Inayat of
Jhok Sharif", "Shah Inayat (from Jhok Sharif)". Present in chunks 001, 003, 006, 007, 011, 012 and 013.

- **Saint**
  - **No death date, no birth date and no silsila is stated for Shah Inayat himself anywhere in the book
    — (not stated)** (chunks 006, 011), though the lodge is repeatedly tied to the Qadiri tariqa through
    its sajjada nashins. **He is never named at all in chunks 001, 003, 007 or 012–013**, where the site
    carries the row on its own.
  - **His master, and that master's grave in India**: "Since he knew that a Sufi Master from Jhok Sharif,
    **Sayyid Shah Mehmud Shah al-Qadiri**, had been buried in Bijapur, in Karnataka, he decided to look
    for his grave. Sayyid Shah Mehmud Shah al-Qadiri was the Master of Shah Inayat of Jhok Sharif."
    (p. 124, folio 111)
  - **The book's separate mention of "Shah Inayat" as one of the "'three great Sufi teachers'"** — "who
    are Shah Inayat, Shah Abd al-Latif and Sachal Sarmast (Boivin 2016)" (p. 49, folio 36) — **was
    deliberately not used by Pass 1 to make this mapping**, and the book separately describes a third man,
    "Shah Inat (1623–1712)… Sayyid Inayat Allah Rizvi" (p. 211, folio 198, Ch. 3 n. 3).

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The shrine located to taluqa level, in a glossary definition — the most precise location the book
    gives for it**: "**Jhok Sharif**: a village with an important Sufi Centre in **Taluqa Mirpur Bathoro
    in Southern Sindh** where **Sufi Shah Inayat's dargah** is." (p. 219, folio 206)
  - **The site characterised and located in the body**: Nasir Muhammad Faqir was "a Qadiri who was the
    disciple of the Master (murshid) of Jhok Sharif, **a great Sufi centre located in the extreme South
    of Sindh**." (p. 141, folio 128) "Jhok, contrary to Daraza, is located in Southern Sindh" (p. 60,
    folio 47). **The murshid of that clause is not named: (not stated).**
  - **Its order, in the Annex 2 chart**: "**Jhok Sharif (Qadiriyya)**", with "Dalpat Sufi Mulchand Faqir
    (via Rakhiyyal Shah) Molchand Kripalani" beneath (p. 227, folio 214). **The chart's cells have run
    together in the text layer.**
  - **Custodianship, named — and the names do not agree.** "spent many years in Jhok Sharif, and he was
    initiated to the Qadiri tariqa by the sajjada nashin, **Sufi Abd al-Sattar Shah**", who set him to
    "cleaning the stable of his Master's mare" and, when he reached "the highest spiritual degree, that of
    salvation… allowed him to give nam" (p. 120, folio 107; pp. 120–121, folios 107–108). Against that:
    "the official website of **Sufi Ataullah Sattari, the sajjada nashin of Jhok**" (p. 128, folio 115).
    **Whether these are one man at two dates, two men, or a reading error is not resolvable from the
    book.** A third form: "**Sufi Hazrat Abdul Sattar Shah** of Jhok Sharif" (p. 101, folio 88). The
    glossary makes the name a recurrence rather than a man: "**Abdul Sattar**: the name of several Sufi
    masters at the dargah of Jhok Sharif." (p. 218, folio not stated) A later, unnamed incumbent: "the
    sajjada nashin of Jhok" (p. 124, folio 111); and "He was a devout follower of the **sajadah nashin**
    of Jhok Sharif." (p. 59, folio 46 — **the book spells the office `sajadah nashin` there and `sajjada
    nashin` on p. 61, folio 48**). **No dates for any of them: (not stated).** Indexed "Shah, Abd
    al-Sattar 107–8" (p. 249, folio 236).
  - **Named as one of the three Sindh seats whose sajjada nashins still hold the lineages of Hindu Sufis
    in India**: "They are the sajjada nashin of Daraza, of Tando Jahaniya and of Jhok Sharif. At each of
    them, a great Sufi poet is attached: Sachal Sarmast, Qutub Ali Shah and **Nasir Faqir**" (p. 27,
    folio 14). **The poet attached to Jhok Sharif by list position is "Nasir Faqir"; the row's principal
    figure, Sufi Shah Inayat Shaheed, is not named anywhere in that chunk: (not stated), nor is any date,
    ʿurs or building detail. The one-to-one pairing of the two lists is the obvious reading but the book
    does not state it.**
  - **Index**: under `dargah(s)`, "of Jhok Sharif 46, 111, 115" (p. 241, folio 228); under `sajjada
    nashin`, "of Jhok Sharif 46" (p. 248, folio 235); under `urs`, "of Jhok Sharif 46" (p. 251,
    folio 238); and as a top-level toponym, "Jhok Sharif 46, 47, 105, 106, 111, 128" (p. 244, folio 231).
    **Dargah, custodian and ʿurs all converge on folio 46. The index does not name the saint at any of
    the three subentries: (not stated).** It is also **indexed a second time, inverted, as "Sharif, Jhok
    14"** (p. 249, folio 236) — an index defect, since folio 14 appears in neither of the other Jhok runs.

- **Practices & Events**
  - **Its ʿurs, and the poetry sung at it**: "His Sufi poetry in Sindhi is voluminous and his verses
    (kafis) are still a part of the main poetry, which is sung in **the urs of Jhok Sharif** (Boivin
    2012b)." (p. 59, folio 46) **No date, month or duration for that ʿurs: (not stated).**
  - **Its poetry and festival calendar reaching India**: Dalpat's kalam is sung "in several dargahs
    located in Sindh and run by Muslim sajjada nashins, as for example in Jhok Sharif" and "It is also
    sung at Jhok Sharif in Sindh" (p. 118, folio 105); the melo at Bijapur is kept "**every year from the
    17th to the 19th of Moharram, as it is celebrated in Jhok Sharif**" (p. 124, folio 111).
  - **Shah Inayat as one of four versis of a Hindu Sufi path**: one of the four is "Shah Inayat (from Jhok
    Sharif)" (p. 122, folio 109), and of the four death anniversaries "the dates… are taken as sacred:
    **the 11, the 15, the 17 and the 27**" (p. 122, folio 109). **Which of the four numerals belongs to
    Shah Inayat is not stated; the list is not matched to the names.** (The glossary later fixes one of
    them to a different figure: Abd al-Qadir Jilani's "death anniversary, known as Yarhen, the 11th, since
    he passed away on the 11th of Rabi al-Sani" — p. 218, folio not stated.)
  - **An institutional claim made online, and restated against a biography**: "the official website
    devoted to Jhok Sharif claims to have a dargah in Bijapur which is affiliated with Jhok
    (http://www.sufisattari.com). Under the heading 'affiliated dargahs', the 'Hazrat Soofi Abdul Malik'
    is mentioned." (p. 124, folio 111) The author immediately flags his own ignorance: "although I do not
    know what the relations were between Molchand and the sajjada nashin of Jhok" (p. 124, folio 111).
    And: "in the narrative found in Molchand's biography, there is no mention that Abd al-Malik's dargah
    in Bijapur is affiliated to Jhok Sharif, while the official website of Sufi Ataullah Sattari, the
    sajjada nashin of Jhok, claims it is." (p. 128, folio 115) The author's verdict on the pilgrimage
    built out of that affiliation: "this attempt at creating a Muslim Sufi pilgrimage from Sindh in India
    is unique, but, unfortunately, it does not look to be very successful." (p. 128, folio 115)

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **Visions of Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani directing a disciple to the shrine**, sourced to the path's own
    published kalam: "from his early childhood on, he had visions involving Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani. In one
    of them, the founder of the Qadiriyya told him **it was time for him to reach Jhok Sharif, to take nam
    from the Sufi Master** (Jogi Sain 1996: 4)." (p. 120, folio 107)

- **Significance**
  - **Its structural importance to the book's argument**: "it is interesting to observe that the Sufi
    centre of Jhok Sharif plays a pivotal role since **most of the Hindu Sufis were in touch with Jhok,
    directly or indirectly, often through their own Sufi Master**." (p. 60, folio 47) "the two main Sufi
    traditions that were very popular among the Hindus were Daraza and Jhok." (p. 60, folio 47) "Again,
    the invocation of Jhok Sharif contributes to the construction of a network of sacred territories which
    are all located in Sindh." (p. 141, folio 128)
  - **Jhok as an initiatory centre for others**: "It was at about the age of forty that Rakhiyyal Shah
    adopted the Sufi path and went to the dargah of Jhok Sharif, where he stayed under the guidance of his
    Master." (p. 59, folio 46) — **his Master is not named: (not stated).** Rohal Faqir "was a disciple of
    Shah Inayat from Jhok Sharif" (p. 107, folio 94); the guru Asardas "was himself a follower of the Sufi
    masters of Jhok Sharif" (p. 118, folio 105); Dalpat Sufi, "Himself a follower of Jhok Sharif" (p. 118,
    folio 105).
  - **Boivin's own article on the site is in the bibliography, and is the only bibliography entry devoted
    to a single archive shrine**: "Boivin, Michel, 'The Sufi Centre of Jhok Sharif in Sindh (Pakistan):
    Questioning the ziyarat as a Social Process', in Clinton Bennett and Charles M. Ramsey (eds), *South
    Asian Sufis: Devotion, Deviation, and Destiny*, New York, Continuum, 2012b, pp. 94–109" (p. 233,
    folio 220). **The suffixed year "2012b" is the book's own.**

### shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan — Shrine at Odero Lal (Udero Lal Teerath Asthan)

The book's names for the site: **"Udero Lal"** (the village and the cult centre), "the darbar of Udero
Lal", "the main darbar devoted to Jhulelal", "the huge sanctuary close to Hyderabad". Its figures are
**"Udero Lal"**, **"Jhulelal"**, **"Amar Lal"** and **"Shaykh Tahir"** (the archive row prints "Sheikh
Tahir"). Present in chunks 002, 003, 009, 010, 011, 012 and 013.

- **Saint**
  - **No death date, no birth date and no founding date for the figure — (not stated).** The book's
    identification of him is hedged twice over: "an attempt made to create a centralized path devoted to
    Jhulelal, then known as Amar Lal or Udero Lal, in the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries.
    Jhulelal was **probably** a modern figure of the Vedic god **Varun**, the god of the Indus River. He
    is briefly mentioned in historical sources" (p. 54, folio 41). **The historical sources are not named:
    (not stated).** And: "The Hindus worship Jhulelal, who they claim to be an avatar of the Vedic God
    Varun." (p. 178, folio 165)
  - **A Sufi genealogy built for him, in an unknown period**: "as is the case with Udero Lal, since a
    tazkira refers to him as a disciple of the same **Bahauddin Zakariyya** already mentioned" (p. 51,
    folio 38). **The tazkira is not named, not dated and not located: (not stated)**; the book's framing
    is "a Sufi genealogy has been built – if in an unknown period" (p. 51, folio 38).
  - **The Muslim figure's silsila, hedged by the book itself**: "The Muslims venerate a Sufi, Shaykh
    Tahir, who **could have been a Sohrawardi**." (p. 178, folio 165) **(not stated) as a fact.**
  - **The book never uses the name "Jhulelal" in chunk 002 and never names "Sheikh Tahir" there: (not
    stated) in both cases.**

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The sanctuary and its date — the only building date the book gives for any Sindh shrine**:
    "Although there are several temples dedicated to Udero Lal, the main centre of the Daryapanth is in
    Udero Lal, the name given to the village which houses **a vast sanctuary dating back to the
    seventeenth century**." (pp. 42–43, folios 29–30) **The district (the archive row says Matiari) is
    (not stated); no custodian, no mela date, no visitor number, no endowment: (not stated).** The
    "several temples dedicated to Udero Lal" are **not named or located: (not stated).**
  - **An architectural fact, from a note**: "the pre-partition temples devoted to Jhulelal in Sindh were
    built with a dome, as were some Sikh samadhis. **The main site known as the darbar of Udero Lal hosts
    many domes** (Boivin and Rajpal 2018)." (p. 210, folio 197, Ch. 2 n. 5) **No building date, no number
    of domes, no custodian: (not stated).**
  - **The two-chapel structure, this row's defining fact**: "In Udero Lal, for example, to which I shall
    return, the Hindus venerate Udero Lal in **a chapel of the sanctuary**, and the Muslims worship Shaykh
    Tahir in **another chapel of the same sanctuary** (Boivin and Rajpal 2018)." (p. 51, folio 38) This is
    the book's third category of shared worship: "although there is a single sanctuary, the Hindus worship
    the saint with a specific name and in a specific chapel, while the Muslims give him another name and
    worship him in another chapel" (pp. 50–51, folios 37–38).
  - **The book's type case for a double-identity shrine, with the traffic running one way only**: "The
    second category is also relatively common. It is a sacred place hosting one or two tombs and
    catafalques, and the saint has a double identity, Muslim and Hindu. **The best example is the darbar
    of Udero Lal.** The Hindus worship Jhulelal, who they claim to be an avatar of the Vedic God Varun.
    The Muslims venerate a Sufi, Shaykh Tahir, who could have been a Sohrawardi. In this case, this is a
    shared space, but **only the Hindus pay a visit to Shaykh Tahir's tomb. The Muslims do not go to the
    space devoted to Jhulelal.**" (p. 178, folio 165)
  - **Located by distance and by neighbour, three ways.** "a meeting organized by several Hindu panchayats
    of Sindh at Udero Lal, in the main darbar devoted to Jhulelal, which was located **about 50 kilometres
    north of Hyderabad**" (p. 54, folio 41). "In April of 2016, I started a new research project on the
    darbar of Udero Lal, **the huge sanctuary close to Hyderabad** where Hindus and Muslims worship a dual
    figure: Jhulelal/Shaykh Tahir." (p. 184, folio 171) **The archive row's "Odero Lal Village (near Tando
    Adam), Matiari District" is not the book's wording and must not be back-filled from the row.**
  - **A well inside the darbar**: "the presence of a well is also strongly reminiscent of Jhulelal, since
    he is said to have disappeared **in a well set up in the darbar of Udero Lal**." (p. 186, folio 173)
    **The book's hedge "is said to have disappeared" is its own.**
  - **Index**: under `darbars (shrine)`, "of Udero Lal 165, 198 n.5" (p. 241, folio 228); "Tahir, Shaykh
    38, 165" (p. 251, folio 238) — **the archive names "Sheikh Tahir" as this row's principal figure and
    no other row names him, so the mapping is unambiguous even though the evidence is a bare inverted
    name; no dates, no place, no shrine, no gloss: (not stated).** The entry "Udero Lal 29–30, 38, 41,
    165" (p. 251, folio 238) carries **exactly the two folios of "Tahir, Shaykh 38, 165"** among its four;
    **the index nowhere states that Shaykh Tahir and Udero Lal are the same figure: (not stated).**

- **Practices & Events**
  - **Two custodianships and two fairs at one site, with disputes**: "The annual fair is performed
    **separately**, and there is a **gaddi nashin for the Hindus** and a **sajjada nashin for the
    Muslims**. Such a scenario should not be idealized since the groups interact very little, and many
    disputes arise regularly." (p. 178, folio 165) **Neither incumbent is named, the fair is dated only as
    "annual", and no dispute is itemised: (not stated).** Against that, "In this configuration, there is
    only one annual fair" is said of a different category (p. 178, folio 165).
  - **The fair given a month and a year once**: "In this darbar, during the annual fair in **April of
    2016**, there was a huge poster staging Shah Abd al-Latif and Jhulelal, with the epigram: Sab ka malik
    ek hai." (p. 186, folio 173) **The fair's name, its length and its attendance are (not stated).**
  - **The cult's management and its ritual calendar**: "It is based on the deification of the Indus River.
    … The Daryapanth was managed by a priestly caste, **the Thakurs**. Most of the rituals were related to
    changes in the level of the Indus during a given year." (p. 42, folio 29) **The custodial body is given
    as a caste, not as a sajjada nashin or a trust.** The Daryapanth is dated only relatively: "The
    Daryapanth is in all probability the oldest of the two" (p. 42, folio 29), the other being the
    Nanakpanth.
  - **The priests, and their migration — recorded with the book's own caveat**: "The Thakurs were the
    priests of **Jhulelal**. In Sindh, they were settled in different cities but they migrated to India
    **from 1947 onwards**. Here, the Thakurs came from **Sehwan Sharif**." (p. 210, folio 197, Ch. 2 n. 6)
    And an orthographic distinction the book insists on: "Not to be confused with the Thakurs, the priests
    of Jhulelal. In Sindhi, the Rajut Thakur is written with a long 'a', while the Jhulelal Thakur is
    written with a short 'a'." (p. 217, folio 204, Ch. 7 n. 7) **This priesthood is of Jhulelal generally,
    not of Odero Lal specifically, and the book says the Thakurs came from Sehwan Sharif, not from Odero
    Lal.**
  - **A communal meeting held in the darbar**, dated only to "the last decade of the nineteenth century"
    (p. 54, folio 41). **The exact year, the panchayats' names and the meeting's outcome are (not
    stated).**

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **Udero Lal's emergence from the Indus**, attributed by the book to tradition: "According to
    tradition, Udero Lal emerged from the river to help the Hindus persecuted by a Muslim ruler in **the
    mid-tenth century**." (p. 42, folio 29) **The era marker is absent and the Muslim ruler is not named:
    (not stated).**
  - **A god who disappeared into a well**, given as report (p. 186, folio 173; quoted above).

- **Significance**
  - **Its social base**: the Daryapanth is one of "two main cults" followed "Among the Lohanas and related
    mercantile groups" (p. 42, folio 29), and a Sindhi's choice between "a Sufi darbar, a Nanakpanthi
    darbar or a Daryapanthi darbar" is the chapter's guiding question (p. 38, folio 25).
  - **A field project of the author's own**: "With a team, we toured in Southern Sindh to map out the
    different shrines devoted to Jhulelal in Southern Sindh." (p. 184, folio 171) **No count of those
    shrines is given: (not stated).**
  - **A bibliography entry linking the site to the diaspora, in Boivin's own co-authored title**: "Boivin,
    Michel and Bhavna Rajpal, 'From Udero Lal in Sindh to Ulhasnagar in Maharashtra: Partition and
    Memories across Borders in the Tradition of Jhulelal', in Churnjeet Main and Anne Murphy (eds),
    *Partition and the Practice of Memory*, New York, Palgrave Mcmillan, 2018, pp. 43–62" (p. 233,
    folio 220).

### dargah-fateh-pur-sharif — Dargah Fateh Pur Sharif

The book's names for him: **"Sayyid Rakhiyyal Shah"**, **"Rakhiyyal Shah"**, **"Rakhiyyal Shah
al-Qadiri"**; the archive spells him "Syed Rakhyal Shah". Present in chunks 001, 003 and 012.

- **Saint**
  - **Life dates printed twice, identically: "(1846–1940)"** (p. 56, folio 43; p. 59, folio 46). **The
    archive row's `figure_died 1940` is supported.** **But a third, different birth year is printed
    elsewhere in the book: "Rakhiyyal Shah (1842–1940)" (p. 101, folio 88). 1846 and 1842 stand side by
    side and the book does not reconcile them.**
  - **Order**: "a Qadiri who was a writer of very popular kalams in Sindhi, Balochi and Persian" (p. 56,
    folio 43) and "a high-level Sufi of the **Qadiriyya**" (p. 59, folio 46). **The archive row's
    `Qadiri` is supported.** The nisba is printed "al-Qadiri" (p. 59, folio 46).
  - **Birth, upbringing and the dargah — with the place-name printed three ways inside two pages**:
    "Rakhiyyal Shah al-Qadiri had been born in the **village Halim Shah of Tehsil Mirpur, Balochistan**.
    His father, Miyyan Nur Maḥmad Shah [text-layer? — possibly `Muḥammad` or `Maḥmūd`], shifted to **the
    dargah of Fatehpur in Tehsil Khandwa**, where Rakhiyyal Shah was brought up." (p. 59, folio 46) And:
    "When he met Mulchand, Rakhiyyal Shah was a Sayyid from **Fathepur**, a village in the **Kachhi plain
    about 100 kilometres from Jacobabad**, but located in the Sindhi-speaking part of Baluchistan."
    (p. 59, folio 46) **The book prints `Fatehpur` and `Fathepur` on the same page, and places it in
    `Tehsil Khandwa` in one sentence and in `the Kachhi plain… 100 kilometres from Jacobabad` in the
    next. Both are recorded; neither is reconciled. The archive row's `Gandawah (Jhal Magsi District),
    Balochistan` is not the book's wording and must not be back-filled from the row.** His father is
    indexed "Shah, Nur Maḥmad 46" (p. 249, folio 236).
  - **His own Sufi formation, at another shrine**: "It was at about **the age of forty** that Rakhiyyal
    Shah adopted the Sufi path and went to **the dargah of Jhok Sharif**, where he stayed under the
    guidance of his Master." (p. 59, folio 46) "He was a devout follower of the sajadah nashin of Jhok
    Sharif." (p. 59, folio 46) **His Master is not named: (not stated).**
  - **His poetry and where it is still sung**: "He sang kalams in **Sindhi, Baluchi and Persian**"
    (p. 59, folio 46) — **the book prints `Balochi` on p. 56, folio 43 and `Baluchi` on p. 59, folio 46
    for the same language.** "His Sufi poetry in Sindhi is voluminous and his verses (kafis) are still a
    part of the main poetry, which is sung in the urs of Jhok Sharif (Boivin 2012b)." (p. 59, folio 46)
    Quoted for doctrine: "Rakhiyyal Shah uses the term **wahad wujud thiya wahdat**, 'With One their
    existence became unique' (Rakhiyyal Shah 2007: 6)" and uses "the word **hekal**, with the ending -al
    which is a mark of endearment in Sindhi" (p. 101, folio 88). On the divine vision: "Rakhiyyal Shah
    claims it is a **wonder** (Rakhiyyal Shah 1923: 39)." (p. 137, folio 124) On breath: "Rakhiyyal Shah
    weaves an inextricable relationship between breathing and remembrance, as in the following Sindhi
    verses" — "Mercy of Beloved is near me not! / Breath by breath (dam dam) remembers (yad kare) Love my
    heart!" (Rakhiyyal Shah 2007: 23) (p. 138, folio 125); "For Rakhiyyal Shah, the breath only allows for
    one to do meditation that leads to the Oneness of God (Rakhiyyal Shah 2005: 39)." (p. 138, folio 125)
    The framing there is "other Sufis of modern Sindh, such as the Qadiriyya" (p. 138, folio 125).
  - **As a master**: "As we shall see, he was Mulchand's Sufi Master." (p. 56, folio 43) "Mulchand became
    Rakhiyyal Shah's disciple, his **taleb**, and he was initiated by him. In Sindhi, it is said that he
    took the name '**nam wanhan**' from him, because during initiation, the Master gives his disciple a
    word on which he will perform meditation." (p. 59, folio 46) "Although he was initiated by Rakhiyyal
    Shah, his murshid is not especially venerated and I do not remember seeing his portrait in the places
    dedicated to Mulchand." (p. 75, folio 62) The Annex 2 chart carries "Dalpat Sufi Mulchand Faqir (**via
    Rakhiyyal Shah**) Molchand Kripalani" under "Jhok Sharif (Qadiriyya)" (p. 227, folio 214).
  - **His recognition of a saintly child**: "Rakhiyyal Shah quickly noticed that Mulchand was not playing
    with other children. **He might have said** that the boy would become a **pohtal**, a local term for
    those who have reached 'the colour of God' (Allah rang), as it is explained by Nabi Bakhsh Baloch
    (Baloch 1985: vol. II 100)." (p. 59, folio 46) **"might have said" is the book's own hedge.**
  - He is figure (c) in Figure 1.1, "Three Hindu Sufis with their Muslim murshids" (p. 58, folio 45;
    listed at p. 10, folio not stated as `(c) Rakhiyyal Shah`). **The illustrations list does not say
    whether he is one of the "Hindu Sufis" or one of the "Muslim murshids": (not stated).**

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **No shrine description, no tomb, no building date, no ʿurs of his own, no custodian and no visitor
    figure is given anywhere: (not stated).** The only dargah attached to his name is "the dargah of
    Fatehpur" his father moved to (p. 59, folio 46).
  - **Index**: under `dargah(s)`, "of **Fatehpur** 46" (p. 241, folio 228), and the place alone as
    "**Fathepur** 46" (p. 242, folio 229) — **two spellings of the same name on facing index pages.** The
    figure: "Shah, Sayyid Rakhiyyal 43, 45, 46, 62, 88, 124, 125" (p. 249, folio 236) and "al-Qadiri,
    Rakhiyyal Shah 46" (p. 239, folio not stated) — **the same man indexed twice under two inversions,
    both carrying folio 46.** "Jacobabad 46" carries its own index line (p. 244, folio 231), again at
    folio 46.

- **Significance**
  - **His two works in the corpus, publication details exactly as printed**: "Rakhiyyal Shah al-Qadiri,
    *Bahar al-ishq*, Jacobabad, 1923 (5th edn, 2005)" and "Rakhiyyal Shah al-Qadiri, Chandiyo, Hakim
    Eijaz Hasan (Compiled by), *Rakhiyyal Shah Jo Kalam*, Kandiyaro, Roshni Publications, 2007" (both
    p. 231, folio 218). **The second entry's author field is malformed as printed.**

### shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya — Shrine of Bahauddin Zakariya

The book's names for him: **"Bahauddin Zakariyya"**, **"Baha al-Din Zakariyya"**, **"Baha al-Din"**,
and **"the famous Sufi of Multan"**. Present in chunks 003, 009, 010 and 013.

- **Saint**
  - **Death year as printed, "(d. 1267)"** (p. 187, folio 174) — **which agrees with the archive row.
    No birth year: (not stated).** Chunks 003 and 009 give him no dates at all.
  - **Order, printed "Sohrawardi" / "Sohrawardiyya", not the archive's "Suhrawardi"**: "a famous Sufi
    Master of the **Sohrawardiyya**" (p. 51, folio 38); "initiated to the **Sohrawardi** tariqa by Baha
    al-Din Zakariyya" (p. 178, folio 165); "both Sufis were members of the Sohrawardi order, and that
    **Shah Rukn-e Alam was none other than the grandson and successor of Baha al-Din Zakariyya**."
    (p. 187, folio 174) **This is the book's only statement of his lineage.**
  - **Named as the initiator of another saint, in four passages**: "they recognize that Pithoro Pir was a
    Muslim Sufi initiated by Bahauddin Zakariyya" (p. 51, folio 38); "a tazkira refers to him as a
    disciple of the same Bahauddin Zakariyya already mentioned" (p. 51, folio 38) — of Udero Lal; Pithoro
    Pir "was initiated to the Sohrawardi tariqa by Baha al-Din Zakariyya. Thus, nobody challenges the
    fact that the saint was a Sufi." (p. 178, folio 165); and twice more in the summing-up, both hedged:
    "there is only one concrete link with Sufism: Pithoro Pir **would have been** initiated to Sufism by
    Baha al-Din Zakariyya. Such an element is incorporated into an oral tradition, and it can be related
    to a kind of legitimization of Pithoro's charisma." (p. 192, folio 179); "Pithoro Pir **is said to
    have been** initiated onto the Sohrawardi path by Baha al-Din Zakariyya." (p. 194, folio 181)

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **No shrine, no tomb, no building date and no toponym beyond the epithet "of Multan" is attached to
    him anywhere in the book: (not stated). The archive row's `Multan City` must not be back-filled from
    the row.**
  - **Index**: "Zakariyya, Baha al-Din 38, 165, 175, 179, 181" (p. 252, folio 239). **Five folios and
    nothing else: no dates, no place, no shrine, no silsila — (not stated).** His folio 175 is also the
    sole folio of chunk 012's "Alam, Shah Rukn-e 175" (p. 239, folio not stated); **the index does not
    connect the two entries: (not stated).** His folio 38 is the index's densest single folio in
    chunk 013, shared with "Tahir, Shaykh 38, 165", "Sitala (smallpox goddess) 38", "Sufism 4–7, 15, 36,
    38", "Sufism — and Bhagti 38", "Sohrawardiyya 38, 43, 85, 126, 187, 204 n.1", "tazkiras … 8, 38",
    "Udero Lal 29–30, 38, 41, 165" and "worship, categories of 37–8" (pp. 250–252, folios 237–239).

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The founding miracle of a Dalit-venerated saint, worked by him while touring in Sindh**: "One Madan
    had asked for the intercession of Baha al-Din Zakariyya (d. 1267) to obtain a son, when the famous
    Sufi of Multan was touring in Sindh. Baha al-Din offered him a small end (**pithu**) of a dried date.
    At the moment his wife tasted the blessed piece, she became pregnant. The son whom they had was named
    Pithoro, or the name of the date end. Thereafter, Pithoro became the disciple of Baha al-Din."
    (p. 187, folio 174) **The book adds that the legend is invariant except in the master's identity:
    "The legend of his birth is always identical, except that sometimes, as for example the case with the
    gaddi nashin, the Sufi Master was not Baha al-Din Zakariyya, but Shah Rukn-e Alam (d. 1335), another
    later saint of Multan." (p. 187, folio 174)**

### sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple — Sadh Belo (Sadh Belo Island Temple)

The book's names for it: **"Sadh Bela"**, **"Sadh Belo"**, "the sacred site of Sadh Belo", "the Mumbai
Sadh Belo". Present in chunks 002, 003, 004, 012 and 013.

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **Named as one of the "remarkable sanctuaries" of the Rohri–Sukkur–Shikarpur triangle**: "It suffices
    to quote some remarkable sanctuaries such as **Khwaja Khizr, on an island, Sadh Bela, on another
    island**, and the dargah of Sachal Sarmast." (p. 31, folio 18) The book places it "on another island"
    and does not name the river at that point, though the surrounding sentence speaks of "both sides of
    the Indus River" (p. 31, folio 18). Elsewhere: "the Sadh Belo settled in **the island in the Indus
    River, in Sukkur**" (p. 69, folio 56).
  - **Its foundation, as an endowment of land by Shia patrons**: "the amirs of Khairpur were themselves
    staunch Shia Muslims, and they built beautiful imambaras in the nineteenth century, but it did not
    prevent them from being generous patrons for all of the religious buildings as, for instance, **the
    famous Udasi temple in Sadh Bela which was created thanks to a gift of land**." (p. 31, folio 18)
    **The book calls it an "Udasi temple", which corroborates the archive row's category `Nanakpanthi /
    Udasi Darbar`. No foundation year, no acreage, no grantor's personal name and no custodian are given:
    (not stated).**
  - **The archive row's principal figure, Baba Bankhandi Maharaj, is not named anywhere in the book:
    (not stated)** — stated separately in chunks 002, 003, 004, 012 and 013.
  - **No ʿurs, no mela, no custodian, no gaddi nashin's name, no visitor figure and no architectural
    description of the Sukkur building: (not stated).**
  - **Index — two entries, two spellings, printed consecutively**: "Sadh Bela (sanctuary) 18" and "Sadh
    Belo 48, 55, 56" (p. 248, folio 235); the first is explicitly glossed "(sanctuary)". A third:
    "**Udais temple, Sadh Bela 18**" (p. 251, folio 238) — the only place in the two index chunks where a
    temple is named at the site, and **the spelling "Udais" is printed on the line immediately above
    "Udasis 40, 196 n.10"**. **All three entries that use the spelling "Sadh Bela" carry folio 18.**

- **Practices & Events**
  - **Its pilgrims' river access triggered a riot**: "the local Hindus opposed this rehabilitation since
    it was located in the harbour on the Indus River, from which the pilgrims reached the sacred site of
    Sadh Belo." (p. 61, folio 48) **That is the whole of the evidence; no date, no custodian, no ʿurs.**

- **Significance**
  - **Transferred to India, with a break in built form**: "there are Sadh Belo – which was transferred
    from **Sukkur to Mumbai (Maharashtra)**" (p. 68, folio 55); "In the Mumbai Sadh Belo, for example, it
    is difficult to see any architectural continuity with the Sadh Belo settled in the island in the
    Indus River, in Sukkur." (p. 69, folio 56)

### ramapir-temple-tando-allahyar — Ramapir Temple, Tando Allahyar

The book's names for him: **"Rama Pir"**, **"Ramdeo"**, **"Ramdev"**, **"Ramdeo Pir"**. Present in
chunks 002, 010 and 012. **Tando Allahyar is not named anywhere in the book: (not stated).**

- **Saint**
  - **Named as an untouchables' deified hero**: "The untouchables venerated Devi as well as a specific
    category of divinities that Christopher Fuller described as '**divinized heroes**' (Fuller 1992:
    49–50). In Sindh, the most popular deified heroes were **Rama Pir (Ramdeo or Ramdev)**, Pithoro Pir,
    Pabuji and Guga Pir." (p. 43, folio 30) **The book's three spellings are given exactly as printed. No
    temple, no location, no date, no custodian, no ʿurs or mela: (not stated).**
  - **His standing among the Sindhi Dalits, and his geography**: "Pithoro Pir is most of the time
    associated with soteriological figures. In this category, the saint most venerated is **Ramdeo Pir**,
    still known in Sindh as Rama Pir. Ramdeo Pir is also the object of widespread worship in **Gujarat and
    Rajasthan**. Although he is very present for the untouchable ones, he is also venerated by the
    Nathpanthis of Gujarat. In Sindh, he is today **integrated into the temples dedicated to the pan-Indian
    divinities**." (p. 192, folio 179)
  - **An asymmetry of cult, stated as a finding**: "The worship is not exclusive: the majority of Meghwars
    who venerate Pithoro Pir also venerate Ramdeo Pir. On the other hand, the reverse is less sure. The
    pir of Pithoro does not mention Ramdeo Pir among the companions with whom Pithoro Pir travelled."
    (p. 192, folio 179) And on iconography: "A most interesting point is the convergence of certain
    elements within these methods of worship, starting with the iconography. The figures of the two saints
    seem to draw with them a common model of holiness based on the renouncement." (p. 192, folio 179)
  - **A painting of him in a Karachi temple**: "In Karachi, for example, a painting of Ramdeo Pir stands
    in the temple of Lakhshmi Narayan." (p. 192, folio 179)
  - **The custodianship the book does NOT attribute to him**: "The organization of their respective cults
    could reproduce the relations of domination between Rajput and the other castes. In the case of
    **Pithoro Pir**, for example, worship was in the hands of the Pithoropirpotas" (p. 43, folio 30).
    **Recorded so that no consolidator transfers the Pithoro Pir custodianship to this row.**
  - **Index**: "Rama Pir 30" (p. 247, folio 234), in the folio-30 cluster of folk pirs with "Pabuji 30",
    "Guga Pir 30", "Pithoro Pir 30, 38, 165–6", "Khetrapal, (festival of disciple of Shiva) 30" and
    "Navratri, (festival of goddess Bhavani) 30" (pp. 240, 242, 246–247, folios 227, 229, 233–234). **A
    second, independent trace in the bibliography**: "Khan, Dominique-Sila, *Conversions and Shifting
    Identities: Ramdev Pir and the Ismailis in Rajasthan*, Delhi, Manohar, 1997" (p. 235, folio 222) —
    the archive's figure is "Ramdev Pir (Ramapir)" and the book prints both forms, "Rama Pir" in the index
    and "Ramdev Pir" in the title. **The book nowhere states that they are the same: (not stated).**

### shrine-of-makhdoom-jahaniyan-jahangasht — Shrine of Makhdoom Jahaniyan Jahangasht

The book's names for him: **"Jalal al-Din Bukhari Makhdum-i Jahaniyyan Jahangasht"**, **"Sayyid Jalal
al-Din Bukhari"**, **"Makhdum Jahaniyyan Jahangasht"**. Present in chunks 003, 010 and 012.

- **Saint**
  - **Two death years, and the book does not reconcile them.** Life dates printed "(**1308–84**)", with
    the second year abbreviated: "Qutub Ali Shah was a descendant in direct line from the great
    Sohrawardi Sufi Jalal al-Din Bukhari Makhdum-i Jahaniyyan Jahangasht (1308–84), who was one of the
    masters of the second Sohrawardiyya period, then based in **Utch near Bahawalpur in Punjab**."
    (p. 56, folio 43) — the archive row's `1384` is supported by the printed `–84`. Against that:
    "Jam Juna I Samma would have to ask for the intercession of **a saint of Multan**, Sayyid Jalal
    al-Din Bukhari, better known under the name of Makhdum Jahaniyyan Jahangasht (**d. 1383**)."
    (p. 189, folio 176) **1384 and 1383 stand side by side; and the book calls him "a saint of Multan"
    where the archive locates the shrine at Uch Sharif. Both conflicts recorded, neither resolved. No
    birth year on p. 189, no silsila there: (not stated).**
  - **Order**: "the great **Sohrawardi** Sufi" and "one of the masters of the second **Sohrawardiyya**
    period" (p. 56, folio 43). The order is indexed separately and much more heavily than the man:
    "Jahaniyya 60, 82, 85, 99, 132, 154, 187" (p. 244, folio 231), with "sajjada nashin — of Jahaniyya
    60" and "— of Tando Jahaniyyan 99" (p. 248, folio 235).
  - **An office under a named sultan**: "He was named **shaykh al-islam** by the sultan of Delhi Muhammad
    bin Tughluq." (p. 56, folio 43) **No regnal date, no year of appointment: (not stated).**
  - **A political intercession, dated**: "In **1367**, his successor, Firuz Shah Tughluq, returned to lay
    siege to Thatha; Jam Juna I Samma would have to ask for the intercession of a saint of Multan…"
    (p. 189, folio 176)

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The book names a base, not a tomb**: "then based in Utch near Bahawalpur in Punjab" (p. 56,
    folio 43). **No shrine description, no building date, no custodian, no ʿurs: (not stated). The archive
    row's `Uch Sharif, Bahawalpur District` is the book's `Utch near Bahawalpur in Punjab`.**
  - **Index**: "Jahangasht, Jalal al-Din Bukhari Makhdum-i Jahaniyyan 43" (p. 244, folio 231) — the full
    printed form carries both "Makhdum-i Jahaniyyan" and "Jahangasht". Folio 43 also carries "Utch
    (Bahawalpur, Punjab) 43" (p. 251, folio 238), "Sohrawardiyya 38, 43, 85, 126, 187, 204 n.1",
    "Tughluq, Muhammad bin 43" and "Sarmast, Sayyid Ali 43". **The index does not connect Utch to any of
    them: (not stated).** A thesis on the lineage: "Matai, V. J., *Jahaniyyan khandan*, Jamshoro,
    Institute of Sindhology, 2003 (M. A. Thesis 1972)" (p. 236, folio 223), and the source note "On Qutub
    Ali Shah and the Jahaniyyan lineage, see the master's thesis by V. J. Matai submitted in 1972,
    recently reprinted by the Institute of Sindhology (Matai 2003)." (p. 210, folio 197, Ch. 1 n. 15)
  - **The "Jahaniyya silsila" is NOT stated by the book to derive from or be named after him.** "the
    Sufis with whom they were in touch, including their masters, mostly belonged to two regional silsilas:
    the Darazi silsila and **the Jahaniyya silsila**." (p. 200, folio 187) **Asserting the link would be
    adding content; it is recorded here as unasserted. The book's Tando Jahaniyya is in Hyderabad, Sindh
    — "Tando Jahaniyya, Hyderabad 14, 47, 82, 85, 99, 132" (p. 251, folio 238) — and the index does not
    connect it to Uch Sharif: (not stated).**

### sant-bhagat-kanwar-ram-temple-chak — Sant Bhagat Kanwar Ram Temple (Chak)

The book's names for him: **"the famous Bhagat Kanwar Ram"**, **"Bhagat Kanwar Ram"**. Present in

- **Saint**
  - **Dates printed "(1885–1939)"** (p. 216, folio 203, Ch. 7 n. 1). **The archive's `figure_died 1939`

  - **His killing, dated only by a three-month window that straddles two years**: "The first victim was

    row's `figure_died 1939` must not be back-filled onto this sentence: the chunk supports only the

  - **A living descendant of the man accused of ordering the assassination**: "About this controversial

    pir of Bharchundi**, is the descendant of the one who was accused of having ordered the assassination
    of Bhagat Kanwar Ram (1885–1939)." (p. 216, folio 203, Ch. 7 n. 1) **The "controversial issue" is not
    restated inside the note: what it is, is (not stated) there. The accused ancestor is not named, and

  - **Index**: "Ram, Bhagat Kanwar 48" (p. 247, folio 234). **One index line, one folio, no dates, no

    "Lakki 48", "Sadh Belo 48, 55, 56" and "Muslims — of Sukkur 48" (pp. 244–249, folios 231–236), plus

### mazar-of-bulleh-shah — Mazar of Bulleh Shah

The book's spellings: **"Bulhe Shah"** (p. 91, folio 78; p. 158, folio 145) and **"Bullhe Shah"**
(p. 149, folio 136; p. 171, folio 158). **The archive spells him "Bulleh Shah (Abdullah Shah Qadri)";
the book's forms are kept as printed.** Present in chunks 005, 008, 009 and 012.

- **Saint**
  - **No death year, no birth year, no Kasur, no tomb, no Qadiri and no silsila anywhere in the book:
    (not stated).** The only descriptor is "**the famous Punjabi poet**" (p. 158, folio 145).
  - **A literary and liturgical reference, four times.** "Sometimes, Sufi poetry authored by non-Sindhis
    also played a **liturgical role** during the performances. One can quote **Bulhe Shah** or Ghulam
    Farid, the latter being a Siraiki-speaking author, this being a language in which many Sindhis also
    composed poetry." (p. 91, folio 78) — **the usable fact for this row is the liturgical use of his
    poetry at Sindhi Sufi performances, which is a practice claim, not a biographical one.** At Dadi
    Dhan's versi in 2012, "Although the majority of the songs were in Sindhi, there was also a
    composition of **Bullhe Shah** and another one of Mirabai" (p. 149, folio 136). In Panjwani's 1959
    anthology, "Some Sufis are not Sindhi, such as the famous Punjabi poet, **Bulhe Shah**, but they are
    very few" (p. 158, folio 145). And in a Siraiki anthology, "Non-Sindhi poets are also quoted,
    including **Bullhe Shah**, Shah Bahu and Ghulam Farid (Ladkani 2000: 170–80)." (p. 171, folio 158)
  - **Index**: "Shah, Bulhe 78, 145, 158" (p. 249, folio 236). **A bare inverted name, three folios: no
    dates, no place, no shrine.** He shares folio 78 with "Farid, Ghulam", "Kabir 78, 147, 153, 198 n.2",
    "Mirabai 76, 78, 136, 147, 198 n.2", "sami 64, 78, 81, 82, 94, 147–8", "Jotwani, Motilal 38–9, 75,
    78, 87" and "al-Karim, Shah Abd 78" — **folio 78 is the book's page of poets.**

### shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam — Shrine of Shah Rukn-e-Alam

The book's name for him: **"Shah Rukn-e Alam"**. Present in chunks 010 and 012.

- **Saint**
  - **Death year as printed, "(d. 1335)"** (p. 187, folio 174) — **which agrees with the archive row. No
    birth year: (not stated).**
  - **Descent and succession, stated**: "**Shah Rukn-e Alam was none other than the grandson and
    successor of Baha al-Din Zakariyya**", both "members of the **Sohrawardi** order" (p. 187, folio 174).
  - **Named as "another later saint of Multan"** (p. 187, folio 174). **No shrine, no tomb, no building
    date and no toponym other than "Multan": (not stated).**
  - **The variant hagiography in which he, not Baha al-Din, is the master — and the source of that
    variant is a named custodian**: "The legend of his birth is always identical, except that sometimes,
    as for example the case with the **gaddi nashin**, the Sufi Master was not Baha al-Din Zakariyya, but
    Shah Rukn-e Alam (d. 1335), another later saint of Multan." (p. 187, folio 174) **The gaddi nashin
    meant there is the current pir of Pithoro, named later as Hathesingh (p. 190, folio 177); the two
    designations are not joined on the same page.**
  - **Index**: "Alam, Shah Rukn-e 175" (p. 239, folio not stated). **That is the whole of the index

    Folio 175 is also one of the five folios of "Zakariyya, Baha al-Din 38, 165, 175, 179, 181"; **the
    index does not connect them: (not stated).**

### shrine-of-shah-abdul-karim-bulri — Shrine of Shah Abdul Karim Bulri

The book's names for him: **"Shah Abd al-Karim"**, **"Shah Karim"**. Present in chunks 005 and 012.

- **Saint**
  - **Three bare mentions in the body, no dates, no place, no shrine.** "He was the great-grandson of
    Shah Abd al-Karim, or Shah Karim." (p. 91, folio 78) — of Shah Abd al-Latif. "tuhnjo Allah hekro,
    'Your God is One' – which **first appears in Shah Karim's verses** and then in Shah Abd al-Latif
    (Lalwani 1890, vol. I: 45)" (p. 100, folio 87). "Also, we can find it [the figure of the jogi] in
    Shah Karim's poetry, but it is fully expressed in Shah Abd al-Latif's verses." (p. 105, folio 92)
    **No birth or death year and no silsila: (not stated).**
  - **A fourth mention the Pass-1 notes deliberately left unmapped**: "only seven verses (baits) were
    known, and only since they were quoted by **Shah Karim** in his own kalam" — of Qazi Qazan's poetry,
    "the oldest known Sufi poetry from Sindh – and, by the way, the oldest piece of Sindhi literature"
    (p. 171, folio 158). **Recorded here under this row because the book uses the same short form it uses
    at pp. 91, 100 and 105; a human must confirm that this is the same man.**

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The toponym Bulri appears once in the whole book, and only in the bibliography**: "*Shah Karim
    **Bulri** wari jo kalam bayan al-arifin va tanbiyya al-ghafaliyyan*, ed. Doctor Omar Muhammad
    Daudpota, Bombay, Qayyimah Press, **1937/1356**" (p. 232, folio 219) — **the only entry in the
    bibliography carrying a double CE/Hijri date.** And a whole monograph on him: "Motwani, Motilal,
    *Shah Abdul Karim – A Mystic Poet of Sindh*, Karachi, Sindi Kitab Ghar/Indus Publications, 1986"
    (p. 236, folio 223).
  - **Index, under two different inversions**: "al-Karim, Shah Abd 78" (p. 239, folio not stated) and
    "Karim, Shah 92" (p. 244, folio 231). **The index does not print "Bulri" at either, and does not
    state that the two entries are the same man: (not stated). No death date, no shrine, no silsila:
    (not stated).**

### mazar-e-iqbal — Tomb of Allama Iqbal (Mazar-e-Iqbal)

The book's name for him: **"Muhammad Iqbal"**. Present in chunks 005, 011 and 012.

- **Saint**
  - **Life dates printed once**: "**Muhammad Iqbal (1877–1938)** was formerly a critic of Hallaj, but

  - **The explanatory note, which is the book's only description of him**: "Muhammad Iqbal was a poet

    of] the greatest Muslim poets in the Indian subcontinent, and one of the founding fathers of
    Pakistan." (p. 212, folio 199, Ch. 3 n. 14) **In that note no birth year and no death year are
    printed: (not stated).**
  - **No tomb, no mausoleum, no honorific "Allama", no silsila, and nothing about a shrine anywhere in
    the book: (not stated).**
  - **Index**: "Iqbal, Muhammad 89, 199 n.14" (p. 243, folio 230). **A bare name, one folio and one
    note.** He sits in the index's folio-89 cluster with "al-Farabi 89", "al-Kindi 89", "Baghdad 89, 110,

The book's name for him: **"Ghulam Farid"**. **No "Khwaja", no dates, no Mithankot, no Chishti, no
shrine: (not stated).** Present in chunks 005, 008, 009 and 012.

- **Saint**
  - **One attribute only, and it is a language**: "One can quote Bulhe Shah or **Ghulam Farid, the latter

### gurdwara-dash-mesh-pita — Gurdwara Dash Mesh Pita

The book's name for him: **"the last Sikh Guru, Guru Gobind Singh"**. Present in chunks 003 and 013.

- **Saint**
  - **Life dates printed "(1666–1708)"**: "they did not follow the tenets of the **Khalsa** order as
    instituted by the last Sikh Guru, Guru Gobind Singh (1666–1708). It included a **code of conduct**,
    and only the official institution embodied by the guru was able to provide salvation through one's
    following the code (Oberoi 1994: 62 passim)." (p. 53, folio 40) **No gurdwara, no Sukkur, no site of
    any kind is attached to him: (not stated).**
  - **Index**: "Singh, Guru Gobind 40" (p. 250, folio 237). **One inverted index line, one folio, nothing
    else.** Folio 40 also carries "Sindhis — devotional literature 40", "Sindhis — Nanakpanthis 40",
    "Udasis 40, 196 n.10", "Ulemas 40" and "Chand, Shri 40" (pp. 240, 250–251, folios 227, 237–238).

### garh-maharaja-shorkot — Garh Maharaja (Shorkot)

The book's names for him: **"Sultan Bahu"** (p. 121, folio 108) and **"Shah Bahu"** (p. 171,
folio 158). **The two forms are fifty pages apart and are not harmonised by the book; the archive
prints "Sultan Bahu".** Present in chunks 006 and 009.

- **Saint**
  - **A literary parallel drawn in the introduction to a Hindu Sufi's breviary**: "there are interesting
    parallels drawn with Sultan Bahu's poetry, he being **an important seventeenth-century Sufi from
    Punjab**, Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani, and, not surprisingly, the Guru Granth Sahib, from which copious
    abstracts are given" (p. 121, folio 108). **That is the whole of the evidence there. No death year,
    no shrine, no toponym, no silsila: (not stated). The only chronology is the epithet
    "seventeenth-century".**
  - **A bare name in a three-name list, under the other spelling**: "Non-Sindhi poets are also quoted,
    including Bullhe Shah, **Shah Bahu** and Ghulam Farid (Ladkani 2000: 170–80)." (p. 171, folio 158)
    **No dates, no shrine, no toponym, no silsila: (not stated).**

### dargah-pir-ratan-nath-jee — Dargah Pir Ratan Nath Jee

The book's name: **"Pir Baba Ratannath"**; the archive's principal figure is "Baba Pir Ratan Nath".
Present in chunks 011 and 012.

- **Saint / Shrine**
  - **Named once, in a chapter-4 endnote on terminology, and placed in Delhi**: "On this 'paradoxical'
    terminology, see also the discussion on the '**dargah mandir**' devoted to **Pir Baba Ratannath in
    Delhi** (Bouillier and Khan 2009: 571 passim)." (p. 214, folio 201, Ch. 4 n. 6) **The book places
    this dargah mandir in Delhi and says nothing about Peshawar, Gor Khatri or Jhanda Bazar: (not
    stated). No dates, no custodian, no order: (not stated).**
  - **Index**: "Pir Baba Ratannath, Delhi 201 n.6" (p. 247, folio 234). **The index locates him at Delhi,
    not at the archive's Peshawar, and the locator is a note reference only.**
  - **A second trace in the bibliography, whose title asserts the figure has multiple identities**:
    "Bouillier, Véronique and Dominique-Sila Khan, 'Hajji Ratan or Baba Ratan's Multiple Identities',
    *Journal of Indian Philosophy*, 37, 2009, pp. 559–95" (p. 233, folio 220). **No dates, no shrine
    description: (not stated).**

### gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur — Gurdwara Pehli Patshahi (Jind Pir), Sukkur

The book's name for it: **"Jinda Pir"**; the archive spells it "Jind Pir". Present in chunks 003
and 012.

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **Its foundation, dated to a decade and given a cause**: "A new darbar was built **on the bank of the
    Indus River in Sukkur**, this coming to be known as **Jinda Pir**. It was engendered by a will of the
    Hindus to develop a distinct Hindu cult of **Khwaja Khizr, also known as Zinda Pir**, after they had
    been expelled from the main sanctuary located on an island in the Indus River." (p. 54, folio 41),
    dated only to "**the last decade of the nineteenth century**" (p. 54, folio 41). **The expelling
    party is (not stated); the island sanctuary is not named.**
  - **The book calls it a darbar of a Hindu cult of Khwaja Khizr; it never calls it a gurdwara and never
    names Guru Nanak, the archive row's principal figure, in connection with it: (not stated).**
  - **Index**: "Jinda Pir 41" appears both as a top-level entry and as a `darbars (shrine)` subentry
    (pp. 241, 244, folios 228, 231); and, separately, "**Zinda Pir 41**" (p. 252, folio 239). **The index
    therefore carries "Jinda Pir" and "Zinda Pir" as separate entries at the identical folio 41,
    differing only in the initial letter, and nowhere cross-references them: (not stated).** Folio 41
    also carries "Khizr, Khwaja 41", "Amar Lal 41", "Jhulelal 41, …", "Gurmukhi script 41, 81",
    "Khudawadi script 41", "Halani darbar 41, 55, 103", "Varun (Vedic god) 41, 165" and "Nanakpanth 8,
    13, 18, 21, 25, 29, 30, 40, 41, …" (pp. 239, 242, 244, 246, 251–252, folios 227, 229, 231, 233,
    238–239).

### khatwari-darbar-shikarpur — Khatwari Darbar, Shikarpur

The book's name: **"the Kathwaro Darbar"**. Present in chunks 004 and 012.

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **Transferred out of Sindh**: "and the Kathwaro Darbar, which was **transferred from Shikarpur to
    Nagpur (Madhya Pradesh)**" (p. 68, folio 55). **That is the whole of the evidence. The row's
    principal figure, Bhai Gurdas Singh (Kanhiya Lal), is not named: (not stated); no date, no custodian,
    no description. Nagpur's state is printed "(Madhya Pradesh)" and is reproduced exactly as printed,
    not corrected.**
  - **Three spellings across two index pages for what the index does not say is one thing**: "Kathwaro
    Darbar 55" and "**Katwara Sahib 101**" (p. 244, folio 231), plus under `darbars (shrine)` the
    subentry "Kathwaro 55" (p. 241, folio 228). The third form appears in the body as "**the Khatwara
    Sahib**", named once in the portrait gallery of Daulatram Motwani's basement samadhi in Ulhasnagar:
    "one can also find the Khatwara Sahib and Gehimal Motwani, Daulatram's father" (p. 114, folio 101) —
    **the same folio 101 as the index's "Katwara Sahib 101". Not glossed, not located, not dated: (not
    stated).** "Nagpur (Madhya Pradesh) 55" carries its own index line (p. 246, folio 233).

### gorakhnath-goraknath-temple — Gorakhnath (Goraknath) Temple

The book's name: **"Gorakhnath"**. Present in chunks 001, 004, 010 and 012.

- **Saint**
  - **Named as the Nathpanthis' founding figure, and as an avatar — the belief attributed, not
    asserted**: the Nathpanthis "are also known as the jogis. They are the followers of Gorakhnath, who
    **is believed to be** an avatar of Shiva." (p. 77, folio 64) **No date, no place, no shrine: (not
    stated).**
  - "in the Persian sources, the main Hindu figure is the jogi. The term '**jogi**' is usually employed
    for the Nathpanthis, a **Shivaite school of renunciants** who were the followers of Gorakhnath."
    (p. 21, folio 8; the sentence carries endnote 7, whose text is not in that chunk)
  - "an ascetic order founded by Gorakhnath" (p. 120, folio 107).
  - **In a legend**: "According to another version, **Gorakhnath would have sent an invisible army** to
    help Goga Pir against Rai Pithora." (p. 188, folio 175)
  - **Index**: "Gorakhnath 8, 64, 107, 175" (p. 242, folio 229), with "Nathpanthis 8, 64, 79, 107, 179;
    *see also* jogis" and "jogis 7, 8, 10, 78, 79, 107" (pp. 244, 246, folios 231, 233); "avatar — of
    Shiva 64, 166" (p. 239, folio not stated). In the bibliography: "Briggs, George Weston, *Gorakhnath
    and the Kanphata Yogis*, New Delhi, Motilal Banarsidass, 1998 (1st edn, 1938)" (p. 234, folio 221).
  - **No temple, no Peshawar, no Gor Khatri and no custodian is attached to him anywhere: (not stated).**

### tilla-jogian — Tilla Jogian

Mapped on the same figure and the same sentences as the row above, under the many-row person rule:
Gorakhnath is the principal figure of both rows and **the book names no site for the Nathpanthis in
Sindh or the Salt Range**, saying only that "the role played by the Nathpanthis in Sindh comes in many
forms" (p. 77, folio 64).

- **Saint**
  - "They are the followers of Gorakhnath, who is believed to be an avatar of Shiva." (p. 77, folio 64)
  - "in the Persian sources, the main Hindu figure is the jogi. The term 'jogi' is usually employed for
    the Nathpanthis, a Shivaite school of renunciants who were the followers of Gorakhnath." (p. 21,
    folio 8)
  - "an ascetic order founded by Gorakhnath" (p. 120, folio 107); "Gorakhnath would have sent an
    invisible army to help Goga Pir against Rai Pithora" (p. 188, folio 175).
  - The glossary: "**Jogi**: wandering Shivaite ascetics who meditate on One, usually affiliated with
    the Nathpanth." (p. 219, folio 206)
  - **No site, no Salt Range, no Jhelum, no temple, no custodian and no date: (not stated).**
