### shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge — Shri Laxmi Narayan Mandir (Native Jetty Bridge)

The book's name for the site: **"the temple of Lakhshmi Narayan"** — **the spelling is reproduced as
printed.** Present in chunk 010.

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **Named once**: "In **Karachi**, for example, a painting of **Ramdeo Pir** stands in the temple of
    Lakhshmi Narayan." (p. 192, folio 179) **That is the whole of the evidence. No address, no custodian,
    no date, no founding and no description beyond the painting: (not stated).**
  - **The identification rests on deity-name plus city alone**: the archive holds exactly one
    Lakshmi-Narayan row in Karachi. The archive's other Karachi rows were considered by Pass 1 and
    refused.

### Place-level and person-level attachments — ruling (a) and the person rule

Every bullet below is a mapping the Pass-1 notes parked under Doubts because they were written before
ruling (a) of 21 September 2026 and the many-row person rule of 22 September 2026. **The attachment in
each case is place-level or person-level, not site-specific**: the book says nothing about the
individual shrines named in the bullet head, and nothing from the head may be read back into the
material. Every id on a head is on one physical line.

- **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan Sharif", also "Sehwan")
  — **place-level.** The town appears throughout the book with no shrine, tomb, saint, ʿurs or custodian
  attached to the clause: "to be born in Sehwan Sharif, in central Sindh where **Vicholi** was spoken (a
  Sindhi dialect which was officially proclaimed to be the official Sindhi language by the British), was
  different from being born in Hyderabad" (p. 30, folio 17); "Nandirani Merani, an Amil from Sehwan
  Sharif who translated many books on different matters such as history, geography and so on" (p. 45,
  folio 32); "In his teens, he [Bedil] went to Sehwan Sharif, where he is reported to have been
  vouchsafed spiritual visions" (p. 97, folio 84) — **what he saw is not described: (not stated)**; "his
  native town of Sehwan Sharif, **a very reputed centre of Sufi pilgrimage**" (p. 113, folio 100);
  "through his family, Mulchand was extremely well integrated into the mystical network of Sehwan
  Sharif" and "the **Shahi Bazar** in Sehwan" (p. 125, folio 112); "Sehwan Sharif, where Mulchand decided
  to stay" (p. 128, folio 115); Paru Chawla "was born as Lalwani in Sehwan Sharif in 1932. **Despite the
  strong Sufi ambiance of the city**, she left at about fifteen years old" (p. 161, folio 148); and two
  clauses naming no saint at pp. 210, 211 (folios 197, 198). **Two substantive place-level findings
  belong here rather than to either shrine**: "Before partition, the Hindus made up about half of the
  total population, and the town hosted **two main Hindu temples. The first one was devoted to Shiva and
  settled in the north-eastern part of the town. The other one was devoted to Jhulelal and was
  established on the north-western side of the town.** From 1947 onwards, almost all of the Hindu
  population migrated to India." (pp. 179–180, folios 166–167) — **neither temple is named, dated or said
  to survive: (not stated)**; and "All the **Thakurs**, the priestly caste of Jhulelal, left Sindh to
  settle in India, especially in Bombay and Ulhasnagar. In Sehwan, they could have been the most powerful
  Hindu group, and in Bombay, there is still a **Sehwani panchayat** which has published several
  directories of the Sehwanis in India and overseas since partition. For some time now, the Sehwani
  panchayat has had a Facebook page." (p. 180, folio 167) Also here: "the Thakurs who were running
  Jhulelal's temple in the northwestern part of the town" migrated (p. 71, folio 58), and "Here, the
  Thakurs came from Sehwan Sharif." (p. 210, folio 197, Ch. 2 n. 6) Sehwan Sharif is also the book's
  example of a small city, against Karachi as a big one (p. 67, folio 54). **The Sehwan material that
  names Lal Shahbaz Qalandar is in his own subsection above and is not repeated here.**

- **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "the Jamshoro district") —
  **place-level, district-level.** "It is in the Jamshoro district and the seat of a taluka." (p. 184,
  folio 171) — said of **Thano Bula Khan**, not of Sehwan; **whether Tando Ahmad Khan shares that
  district is not stated.**

- **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Lakki") — **place-level.** "the 'Sukkur riots' occurred
  beyond the city, in other cities such as Rohri, and also in other parts of Sindh, such as **in Lakki**"
  (p. 61, folio 48). Indexed "Lakki 48" (p. 244, folio 231). **No shrine, tomb or saint is attached to
  the name: (not stated).** Note also "Lakkiyyaris 168" and "Lakkiyyari, Gul Muhammad 170" (pp. 244–245,
  folios 231–232) — **a Sehwan Sayyid family, not the Laki shrine; the resemblance is not evidence.**

- **bhagnari-mandir**, **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge**, **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi**, **shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi**, **shri-varun-dev-mandir**, **shrine-of-abdullah-shah-ghazi**, **shrine-of-pir-mangho**, **shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya** (the book's "Karachi")
  — **place-level; the book names Karachi only as the site of secular and colonial institutions, never of
  a shrine, and distinguishes none of these eleven rows.** "the growth of new emporium in Sindh,
  especially Karachi" (p. 44, folio 31); Reverend Bambridge's debates "in Karachi" (p. 46, folio 33);
  "the conqueror and first governor of Sindh, Sir Charles Napier, built a **Masonic lodge in 1843** in
  Karachi" (p. 46, folio 33); "In Karachi, the **Theosophical Society was founded in 1896**" (p. 46,
  folio 33) — its three founders were "Dhunjishaw, Jamshed Nusserwanji and Dayaram Jethmal" (p. 46,
  folio 33), it first met at "the new Dayaram Jethmal Science College", and "Jamshed Nusserwanji then
  decided to build a new building with an auditorium, a library and a lecture room.7" (p. 47, folio 34);
  "a printing press was brought to Karachi **in the 1850s**" (p. 48, folio 35); "Pandit Brahmashankar
  Mishra (1861–1907), another prominent member, spent years in Karachi and Hyderabad", yet "**no formal
  relation between Sindh and the Hindu branch of the Naqshbandiyya has been found**" (p. 26, folio 13);
  Rochaldas "was working as a medical doctor in **the sanatorium he had created in Karachi**" (p. 70,
  folio 57) and later created "a dispensary, as well as in Karachi and Hyderabad" (p. 78, folio 65);
  Hiranand Advani "hastily left his work in Karachi and went to Calcutta" (p. 103, folio 90); "the leper
  asylum in Mangho Pir" (p. 104, folio 91); Dadi Dhan "obtained a M.A. in Philosophy from Karachi, which
  was then under the University of Bombay" (p. 115, folio 102); Ram Panjwani "started his career in
  education as teacher at the **D. J. Sindh Government Science College** in Karachi" (p. 156, folio 143);
  and "the activities of the society stopped in Karachi after the **2007 assassination** of the society's
  president, the late Dara Feroze Mirza (1937–2007)" (p. 209, folio 196, Ch. 1 n. 7) — **the society is
  not named in that note: (not stated).** Further bare occurrences at pp. 56, 57, 62, 66, 67 (folios 43,
  44, 49, not stated, 54) and pp. 94, 103 (folios 81, 90). **The one Karachi sentence that names a temple
  is mapped site-specifically to `shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge` above.**

- **shrine-of-pir-mangho** (the book's "**Mangho Pir**") — **place-level, and the word order is
  reversed against the archive's "Pir Mangho", so a name-matching script will miss it.** "Among these
  were the leper asylum in Mangho Pir and an orphanage in Sukkur." (p. 104, folio 91) **The locality
  carries a charitable institution and no shrine, saint, ʿurs or custodian: (not stated).**

- **guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat** (the book's "Hyderabad", the archive's only Hyderabad row) —
  **place-level.** "the late capital of the independent Sindh before the British conquest" (p. 30,
  folio 17); Annie Besant "visited this city as well as Hyderabad, where she delivered a number of
  lectures" and "in Hyderabad, her speech was about Hindu religion (Sadhu Vaswani 2001: 31)" (pp. 46–47,
  folios 33–34); "the printing house he founded in Hyderabad was named **Blavatsky Printing**" (p. 48,
  folio 35); "In 1901, aged 22, Rochaldas married and settled in Hyderabad to enrol as a student at the
  medical college of the city" (p. 56, folio 43); Mulchand "studied up to class 6 at the **Hiranand
  Academy** in Hyderabad" (p. 59, folio 46); Qutub Ali Shah "was a famous Sufi from Hyderabad… **His
  dargah is located in Jahaniyya, a neighbourhood in Hyderabad**" (p. 95, folio 82) and "both had the
  dastarbandi ceremony performed by the sajjada nashin of Qutub Ali Shah's dargah from Hyderabad. The
  Sufi pir came for it from Pakistan twice, **in 1957 and in 1980**" (p. 167, folio 154); Hiranand Advani
  "was born in Hyderabad in a family of **Khudabadi Amils**" (p. 103, folio 90); Dalpat Sufi "founded an
  **astano**, a place of residence for a renunciant, in Hyderabad, **on the Sipahimalani Lane**. He is
  said to have attracted many followers, Muslims and Hindus, and the **Talpur mirs are said to have
  looked after it**" and, after his death, it "became a pilgrimage centre under the guidance of his
  successor, Keshavram Sipahimalani. The place was known as **Guru Sehwani's place**" (p. 118, folio 105)
  — qualified by the author's own note, "Apparently, he used to stay in a place in Hyderabad which can be
  called a darbar" (p. 209, folio 196, Ch. 1 n. 11); "The Sipahimalanis were a well-known Amil family of
  Hyderabad" (p. 119, folio 106); Molchand Kripalani "born in Hyderabad in 1887 in a Krishnaite family
  (Kripalani 1986: 3)" (p. 123, folio 110); "the huge sanctuary close to Hyderabad" (p. 184, folio 171);
  "the railway line which formerly connected **Hyderabad (Sindh)** to Barmer and Jodhpur" (p. 189,
  folio 176); and "Tando Jahaniyya in Hyderabad" as one of three Sufi traditions (p. 60, folio 47),
  indexed "Tando Jahaniyya, Hyderabad 14, 47, 82, 85, 99, 132" (p. 251, folio 238). Further bare
  occurrences at pp. 54, 56, 59, 60 (folios 41, 43, 46, 47), p. 78 (folio 65) and pp. 95, 103 (folios 82,
  90). **The book separately names "Hyderabad Deccan" (p. 210, folio 197), a different city, which must
  not be folded in. Note that the named Hyderabad sites — Qutub Ali Shah's dargah in Jahaniyya, Dalpat's
  astano on Sipahimalani Lane — match no archive row at all.**

- **gurdwara-sach-khand-sahib**, **khatwari-darbar-shikarpur**, **sant-bhagat-kanwar-ram-temple-chak**
  (the book's "Shikarpur") — **place-level.** "Shikarpur, the northern city of Sindh where the Northern
  dialect of Sindhi known as **Utradi or Siraiki** was spoken, was a hub of international commerce at
  this time. It was the core of merchant networks that allowed them to control commerce between Central
  Asia and overseas countries. Here, the **baniyas** were the dominant group." (p. 30, folio 17); one
  corner of "the triangle of Rohri-Sukkur-Shikarpur, on both sides of the Indus River, [which] was the
  locale for an amazingly rich dialogue between different creeds" (p. 31, folio 18); Nimano Faqir "born
  into a rich Hindu family of Shikarpur" (p. 60, folio 47); Sami "born in a well-to-do family in
  Shikarpur. He was himself a cloth merchant and travelled a great deal between Shikarpur and Amritsar in
  Punjab." (p. 94, folio 81); and a further bare occurrence at p. 214 (folio 201).

- **gurdwara-dash-mesh-pita**, **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple**, **sant-baba-asudaram-darbar-panno-aqil** (the book's "Sukkur")
  — **place-level.** A corner of the Rohri–Sukkur–Shikarpur triangle (p. 31, folio 18); the city of the
  "'**Sukkur riots**'", dated "between December of 1939 and February of 1940", whose first victim was
  Bhagat Kanwar Ram and where "the local Hindus opposed this rehabilitation since it was located in the
  harbour on the Indus River" (p. 61, folio 48; indexed "Sukkur riots 48–9", p. 251, folio 238); the city
  of the **Manzilgah** riots, "Contrary to other cities in Northern Sindh – for instance, Sukkur,
  following the Manzilgah riots – no communal violence had ever been registered" in Sehwan (p. 71,
  folio 58; indexed "Manzilgah Affair 48, 58, 197 n.20"), where "**Fateh Muhammad Sehwani (1882–1942)**,
  a Sehwani literati … himself a Muslim who was involved in the Manzilgah Affair, advocated the Hindus'
  claim" (p. 71, folio 58); "an orphanage in Sukkur" (p. 104, folio 91); Dadi Dhan "was born in Sukkur,
  Sindh, and her father was a famous lawyer" (p. 115, folio 102); and Sadh Belo's origin at pp. 68–69
  (folios 55–56).

- **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple** (the book's
  "Rohri", also "Rohri taluqa" and "Rohri dham") — **place-level; these are the only two archive rows
  whose location names Rohri, both as "near Sukkur/Rohri", and neither is the Rohri shrine the book
  actually describes.** "Still another experience was to be born in Shikarpur or in Rohri, which was
  another story entirely" (p. 30, folio 17); a corner of the triangle (p. 31, folio 18); Rochaldas "born
  in 1879 in Rohri, in the north of Sindh, to a family that his hagiographers call modest", his eldest
  brother "became chief officer of the municipality of Rohri" (p. 55, folio 42), and the port where he
  found himself "wandering high in the sky above the port on the Indus at Rohri" (p. 57, folio 44); a
  city of the riots (p. 61, folio 48); "**Rohri dham**" (p. 78, folio 65); Bedil "a Sufi born in Rohri"
  and Rochaldas "born in Rohri, like Bedil, but seven years after his demise" (pp. 97–98, folios 84–85)
  — **Rochaldas's birth year is not printed there and is not computed: (not stated)**; "Kandri is a small
  town located in **Rohri taluqa**, in the Khaipur [text-layer? Khairpur] district" (p. 107, folio 94);
  and a further bare occurrence at p. 209 (folio 196). **Two Rohri shrines the book does name belong to
  no archive row and are listed in the second section below: Sain Vasan Shah's "original sanctuary"
  (p. 68, folio 55; "Vasan Shah's darbar is located in Ulhasnagar, while another one is in Rohri, on the
  bank of the Indus River", p. 209, folio 196) and Isarlal's shrine (p. 150, folio 137).**

- **dargah-of-pir-muhammad-rashid-roze-dhani-pir-jo-goth**, **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast** (the book's
  "Khairpur") — **place-level.** "many Kayasht migrated to work in the Princely States, including the
  **Khairpur emirate** in Sindh" (p. 26, folio 13); "**the amirs of Khairpur were themselves staunch Shia
  Muslims, and they built beautiful imambaras in the nineteenth century**, but it did not prevent them
  from being generous patrons for all of the religious buildings" (p. 31, folio 18) — **none of those
  imambaras is named or located: (not stated)**; Halani lies "between Nausharo Firuz and Khairpur"
  (p. 68, folio 55); the Darazi silsila "located close to Khairpur in Northern Sindh" (p. 93, folio 80);
  Kandri "in the Khaipur [text-layer? Khairpur] district" (p. 107, folio 94); "the Jalalani tariqa, whose
  dargah is located in Northern Sindh close to Khairpur" (p. 122, folio 109); Daraza "not far from
  Khairpur, in Sindh" (p. 150, folio 137); and "**Hingorja** near Khairpur", Bhagwan K. Ajwani's
  birthplace (p. 169, folio 156). **The Daraza material itself is mapped site-specifically above.**

- **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's "Multan")
  — **place-level; the book's four bare Multan clauses distinguish none of these eight rows.** Bedil "was
  the scion of a Qurayshi family that **migrated from Multan two generations back**" (p. 98, folio 85,
  endnote 11); "in **1397**, Pir Muhammad was in Multan" (p. 189, folio 176); "with Multan as capital"
  (p. 216, folio 203); "settled in Multan" (p. 227, folio 214). **The three Multan occurrences that carry
  a saint — Baha al-Din Zakariyya "the famous Sufi of Multan", Shah Rukn-e Alam "another later saint of
  Multan", Makhdum Jahaniyyan Jahangasht "a saint of Multan" — are in those rows' own subsections above.
  Note also that the book's "Ali Akbar" (p. 41, folio 28) is a member of Husayn's family and is NOT the
  archive's Shah Ali Akbar Shamsi, and that "the Sabzwaris" (p. 180, folio 167; index "Sabzwaris 167")
  are a Sayyid lineage of Sehwan, not the Multan saint.**

- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "Lahore")
  — **place-level, and thin: the book names Lahore exactly twice, both times without a shrine.**
  "Muhammad Iqbal was a poet born in **Lahore**, who was granted a PhD by the University of Munich."
  (p. 212, folio 199, Ch. 3 n. 14); and as a publisher's city in the bibliography, "Lalwani, L. W., *The
  Life, Religion and Poetry of Shah Latif: A Greatest Poet of Sind*, **Lahore**, Sang-e Meel
  Publications, 1994 [1890]" (p. 236, folio 223). **`mazar-e-iqbal` is already mapped on the person
  above, so ruling (a) adds nothing to that row here.**

- **shrine-of-makhdoom-nooh-hala** (the book's "Hala") — **place-level.** "He was born in **Hala** in a
  Sayyid family." (p. 91, folio 78) — of Shah Abd al-Latif. **No shrine, saint, ʿurs or custodian is
  attached to the town, and Makhdoom Nooh is not named anywhere in the book: (not stated).**

- **shrine-of-jalaluddin-surkh-posh-bukhari-jalaluddin-bukhari**, **shrine-of-makhdoom-jahaniyan-jahangasht**, **tomb-of-baha-al-halim-uch-sharif**, **tomb-of-javindi-bibi**, **tomb-of-ustad-nuriya** (the book's "Utch near Bahawalpur in Punjab", indexed "Utch (Bahawalpur, Punjab)")
  — **place-level.** "one of the masters of the second Sohrawardiyya period, then based in **Utch near
  Bahawalpur in Punjab**" (p. 56, folio 43); indexed "Utch (Bahawalpur, Punjab) 43" (p. 251, folio 238),
  **the index's gloss giving district and province and nothing more: no shrine, tomb, saint, ʿurs or
  custodian is attached: (not stated).** **The book spells the town "Utch" and the archive "Uch Sharif".
  Bahawalpur taken alone matches only four of these five rows — `tomb-of-javindi-bibi` carries Uch Sharif
  without Bahawalpur in its location — so the five-row head follows the "Uch Sharif" join.**

- **shrine-of-makhdoom-abdul-rahim-girhori**, **umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir** (the book's "Pithoro",
  "Umarkot" and "Umerkot") — **place-level, and the weakest join in this file: the book never names
  Makhdoom Abdul Rahim Girhori, Girhor Sharif or a Shiv Mandir at Umerkot.** The first row is joined
  because its location reads "Girhor Sharif, near **Pithoro**, Umarkot, Sindh" and the book names Pithoro
  as a hamlet: "The tomb of Pithoro Pir is located in **the hamlet of Pithoro**, between Mirpur Khas and
  Umarkot, on the railway line which formerly connected Hyderabad (Sindh) to Barmer and Jodhpur, in
  Rajasthan. As for many other saints, the site of their sanctuary bears the name of the saint." (p. 189,
  folio 176) The second row is joined on the town: "Another important untouchable group was made of the
  **Menghwar**, mostly settled around **Umerkot**. Part of their devotional literature was and still is
  in Sindhi." (p. 40, folio 27); "between Mirpur Khas and **Umerkot**" (p. 178, folio 165); "The area of
  **Umarkot** where he lived had been a place of rough combat between two groups of Rajputs, the Somras
  and the Sodhas" (p. 188, folio 175); "The area of Umarkot was at the centre of conflicts of local,
  regional and central interests" and "Although the latter officially took power from the first in
  **1351**, the Somras had preserved their control of certain outlying areas of Sindh, including,
  precisely, the area of Umarkot" (p. 189, folio 176); "Their chief, Parmar Sodha, would have been
  established as king (**rano**) at Umarkot… **The Gazetteer of 1907 describes them as worshippers of
  Shiva.** No mention of Pithoro Pir appears there." (p. 191, folio 178) — **that last sentence is the
  one place-level fact in the book with any bearing on a Shiv Mandir at Umerkot, and the Gazetteer's full
  title is (not stated).** Indexed "Umerkot 27" (p. 251, folio 238). **The archive spells the town two
  ways across the two rows and so does the book; the head follows both spellings.**

- **churrio-jabal-durga-mata-temple**, **gori-temple-gori-jo-mandar**, **nagarparkar-jain-temples-nagarparkar-cultural-landscape** (the book's "Thar Parkar")
  — **place-level, off one clause.** "They officiate in the altars of villages, especially in **Thar
  Parkar**." (p. 190, folio 177) — of the **Pithoro Pota Pirs**, "the priests (**gurara**) of the sect".
  **The altars meant are Pithoro Pota Pir altars, not any of these three sites: (not stated).**

- **dargah-of-khwaja-muhammad-zaman-luari-sharif** (the book's "**Lowari Sharif**", also "Lowari Shari
  [text-layer? Lowari Sharif]"; the archive spells it "Luari Sharif") — **place-level.** "Naqshbandi
  scholars from Lowari Sharif, **a village close to Badin in Southern Sindh**" (p. 22, folio 9), of whom
  "The main author was **Makhdum Muhammad Hashim (1692–1762)**, who was qazi in Thatta". And, in the
  Annexes, the book's only substantive prose about the centre: the Naqshbandis "have several important
  centres in Sindh, especially that of Lowari Shari [text-layer? Lowari Sharif] located in Southern
  Sindh, near Badin" and "**the official book devoted to the Naqshbandis of Lowari Sharif was written by
  a Hindu follower**" (p. 228, folio 215). Indexed "Lowari Sharif (Badin, Sindh) 9" (p. 245, folio 232).
  **No saint, no tomb, no ʿurs and no custodian is named there, and the row's principal figure, Khwaja
  Muhammad Zaman, is not named anywhere in the book: (not stated). The spelling difference —
  `Lowari` against the archive's `Luari` — is predicted by the book's own stated refusal of academic
  transliteration (p. 12, folio not stated).**

- **shrine-of-shah-abdul-karim-bulri** (the book's "Tando Muhammad Khan") — **place-level.** "Mohsin
  Shah, the gaddi nashin of **Tando Saindad near Tando Muhammad Khan** (Kripalani 1986: 38), a Sufi who
  was affiliated to the **Qalandariyya**" (p. 123, folio 110). **The site the book describes is Tando
  Saindad, which has no archive row; the join is on the district name alone. The book's other Tandos —
  Tando Ahmad Khan, Tando Adam, Tando Jahaniyya(n) — are different places and were refused.**

- **swami-dharmdas-darbar-larkana-kennedy-market** (the book's "Larkana") — **place-level.** "Ram
  Panjwani was born in **1911 in Larkana** in a zamindar family." (p. 156, folio 143) **No shrine, saint,
  ʿurs or custodian is attached, and Swami Dharmdas is not named anywhere in the book: (not stated).**

- **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed** (the book's "Jhok Sharif", in its two bare occurrences) —
  **place-level.** "He also visited **Jhok Sharif** and Daraza" — of Bedil (p. 97, folio 84); and "**Sufi
  Hazrat Abdul Sattar Shah of Jhok Sharif**, speaks of jam wahdat jo, 'the cup of unity' (Nasir Faqir
  1972)" (p. 101, folio 88). **The second attaches a person to the place, but not the row's principal
  figure, and Sufi Shah Inayat Shaheed is not named in either clause: (not stated).**

- **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir** (the book's "**Hinglaj**") — **place-level / site-level.**
  The book names it three times and once gives it a category: "Parsram claimed that the universal wisdom
  is kept in Sindh, **in Hinglaj to be exact, which is a pilgrimage centre located in the Sindhi-speaking
  area of Baluchistan**." (p. 50, folio 37) — the claim is Parsram's, reported by the book, and it stands
  inside a Theosophical narrative of "a universal wisdom, a kind of primordial wisdom through which
  humanity could reach immortality… lost many centuries ago, except for in a remote area Blavatsly
  [text-layer? — possibly `Blavatsky`] usually located in Tibet" (p. 50, folio 37). "He probably
  travelled with them on a number of **Hindu pilgrimages, including Dwarka and Hinglaj**." (p. 92,
  folio 79) — of Shah Abd al-Latif with the Nathpanthi jogis, **the hedge "probably" being the book's
  own; this is the sentence that links `bhit-bhit-shah` to this row.** "There are also a number of sacred
  Hindu places, such as **Hinglaj**, Kasi, or Kashi (Banaras), Sri Lanka and the Ganga" (p. 106,
  folio 93) — in Sachal Sarmast's poetry. Indexed "Hinglaj 37, 79, 93" (p. 243, folio 230), beside
  "Junagarh 79", "Dwarka 79", "Diach, Rao 79", "kamach 79", "chang 79", "charan 79" and "Karbala 79, 80,
  196–7 n.14" (pp. 240, 242, 244, folios 227, 229, 231) — folio 79 is the book's page of pilgrimage
  places and instruments. **The book gives Hinglaj no deity, no temple description, no custodian and no
  ʿurs: (not stated).** **The Pass-1 notes refused this mapping four times over on the ground that the
  archive row "carries an empty id cell" and called the defect blocking. That is a misreading of
  `shrine_index.tsv`: the row's `id` is `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir` and it is present; the
  empty cells on that line are `category` and `principal_figure`. The material is therefore mapped here.
  See the report to the Doubts worker.**

- **gurdwara-babay-de-ber**, **gurdwara-balila-sahib-bal-lila-sahib**, **gurdwara-chakki-sahib**, **gurdwara-choa-sahib**, **gurdwara-darbar-sahib-kartarpur**, **gurdwara-khoohi-bhai-lalo-bhai-lalo-di-khooi**, **gurdwara-malji-sahib**, **gurdwara-panja-sahib**, **gurdwara-patti-sahib**, **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **gurdwara-rori-sahib**, **gurdwara-sach-khand-sahib**, **gurdwara-sacha-sauda**, **gurdwara-sahib-saidpur-guru-nanak-dev-ji**, **gurdwara-sri-kiara-sahib**, **gurdwara-sri-tilganji-sahib**, **gurdwara-tambo-sahib**, **gurudwara-janam-asthan-nankana-sahib** (the book's "Guru Nanak", also "Shri Guru Nanak")
  — **person-level: all eighteen rows of which Guru Nanak is a principal figure, since the book names him
  substantively and names no gurdwara anywhere.** "Sain Vasan Shah became famous for reciting the **banis
  of Shri Guru Nanak** and the kalams of the Sufis." (p. 68, folio 55); "He became famous for his singing
  of **Guru Nanak's banis** as well as Sufi kalams." (p. 209, folio 196, Ch. 1 n. 13); named as **Shri
  Chand's father** — "the eldest son of Guru Nanak who was not recognized as guru by the mainstream Sikh
  community", whose tradition the Udasis follow (p. 53, folio 40); "a growing similarity between the
  iconography of Guru Nanak and Jhulelal" (p. 54, folio 41); as a Nirguni poet (p. 89, folio 76); "Guru
  Nanak was the greatest poet of the Bhakti" in Ajwani's summary, and "it is a seductive hypothesis that
  the Bhakti could have permeated Sindh through the teaching of Guru Nanak… as preserved in the **Granth
  Sahib**" (p. 90, folio 77); as one of "the heralds" with Kabir and Mirabai (p. 91, folio 78); as the
  figure a Nanakpanthi follows, "or of his son Shri Chand" (p. 103, folio 90); "the late participaters in
  the Bhakti movement which had a major impact in Sindh **through the spread of Guru Nanak's teaching**"
  (Motwani 1986: 155) (p. 91, folio 78); his picture on Rochaldas's wall among "pictures of Guru Nanak,
  Qutub Ali Shah, Sai Baba, Ramachandra and Krishna" (p. 134, folio 121); named in the jai formula "the
  gods, including Guru Nanak, Jhulelal as well as Mast qalandar" (p. 141, folio 128); "**The book starts
  with Guru Nanak and Jhulelal**, here named Darya Shah (and Zinda Pir in the caption of the image)"
  (p. 156, folio 143); Sachal's portrait "in a pose that could be reminiscent of **Guru Nanak's
  iconography**" (p. 151, folio 138); "the message of the Sufis can also be found through Guru Nanak,
  Mirabai and Kabir, as well as in other sants' poetry" (p. 160, folio 147); "**Guru Nanak's poetry has
  been kept** in this new edition" of Nimano Faqir's kalam, where Sachal's was dropped (p. 172,
  folio 159); and "the Guru Granth Sahib and the ten Sikh gurus" (p. 199, folio 186). Indexed "Guru Nanak
  40, 41, 55, 76, 77, 78, 90, 120, 121, 128, 138, 143, 147, 159, 196 n.13, 203 n.7" (p. 242, folio 229);
  in the bibliography, "Rana, Nimrita, *An Exploration into the Holy Mission of Guru Nanak*, Sindhi
  Community House in Cricklewood, North London, M. A. Dissertation, Department of African Studies and
  Anthropology, University of Birmingham, 2014" (p. 237, folio 224). The glossary: "**Gurdwara**: the
  Sikh place of worship." (p. 219, folio 206) **No gurdwara, no Nankana Sahib, no date and no bani text
  is given anywhere: (not stated).**

- **amb-temples-amb-sharif**, **bhagnari-mandir**, **chandragup-baba-chandragup**, **katas-raj-temples**, **shahwala-teja-singh-mandir**, **shiv-mandir-chiti-ghati**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir** (the book's "Shiva", "Shivaite", "Mahadev" does not occur)
  — **person-level: all eight rows of which Shiva is the principal figure; the book names no Shiva temple
  that matches any of them.** "Gorakhnath, who **is believed to be an avatar of Shiva**" (p. 77,
  folio 64); "the Nathpanthis, **a Shivaite school of renunciants**" (p. 21, folio 8); "a small statue of
  Shiva **in the position of the mahayogi**" in the gaddi nashin's flat at Rochaldas's darbar (p. 80,
  folio 67); "Shiva with the snake (**nanga**)" (p. 119, folio 106); "There is also in the courtyard **an
  altar devoted to Shiva**" at Pithoro Pir's darbar (p. 179, folio 166); the pre-partition Sehwan temple
  "devoted to Shiva and settled in the north-eastern part of the town" (pp. 179–180, folios 166–167); at
  Tando Ahmad Khan, "**a shrine devoted to Shiva**" at the dargah entrance and, "about 100 metres away…
  a compound with **several Shivaite temples**" (p. 185, folio 172); "The Gazetteer of 1907 describes
  them as **worshippers of Shiva**" — of the Sodhas of Umarkot (p. 191, folio 178); "**Devi, the wife of
  Shiva**" (p. 192, folio 179); "Khetrapal, a disciple of Shiva" (p. 43, folio 30). The glossary:
  "**Jogi**: wandering Shivaite ascetics who meditate on One" (p. 219, folio 206). Indexed "Shiva (god)
  30" and, under `avatar`, "of Shiva 64, 166" (pp. 239, 249, folios not stated and 236). **None of the
  Shiva shrines the book names is any of these eight rows: (not stated).**

- **krishna-mandir-kabari-bazar**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road** (the book's "Krishna", also "Shyam",
  "Kaniyya", "Kanhaiya") — **person-level: both rows of which Krishna is the principal figure; the book
  names no Krishna temple.** "Rochaldas had a special devotion to Krishna and, for him, the two
  additional attributes that Krishna possessed were **love (prem/ishq) and a bad reputation
  (ninda/malamat)**" (p. 57, folio 44); "Sachal mentions different Hindu gods, such as Ram, Hanuman, Sita
  and **especially Krishna**", where "he is named **Krishna or Shyam**… Qutub Ali Shah calls him
  **Kaniyya**, the Hindi equivalent being **Kanhaiya**, a name for Krishna when he was a teenager.
  **Shyam would mean dark, as is the meaning of Krishna**" (p. 106, folio 93); "Qutub Ali Shah was fond
  of Krishna" (p. 96, folio 83); "Gehimal called Krishna **ashiq baghwan, the Lord of Love**" (p. 135,
  folio 122); Krishna with Radha as the symbol of "divine love… known as **Bhakti, or in Sindhi
  Bhagti**" (p. 132, folio 119) and "represented as the embodiment of mystical love" (p. 198,
  folio 185); "there is **Krishna as a flute player, without Radha**" on the Rohani Rah altar (p. 152,
  folio 139); "The next step of the initiation ritual shows the importance of Krishna among the Sindhi
  Sufis, including Muslim Sufis such as Sachal and Qutub Ali Shah" (p. 136, folio 123); "the
  **Krishnaite Bhakti** was influential among wujudi Sufis" (p. 198, folio 185); Sami "the most
  influential Sindhi author of the Bhakti, with a strong devotion for the god Krishna" (p. 94, folio 81);
  Darya Khan "excelled in his verses on Krishna 'as if he were another devotee like Mirabai' (Ajwani
  1970: 116)" (p. 107, folio 94); Vallabhacharya's "worship was mostly devoted to Krishna" (p. 214,
  folio 201, Ch. 5 n. 4); and Ramakrishna's absorption "in other incarnations such as Hanuman, Sita and
  Krishna" (p. 104, folio 91). Indexed "Krishna (god) 32, 35, 44, 76, 77, 81, 93–4, 121, 122, 200 n.19",
  with "Kaniyya *see* Krishna (god)" and "Shyam *see* Krishna (god)", and — separately — "**Krishna
  (Sufi) 119, 120, 122**", a person of that name (pp. 244–245, folios 231–232). **No temple, no
  Rawalpindi, no Lahore: (not stated).**

- **gorakhnath-goraknath-temple**, **tilla-jogian** (the book's "Gorakhnath") — **person-level: both
  rows of which Guru Gorakhnath is the principal figure.** The material is set out in the two subsections
  above (pp. 21, 77, 120, 188, folios 8, 64, 107, 175; index p. 242, folio 229). **The head is repeated
  here so that the person-level character of the attachment is on the record: the book names no temple
  and no site for him at all.**

- **lal-kurti-temple-balmiki-mandir-rawalpindi**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **valmiki-swamiji-mandir-gracy-lines-rawalpindi** (the book's "Valmiki")
  — **person-level: all three rows of which Valmiki is the principal figure.** Named once, as an author:
  the Shri Yoga Vaisishtha is "a spiritual dialogue between Shri Ramchandra, an incarnation of Vishnu,
  and Shri Vasishtha as rendered by **Valmiki, the legendary author of the Ramayana**" (p. 163,
  folio 150). Indexed "Valmiki 150" (p. 251, folio 238). **No temple, no site, no date, no gloss beyond
  that clause: (not stated).**

- **ram-mandir-saidpur-ram-kund-mandir** (the book's "Rama", "Ram", "Ramachandra", "Shri Ramchandra",
  "Sri Ramchandra") — **person-level.** "Sachal mentions different Hindu gods, such as **Ram**, Hanuman,
  Sita and especially Krishna" and "Sachal also refers to this episode of the Ramayana" — Diwali and
  Ram's return to Ayodhya (p. 106, folio 93); "Murad stated that the jogis are fascinated by **Rama**
  (Paniker 2000: 437)" (p. 108, folio 95); "Qutub Ali Shah gives the names of Laxman and Sita, **Ram** and
  Krishna" (p. 107, folio 94); "**Ramachandra**" among the pictures on Rochaldas's wall (p. 134,
  folio 121); "**Sri Ramchandra**" (p. 139, folio 126); "**Shri Ramchandra, an incarnation of Vishnu**"
  in the Yoga Vasishtha dialogue (p. 163, folio 150). Indexed "**Rama (god)** 76, 77, 94, 121, 126, 150"
  (p. 247, folio 234). **The mapping is made on the index's own headword "Rama (god)", which is the
  archive row's principal figure exactly; equating the book's "Shri Ramchandra" with "Rama" is a step the
  book does not print and is flagged rather than taken. No temple, no Saidpur, no Islamabad: (not
  stated).**

- **shri-varun-dev-mandir** (the book's "Varun", "the Vedic god Varun") — **person-level.** "Jhulelal was
  probably a modern figure of **the Vedic god Varun, the god of the Indus River**" (p. 54, folio 41);
  "The Hindus worship Jhulelal, who they claim to be **an avatar of the Vedic God Varun**" (p. 178,
  folio 165). Indexed twice, inverted, with identical runs on facing index pages: "Varun (Vedic god) 41,
  165" and "Vedic god Varun 41, 165" (pp. 251–252, folios 238–239), with "avatar — of Vedic god Varun
  165" (p. 239, folio not stated). **No temple, no Manora, no Karachi: (not stated).**

- **kalat-kali-temple**, **kali-bari-mandir**, **kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi** (the book's
  "Kali") — **person-level; the third row is the weakest of the three, since the archive prints its
  principal figure as "Kalka Devi (Kali)".** "He mentioned Ramakrishna had attained **fanai (absorption)
  in the goddess Kali**, as well as in other incarnations such as Hanuman, Sita and Krishna, as well as
  Muhammad and Jesus (Hari 1995: 316)." (p. 104, folio 91) "The most frequent forms are **Sitala Mata**,
  the very powerful goddess of smallpox, and **Kali**." (p. 192, folio 179) Indexed "Kali (goddess ) 91"
  (p. 244, folio 231 — **the stray space before the closing parenthesis is as printed**). **No temple, no
  Peshawar, no Kalat, no Aror: (not stated).**

- **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi** (the book's "Hanuman") — **person-level.** "In his poetry,
  Sachal mentions different Hindu gods, such as Ram, **Hanuman** (as we saw above), Sita and especially
  Krishna" (p. 106, folio 93); "Sachal mentions Hanuman while Qutub Ali Shah gives the names of Laxman
  and Sita, Ram and Krishna" (p. 107, folio 94); Ramakrishna's absorption "in other incarnations such as
  **Hanuman**, Sita and Krishna" (p. 104, folio 91). Indexed "Hanuman (god) 94" (p. 243, folio 230).
  **No temple, no Soldier Bazaar, no "Panchmukhi": (not stated).**

- **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan** (the book's "Jhulelal", also "Udero Lal", "Amar Lal", "Darya Shah", "Zinda Pir")
  — **person-level: all three rows that name this figure as a principal figure; the site-specific Udero
  Lal material stays in its own subsection above and is not repeated here.** "the **isht devta** of the
  Hindu Sindhis of India, Jhulelal" (p. 70, folio 57); "the famous god of the Hindu Sindhis" (p. 184,
  folio 171); the inscription "**Jhulelal bera par** (May Jhulelal safely guide our nave)" (p. 186,
  folio 173); "The book starts with Guru Nanak and Jhulelal, here named **Darya Shah** (and **Zinda Pir**
  in the caption of the image)" (p. 156, folio 143); "the consecration of Jhulelal as **isht devta, or
  community God, of the Hindu Sindhis of India**" (p. 156, folio 143); "rather focusing on Jhulelal as
  the embodiment of the Sindhi culture in India" (p. 157, folio 144); "the last four pages of the book
  are the devotional songs devoted to Jhulelal (**panjro**)" (p. 158, folio 145); "Jhulelal was already
  worshipped by some segments among the Hindus of Sindh, but as we saw before, **they were in majority
  Nanakpanthis**" (p. 158, folio 145); "from the 1960s onwards, it comes after Jhulelal’s worship, which
  can be seen as a kind of **soft hinduization** of the Sindhi legacy in India" (p. 159, folio 146);
  "**With the spread of the Jhulelal's apotheosis, room for the Sufi legacy was reduced** among the
  Hindu Sindhis" (p. 201, folio 188); "the Sindhi god of India par excellence" (p. 174, folio 161); named
  in the jai formula (p. 141, folio 128) and among Pithoro Pir's travelling companions (p. 192,
  folio 179); "a growing similarity between the iconography of Guru Nanak and Jhulelal" (p. 54,
  folio 41). Two temples of his are named without an archive row: "the Thakurs who were running
  **Jhulelal's temple in the northwestern part of the town** [Sehwan]" (p. 71, folio 58), and in
  Ulhasnagar "Gehimal was given a piece of land almost facing a **temple of `Jhulelal Lal` [text-layer?
  Jhulelal]** that had recently been built by Sehwani Thakurs" (p. 83, folio 70) — **neither is named or
  dated beyond "recently"; no custodian beyond "Thakurs": (not stated).** Indexed "Jhulelal 41, 57, 58,
  70, 128, 140, 143–8, 157, 161, 165, 167, 171, 173, 179, 181, 188, 198 n.5, 198 n.6" (p. 244,
  folio 231) — **the heaviest index run in the book for any figure the archive holds as a principal
  figure** — with "Amar Lal 41" indexed separately and **the book nowhere stating that Amar Lal is
  Jhulelal: (not stated)**; "Udero Lal 29–30, 38, 41, 165" (p. 251, folio 238) and "Zinda Pir 41"
  (p. 252, folio 239).

## Province-level material

*Swept from the raw chunks in two parts by two workers, whose bullets were merged and then de-duplicated (§9.267): part A `chunk_001`–`chunk_006` (PDF pp. 1–125), part B `chunk_007`–`chunk_013` (PDF pp. 126–253: the end of Ch. 4, Chs 5–7, the General conclusion, Notes, Glossary, Annexes; bibliography and index skipped as locator-only). Under ruling (c), only statements about a province or region in general are kept; statements really about one named town or site stay with that toponym and are listed under Sweep notes. The book is about the Hindu Sindhis and the Sufi tradition of Sindh after their migration to India, so "Sindh"/"Sindhi" occur hundreds of times. "Sindhi" used as the ethnonym of the migrant community in India, or for the language or its literature, is not treated as a statement about Sindh, and statements about the community's institutions in India are left out. Register is marked on every bullet: **author's argument** (Boivin's own claims), **book's historical prose** (his narrative of colonial Sindh and Partition, usually with a cited source), **ethnographic present** (what the author observed or reports as current), **author's anecdote**, or **quoted source** (named). Endnote, glossary and annex texts are cited by the PDF page they are printed on. Folio = PDF page − 13, as printed in the running heads; chapter-opening pages print none and are cited "folio not stated". Every bullet is province-level: weigh accordingly.*

### province: punjab

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the eighteenth century"; book's historical prose, hedged "It is possible"): on the Nanakpanth among Sindh's Hindus: "The Nanakpanth would have been initiated later. It is possible that the Aroras imported this cult from Punjab during their migrations in Sindh in the eighteenth century. The Nanakpanth is a form of Sikhism that does not recognize the authority of the Khalsa" (p. 43, folio 30).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the second half of the nineteenth century"; book's historical prose, citing Oberoi 1994): "the first attempt to rationalize and uniformize the Sikh tradition was implemented. This process of normalizing the Sikh tradition was centred on Punjab, and it did not concern the Sindhi Nanakpanthis who were heterodox Sikhs" (p. 53, folio 40).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, historical; author's argument citing Lorenzen 1996): "The Nirguni tradition was more influential in Northern India, especially in Punjab, where the Sikh tradition historically derived from the Nirguni tradition of the Bhakti" (p. 89, folio 76).
- (book's word: "the southern present Punjab"; refers to: an unstated past time ("at the time when"); author's argument): the shared devotional culture "would be called Sindhian culture, which refers to a set of social, religious and cultural traits that existed in the Sindhi area at the time when it encompassed the southern present Punjab, and also the territories of Rajasthan and Gujarat" (p. 53, folio 40). The book does not say whether "present Punjab" means the Pakistani province. The Rajasthan/Gujarat half is under `not attached`.
- (book's word: "Southern Punjab", "South Punjab"; refers to: present day for the language; "the Middle Ages" for the historical claim; author's argument, in an endnote, the glossary and the main text): Ch. 6 n. 11: "The Siraiki language is mainly spoken in Southern Punjab, with Multan as capital. Interestingly, this area was a part of Sindh during the Middle Ages" (p. 216, folio 203). Glossary: Siraiki is "a language spoken in Northern Sindh and Southern Punjab" (p. 220, folio 207). In the main text, for some specialists the Siraiki of Northern Sindh is "a distinct language from the Siraiki of South Punjab" (p. 171, folio 158).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument, in an endnote and Annex 2): "the Chishtiyya was not widespread in Sindh, while it is dominant in Punjab and North India" (General conclusion n. 1, p. 217, folio 204). "The Chishtiyya is usually considered to be one of the most powerful Sufi orders in Northern India and Punjab" (p. 227, folio 214). The book does not say which Punjab.
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the Pax Britannica imposed by the colonial rule", and present-day scholarship; author's argument): scholars of Sindhi studies treat Sindh's religious encounters "as if comparable dynamics were not implemented in other regions, be they neighbouring Sindh, such as Punjab, Rajasthan or Gujarat, or elsewhere". "During the Pax Britannica imposed by the colonial rule, the exchange of goods and products was reinforced between the different regions of the north-western Indian subcontinent, including Punjab, Rajasthan, Gujarat and Sindh". Beyond the merchants there were "networks of circulating knowledge, and especially religious knowledge" (p. 196, folio not stated). The book does not give this colonial "Punjab" any boundary.

### province: sindh

- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: before the migration "from 1947 onwards", as the book contrasts it; author's argument): "I was well aware that the Sindhi Hindus of Sindh had gone to Sufi shrines, and that they shared their passion for Sufi poetry with Muslim Sindhis and others, especially through Shah Abd al-Latif's Shah jo Risalo" (p. 14, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sufi culture of Sindh", "the region"; refers to: "the turn of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries", "as early as the middle of the nineteenth century"; author's argument): it is necessary "to understand why and how it constituted the dominant form of the episteme of the region at the turn of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. The historical context is fundamental: it is dominated by British colonization". The emergence rested on "interaction between new local elites and colonial administrators, as early as the middle of the nineteenth century" (p. 17, folio 4). "The Sufi culture of Sindh was nonetheless the most shared product, but this does not prevent the society from being highly fragmented" (said of the Sindhicate area, p. 19, folio 6). "Sufi poetry is the backbone of Sufism in Sindh, including for the Hindu Sufis" (p. 26, folio 13).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "As early as 1851" and 1856, and "During British colonization" (Burton's book "published in 1851") with the present tense for the dual identities; quoted source: Burton 1851, Raikes 1856, and author's argument): "Richard Burton pointed out that certain populations of Sindh could not be considered a Muslim or a Hindu (Burton 1851: 307, 323)". Raikes "spoke of populations that are not recognized by Muslims or Hindus (Raikes 1856: 4)". This "neither…nor" category "met the credo of the Sufis of Sindh, whose works known as kalams constituted the foundations of Sufi culture" (pp. 19–20, folios 6–7). Also: "As Richard Burton noted as early as 1851, it is indeed the worship of the intercessors that constitutes the common religious idiom of Hindus and Muslims (Burton 1851: 325)" (p. 51, folio 38). Restated in Ch. 7: "In Sindh, we already know that a number of sacred figures have a dual religious identity, Muslim and Hindu. The Muslim identity is always related to Sufism, while the Hindu side usually belongs to the Nathpanth. During British colonization, there was much evidence of the flexibility of religious belonging in Sindh". Burton "stated that different populations of Sindh were 'neither Hindoos nor Moslems'", applied explicitly to the Shikaris and the Dheds, "while other authors consider the Shikaris as Muslims, and the Dheds as Hindus" (p. 187, folio 174). The Pithoro Pir case shows "the variety of the negotiations between Sufism and Hinduism in Sindh" (p. 192, folio 179).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the Muslim Sindhis"; refers to: "before the eighteenth century" and "the early eighteenth century"; book's historical prose): "Historical sources provide evidence that, before the eighteenth century, the people who identified themselves as Muslims were few", "probably" because the denomination "was restricted to the sayyids and their dependents". In the early eighteenth century, Naqshbandi treatises "were written in Sindh by Naqshbandi scholars from Lowari Sharif". These scholars showed "that the Muslim Sindhis did not know the Quran", wrote paraphrases and then "a Sindhi translation of the Arabic Quran", and "condemned some rituals, especially coined as Hindu or Shia, as non-Islamic" (pp. 22–23, folios 9–10).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: a long-term sequence: an "Ismaili step", then "From the thirteenth century onward" classical Sufism, then "the colonial step"; author's argument after Pollock): "Manuscripts and early lithographed books provide evidence that the religious culture of Sindh had been vernacularized". "In Sindh, the first phase was usually a linguistic translation", from "Arabic and Persian into Sindhi". The colonial step meant that, "because of a number of new factors (including the census), the people had more and more to identify themselves according to their affiliation to 'great religions'… Before, they had not identified themselves through a religious persuasion, but according to their locality, caste and ethnicity" (p. 23, folio 10).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the Khairpur emirate in Sindh"; refers to: "after 1857"; quoted source: Dahnhardt 2002): after 1857 "many Kayasht migrated to work in the Princely States, including the Khairpur emirate in Sindh". Despite Brahmashankar Mishra's years in Karachi and Hyderabad, "no formal relation between Sindh and the Hindu branch of the Naqshbandiyya has been found" (p. 26, folio 13).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the literati in Sindh"; refers to: "the nineteenth century", "During the Raj"; quoted source: Narsain 1932): Narsain "clearly states that the integration of Sufism inside of the religious environment of the Hindu Sindhis dates back to the nineteenth century, and that the main actors involved were the Amils, the literary class". "In the context of Sindh, the Sufi Master shares many features with what Weber names a guru, but his followers, the Amils, belong to the literate class" (p. 27, folio 14). "while the Amils were the bulk of the literati in Sindh, they were also instrumental in incorporating Sufism in the religiosity of the local Hindus" (p. 45, folio 32).
- (book's word: "Sindh, Pakistan"; refers to: present day as of Thapan 2005; quoted source: Thapan 2005): Thapan "rightly observed that the spiritual masters (sajjada nashin or pir) are still in Sindh, Pakistan", namely those of Daraza, Tando Jahaniya and Jhok Sharif (p. 27, folio 14).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: period not stated; quoted source: Kothari 2009, and author's argument): Kothari "finds explanations in the localization of Sindh as a frontier. This means there was a permanent influx of migrants… Sindh was localized far from the main imperial centres" (p. 28, folio 15). Boivin: "It is a fact that Sindh, because of its geographical location both as an isolated and a buffer zone, has hosted many unorthodox versions of normative religions, especially Hinduism and Islam. This plays out as a breeding ground for new religious elaborations that flourished especially in the nineteenth century" (p. 29, folio 16).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "colonial Sindh"; refers to: "the nineteenth century", "from 1850 onwards"; author's argument): "In Sindh, another Persian word was mostly used, that of darbar"; "In Sindh, the darbar was the sacred territory in which the Sufi culture was at work". "I argue that Sindh hosted a specific shared religious culture resulting from a process of vernacularization, which is not contradictory of the fact that some of its features can also be found in neighbouring provinces". The result of colonization was "the spread of intelligentsia from 1850 onwards" and a renewed religiosity "among the Sindhis, whether they were Muslim, Sikh or Hindu", performed in the darbar, a term "used by the Sikhs, the Muslims and the Hindus" (pp. 37–38, folios 24–25; announced at p. 30, folio 17, as "the darbar culture" of "colonial Sindh").
- (book's word: "Sindh", "a Muslim-dominated region"; refers to: "the late nineteenth century", up to Partition; author's argument): "the Sindhis of the late nineteenth century were more and more inclined to identify themselves with a religion as it would be acknowledged by the colonial power" (p. 51, folio 38). The migrants came "From a Muslim-dominated region… While through the late census in Sindh, they identified more and more as Hindu" (p. 33, folio 20).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: period not stated, with colonial rule as the renewing force; author's argument): "Frequently, the claim arises that there was no caste system in Sindh, or at least not so rigid a caste system as seen in other parts of India, because of the very small number of Brahmans. Indeed, it is possible that Sindh hosted a specific social fabric", but the colonial power "operated a deep renewal of the social fabric, in Sindh and of course everywhere else in India" (pp. 36–37, folio not stated / folio 24).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "the first census of 1872"; quoted source: Hughes 1876): "Sindh's population statistics are not available in the first census of 1872. The population was 2,333,527 million. Sindh had 1,718,688 Muslims, 393,092 Hindus, 5,034 Christians and 86,663 persons put in a category titled 'Other castes such as Sikhs, Parsis, Jews, etc.' (Hughes 1876: 878)". The figures are kept as printed. See Sweep notes (p. 38, folio 25).
- (book's word: "the society of Sindh", "the Muslim society of Sindh"; refers to: "1843", "Before 1843", Talpur and earlier Kalhora rule; book's historical prose citing Ansari 1992, Westphal-Hellbusch 1964, Burton 1851): asking "How was the society of Sindh organized in 1843?", the book says Muslim society "was segmented according to ethnic criteria that distinguished the Ashrafs and the Ajlafs". "Given the political domination of the Talpurs, the Baluchis were at the top of local society", having provided since the eighteenth century "the bulk of the troops of the Kalhoras, who had ruled Sindh before them", and holding jagirs. "In Sindh, the Sayyids had two characteristics. The majority of them were landowners (zamindar), but they were also pir". "The pirs of Sindh play the role of mediators between the tribes, and between the rulers and the governed". "The power of the pir was reinforced in the eighteenth century when the Kalhora developed the irrigation system" (Ansari 1992: 28–9). "Apart from a few groups of Afghans and Pathans in the northwest, the bulk of the Muslims were Sindhis, most of whom were landless farmers (hari)". The trading Muslims were "the Memon, the Khojah and the Bohrah", and excluded groups such as the Shikaris and the "Shidi" stood at the bottom. "Before 1843, Muslims were divided between being Sunnis and Shiites. The Sunnis were in the majority, but the sovereigns, the Talpur, were themselves Shiites"; Burton "asserted that Arabic was known only to Muslim scholars" (pp. 38–41, folios 25–28).
- (book's word: "the Hindu society of Sindh", "Eastern Sindh", "rural Sindh"; refers to: Talpur and British periods, as stated; book's historical prose citing Aitken 1907, Thakur 1959): "In the Hindu society of Sindh, the British knowledge is often limited to mercantile castes from which two points arose: the absence of a real system of castes and the small role played by the Brahmans". The Saraswat Brahmans "accepted food from the Lohanas". The Lohanas "represented half of the total Hindu population" and were divided into Amils and Bhaibands. "In Eastern Sindh, the Rajput had retained their feudal prerogatives under the Talpur, and the British had reinstated them in exchange for their loyalty". "The panchayat system prevailed among the Hindus of Sindh. In rural Sindh, the panchayat of the Lohanas could have authority over other castes" (pp. 40–41, folios 27–28).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the east of the province"; refers to: "From the beginning of the nineteenth century onwards" and "between 1843 and 1872"; book's historical prose citing Thakur 1959 and the first census): "Contrary to popular belief, there were many untouchables in Sindh", among them "Bhils or Kohlis" and "the Menghwar, mostly settled around Umerkot". Then: "it is certain whether these populations were established in the east of the province, which is none other than the ultimate extension of the great Thar Desert" (printed so; the sense seems to need "uncertain", not supplied). "The first census reports that many would have migrated from Gujarat or Rajasthan between 1843 and 1872"; most "worked in agriculture", and "crafts were largely in the hands of Muslims" (p. 40, folio 27).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the Naqshbandis of Sindh"; refers to: the eighteenth-century manuscripts, period otherwise not stated; book's historical prose): "The Naqshbandis of Sindh followed the reforming enterprise led by Shah Waliullah in Delhi". Of Shia devotional poetry (marsiya): "It is noteworthy that, in Sindh, this devotional poetry is cherished by both Muslims and Hindus, the latter being performers as well as authors" (p. 42, folio 29).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "nineteenth-century Sindh"; refers to: tradition "in the mid-tenth century", a sanctuary "dating back to the seventeenth century", "nineteenth-century Sindh"; book's historical prose citing Thakur 1959, Fuller 1992): "Among the Lohanas and related mercantile groups, two main cults were followed: the Nanakpanth and the Daryapanth". The Daryapanth "is in all probability the oldest of the two. It is based on the deification of the Indus River", run by "a priestly caste, the Thakurs". The Lohana practised animal sacrifice at Navratri and for Khetrapal. "The Rajputs did not practise in either". "In Sindh, the most popular deified heroes were Rama Pir (Ramdeo or Ramdev), Pithoro Pir, Pabuji and Guga Pir" (pp. 42–43, folios 29–30). "In Sindh, many Nanakpanthis belonged to a subgroup known as the Udasis" (p. 53, folio 40). "In nineteenth-century Sindh, the Nanakpanth, or 'the path of Nanak', was probably the dominant religious path among the Hindu Sindhis" (p. 90, folio 77).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "peripheral regions like Sindh", "the rural areas of Sindh"; refers to: "the last quarter of the nineteenth century", "the end of the nineteenth century"; author's argument citing Cheesman 1997): "Sindh, like most of the regions of India, was subject to this change", the "objectification of the Sufi legacy in Sindh". "the complex process through which new classes appeared in peripheral regions like Sindh has not yet gotten such attention", except "the domination of new Hindu groups in the rural areas of Sindh". After "the growth of new emporium in Sindh, especially Karachi", the Lohanas invested "in the agricultural lands, mainly cotton, and they became powerful landowners, locally known as zamindars, at the end of the nineteenth century" (pp. 43–44, folios 30–31).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the commissioner in Sindh"; refers to: "the Talpur rule (1783–1843)", "when the British conquered Sindh", "the 1850s"; book's historical prose): the Amils, "probably from the eighteenth century onwards" in administration, "almost monopolized the function of kardar" under the Talpurs. "when the British conquered Sindh, they had already formed a separate social class". "When Persian as official language was given up in the 1850s, Sindhi and English were the two languages used in the administration". The Amils led "the huge campaign of translation launched by Sir Bartle Frere (1815–84), the commissioner in Sindh" (p. 44, folio 31). Conclusion: "In Sindh, the main social structure was a quasi-feudal system, with a civil servant class and the groups involved in trade, but the policy implemented by the British commissioners, especially Sir Bartle Frere, gave birth to the Sindhi intelligentsia" (p. 63, folio 50).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "1843", 1885, 1896–1907; book's historical prose citing Gidumal 1932, Hughes 1872, Aitken 1907, Sadhu Vaswani 2001): of European spiritual movements, "The first to reach Sindh was that of Freemasonry, since the conqueror and first governor of Sindh, Sir Charles Napier, built a Masonic lodge in 1843 in Karachi". After Reverend Bambridge's Karachi lectures, "In Sindh, the lectures led to a revival of religious and theological studies" (p. 46, folio 33). Annie Besant "was known in Sindh as Basanti Devi" (p. 47, folio 34).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "the 1850s", "1866", "the 1890s"; author's argument): "In Sindh, a printing press was brought to Karachi in the 1850s, but the main event in relation to Sufism came with the printing in 1866 of the Shah jo Risalo". This was followed by the "rationalization" of the poetry "initiated by Hindu literati in the 1890s" (pp. 47–48, folios 34–35).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: Parsram's own day (book published 1924) and an unstated past; quoted source: Jethmal Parsram, *Sind and its Sufis*, 1924): Parsram summarized "the many religions that have flourished in Sindh, like Buddhism, Jainism and Hinduism. More recently, he added, two other religions had become dominant: Sikhism and Sufism". He wrote: "there is hardly a country in the whole of Asia, including India, in which the mystic thought of two great civilizations, the Indian and the Arabic-Iranian, is seen as in so beautiful a union as in Sind" (Parsram 1924: 83–4). He also gave "what he called the mystic doctrine of Sind" (p. 49, folio 36).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "rural Sindh"; refers to: period not stated ("for centuries"), with "the late nineteenth century" as the turning point; author's argument citing Thakur 1959): "In rural Sindh, they [Muslims] worshipped the smallpox goddess Sitala when epidemics struck the village". "In the religiosity of Sindh, the devotional relationship of a follower with a spiritual Master was prevalent, rather than the focus being on affiliation to an institutionalized religion" (p. 51, folio 38). "Sindh had drawn – for centuries – a kind of specific religiosity based on devotion, and which largely considered faith as being located beyond any formal belonging to an institutionalized religion" (p. 66, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the Sindhi area"; refers to: "before on the eve of British colonization" (printed so) and "the Sindh of the nineteenth century"; author's argument): the pothi "allow[s] us to bring to light a first characteristic of the episteme of Sindh before on the eve of British colonization". "Devotional literature in Sindhi thus formed the basis of a culture that transcended social, religious and ethnic divisions". The culture "was not transmitted by the Ulemas or the pandits… this culture was primarily passed on to the villages by the caste musicians (manghanar or mirasi)" (pp. 52–53, folios 39–40). (The "Sindhian culture" area sentence is under `punjab` and `not attached`.)
- (book's word: "colonial Sindh", "Sindh"; refers to: "the late eighteenth century", "the second half of the nineteenth century", "the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries", "the last decade of the nineteenth century", and "the late nineteenth century and early twentieth century" with the "Gurudwara Reform Act of 1925" (General conclusion); book's historical prose, and author's argument plus an endnote citing Jatt 2016): the recomposition of the religious scene "is attested to by the creation and the development of several important Nanakpanthi darbars throughout Sindh, the attempt to centralize the Daryapanth and the making of Hindu Sufi paths". "although some darbars had been created in the late eighteenth century, there was a renaissance of the Nanakpanthi darbars in the second half of the nineteenth century". In colonial Sindh there was "the attempt made to create a centralized path devoted to Jhulelal, then known as Amar Lal or Udero Lal", with "an attempt to impose a specific script, the Khudawadi", and "a meeting organized by several Hindu panchayats of Sindh at Udero Lal" (pp. 53–54, folios 40–41). Restated in the General conclusion: "Sindh was submitted to a religious recomposition in the late nineteenth century and early twentieth century, as is attested by the building of many new Nanakpanthi darbars all over Sindh" (p. 199, folio 186). After the Singh Sabha process, "The Sindhi Nanakpanthis were thus not included in the Sikh community, and they identified themselves as being a Hindu religious sect". Also: "A main factor in the religious renewal in Sindh was the new formulation of Hinduism that spread all over India in the late nineteenth century: the Neo-Vedanta as built by Ramakrishna" (p. 200, folio 187). And: "Due to the spread of the Nanakpanth in Sindh, the Hindu Sufis were very familiar with Sikh terminology … in Sindh, the groups of Sufis and Nanakpanthis did not have a central organization ruling an encompassing body of followers" (p. 202, folio 189). Endnote: "The Udasis of Sindh are coined as being a heterodox Sikh tradition, in one of the very few, if not the sole, academic papers devoted to the Nanakpanth in Sindh (Jatt 2016)" (Ch. 1 n. 10, p. 209, folio 196).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "Northern Sindh"; refers to: "sometime after the British conquest"; author's argument, hedged "could be a clue"): since two of the Hindu Sufis came from Northern Sindh (Shikarpur, Rohri), "This could be a clue suggesting that the devotional centres of Sindh had shifted north sometime after the British conquest, despite the role played by the Hyderabadi Amils in the spread of the Sufi culture" (p. 60, folio 47).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the province"; refers to: Partition, "1947", "between December of 1939 and February of 1940" for the precedent; book's historical prose and author's argument, citing Kumar and Kothari 2016, Zamindar 2007): "it is a common trope to claim that Sindh did not suffer the violence experienced by Punjab". Yet "dramatic reports about Punjab were reaching the province", and "there was a precedent of communal violence in Sindh, this being the 'Sukkur riots'". Those riots "occurred beyond the city, in other cities such as Rohri, and also in other parts of Sindh, such as in Lakki" (p. 61, folio 48). "even if it never reached the level of violence in Punjab, it is irrelevant to claim there was no communal violence in Sindh" (p. 62, folio 49). The Rochaldas narrative "clearly alludes to the fact that the spread of violence throughout Sindh was not perpetrated by the Muslim Sindhis, but by the Mohajirs who clearly wanted to take over the houses of the Hindus, at least in Karachi". Also, "a number of Hindus had sent their wives and children to India already, while they were staying in Sindh" (pp. 70–71, folios 57–58). "From the 1920s onwards, there was an increase of the riots between Hindus and Muslims, a growing tension that culminated with partition" (p. 64, folio 51).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "1941" and "1947"–"14 January" [1948]; book's historical prose citing Khuhro 1998, Dawn): "In 1941, the Hindus made up about 21 per cent of the total population of Sindh, including 6 per cent of the scheduled castes; therefore, they would have numbered at less than 1 million – exactly 0.9 million". The migrants "would have been closer to 0.8 million, if not less… there is a kind of consensus that they numbered around 1 million" (p. 61, folio 48). "By early November of 1947, 246,000 Hindus had already left Sindh by sea, air or rail. As of 1 January 1948, it was estimated that nearly 500,000 people had sought refuge in India. Hindus from interior Sindh enjoyed special trains chartered by the Sindh government" (p. 62, folio 49). "in December, with the addition of a train, 140,000 Hindus crossed the border". "on 14 January, the government of India appointed a director general of evacuation to formally assist non-Muslims who wanted to migrate from Sindh to India" (p. 63, folio 50). The Hindu Sindhis "started leaving Sindh especially after the Karachi riots in January 1948" (p. 66, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "colonial Sindh", "Sindh"; refers to: British colonization through to Partition; author's argument): "The impact of British colonization in Sindh was twofold. First, it contributed to the objectification of Sufism, enthroning it as the fundamental of Sindhi culture at large. The second consequence was a renewal of religiosity in relation to the spread of a composite middle class. It was marked by the emergence of the darbars, spreading a darbar culture" (p. 64, folio 51).
- (book's word: "the darbars in Sindh", "the Sufi places in Sindh, now in Pakistan"; refers to: after 1947; book's historical prose): "The darbars in Sindh were most of the time preserved with different arrangements regarding their management", often with Indian gaddi nashins or followers paying keepers. "despite the fluctuant diplomatic relations between Pakistan and India, it was always possible to travel across the border" (p. 68, folio 55). In contrast, "After partition, Molchand was worried that it was difficult for Hindu Sindhi Sufis to pay a visit to the Sufi places in Sindh, now in Pakistan" (p. 124, folio 111).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "eighteenth-century Sindh"; refers to: Shah Latif's time ("eighteenth-century Sindh"), otherwise period not stated; author's argument citing Dalpat Sufi 1965, Briggs 1998): "In Sindh, the name of sami is generally given to a group of wandering faqirs"; "Verangins (or Berangins)" is "the local Sindhi name for the Nathpanthis"; "the role played by the Nathpanthis in Sindh comes in many forms" (p. 77, folio 64). Shah Latif's vocabulary for the jogis "gives evidence that they were Nathpanthis, and that they were therefore an important religious community in eighteenth-century Sindh". His poetry also shows "the importance of music both to him personally and, historically, to the region" and "the role played by the musician in the regional society" (p. 92, folio 79). The bayragins are relics: "In Sindh, once again irrespective of religious belonging, they are the mark of renunciation" (p. 120, folio 107).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "Northern Sindh"; refers to: "the sixteenth century" (Ajwani) and the Sami period; quoted source: Ajwani 1970, "Motwani 1986" as printed, and author's argument): "it is easy to find many shared characteristics between the Bhakti movement and Sufism, especially as it stood in colonial Sindh". Ajwani states "that in Sindh, due to its geographical isolation, the Bhakti was spread only in the sixteenth century (Ajwani 1970: 52)", but gives no evidence "of the influence of these sants on the spiritual life of Sindh" (p. 90, folio 77). Jotwani called Shah Latif and Sachal "the late participaters in the Bhakti movement which had a major impact in Sindh through the spread of Guru Nanak's teaching" (cited as "Motwani 1986: 155") (p. 91, folio 78). "one can surmise that Sami's slokas were widespread among the Hindus of Northern Sindh" (p. 104, folio 91).
- (book's word: "the landscape of Sindh"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument): "the Sufi brotherhoods that are dominant in the landscape of Sindh, such as the Qadiriyya or the Sohrawardiyya" (p. 98, folio 85).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: period not stated for the popularity, "905" for the visit; author's argument, an endnote and the glossary): "In Sindh, Hallaj is a very popular figure, and many Sufi poets mention his name in their verses" (p. 102, folio 89). Mansur Hallaj "was very popular in Sindh" (Ch. 3 n. 8, p. 212, folio 199). The glossary adds: "He visited Sindh in 905" (p. 219, folio 206).
- (book's word: "the Muslim Sufis of Sindh", "Upper Sindh", "the wujudi Sufism of Sindh"; refers to: "the nineteenth century, during colonial domination"; author's argument): "the Muslim Sufis of Sindh were mostly attracted by the Vishnuite school of Hinduism" (p. 107, folio 94). The Sufis to whom the Hindus refer "mostly belong to Upper Sindh, with Siraiki as a language used for Sufi poetry besides Sindhi", and "all belong to the wujudi school of the Sufism of Sindh, and thus the Naqshbandis are excluded" (p. 108, folio 95). "this trend in the wujudi Sufism of Sindh seems to have developed in the nineteenth century, during colonial domination" (p. 109, folio 96).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the fourteenth century — "1351", "1367", "1397" and "the following year", as printed; book's historical prose): of Pithoro Pir's lifetime, "the period when he lived was turbulent". "At the regional level, this period was that of the transition between the Somras and the Sammas. Although the latter officially took power from the first in 1351, the Somras had preserved their control of certain outlying areas of Sindh, including, precisely, the area of Umarkot. In 1351, the sultan of Delhi, Muhammad Shah Tughluq, arrived in Sindh in pursuit of a rebel". In 1367 Firuz Shah Tughluq "returned to lay siege to Thatha", and "Jam Juna II, who controlled Sindh then, sought alliances with the sultan of Gujarat" (p. 189, folio 176).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the Indus Valley"; refers to: "from the thirteenth century onwards" for the Sohrawardiyya, "modern Sindh", and otherwise period not stated; author's argument, in the main text, an endnote and Annex 2): "three main Sufi tariqas were dominant among the Hindu Sufis of Sindh (Boivin 2015)":
  - the Qadiriyya, "the most influential";
  - the Qalandariyya, which "in the case of Sindh … reflects the radiation of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar and the pilgrimage centre of Sehwan Sharif";
  - the Sohrawardiyya, "the oldest Sufi order that settled in Multan and was predominant in the Indus Valley from the thirteenth century onwards".
  "Maybe because of the Sohrawardiyya's hold on Sindh, the Chishtiyya could not spread in Sindh as it did in the rest of South Asia. Notwithstanding, music appeared very early in the Sufism of Sindh" (pp. 227–228, folios 214–215). See also "the Chishtiyya was not widespread in Sindh" (p. 217, folio 204). Elsewhere: "The Sufis of Sindh we have been dealing with were mostly in touch with the Sohrawardiyya and the Qadiriyya" (p. 139, folio 126). Breath discipline is "attested to in other Sufis of modern Sindh, such as the Qadiriyya" (p. 138, folio 125). And: "in Sindh, Sufi practice was more based on a personal allegiance with an individual than any affiliation with a Sufi tariqa. Furthermore, Sufis could be affiliated with several different tariqas … This topic is one of the numerous ones that deserves to be studied further in Sindh" (p. 200, folio 187).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "Southern Sindh"; refers to: "from the sixteenth century onwards", "From the end of the seventeenth century onwards", "after the beginning of the eighteenth century", and the present tense; author's argument, Annex 2):
  - The Naqshbandiyya "came to Sindh from the sixteenth century onwards, with the coming of the Tarkhans and the Arghuns, two Central Asian dynasties that ruled Sindh".
  - It has "several important centres in Sindh, especially that of Lowari Shari [text-layer?] [Lowari Sharif] located in Southern Sindh, near Badin".
  - Its later treatises on how to follow Islamic rules lead to the conclusion that "the Sindhis, even when they were officially Muslim, did not practise 'Orthodox Islam'".
  - A Hindu Naqshbandi branch "is hardly the case in Sindh", and "the majority of the Naqshbandis in Sindh are strongly opposed to music and to the singing of devotional songs" (p. 228, folio 215).
  - In the main text: "In Sindh, save the Naqshbandis, Sufism is dominated by poetry expressing the concept of wahdat-e wujud" (p. 162, folio 149).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the time of "Fath Ali Shah (d. 1824)", Talpur rule; endnote reporting the view of Ingvild Flaskerud): Flaskerud "thinks the first portrait of Ali was first drawn in Sindh and then brought as a gift to Fath Ali Shah (d. 1824), the shah of Persia who had friendly relations with the Talpurs, the emirs of Sindh, who were themselves Shia (Flaskerud : 25)" (Ch. 4 n. 5, p. 213, folio 200).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "1226" according to the Sodhas' own tradition, and "The Gazetteer of 1907"; book's historical prose reporting the Sodhas' tradition and the Gazetteer): "The Sodhas form the dominant group among Rajputs of Sindh and, according to their tradition, they would have come from Ujjain, in Malwa, in 1226. Their chief, Parmar Sodha, would have been established as king (rano) at Umarkot. The majority of them became landowners and farmers. The Gazetteer of 1907 describes them as worshippers of Shiva" (p. 191, folio 178).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the Hindus of Sindh"; refers to: "since the nineteenth century" and the present day; author's argument / ethnographic present): "In the field of Sindh studies, however, there is no published research related to Sufism and the Hindu Sufis in Sindh". "In Sindh, there are different ways through which the Hindus are related to Sufism", and the book sorts them into three categories, each illustrated by one site:
  - "the most common": a Sufi tomb "visited both by Muslims and Hindus" (example: Bhit Shah);
  - "also relatively common": a saint with "a double identity, Muslim and Hindu" (example: Udero Lal);
  - "a Sufi darbar … managed by the Hindus" (example: Pithoro Pir) (pp. 177–178, folios 164–165).
  The book adds: "the Hindus of Sindh are coming to the Sufi mausoleums since the nineteenth century" (p. 183, folio 170). "The large majority of the Hindus used to pay a visit to the Sufi shrines, but does it mean they are Sufi? Of course not. It does mean that, for them, the Sufis are a part of their religious legacy" (pp. 192–193, folios 179–180). The Hindus of Sindh in Pakistan incorporate the Sufi legacy through processes that "depend on the social milieu, the caste system and the urban/rural context" (p. 194, folio 181).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: present day, contrasted with the post-1947 darbars in India; author's argument / ethnographic present): the Sindhi darbars of India "are not centred on tombs, unlike those of Sindh" (p. 131, folio 118). In India the photographic portrait takes over the sacredness of the space "in place of the tomb, as is the case in the dargahs of Sindh" (p. 132, folio 119). The shahnais are "very common in any religious ritual performed in Sindh" (p. 145, folio 132). In India "a number of typical Sufi rituals performed in Sindh have been suppressed, such as the alam or the dhamal" (p. 152, folio 139).
