- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: period not stated, in the discussion of the Lal Kunwar custom that "disappeared during the partition in 1947"; author's argument): "The colour saffron, which corresponds in Sindh to a bright red, is both a religious symbol and a symbol of marriage for the Daryapanthis" (p. 181, folio 168).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "late-nineteenth-century Sindh", "the nineteenth century", "nineteenth-century Sindh"; author's argument with quoted sources Narsain and Kalhoro): "Krishna's worship had already developed in late-nineteenth-century Sindh among the Amils. Narsain states that, in the nineteenth century, the Vallabhachari sect was accepted by some members of the Amil community (Narsain 1932: 1939)" (p. 135, folio 122). "A number of Sufis from nineteenth-century Sindh, such as Sachal, Qutub Ali Shah and Bedil, themselves include Krishna in their Sufi poetry … despite the fact that Sindh was Muslim dominated, the Krishnaite Bhakti was influential among wujudi Sufis" (p. 198, folio 185). An endnote adds that wall paintings of Krishna in Hindu temples "could mean there was a kind of Krishnaite revival in the nineteenth century among the Sindhi Hindus (Kalhoro 2014: 238–44)" (Ch. 3 n. 19, p. 213, folio 200).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "a modern Sindhi province"; refers to: period not stated beyond the past tense of the argument about why Hindus took Muslim Sufi masters; author's argument): "Sindh was a largely Muslim-dominated country, making up about 75 per cent of the whole population, where the power had been in the hands of Muslims for centuries. Therefore, there was a kind of 'cultural islamization' working beyond the religious affiliation itself, to which the Hindus were automatically and unconsciously surrendering". Also: "the Muslim Sufi market was far more developed than the Hindu market". And: "to be a Sufi for a Hindu was to participate fully in the process of building a modern Sindhi province, so far as the Sufi poetry was itself very open, if not integrative" (p. 199, folio 186).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "the late nineteenth century", "late nineteenth-century Sindh", "before partition", "up through partition"; author's argument): on how the Sufi legacy "was constructed in Sindh", where Hindus could still play a ritual role at Sufi places: "In Sindh, it was possible thanks to an ideological framework provided by the wujudi ideology, reformulated in the Theosophical perspective through its congruence with advaita vedanta. Consequently, this construction was expressed in the late nineteenth century" (p. 204, folio 191). "As it was for the majority of the Sindhi literati, be they Hindu or Muslim, the ideology of the society" (the Theosophical Society, named in the preceding sentence) "provided the framework for a reinterpretation of the Sufi legacy of Sindh". This held "for most of the Sindhi intellectuals before partition" (p. 157, folio 144). The book also notes "the key role played by the Hindus of Sindh in the transmission of the Sufi legacy up through partition" (p. 170, folio 157), and that in publishing Sufi books the Hindu Sindhis "already played a leading role in this process in late nineteenth-century Sindh" (p. 173, folio 160).
- (book's word: "pre-partition Sindh"; refers to: before Partition and the present ("still"); "Since the eighteenth century onwards" in the endnote; author's argument): "The Lohanas, once the dominant Hindu community in pre-partition Sindh, are still mostly involved in trade. Many Lohanas, especially those belonging to the subcaste of the Amils, have played a leading role in integrating Sufism as a main component of Sindhi identity" (p. 193, folio 180). Endnote: "The Amils were a subcaste of the Lohanas. Since the eighteenth century onwards, they specialized in becoming the official scribes in the administration of the Kalhoras and the Talpurs" (Ch. 4 n. 10, p. 214, folio 201). This note does not itself name Sindh.
- (book's word: "Sindh", "Southern Sindh"; refers to: pre-Partition, "from 1947 onwards", and "April of 2016"; author's argument, endnotes, author's anecdote): "Jhulelal was already worshipped by some segments among the Hindus of Sindh, but as we saw before, they were in majority Nanakpanthis" (p. 158, folio 145). Two endnotes on the same page: "the pre-partition temples devoted to Jhulelal in Sindh were built with a dome, as were some Sikh samadhis" (Ch. 2 n. 5), and "The Thakurs were the priests of Jhulelal. In Sindh, they were settled in different cities but they migrated to India from 1947 onwards" (Ch. 2 n. 6) (p. 211, folio 198). In April 2016 the author and a team "toured in Southern Sindh to map out the different shrines devoted to Jhulelal in Southern Sindh" (p. 184, folio 171).
- (book's word: "pre-partition Sindh"; refers to: before Partition, contrasted with "independent India"; author's argument): "While in pre-partition Sindh, there are many stories where businessmen decided to give up all of their worldly goods and activities to become renunciants, and not only in relation with Sufism, I have never heard such a story in independent India" (p. 205, folio 192).
- (book's word: "Norther [text-layer?] [Northern] Sindh"; refers to: Sachal Sarmast's following before Partition, period not stated, up to the migration "in the wake of partition"; author's argument): Sachal "had a large number of followers among the Hindu trade castes of Norther [text-layer?] [Northern] Sindh, and some of them transferred their love for singing Sachal's poetry to India when they migrated in the wake of partition" (p. 150, folio 137).
- (book's word: "Northern Sindh"; refers to: present day, with "the Middle Ages" in the endnote; author's argument): Siraiki is "the Sindhi dialect mostly spoken in Northern Sindh, and especially among the Baluchi population", and "many Sindhi Sufi poets, especially from Northern Sindh, composed Sufi poetry in several languages" (p. 171, folio 158). The glossary gives Siraiki as "a language spoken in Northern Sindh and Southern Punjab" (p. 220, folio 207). An endnote says the Siraiki-speaking area of Southern Punjab "was a part of Sindh during the Middle Ages" (Ch. 6 n. 11, p. 216, folio 203). That is the book's own statement of a differently bounded historical Sindh. See Doubts.
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: present day, the author's 2016 visit; author's anecdote): Thano Bula Khan "is located in the Kohistan, the western part of Sindh that stands close to Baluchistan" (p. 184, folio 171).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: present day; ethnographic present, reporting the pir of Pithoro): among Pithoro Pir's disciples, "Among the Muslims of Sindh, one can take note of Sindhis like Sammas, Panhwars and Baluchs like the Maris and the Legharis". "The disciples of Pithoro Pir mix in Sindh, whereas in Kuch [text-layer?] [Kutch], the untouchable ones practise separate worship" (p. 191, folio 178). Ramdeo Pir is "still known in Sindh as Rama Pir … In Sindh, he is today integrated into the temples dedicated to the pan-Indian divinities" (p. 192, folio 179).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the province of Sindh", "Pakistani Hindus of Sindh"; refers to: "from 1947 onwards", "Since partition", and the present ("now"); author's argument): "Although a large majority of Hindus have migrated to India from 1947 onwards, Sindh is the province of Pakistan with the most significant Hindu population. The province of Sindh now belongs to Pakistan, a country that has been through different steps of islamization" (pp. 176–177, folios not stated and 164). "In Pakistan, despite the mass migration, the Sindhi Hindus number several millions, with a majority of them belonging to the so-called scheduled castes, mostly Menghwars, Bhils and Kohlis" (p. 175, folio 162). "Since partition, the majority of the Pakistani Hindus of Sindh have belonged to the low castes. They are Dalits, as they are named by some, and some even claim they are not Hindu", and "the Sufi legacy is still alive among the Dalits of Sindh" (p. 194, folio 181). "In Sindh now, there is a debate among the Hindus to determine if the term 'Dalit' is relevant" (p. 187, folio 174).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: present day, "Seventy years after partition", with Pakistan's islamization "since the late 1980s"; author's argument): "A possible explanation is that the Hindu population of Pakistan, including Sindh, is more and more under the threat of the radical Muslims of the country" (p. 127, folio 114). "Despite the islamization, though, and despite the spread of Islamist terrorism in Sindh as in other parts of Pakistan, I argue that the Sufi culture still pervades Sindhi society". He aims "to balance the representation suggesting that the integrative function performed by Sufism is currently weakened in Sindh". He also writes: "Sufism does not organically imply tolerance towards other religions. For example, the most active people working for the conversion of young Hindu girls to Islam are well-known Sufi pirs" (p. 177, folio 164). Elsewhere: "the Sufi dargahs are now targets of Islamist terrorism" (p. 186, folio 173).
- (book's word: "the Hindus of Sindh", "the Lohanas of Sindh"; refers to: present day, "for some years"; author's argument / ethnographic present): the formula Sab ka malik ek hai "has for some years been very popular among the Hindus of Sindh" and "signifies a new policy implemented by the Hindus of Sindh" (p. 186, folio 173). The Hindu dargah "shows that the Hindus of Sindh are strongly related to their Sufi legacy". The book places this "case that concerns the middle and high Hindu classes of Sindh" (p. 187, folio 174).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: after 1947, present day; author's argument about Sindh as seen from the darbars in India): Rochaldas's darbar is made "a territory that extends from an original sacred territory which is located in Sindh, the holy city of Sehwan Sharif". Invoking Jhok Sharif "contributes to the construction of a network of sacred territories which are all located in Sindh" (p. 141, folio 128). The mehndi of Lal Shahbaz "makes it possible to maintain a direct link through the ritual with the lost territory of Sindh" (p. 147, folio 134). Singing Sindhi Sufi poetry keeps "a link, through language and music, with Sindh as a fatherland" (p. 205, folio 192). The "'living' link" is "with the Master who stays in Sindh, nowadays in Pakistan" (p. 203, folio 190).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindh"; refers to: Ram Panjwani's view, "in 1945, thus before migrating to India" and in his 1987 book; quoted source Ram Panjwani, *Sindhi ain Sindhiyaat*, 1987, with the author's gloss): "Already, in Sindh, Panjwani was convinced that Sufism was the core of Sindhi culture, a culture shared by all creeds and social milieus" (p. 157, folio 144). His preface aims to acquaint Sindhis "with their past and the glorious cultural heritage of Sind which was inhabited by their forefathers" (p. 159, folio 146). "Panjwani's vision of Sindh is that of 'a land of the mystic poets, who are humanists as well'" (p. 160, folio 147). "When Panjwani referred to the religious culture of Sindh, he was referring to the spirit of Sufism" (p. 161, folio 148).

### province: balochistan

- (book's word: "Baluchistan"; refers to: period not stated (Parsram 1924; Rakhiyyal Shah, 1846–1940); author's prose): twice the book places a site in "the Sindhi-speaking area of Baluchistan": Hinglaj, "a pilgrimage centre located in the Sindhi-speaking area of Baluchistan" (p. 50, folio 37), and Fathepur, "a village in the Kachhi plain about 100 kilometres from Jacobabad, but located in the Sindhi-speaking part of Baluchistan" (p. 59, folio 46). Kept only for the general claim that Baluchistan has a Sindhi-speaking part. Each sentence is anchored to one site.
- (book's word: "Baluchistan"; refers to: present day, the author's 2016 visit; author's anecdote): the Kohistan is "the western part of Sindh that stands close to Baluchistan" (p. 184, folio 171).

### not attached

- "the territories of Rajasthan and Gujarat": part of the former "Sindhian culture" area, "at the time when it encompassed the southern present Punjab". These are outside Pakistan (p. 53, folio 40).
- "Punjab" at Partition: "Sindh did not suffer the violence experienced by Punjab" (p. 61, folio 48); "dramatic reports about Punjab were reaching the province" (p. 61, folio 48); "it never reached the level of violence in Punjab" (p. 62, folio 49); "mass massacres, as happened in Punjab"; "the Punjabis and Bengalis whose provinces were divided between India and Pakistan" (p. 64, folio 51). The book itself describes this 1947 Punjab as a province divided between the two countries, so these do not take the Pakistani `punjab` key (same handling as the template's "rivers of Punjab").
- "Amritsar in Punjab": Sami (1743–1850) "travelled a great deal between Shikarpur and Amritsar in Punjab" (p. 94, folio 81). The place is outside Pakistan, and this is a single-town statement.
- "Kutch" / "Kuch" [text-layer?] [Kutch]: Pithoro Pir's devotees there "are mainly Meghwars, Bhils, Kohlis, Sutars (carpenters) and Sodhas, whereas it does not seem that the Muslims venerate Pithoro Pir". There "the untouchable ones practise separate worship". "In Sindh and Kutch, Pithoro Pir is associated with two charismatic figures" (pp. 191–192, folios 178–179). Kutch is outside Pakistan.
- "Gujarat and Rajasthan": Ramdeo Pir is "the object of widespread worship in Gujarat and Rajasthan" and "venerated by the Nathpanthis of Gujarat" (p. 192, folio 179). Outside Pakistan.
- "Kashmir": Lal Das of Sehwan "was a Hindu from Kashmir" (p. 180, folio 167; p. 183, folio 170). The book does not say which Kashmir, and this is a person's origin, not a statement about the region.

*Sweep notes (first part, chunks 001–006):* Hit counts are matching lines in chunks 001–006, case-sensitive: `\bSindh?\b` 253 (001: 48, 002: 66, 003: 68, 004: 28, 005: 23, 006: 20), of which bare "Sind" is 3 (p. 48 book title *Sind and its Sufis*; p. 49 twice, in the Parsram quotation). `Sindhi(s)` 281 (001: 51, 002: 55, 003: 44, 004: 25, 005: 71, 006: 35); almost all are the language, the literature or the community in India, and none was used as a bullet on its own. `Punjab/Punjabi` 15 lines (p. 18 ×2 Farina Mir's "Punjabi literary formation", which concerns language; p. 43; p. 52 "Punjabi" as a language; p. 53 ×2; p. 56; pp. 61–64 ×5; p. 89; p. 94; p. 121). `Baluchistan/Balochistan` 3 (pp. 50, 59 ×2). "Baluchi(s)/Baluch/Balochi/Baloch" 6 (Baluchis as a group in Sindh, p. 38; "a Baluch", p. 39; the languages Balochi/Baluchi, pp. 56, 59; Nabi Bakhsh Baloch as a person, pp. 18, 59), none treated as a Balochistan statement. `Frontier` 1 (p. 28, Kothari's "Sindh as a frontier", a description of Sindh and not the Frontier Province). Kashmir, Pakhtunkhwa, NWFP, suba, Multan province: 0. No "Sind under Bombay" wording occurs in this range. The nearest is Karachi "then under the University of Bombay" (p. 115, folio 102), a statement about one university and town, so it is excluded. Toponym-only hits excluded (ruling a): "Utch near Bahawalpur in Punjab" (p. 56, folio 43); Hyderabad "the late capital of the independent Sindh before the British conquest", Sehwan Sharif "in central Sindh where Vicholi was spoken", Shikarpur "the northern city of Sindh" with its Utradi/Siraiki dialect (p. 30, folio 17); the Rohri-Sukkur-Shikarpur triangle and the "amirs of Khairpur" (p. 31, folio 18); Rochaldas born "in Rohri, in the north of Sindh" (p. 55, folio 42); the Halani darbar, where "the Talpurs had defeated the Kalhoras in 1783" (p. 68, folio 55); Sehwan, where "no communal violence had ever been registered" (p. 71, folio 58); Daraza / the Darazi silsila "close to Khairpur in Northern Sindh" (pp. 60, 93, folios 47, 80); Jhok "in Southern Sindh" (p. 60, folio 47); the Sukkur Manzilgah riots as an event in themselves, the 1 September 1947 train "near Nawab Shah", and the 6 January 1948 Karachi riot with its "two hundred Sikhs from the inner Sindh" (pp. 61–62, folios 48–49); "Halim Shah of Tehsil Mirpur, Balochistan" (p. 59, folio 46); Kandri "in Rohri taluqa, in the Khaipur [text-layer?] [Khairpur] district" (p. 107, folio 94); Thattah's "400 madrasats" at the end of the seventeenth century (p. 41, folio 28); Bedil's family "migrated from Multan" (p. 98, folio 85); Sultan Bahu "an important seventeenth-century Sufi from Punjab" (p. 121, folio 108), which describes a person and not the region; and Mulchand's kafi, where "In the Islamic Republic of Pakistan, no ritual is performed" (p. 113, folio 100). Also excluded as about Sindhis in India: the move into "Gujarat and Maharashtra" (p. 33, folio 20) and the attempt "to build such a space in Kutch" (p. 64, folio 51). Flags for a human: (1) p. 38: the 1872 census sentence is self-contradictory as printed ("not available in the first census of 1872", then figures from Hughes 1876), and gives "2,333,527 million". The four printed group figures sum to 2,203,477, not 2,333,527; all are kept as printed. (3) p. 52: "the episteme of Sindh before on the eve of British colonization" is printed so. (4) p. 91: Jotwani's book is cited as "Motwani 1986" right after "Motilal Jotwani"; kept as printed. (6) The `balochistan` bullet rests on two site-anchored sentences; drop it if the bar is "province in general" strictly. (7) Sachal Sarmast's dates differ inside this range (1739–1825 at p. 93; 1739–1829 at p. 101). They are not used in any bullet and are noted only for cross-checking.

## Cross-cutting material

### Shrine practice and pilgrimage

- **The two words for a sacred place, distinguished rather than equated.** "In South Asia, the Sufi
  sanctuary was centred at the tomb of the Sufi saint, and it was mostly known as the dargah from a
  Persian word, with the meaning of threshold. In Sindh, another Persian word was mostly used, that of
  darbar, or court. Of course, one can find many other words for a sacred place, but the term darbar was
  used by different religions, including the Sikhs.1 In South Asia, it referred first to the court of the
  king, and through a symbolic interpretation, it was the court of God. In Sindh, the darbar was the
  sacred territory in which the Sufi culture was at work. It was therefore a public culture, shared with
  the public, and an open space." (p. 37, folio 24)
- **Why a shrine is structurally necessary to this religiosity, in Boivin's argument.** "this religious
  culture based on devotion and emotion needed a place to be embodied, a territory where the Sufi songs
  could be sung, and the rituals performed. Thus, the shrine plays a leading role in their devotional
  agency, and this culture has been called a 'shrine culture' by some scholars, or more precisely, the
  'dargah culture', – this being a place where specific worship belonging to the saints' cults was
  performed." (p. 52, folio 39)
- **ziyarat defined by what both communities do at a tomb.** "certain Sufi cults are practised by
  Muslims and Hindus. Usually, they worship a Sufi saint who is buried in a dargah. Known as ziyarat,
  both Hindus and Muslims bring textiles to put on the tomb, or they only touch and kiss the tomb for
  obtaining blessings from the buried saint." (pp. 50–51, folios 37–38) The index gives `ziyarat 37, 191`
  — two folios only, and **it is the last entry in the index** (p. 252, folio 239).
- **Daily and annual Hindu attendance at a Muslim tomb, described as a circuit.** **bhit-bhit-shah**
  (the book's "Bhit Shah"): "The Hindus belonging to different paths and castes come every day and for
  the annual urs. They go to the tomb, circumambulating around it, touching it, and then attend the Shah
  jo rag, the singing of Shah's poetry by the local faqirs." (p. 178, folio 165)
- **A doctrinal statement written on a shrine gate.** **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast** (Daraza): "the
  inscription written on the main gate of Sachal's dargah is haqq mawjud" (p. 102, folio 89).
- **Pilgrimage, and Boivin's dissatisfaction with it as an explanation of the encounter.** "In the
  2000s, a number of studies underscored that the Sufi dargahs in South Asia were important places of
  'encountering' between Islam and Hinduism. But what does that mean, exactly? Most of the time, this
  assumption was related to the fact that Hindus used to go to a Sufi dargah as a pilgrimage." (p. 25,
  folio 12)
- **The darbar as a pilgrimage place with an accommodation obligation.** "The darbar also works as a
  place of pilgrimage where followers come to get rest and attain a peaceful feeling. It is thus
  necessary to plan accommodations where they may stay since some have engaged in long travel to visit."
  (p. 112, folio not stated) At Rochaldas's darbar "Other buildings accommodate pilgrims" (p. 80,
  folio 67); at Haridwar "there are thus only two small rooms, but the broad roof can nevertheless work
  as a sleeping place" (p. 134, folio 121). **No bed count, no charge, no visitor number: (not stated).**
- **A pilgrimage founded in India to replace one lost in Pakistan.** "After partition, Molchand was
  worried that it was difficult for Hindu Sindhi Sufis to pay a visit to the Sufi places in Sindh, now in
  Pakistan… In 1965, he was able to discover the dargah and he started to celebrate the melo every year
  from the 17th to the 19th of Moharram, as it is celebrated in Jhok Sharif" — **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed** is the model (p. 124, folio 111).
- **The visa as the terminal condition on Pakistani pilgrimage — the sharpest Partition fact in the
  practices material.** **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan
  Sharif"): "In the Islamic Republic of Pakistan, no ritual is performed there, although the Mulchand
  kafi is private property. Unfortunately, his followers can hardly perform the pilgrimage to the kafi
  because of the difficulties involved in them getting a Pakistani visa. Consequently, the main
  pilgrimage place remains the darbar located in Haridwar" (p. 113, folio 100); and "In the 1950s,
  Gehimal visited Sehwan twice during Mulchand's lifetime. Now, it is almost impossible for Mulchand's
  followers to obtain a Pakistani visa." (p. 128, folio 115) **This attachment is place-level, not
  site-specific.**
- **Haridwar's own pilgrimage standing, from the Notes.** "Haridwar (the Gate of God) is one of the most
  famous places of pilgrimage for the Hindus… It is one of the **four places where the kumbhamela**, a
  major religious gathering **when the sun stands in the Aquarius, is held every twelve years**."
  (p. 210, folio 197, Ch. 1 n. 19)
- **A shrine that functions one day a year.** Of Mulchand Kafi at Sehwan: "Only Ramchand's family used to
  come, but the kafi is closed most of the time. The only time in a year when it plays a social and
  religious role is the time of the mehndi procession, on the 19th Shaban." (p. 127, folio 114)
- **A restriction on Muslim visitors, reported without endorsement.** "The reason behind this is not
  clear. According to some Hindu informants, **the Muslims are forbidden to visit.**" (p. 216, folio 203,
  Ch. 7 n. 2) **Which site is meant is not restated in the note: (not stated).**

### Hindu–Sufi participation, the encounter that gives the book its title

- **The four-part typology of shared worship, the book's organising frame for the encounter**
  (pp. 50–51, folios 37–38). (1) Sufi cults practised by both communities at a dargah, as ziyarat.
  (2) One place of worship with two names — **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar**
  (the book's "Sehwan Sharif"). (3) One sanctuary with two names **and two chapels** —
  **shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan** (the book's "Udero Lal"). (4) "saints of Muslim origin
  who are massively venerated by the Hindus, and only marginally by the Muslims", the example being
  Pithoro Pir, whose "guardians… are Hindu Rajputs".
- **The same typology restated in Chapter 7 as three categories, each attached to a place.** "In Sindh,
  there are different ways through which the Hindus are related to Sufism, according to which three
  categories of encountering can be distinguished. Most of the time, the encountering process is attached
  to a place." (p. 177, folio 164) The three: the shared Sufi tomb (**bhit-bhit-shah**), the
  double-identity shrine (**shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan**) and the Hindu-managed Sufi
  darbar (Pithoro Pir) (pp. 178–179, folios 165–166). Boivin at once concedes the typology leaks: "the
  basic typology presented in the beginning of the chapter has excluded some elements that cannot fit
  into these categories; in other words, there are some exceptions." (p. 179, folio 166)
- **Initiation running in the unexpected direction.** Against Thapan's rule that "A Hindu who calls
  himself a Sufi was normally one who had taken initiation from a Sufi pir" (p. 27, folio 14), Boivin
  sets **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**: "a Hindu disciple of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar initiates Muslims to the path
  (tariqa) of Qalandariyya shahbaziyya" (p. 20, folio 7).
- **The "neither…nor" category, taken from colonial officers and found to match the Sufis' own credo.**
  "I will temporarily designate them as 'outcasts', although the expression formerly formulated by the
  British seems more pertinent, that of their being 'neither Muslim nor Hindu'" (p. 19, folio 6); "The
  most amazing point is that, coincidentally, the category of 'neither…nor' met the credo of the Sufis of
  Sindh, whose works known as kalams constituted the foundations of Sufi culture." (p. 20, folio 7) The
  Sindhi formula is glossed in the book itself: `hindu momin nahyan` = "'neither…nor' is the exact English
  translation" (p. 20, folio 7).
- **The doctrine that makes the encounter possible.** The poetry "contributes to the creation of an
  imaginal world of reference, as well as the ideology of the 'unity of being', wahdat-e wujud, the
  Persian version of the Arabic wahdat al-wujud, introduced as the encompassing paradigm which should be
  the basis of all human relations, and also the basis of the relationship between mankind and God"
  (p. 87, folio 74); it is "a principle without which the meeting of Sufism and Hinduism would not have
  been possible" (p. 87, folio 74). Boivin's mechanism is vernacularization in two layers, the second
  being "the merging of the haqq mawjud with the vedantic advaita principle, as nurtured by the
  neo-vedantism of Vivekananda. Thus, the Oneness of God did meet the non-duality of God, and once again,
  the best exponent of this working congruence was Nimano Faqir." (p. 109, folio 96)
- **The lexicographic evidence for that merging, dated.** `wahadata` in 1879 means "unity, oneness, but
  there is no word equivalent to existence or wujud built from the Arabic root, WJD" (p. 99, folio 86);
  Lalwani 1890 has `wahadaniyyat` and `wahdat`, both "unity", and no word for `wujud` (p. 100, folio 87);
  Beg 1913 renders `wahdat` as `hekro` plus "Khoda jo Nalo, 'the name of God'", and also as `hekra'i`
  (p. 100, folio 87). "Therefore, from the late nineteenth century onwards, the wahdat, as a central dogma
  of Islam, was already vernacularized by Hindu Sindhi literati in using the term hekro, one." (p. 100,
  folio 87)
- **A rival expression that pre-empted it, and Boivin's hedge.** "We have observed that, in pre-partition
  Sindhi authors, there are but few uses of words or expressions directly based on wahdat-e wujud. A
  leading reason is perhaps that another expression, haqq mawjud, was being used by the Sindhis and is
  very close to wahdat-e wujud." (p. 101, folio 88) Analysed: "'Truth' or 'haqq' is one of the ninety-nine
  names of God… Furthermore, it is an implicit allusion to the sentence ana al-haqq"; "The word mawjud
  reinforces the immanence of God since, in Arabic, mawjud means the very presence of God. The expression
  hu mawjud has a similar meaning: 'He is here'." (p. 102, folio 89) "Nonetheless, using a new expression
  means a new step has been taken for establishing an interfaith dialogue." (p. 101, folio 88)
- **The equivalence tables, printed twice with different values and not reconciled.** Boivin in his own
  voice: "The first part, sat, stresses the divine as the Truth, as it is in haqq mawjud. Thus, sat
  corresponds to haqq. The second part, chit, roughly means consciousness, while ananda means bliss and
  happiness. Akhanda means non-divisible – that is, unity, wahdat." (p. 103, folio 90) As Rochaldas set it
  out: "the three words making satchidananda mean existence (sat), knowledge (chit) and bliss (ananda).
  Furthermore, they are the exact equivalents of haqq (truth), ishq (love) and husn (beauty) (Hari 1995:
  195)." (p. 104, folio 91) **`sat` is glossed "the Truth" by Boivin and "existence" by Rochaldas; both
  are recorded, neither preferred.** Hari's own tables: "'The stage of ecstasy and trance is absorption in
  the "sirgun" form of Atma. There are three ways to attain this stage. (i) Abhyas-Sumran (Recitation of
  mantras-zikur), (ii) Vivek- (Deep thinking-Fikur-Ghour Khouz), (iii) Dhyan (Meditation-Tasawur)' (Hari
  1982: 182)" and "'Dharma=Religion=Sharyat', then 'Karma=Action=Tariqat', 'Bhakti=Devotion=Haqiqat', and
  'Gyan=Perfect Knowledge of Atma=Marfat' (Hari 1982: 17–18)." (p. 164, folio 151)
- **Which Hinduism the Muslim Sufi poets drew on: Vaishnava, and specifically Krishnaite.**
  **krishna-mandir-kabari-bazar**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road** (Krishna, principal figure of both rows;
  the attachment is person-level, not site-specific): "the Muslim Sufis of Sindh were mostly attracted by
  the Vishnuite school of Hinduism. The most quoted gods are Ram and Krishna, two avatars of Vishnu."
  (p. 107, folio 94) "This shows a deep influence of the Bhakti and, through it, more bridges being built
  between the wujudi Sufism and Hinduism." (p. 107, folio 94) Boivin adds a possible dated revival: "The
  many wall paintings of Krishna in the Hindu temples could mean there was a kind of **Krishnaite revival
  in the nineteenth century** among the Sindhi Hindus (Kalhoro 2014: 238–44)." (p. 213, folio 200,
  Ch. 3 n. 19)
- **The jogi as the Sufis' Hindu type.** **gorakhnath-goraknath-temple**, **tilla-jogian** (Gorakhnath,
  principal figure of both rows; person-level attachment): "in the Persian sources, the main Hindu figure
  is the jogi. The term 'jogi' is usually employed for the Nathpanthis, a Shivaite school of renunciants
  who were the followers of Gorakhnath." (p. 21, folio 8) And "the paradigm of Sufism is renunciation, and
  the jogi is the pattern of the renunciant." (p. 105, folio 92)
- **The profile of the Muslim Sufis the Hindus chose, with an explicit exclusion.** "First, they mostly
  belong to Upper Sindh, with Siraiki as a language used for Sufi poetry besides Sindhi. Second, they all
  belong to the wujudi school of the Sufism of Sindh, and thus the Naqshbandis are excluded. But even
  among the wujudi school of local Sufism, they form a group whose aim is to find out equivalencies
  between Islam, Hinduism and Sikhism." (p. 108, folio 95) The limit is stated rather than filled: "It is
  not known the extent to which these Muslim Sufis knew Hinduism and Sikhism." (p. 108, folio 95)
- **Why they did not follow the Naqshbandis, argued causally and then qualified.** "in Sindh, save the
  Naqshbandis, Sufism is dominated by poetry expressing the concept of wahdat-e wujud" (p. 162,
  folio 149); "the majority of the Naqshbandis in Sindh are strongly opposed to music and to the singing
  of devotional songs: these are strictly forbidden. This opposition can explain why the Hindu Sindhis did
  not look after spiritual masters belonging to the Naqshbandiyya. But, here again, there are exceptions."
  (p. 228, folio 215) The exception given is a book, not a person: "the official book devoted to the
  Naqshbandis of Lowari Sharif was written by a Hindu follower" (p. 228, folio 215) — the archive's
  **dargah-of-khwaja-muhammad-zaman-luari-sharif** is the Luari Sharif row, and **the book's spelling is
  "Lowari Sharif"; the book's title and the follower's name are (not stated).**
- **The Hindu participation at Sehwan, weighed as survival or exception and left open.**
  **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar**: "The first case is related to a probably old
  tradition, pre-partition situation, where the Hindus were associated with the performance of the main
  rituals… Due to the situation in Sehwan, a capital question arises: is Sehwan an exception? Does the
  system with the Hindus reflect an ancient order in the city, where the dominant elites shared the
  symbolic power? All in all, it is relevant to think the involvement of the Hindus is a remnant of an old
  past, but whatever the explanations can be, it is in any case remarkable that it has survived in a
  post-partition context." (p. 193, folio 180) And again: "One can claim that the Sehwan system is an
  exception in Pakistan. It may be that it is, indeed, but this does not prevent us from stating that it
  is still at work." (p. 183, folio 170)
- **The explanation Boivin gives for that survival, and the category it turns on.** "This resilience can
  be explained primarily by the desire to preserve a historic heritage. But beyond this integrative
  dynamic of Sufism, it must be understood that the Hindus do not exercise this function as Hindus, but as
  Hindu Sufis." (p. 183, folio 170)
- **The "Sindhi touch", defined by ritual office rather than by attendance.** "the Sufi culture is a part
  of the darbar culture, but with a main distinction which can perhaps be called the **Sindhi touch**:
  sometimes, it can be a place where not only the Hindus, or non-Muslims, come for the pilgrimage
  (**ziyarat**) but also a place where **the Hindus still play a ritual role**." (p. 204, folio 191)
- **What Hindu visiting does and does not prove — Boivin answering his own question.** "the large
  majority of the Hindus used to pay a visit to the Sufi shrines, but does it mean they are Sufi? Of
  course not. It does mean that, for them, the Sufis are a part of their religious legacy." (pp. 192–193,
  folios 179–180) "Mostly, the reverence they have for the Sufis belongs to the charismatic realm, in
  which people ask a saintly figure to help in curing a disease, providing a job, the giving of a son, and
  so on." (p. 193, folio 180)
- **A Hindu-controlled dargah with its founder's Shia affiliation suppressed.** "In Pirana, a place
  located close to Ahmedabad, the dargah of Imam Shah is now under Hindu control, and the official
  narrative does not mention that he was an Ismaili Shia preacher. The shared devotional literature is
  also 'hinduized' through the substitution of Arabic words for Sanskrit ones." (p. 25, folio 12)
- **The mirror purge, on the Muslim side, itemised — both directions recorded side by side.** Ismaili
  literature "went through a process of islamization which led to the withdrawal of Hindu references from
  the corpus, be they names of gods, elements of the technical lexicon such as avatar, references to Hindu
  fairs, and so on. Interestingly, in some parts of India, especially Gujarat, some segments of the
  community were submitted to a hinduization process through which all of the so-called Islamic elements
  were suppressed." (p. 24, folio 11)
- **A sanctuary whose users and dedications do not match.** Hussein Tikri: "while the place is not used as
  a Shia place by the majority of the users, all the dargahs are devoted to Shia figures (Bellamy 2011)"
  (p. 25, folio 12) — note the plural, one sanctuary containing several dargahs.
- **Moharram as a cross-communal observance.** "During the first ten days of the Muslim month of Moharram,
  the Shia Muslims commemorate the martyr [text-layer? martyrdom] of their third imam, Husayn… Different
  events are organized in commemoration, especially including processions of **réplicas**
  [text-layer? replicas] of his tomb, known as **tazia**. **Many non-Shias participate, such as Sunni
  Muslims and Hindus.**" (pp. 209–210, folios 196–197, Ch. 1 n. 14) Shiite devotional poetry likewise: "in
  Sindh, this devotional poetry is cherished by both Muslims and Hindus, the latter being performers as
  well as authors." (p. 42, folio 29)
- **Intercession named as the shared idiom, on a nineteenth-century authority.** "As Richard Burton noted
  as early as 1851, it is indeed the worship of the intercessors that constitutes the common religious
  idiom of Hindus and Muslims (Burton 1851: 325). More than a century later, Motilal Jotwani would call
  the Sindhi Sufis 'the Great Integrators' (Jotwani 1996: 150–8)." (pp. 51–52, folios 38–39)
- **A refusal to identify two figures, on evidentiary grounds.** **lal-shahbaz-qalandar** and the three
  rows whose principal figure is Jhulelal — **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan** (person-level attachment for Jhulelal): "It is tempting to think of a connection, or even of a fusion, between Lal Shahbaz Qalandar and Jhulelal, **but none of the sources allow for us to go that far**. That said, both are today called Jhulelal." (p. 216, folio 203, Ch. 7 n. 5)

### Partition, migration and the reconstitution of shrines in India

- **Partition as the book's "condition of possibility".** "Throughout this study, great attention is
  given to what Michel Foucault calls the conditions of possibility, which can explain why and how a
  tradition has developed in a given way and not in others. In this study, the Hindu Sufi traditions are
  understood to have been greatly affected by a main historical event: partition." (p. 32, folio 19)
- **Partition demography, with three totals the book prints and does not choose between.** "In 1941, the
  Hindus made up about 21 per cent of the total population of Sindh, including 6 per cent of the scheduled
  castes; therefore, they would have numbered at less than 1 million – exactly 0.9 million. And since the
  scheduled castes mostly stayed in Pakistan, and a number of other Hindus did not migrate, the migrant
  Hindus would have been closer to 0.8 million, if not less. Nonetheless, there is a kind of consensus
  that they numbered around 1 million." (p. 61, folio 48) **All three are recorded; the book reconciles
  none of them.**
- **Rates and means of departure.** "By early November of 1947, 246,000 Hindus had already left Sindh by
  sea, air or rail. As of 1 January 1948, it was estimated that nearly 500,000 people had sought refuge in
  India." (p. 62, folio 49) **bhagnari-mandir**, **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge**, **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi**, **shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi**, **shri-varun-dev-mandir**, **shrine-of-abdullah-shah-ghazi**, **shrine-of-pir-mangho**, **shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya** (the book's "Karachi"): "in a week's time, 11,000 Hindus left Karachi (Dawn, 13 January 1948)" (p. 62, folio 49). "Hindus from interior Sindh enjoyed special trains chartered by the Sindh government" (p. 62, folio 49); "The Indian government had chartered ships from Bombay and, in December, with the addition of a train, 140,000 Hindus crossed the border" (p. 63, folio 50); "on 14 January, the government of India appointed a director general of evacuation to formally assist non-Muslims who wanted to migrate from Sindh to India" (p. 63, folio 50). **These are place-level for Karachi, not site-specific.**
- **A temple burned in Karachi, with two death tolls that do not agree.** "Two hundred Sikhs from the
  inner Sindh, who were to have been evacuated by boat, were installed in a temple in the city centre.
  During the night, a crowd of 8,000 Mohajir surrounded the temple compound and set fire to it, forcing
  the Sikhs out. The Mohajir threw themselves on the Sikhs and began to stab them." (p. 62, folio 49) "The
  death toll ended up being between what was reported by the daily, Dawn, at forty-two Sikhs killed and
  eighty-two wounded (8 January 1948), and the figures reported elsewhere as numbering fifteen killed and
  many looted, according to Sindh chief minister Muhammad Ayyub Khuhro (Khuhro 1998: 327)." (p. 62,
  folio 49) **The temple is not named and is not located beyond "the city centre": (not stated).** Also
  "On 1 September 1947, sabotage derailed a train near Nawab Shah, and an armed crowd threw themselves on
  Sikhs, killing fifteen and wounding seventeen (Khuhro, 1998: 320)." (p. 62, folio 49)
- **Property, panic and a government directive.** "He had to enact a government directive in order to
  prohibit owners from selling their property. He hoped to stop the panic that had taken hold of the
  Hindus, who were afraid of being dispossessed, and to deter the Mohajirs from threatening the Hindus in
  hopes that they would sell their goods at low prices." (p. 62, folio 49) "600 Hindu families demanded
  evacuation within two days"; "The chief minister of Sindh, Muhammad Ayyub Khuhro, arrested 500 Mohajirs"
  (p. 62, folio 49). And the seizure mechanism: "the spread of violence throughout Sindh was not
  perpetrated by the Muslim Sindhis, but by the Mohajirs who clearly wanted to take over the houses of the
  Hindus, at least in Karachi. Even before the riots, a number of cases are known where Mohajirs took a
  house by force while its owners had left for work (Zamindar 2007: 67)." (pp. 70–71, folios 57–58) The
  Mohajirs' own claim is recorded from the Notes: "**They had left all of their belongings in India, and
  many thought they were legitimate to take over the goods left by the Sindhi Hindus, or even they had the
  right to seize their properties.**" (p. 210, folio 197, Ch. 1 n. 21)
- **Boivin's refusal of the "Sindh was spared" trope, and his qualification of it.** "it is a common trope
  to claim that Sindh did not suffer the violence experienced by Punjab" (p. 61, folio 48); "even if it
  never reached the level of violence in Punjab, it is irrelevant to claim there was no communal violence
  in Sindh" (p. 62, folio 49); rumour as a cause, "dramatic reports about Punjab were reaching the
  province, leading many people to feel 'uncertain of the future' (Zamindar 2007: 66)" (p. 61, folio 48);
  and the evidentiary difficulty, "The role played by politicians… is not always easy to disentangle from
  behind the official discourse which was a genuine attempt at trying to convince the Hindus to stay."
  (p. 62, folio 49)
- **The Sindhi displacement as structurally different from the Punjabi and Bengali ones.** "the Sindhis
  had no new territory where they could settle, despite their attempts to build such a space in Kutch, as
  was the case to the Punjabis and Bengalis whose provinces were divided between India and Pakistan.
  Therefore, the Sindhis had to settle in a new environment where, on many occasions, they were not
  welcomed." (p. 64, folio 51) "Even if the Hindu Sindhis were not targeted by mass massacres, as happened
  in Punjab, they had to face the trauma of leaving all of their belongings and their ancestral home in a
  very short time" (p. 64, folio 51). Kumar and Kothari's displacement of the blame is reported: "the main
  trauma for the Sindhis did not come from the Muslims in Sindh, but from the behaviour of the Indian
  administration and local Hindus who did not welcome them, and who often openly discriminated against the
  Hindu Sindhis who were accused of not being 'true' Hindus (Kumar and Kothari 2016: 776)." (p. 61,
  folio 48)
- **Partition as a category crisis, not only a migration — Boivin's argument.** "While through the late
  census in Sindh, they identified more and more as Hindu, they quickly realized that what they call
  Hinduism was not considered as such by some peoples in the new regions they were to settle, mostly
  Gujarat and Maharashtra. Obviously, this new situation forced them to renegotiate their relation to
  Sufism, and to construct a new bridge between their Sufi legacy and the religion that was now a kind of
  normative Hinduism." (p. 33, folio 20) "When the Sindhis migrated to India in 1947, they were often
  tagged as Muslims, or at least not authentic Hindus, because of the Sufi cults (Kothari 2007)."
  (p. 143, folio 130)
- **Renegotiation conducted through space.** "often, the tool of renegotiation was transacted throughout
  space and territory. This means that not only settlement could be renegotiated but also that space
  itself was devoted to the sanctuaries." (p. 33, folio 20) "for the Sindhis who had decided to leave
  Sindh and to migrate to India, it was impossible not to migrate with their religious legacy" (p. 67,
  folio 54); "to build a physical structure was a necessary process for not only enacting the Sufi cults
  but also appropriating the new soil" (p. 31, folio 18).
- **The transfer of darbars as an institution in its own right, and its finality.** "It was not possible
  to shift the physical structure of the darbar, however. In many cases, the original darbar was left in
  Sindh and a new one was built in India. Many darbars were transferred from Sindh to India from 1947
  onwards. Of course, this means that the followers never thought they would come back to the land of
  their ancestors. More research needs to be undertaken on this neglected issue of transference" (p. 67,
  folio 54). The rule: "In the context of migration of Sindhi religious figures to India, the transference
  of a Sindhi darbar usually follows the migration of its gaddi nashin. Hence, a number of important
  darbars of Sindh were recreated in India by the gaddi nashins who had migrated." (p. 68, folio 55)
- **The three principal cases were not transfers, and Boivin says so twice.** "his darbar was to be a
  creation – Rai Rochaldas had not had any darbar in Sindh – and as for the other, Nimano Faqir, she was
  asked by her Sufi Master in Sindh to go to India for the purpose of creating a 'branch' of the dargah of
  Daraza" (p. 67, folio 54), i.e. of **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast**; and "Before partition, none of the
  three figures had created a distinct path, let alone a darbar, in Sindh." (p. 55, folio 42) "none of the
  three Sufi figures focused upon here did run a darbar, or even an astano, in Sindh. They were themselves
  the followers of a Sufi Master, who was always Muslim." (p. 70, folio 57)
- **The chapter's central thesis: the Hindu Sufi darbar is an artefact of Partition, not a transplant.**
  "it is obviously the migration to India that played a role in the foundation of a darbar. Thus, we can
  reasonably claim that the creation of a Sufi darbar was an answer given in the context of a traumatic
  event, that of partition followed by migration." (p. 70, folio 57) Boivin at once widens it:
  "Nevertheless, one can claim this scenario was restricted to the Hindu Sufis: the transference of the
  above-mentioned darbars answered the same issue." (p. 70, folio 57) And: "partition created a new and
  unexpected situation that would compel the Hindu Sufis to create new darbars in order for their Sufi
  persuasion to be maintained." (p. 55, folio 42)
- **Three cases, three different causes, against a single migration narrative.** "Rochaldas migrated due
  to the violence of 6 January 1948; Nimano Faqir apparently migrated due to her Sufi Master's request;
  and Mulchand Faqir did not migrate, while his main disciple Gehimal Motwani did. While they belonged to
  well-to-do Hindu families of Sindh as part of the emerging middle classes, migration does not seem to
  have been a crucial issue." (p. 70, folio 57) Why Mulchand stayed: "The most convincing explanation is
  the local milieu. As we saw before, through his family, Mulchand was extremely well integrated into the
  mystical network of Sehwan Sharif." (p. 125, folio 112) — **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar**. "This situation reflects a unique feature resulting from partition: the Master disagreed with his main disciple and future successor, Gehimal Motwani." (p. 125, folio 112)
- **A sanctuary sited on the Ganges because the Indus was lost.** "The location of the new darbar is
  explained by Basant: 'We don't have the Sindhu anymore, so we are now going to the Ganga.' The new
  darbar was inaugurated on 5 April for Mulchand's birth anniversary." (p. 77, folio 64) **No year for
  the inauguration: (not stated).**
- **Naming as the first act of sacralization, and a fifth dham invented for a lost town.** "we can state
  that naming a place is the first step in its sacralization" (p. 78, folio 65); **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple** (the book's "Rohri"): "The text of Rochaldas's darbar states 'Rohri dham' – the abode of Rohri, or seat of Rohri… there is a reference to the Hindu ideology of the char dham, the four sacred places a Hindu is supposed to visit… as if Rochaldas had wished to locate his darbar in the sacred geography of India, in adding a fifth dham, the 'Rohri dham', as well as an extension of his lost native country." (p. 78, folio 65) **The attachment is place-level for Rohri.** "While the word darbar was currently used in conversations, Rai Rochaldas obviously discarded its use in favour of the official inscription" (p. 78, folio 65).
- **Territory manufactured by ritual, and a network of lost places invoked.** "The purpose of the ritual
  is clearly to make the darbar of Rochaldas a territory that extends from an original sacred territory
  which is located in Sindh, the holy city of Sehwan Sharif." (p. 141, folio 128) "Again, the invocation
  of Jhok Sharif contributes to the construction of a network of sacred territories which are all located
  in Sindh." (p. 141, folio 128) — **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** for Sehwan
  Sharif, **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed** for Jhok Sharif. And "The mehndi of Lal Shahbaz makes
  it possible to maintain a direct link through the ritual with the lost territory of Sindh." (p. 147,
  folio 134)
- **No tomb in India — the structural fact the whole iconography chapter turns on.** "The decisive role of
  iconography is due to the fact that the Sindhi darbars of India are not centred on tombs, unlike those
  of Sindh: none of them contain any tomb or cenotaph. This is the point of major divergence from the
  paradigm of the dargah. In India, it is actually the samadhi that replaces the tomb." (p. 131,
  folio 118) "No tomb is therefore located in India, which explains why the photographic portrait
  polarizes the sacredness of the space in place of the tomb, as is the case in the dargahs of Sindh. It
  materializes a presence, and almost all of the rituals are realized in front of it." (pp. 131–132,
  folios 118–119)
- **Precariousness in the refugee years, at its sharpest.** "Rochaldas had a single room in Ulhasnagar,
  its function changing according to the different parts of the day and the night. At some times, it was a
  free dispensary, and then it would be turned into a spiritual place where satsangs were organized, and
  later on it would become a dormitory." (p. 165, folio 152)
- **Where the shrines concentrated, with the only quantity of its kind.** "Ulhasnagar was mostly populated
  by Sindhi refugees but throughout the years, other populations from India and beyond migrated to the
  city and today, **the Sindhis would be about 35–40 per cent of the total population**. Nonetheless,
  **Ulhasnagar probably hosts the most numbers of Hindu Sindhi shrines compared to rest of India.**"
  (p. 210, folio 197, Ch. 1 n. 17) **No count of shrines is given: (not stated).**
- **Siting by the catchment of followers.** "The Mul Kutiyya was located in Ulhasnagar because most of the
  followers were there, in an area known as 'Sehwani Paro', the 'Sehwani area' where a number of Sehwani
  Thakurs were settled." (p. 83, folio 70)
- **The seat of one path shifted four times, in a sentence that contradicts itself.** "the seat of the
  path has been shifted quite often. Before partition, it was settled in Sehwan Sharif in Pakistan after
  1947. In a second period, it was shifted to Ulhasnagar after the followers had migrated to India. A
  transitional step can be seen as occurring in Delhi then, and finally a new shrine was built at
  Haridwar." (pp. 113–114, folios 100–101) **"Before partition… after 1947" is printed as such and is not
  emended here. No building date for the Haridwar shrine: (not stated).**
- **Demography at Sehwan before and after, in the book's own approximations.**
  **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar**: "Before partition, the Hindus made up about
  half of the total population… From 1947 onwards, almost all of the Hindu population migrated to India."
  (pp. 179–180, folios 166–167) And for Pakistan as a whole: "despite the mass migration, the Sindhi
  Hindus number several millions, with a majority of them belonging to the so-called scheduled castes,
  mostly Menghwars, Bhils and Kohlis." (p. 175, folio 162) **"about half" and "several millions" are the
  book's own; no census is cited: (not stated).**
- **A diaspora institution that kept counting the dispersed.** "in Bombay, there is still a Sehwani
  panchayat which has published several directories of the Sehwanis in India and overseas since partition.
  For some time now, the Sehwani panchayat has had a Facebook page." (p. 180, folio 167)

### Custodianship, lineage and institutional form

- **The two custodial titles distinguished by community and by furniture — the definition is in the
  Notes, not the Glossary.** "The gaddi nashin is the one who sits on the throne, the gaddi. The gaddi
  symbolizes the darbar, and the gaddi nashin is thus the one who rules on it. **The expression is used
  by both Muslims and Hindus.** Nonetheless, in the Sufi context, the Muslims also use the word
  **sajjada nashin**, this meaning the one who sits on the carpet." (p. 211, folio 198, Ch. 2 n. 3)
  Earlier in the book the terms are used without being distinguished — "the spiritual masters (sajjada
  nashin or pir) are still in Sindh, Pakistan" (p. 27, folio 14) — and a third spelling appears in
  Parsram's phrase "holders of the Gadi", with the book's own square brackets and capital `G`, nine pages
  before `gaddi` (p. 49, folio 36; p. 58, folio 45). A fourth form is flagged: "the Hindu belonging of the
  `gadi nashin` [text-layer? gaddi nashin]" (p. 74, folio 61).
- **`silsila` defined and equated with `tariqa`.** "The word silsila is an Arabic word very often used in
  Sufism. While it can be employed as an equivalent of **tariqa, or path, and brotherhood**, the first
  meaning is **chain**. Thus, it refers to the idea of transmission and legacy." (p. 210, folio 197,
  Ch. 2 n. 2) Against which the Hindu Sufis' own usage: "It is hardly used by the Hindu Sufis, and absent
  from Gajwani's glossary. Several Hindu Sufis, such as Nimano Faqir, Molchand Kripalani and Jogi Sain,
  refer to the group made by their followers as the sangat." (p. 202, folio 189) Gajwani translates
  `tariqa` as "'discipline, methodology, system' (Gajwani 2000: 307)" (p. 202, folio 189).
- **piri-muridi as the institution that survived, against the tariqa.** "The close relationship known in
  the Sufi context as piri-muridi, or the relation between Master and disciple, was never broken.
  Obviously, the legitimizing link was formed more through an individual, a Master, rather than through an
  institution, such as a tariqa, the name given to an institutionalized Sufi order." (p. 73, folio 60)
  Restated as a warning that bears on any silsila field: "although some of these Sufi masters have claimed
  a relation with the Qadiriyya, the Qalandariyya or the Sohrawardiyya, the ties with the order were
  flexible, at least: in Sindh, Sufi practice was more based on a personal allegiance with an individual
  than any affiliation with a Sufi tariqa. Furthermore, Sufis could be affiliated with several different
  tariqas.1 This topic is one of the numerous ones that deserves to be studied further in Sindh." (p. 200,
  folio 187)
- **The transnational custodial dependency, stated flatly.** Three Indian Hindu Sufi lineages whose
  masters "are still in Sindh, Pakistan", at Daraza, Tando Jahaniya and Jhok Sharif (p. 27, folio 14) —
  **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast** (Daraza) and **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed** (Jhok Sharif).
  "the seats are in one state and the followers in another." And later: "First, there is the 'living' link
  with the Master who stays in Sindh, nowadays in Pakistan. Still, the Pakistani sajjada nashin travels to
  India to meet his followers." (p. 203, folio 190) **He is not named there: (not stated).**
- **The cross-border visit as the shrine's charter event, and baraka as a ranked quantity.** "the most
  spectacular being the visit of the Pakistani murshid to his now Indian Hindu followers. The climax of
  the relation could be for the very foundation of the Indian darbar, or for the dastarbandi, or
  coronation, of a successor after a deceased Master in India." Otherwise the murshid came "only for him
  to offer a blessing, or if a neologism is relevant here, to 'baraka-ize' the new shrine. In the
  spiritual hierarchy, the Master of the original shrine located in Sindh would always be considered as
  owning more baraka than the head of the Indian shrine." (p. 73, folio 60)
- **Legitimation described as a procedure, and Boivin's conclusion from it.** "The process of legitimizing
  authority was quite similar in the three darbars noted here. It was established as a progression of what
  had been previously accepted: the founder of the darbar in India was already the follower of a murshid,
  now based in the country of Pakistan. It was made clear that, in different circumstances as related to
  Rochaldas and Nimano, the Pakistani murshid did visit the Indian darbars as a final way of legitimizing
  them." (p. 65, folio 52) "In this respect, partition did not alter the dynamics of authority, without
  which no Sufi path can develop." (p. 65, folio 52) And: "the authority exercised by a migrant Sufi was
  always legitimized by the Master of the founder of the Indian shrine, and in all of the three cases
  examined here, the Master had stayed in Pakistan. For the three Hindu Sufis, the Master was a Muslim,
  and thus he had no reason to migrate to India." (p. 77, folio 64)
- **Succession by turban, with two dated performances and a Hindu overlay.** "the coronation ceremony is
  performed when a new gaddi nashin is enthroned: it is named with the Sufi lexicon of dastarbandi, the
  coronation with the turban, the dastar, or sometimes pag, which is a symbol of the spiritual power."
  (p. 73, folio 60) "the sajjada nashin of Jahaniyya, Nur Husayn Shah, who is still the Master of the
  silsilo, came to Ulhasnagar in 1957 to tie a turban of ochre cloth around Hari's head (Gajwani 2000:
  199)." (p. 73, folio 60) For 1980: "'He (Nur Husayn Shah) first applied the tilak on the forehead of Sri
  Damodar, tied dastar (folded sheet of ochre cloth) around his head and then blessed him. … Thus Sri
  H. M. Damodar was formally consecrated to the spiritual seat after Dada Sai (Dr) R. M. Hari in the
  lineage of Sai Rochaldas Sahib' (Gajwani 2000: 294–5)." (p. 73, folio 60) **The ellipsis is the book's
  own; Gajwani "does not refer to a dastarbandi of Rochaldas" himself** (p. 73, folio 60). A second,
  differently attributed account of the same visits appears at p. 167, folio 154: "both had the
  dastarbandi ceremony performed by the sajjada nashin of Qutub Ali Shah's dargah from Hyderabad. The Sufi
  pir came for it from Pakistan twice, in 1957 and in 1980." **There the officiant is the sajjada nashin
  of Qutub Ali Shah's dargah at Hyderabad, and at p. 73 he is Nur Husayn Shah, sajjada nashin of
  Jahaniyya; both are recorded, neither reconciled.** The Hyderabad location is place-level for
  **guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat**.
- **A Muslim custodian performing a Hindu act, and a Hindu funeral on the twelfth day.** "while the
  ceremony is Muslim Sufi, so to say, the Hindu belonging of the `gadi nashin` [text-layer? gaddi nashin]
  is exhibited through the application of the tilak, the red dot on the forehead. And it was not a Hindu
  priest who put it on the front, but the Muslim sajjada nashin, Nur Husayn Shah. Furthermore, the funeral
  rituals performed before the `dastarband` [text-layer? dastarbandi] were all Hindu, and specifically
  Sindhi Hindu, done on the twelfth day after the death instead of on the thirteenth day as they are
  performed in most Hindu communities." (p. 74, folio 61) "Nonetheless, this very Sufi ceremony is not
  regularly performed in the two other Hindu Sufi paths." (p. 74, folio 61) And the annual repetition:
  "the dastarbandi is performed every year when R. M. Hari's versi is celebrated, but not by Nur Husayn
  Shah." (p. 73, folio 60) The re-enactment's meaning: "It reproduces the enthronement of the gaddi nashin,
  when the ritual was performed by Nur al-Din, the sajjada nashin of Tando Jahaniyyan in Pakistan. The
  re-enactment of his authority opens a kind of new spiritual year. For the occasion, the disciple
  reaffirmed his loyalty and surrendered towards the Master, as symbolized by the kissing of the dastar."
  (p. 145, folio 132) **The officiant is "Nur Husayn Shah" at pp. 73–74 and "Nur al-Din" at p. 145; both
  as printed.**
- **A Pakistani sajjada nashin performing a Hindu coronation, undated.** "Amazingly, the Pakistani sajjada
  nashin of Tando Jahaniyyan performed a Hindu ritual for the coronation of the new gaddi nashin of
  Rochaldas's darbar." (p. 112, folio not stated) **Undated: (not stated).**
- **Hereditary office as the exception, not the rule.** "His darbar, however, is the only one where the
  charge of gaddi nashin is hereditary." (p. 133, folio 120) And a custodian who refuses the office
  altogether: Dhuru "claims that, as it was already the case with his father named Lachmandas, he is not
  gaddi nashin: he only takes care of the samadhi in order to give access to it to Dalpat's followers –
  but he does not play any spiritual function." (p. 119, folio 106)
- **A succession split into two branches, in Weber's terms.** "After Gehimal's demise, it seems that two
  branches tried to manage Mulchand's legacy. The first one is related to what Max Weber coined
  'hereditary charisma': Gehimal's family." (p. 75, folio 62) "The second branch is that of Basant
  Jethwani, the head of the followers who are not Gehimal's family members." (p. 76, folio 63)
- **Institutional failure, domestication and sale.** "After Gehimal's death, his brother Tirthdas Motwani
  took care of the Mul Kutiyya, and after his death, it quickly appeared that nobody among the followers
  was able to manage the darbar… the three main followers – Sonu, Basant and Devi (Gopi had passed away in
  the meanwhile) – decided to sell the darbar." (pp. 83–84, folios 70–71) Elsewhere the same event is
  "the closure of Mul Kutiyya in Ulhasnagar" (p. 76, folio 63), and later "After the closure of the Mul
  Kutiyya, and finally its destruction, Mulchand's cult was celebrated in private spaces" (p. 143,
  folio 130). **No date and no agent for the destruction: (not stated).** A parallel loss: "In the case of
  Isar Lal (1835–95), there was one, but after the departure of the main followers, nobody was able to
  take care of it. Consequently, we could speak of individual chains of the transmission of Sufism."
  (p. 169, folio 156)
- **Custodianship at Daraza — three sajjada nashins named across the book.**
  **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast**: Sachal's father "the sajjada nashin of Daraza, Khwaja Muḥammad Ḥafiẓ, also
  known as Sahiḅ Dino I"; his brother "the sajjada nashin Khwaja Abd al-Ḥaqq" (p. 93, folio 80); and in
  the present "Sakhi Qabul Muhammad Faruqi, the sajjada nashin of Daraza" (pp. 96–97, folios 83–84), who
  "quotes, word for word, Hari's translation, after giving the Sindhi text (Faruqi 2002: 35–6)" (p. 97,
  folio 84).
- **A Pakistani custodian who sets the festival date for two Indian darbars that will not meet each
  other.** **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast**: "The two in charge are said to have been in touch but since
  twenty years, they have stopped meeting for an unknown reason. Notwithstanding, still they are in touch
  with the sajjada nashin of Daraza since, in both cases, he calls on them to give the date of Sachal's
  versi." (p. 151, folio 138) At one of them, "The sajjada nashin of Daraza has paid three visits to the
  darbar." (pp. 150–151, folios 137–138)
- **Custodianship at Sehwan, in four named offices.** **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar**: the head of the Lakkiyyaris, Murad Shah, eldest son of Gul Muhammad Lakkiyyari, leading the first mehndi (pp. 181, 183, folios 168, 170); Ramchand, son of Mohan Lal, leading the second since 1981 (p. 183, folio 170); Lal Gul, "alleged son" of Lal Das, leading the third since the Kanungos' migration in 1981 (pp. 181, 183, folios 168, 170); and, in a figure caption alone, "Asan Gul, a Hindu sajjada nashin in Sehwan Sharif" (p. 182, folio 169; the same caption at p. 10, folio not stated).
- **A hereditary ritual duty transferred twice within twenty years, both transfers dated.** "After the
  death of Mulchand Faqir in 1962, the duty of the mehndi was transmitted to his cousin, Mohan Lal, who
  then passed it on to his son, Ramchand, in 1981." (p. 183, folio 170) And the office refilled from
  outside Sindh: "The Kanungos migrated to India in the same year and the burden fell to Lal Das, a Hindu
  from Kashmir." (p. 183, folio 170)
- **A hereditary ritual office split in two by the border.** The Kanungos' third-day mehndi duty at Sehwan
  followed them to Ulhasnagar in 1962 at Hari's invitation, while "In Sehwan Sharif, another Hindu named
  Lal Das then became mehndibardar" (p. 147, folio 134) — **one office becomes two, on two sides of the
  border.** The Indian line then leaves the family: Murlidhar was in Baroda, Lachhman "was not allowed",
  and "it was the son of Hari, Damodar, who led the mehndi from that point on" (p. 147, folio 134).
  **The 1962 date at p. 147 and the 1981 date at p. 183 are both printed of the Kanungos' migration; both
  are recorded and neither is emended here.**
- **Sehwan's lodges counted, with two of them Hindu.** "Sehwan Sharif hosts about twenty kafis, or Sufi
  lodges, of which two are Hindu. Already, we know about the Mulchand Kafi, but it does not play a ritual
  role for the Hindu community, although it is used for cremation. Nonetheless, the sajjada nashin is
  Ramchand, Mulchand's nephew, and he performs the mehndi ritual every year, leading a procession from
  Mulchand Kafi up to Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's darbar. There is another 'Hindu kafi' whose sajjada nashin
  also leads a mehndi procession." (p. 193, folio 180) **"about twenty" is the only count of shrine
  institutions in a Pakistani town anywhere in these extracts.**
- **A kafi doing three jobs at once — tomb, community worship and cremation ground.** "Although Hindus are
  very few in number in Sehwan today, it should be noted that Mulchand Kafi works to a certain extent as a
  place of community worship… certain ceremonies of the cycle of life are always realized there…
  Additionally, the kafi shelters the cremation ground of the Hindus of Sehwan." (p. 180, folio 167)
- **The death anniversary named as the instrument of community, as a rule of the Sehwan kafis.** "cults of
  Sufi saints are displayed in all of the other kafis of Sehwan. The ancestors' tombs are always the most
  sacred place in the kafi, and the death birthday of most of them is celebrated every year: it is a main
  tool to keep the community united." (p. 127, folio 114) **"the death birthday" is printed thus.**
- **A shared shrine with two custodianships, two fairs and regular disputes.**
  **shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan**: "The annual fair is performed separately, and there is
  a gaddi nashin for the Hindus and a sajjada nashin for the Muslims. Such a scenario should not be
  idealized since the groups interact very little, and many disputes arise regularly." (p. 178, folio 165)
- **A Hindu-managed Sufi darbar, with custodianship by descent counted to the twenty-second holder.** "the
  shrine is managed by Hindus claiming to belong to the Rajputs. These would be the descendants of
  Pithoro's brother, since he was himself a renunciant who never married" (pp. 178–179, folios 165–166);
  "The current pir of Pithoro, Hathesingh, is the direct descendant of Bhanwarji, and he is the
  twenty-second pir of the line. His authority as pir comes only from this filiation." (pp. 189–190,
  folios 176–177) **No dates for any holder and no date for Hathesingh's accession: (not stated).**
- **A hereditary priesthood with its title, its function and its reach.** "The other descendants of the
  brother of Pithoro Pir form a kind of sacerdotal caste in the sect. Known under the name of Pithoro Pota
  Pir… they are the priests (gurara) of the sect. They officiate in the altars of villages, especially in
  Thar Parkar. In the temple of Bhuj, the pujari is a Pithora Pota Pir, whereas the singer of bhajans is a
  Sodha Rajput." (p. 190, folio 177) The Thar Parkar reference is place-level:
  **churrio-jabal-durga-mata-temple**, **gori-temple-gori-jo-mandar**. **The number of village altars is
  (not stated).**
- **Scripture as a source of priestly authority, with the nineteenth-century source and the present
  custodian in disagreement.** "Burton mentions they have scriptures known as pothis. According to the
  current pir, the tradition of Pithoro Pir is mainly oral, and his version has been confirmed by many
  disciples I met in various places. Nonetheless, the Pirpanthis could have pothis." (p. 190, folio 177)
  **"the Pirpanthis" is not glossed: (not stated).** Both accounts are recorded as the book records them.
- **Sayyids as the custodial class of Sindh's shrines, in Boivin's two-part formula.** "In Sindh, the
  Sayyids had two characteristics. The majority of them were landowners (zamindar), but they were also
  pir. They were responsible for managing the sanctuaries where the Muslim saints were buried. We are
  talking about material management as well as charismatic management." (p. 39, folio 26) **No shrine and
  no lineage is named: (not stated).** With the earlier identity claim: "before the eighteenth century,
  the people who identified themselves as Muslims were few: probably, this is related to the fact that the
  denomination of Muslims was restricted to the sayyids and their dependents." (p. 22, folio 9)
- **The pir as mediator, and the irrigation origin of his power.** "The pirs of Sindh play the role of
  mediators between the tribes, and between the rulers and the governed, as well. The power of the pir was
  reinforced in the eighteenth century when the Kalhora developed the irrigation system. The pir had
  created a new system of social relations that, although inspired by the tribal system, was based more on
  association than on blood ties (Ansari 1992: 28–9). There was in fact a close intertwining between
  tribal power, feudal power and charismatic power" (p. 39, folio 26). And a tribal chief who is a
  spiritual leader without being a pir: "although the Jats did not regard him as a pir, he was for many of
  his dependents more than a tribal chief: he was also a spiritual leader (Westphal-Hellbusch and Westphal
  1964: 22)." (p. 39, folio 26)
- **The absence of a central authority, in Sindh and in the paths.** "in Sindh, the groups of Sufis and
  Nanakpanthis did not have a central organization ruling an encompassing body of followers. Thus, the
  process of communalization is very fragmented and is unconducive to creating a community including all
  of the Master's followers, a sangat." (p. 202, folio 189) "authority among the Hindu Sufis is
  traditional, with many convergences with the authority as conceived in the Hindu paths where there is no
  central authority, contrary to the organization among the Sikhs." (p. 202, folio 189)
- **Branch relations, good or dead.** "Sometimes, the Indian darbar is seen as a branch of the initial one
  located in Sindh. The relationship between them can be good, or sometimes non-existent. But despite this
  situation, the sanctuaries claim to belong to the same tradition." (pp. 69–70, folios 56–57)
- **The network described as a corporate structure, with a collection.** "Most darbars are the centres of
  a spiritual network crossing over India, and sometimes abroad… Places such as these are like
  subsidiaries of the darbar, which would be their 'parent company'. Often, the subsidiaries are run by
  family members of the darbar's gaddi nashin. The gaddi nashin himself regularly visits the places,
  grants them of his blessings and also collects the gifts made by the followers." (p. 112, folio not
  stated) "The subsidiaries are not made of separate buildings, as the darbar would be, but they are
  organized inside a house or a flat depending on how many followers are living nearby." (p. 112, folio
  not stated) **No sums, no numbers of followers: (not stated).** Boivin admits the limit: "The network
  built by around darbar is not always easy to understand." (p. 115, folio 102) **"built by around
  darbar" is reproduced as printed.**
- **Four ways an alternative structure comes into being, from the chapter-4 conclusion.** (i) "a structure
  affiliated to one of the three studied darbars"; (ii) one that "can also reflect the diaspora followers,
  where subsidiaries could have been created"; (iii) one "born from the radiation of a follower, as was
  the case with Dadi Dhan in Mulund"; (iv) "a samadhi as an extension of a darbar can be created from the
  will of a follower" (pp. 127–128, folios 114–115). A condition of ownership attaches: "Other samadhis
  are independent Sufi structures, although their owners are required to be the follower of a Sufi Master
  who runs a darbar" (p. 112, folio not stated); "most of the time, the main authority of the samadhi is
  its founder" (p. 113, folio 100). And a cross-border case: "as is the case with Jogi Sain's samadhi in
  Mumbai, one is directly linked to the spiritual authority of a Muslim sajjada nashin who stays in Sindh,
  in Pakistan" (p. 113, folio 100).
- **The three structure-words, and the samadhi's four senses.** `darbar`, `samadhi` and `Sufi mandir` are
  used as distinct types and none is glossed at first use (p. 9, p. 10, folios not stated). The samadhi is
  later defined four ways at once: "In Hindu theology, the samadhi is the union with God in the Absolute.
  Sometimes it can be a state of high consciousness. Last, as it is used here, it is a funerary monument
  devoted to a saint. Since most of the Hindu saints are burnt, there is a box containing the ashes of the
  saint's body. In the Sindhicate context, the samadhi also designates different religious places. For
  example, in the darbar, the term is used for the altar, the place where the rituals are performed."
  (pp. 117–118, folios 104–105)
- **The index's custodianship pointers, with their runs** (locators are the index's own folio runs, not
  page references): "sajjada nashin 198 n.3", with "of Daraza 14, 59, 61, 80, 122, 130, 138, 166, 204
  n.1"; "of Jahaniyya 60"; "of Jhok Sharif 46"; "for Muslims 165"; "of Tando Jahaniyyan 99" (p. 248,
  folio 235). "gaddi nashins 55, 67, 69, 114, 119, 120, 122, 132, 136, 174, 175, 198 n.3", with "Baba
  Sadhuram 55"; "Hindus 165"; "Indian 55"; "migration 55"; "Nimanal Sangam 159"; "Nimano 61, 100"; "of
  Rochaldas's darbar 99, 149"; "Shah, Vasan 57"; "of Tando Saindad 110" (p. 242, folio 229).
  "**dastarbandi (coronation) 60–1, 113, 132–3, 136, 154**"; "piri-muridi 60"; "pag 60" (pp. 241, 247,
  folios 228, 234). **The archive's `Auqaf` has no index entry: the index runs "attendants 1 / avatar /
  Ayaz, Shaykh" with nothing between them (p. 239, folio not stated), and chunk 013 confirms the absence
  for the whole index (p. 251, folio 238).** Orders indexed with runs: "Qadiriyya 46, 84, 85, 110, 119,
  125, 126, 187"; "Naqshbandiyya 13, 125"; "Chishtiyya 204 n.1" — **a single note reference, the thinnest
  order entry in the index**; "Jahaniyya 60, 82, 85, 99, 132, 154, 187"; "silsila Daraza 54, 59, 61, 65,
  80, 187, 188, 189, 190, 192, 197 n.2"; "Sohrawardiyya 38, 43, 85, 126, 187, 204 n.1" (pp. 240–250,
  folios 227–237). **There is no top-level `tariqa` entry** (p. 251, folio 238).
- **Annex 2's chart of Hindu Sufi silsilas, as the text layer gives it** (p. 227, folio 214; **the cell
  boundaries have not survived**): `Darazi (Qadiriyya)` — Nimano Faqir, Radha Kishin, Pratap Rajani;
  `Jahaniyya (Sohrawardiyya)` — Rochaldas; `Jalalaniyya (Qadiriyya)` — Jogi Sain; `Saindad
  (Qalandariyya)` — Mulchand Kripalani; `Sehwan Sharif (Qalandariyya)` — Rochaldas, Dalpat Sufi;
  `Jhok Sharif (Qadiriyya)` — Dalpat Sufi, Mulchand Faqir (via Rakhiyyal Shah), Molchand Kripalani.
  **Six silsilas, each with an order-label in parentheses; three men appear under two silsilas each —
  Rochaldas, Dalpat Sufi and Molchand/Mulchand Kripalani, the last spelled differently in the two
  places.** Two of the six are named for archive sites: **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** for Sehwan Sharif and **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed** for Jhok Sharif.
- **Boivin's reading of that chart — the single most citable structural claim in the Annexes.** "The chart
  above clearly shows that **three main Sufi tariqas were dominant among the Hindu Sufis of Sindh**
  (Boivin 2015). **The Qadiriyya is the most influential, as is the case in the whole Indian
  subcontinent.** The Qalandariyya is not so widespread over it, but in the case of Sindh, it reflects the
  radiation of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar and the pilgrimage centre of Sehwan Sharif (Boivin 2012). The third
  tariqa is the **Sohrawardiyya** (Al-Huda 2003), **the oldest Sufi order that settled in Multan and was
  predominant in the Indus Valley from the thirteenth century onwards**. In this chart, there are **two
  significant absences: the Chishtiyya and the Naqshbandiyya.**… **Maybe because of the Sohrawardiyya's
  hold on Sindh, the Chishtiyya could not spread in Sindh as it**" (p. 227, folio 214) — **the sentence
  breaks off at the chunk boundary.** The Multan reference is place-level:
  **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak**. The Chishti absence is argued twice: "it is amazing to observe that **the Chishtiyya was not widespread in Sindh, while it is dominant in Punjab and North India**." (p. 217, folio 204, General conclusion n. 1)
- **Two regional silsilas, and a negative finding about a named order.** "the Sufis with whom they were in
  touch, including their masters, mostly belonged to two regional silsilas: the Darazi silsila and the
  Jahaniyya silsila. Contrary to the Hindu Sufis studied by Dahnhardt, one cannot count a single
  Naqshbandi." (p. 200, folio 187) — **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast** for the Darazi silsila.
- **The Naqshbandiyya's own arrival and character in Sindh** (Annexes tail, p. 228, folio 215): "It came
  to Sindh from the sixteenth century onwards, with the coming of the Tarkhans and the Arghuns, two
