  Central Asian dynasties that ruled Sindh"; "From the end of the seventeenth century onwards, different
  Naqshbandis were prolific authors, and after the beginning of the eighteenth century, they composed
  treatises on Islam, especially to explain how to follow the Islamic rules properly, such as how to pray";
  and "All over South Asia, the Naqshbandis are orthodox Sufis since they give great importance to
  following sharia. No Muslim Sufi, at any level he could have reached, is exempt from submitting himself
  to the prescriptions of Islamic law." (p. 228, folio 215)
- **The Udasis of Sindh, and the thinness of the scholarship — directly relevant to the archive's
  Nanakpanthi / Udasi Darbar rows.** "The Udasis of Sindh are coined as being a **heterodox Sikh
  tradition**, in one of the very few, **if not the sole, academic papers devoted to the Nanakpanth in
  Sindh** (Jatt 2016)." (p. 209, folio 196, Ch. 1 n. 10) **No site is named: (not stated).** In the body:
  "In Sindh, many Nanakpanthis belonged to a subgroup known as the Udasis"; "the Sindhi Nanakpanthis who
  were heterodox Sikhs and as such, they did not follow the requirements of the Khalsa"; "They were
  organized in separate communities" (p. 53, folio 40).
- **Attendants and lesser offices, named in Sindhi.** "the shewadaris, the attendants who were in charge
  of the centre" (p. 14, folio not stated); the `bawas`, "the Nanakpanthi priests" (p. 103, folio 90); the
  `mehndibardar`, "the one who bore the sacred henna" (p. 57, folio 44).

### Festivals, ʿurs, versi and mela

- **The ʿurs defined as a wedding, which is what the mehndi is for.** "The death of a Sufi saint is
  commemorated as **his wedding with God**. The Sufi is represented as the bride and God as the
  bridegroom. In South Asia, the bride is decorated with **henna** for her wedding." (p. 210, folio 197,
  Ch. 1 n. 16) In the body: "In the Sindhi darbars of South Asia, the annual fair is meant to commemorate
  the death of the Sufi saint. The death is transmutated into the wedding of the Sufi with God… A number
  of rituals are performed during the urs, among which the mehndi is at the apex of all of the urs. The
  mehndi, or henna, is the very symbol of the wedding (shadi) in South Asia. The bride is supposed to put
  henna on her hand. In the Sufi context, the mehndi is brought to the tomb, inside the dargah, where it
  is deposited at the foot of the tomb." (p. 144, folio 131)
- **The versi defined against the ʿurs, and Rochaldas's dated to a Hindu month.** "The versi, the name
  given to the urs, commemorates the day when the Hindu Sufi left the physical world. For Rochaldas, the
  versi is celebrated according to the Hindu lunar-solar calendar of Vikram Samvat, on the third day of
  the dark face of the moon in the month of Margashirsa. This month, also known as Agrahayana, starts on
  16 December and ends on 16 January. The celebrations last three days and are marked by the chanting of
  many Sufi kalams, and by the mehndi ceremony." (p. 145, folio 132) The book's contents page carries the
  shift as a section title: `Annual fairs: From urs to versi 131` (p. 9, folio not stated).
- **Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's ʿurs, dated to three consecutive days of Shaban, with a procession each day.**
  **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**: "on the 18th of Shaban, it is the procession led by the Lakiyyaris, on the
  19th it is the one led by Ramchand and on the 20th is that of Lal Gul." (p. 181, folio 168) **The year
  is (not stated); the month is lunar and no Gregorian equivalent is given.** The second-day mehndi is the
  Hindu families' hereditary right: the Navanis and the Kanungos "used to perform mehndi since centuries
  during the urs of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar… on the second day of the urs, the nineteenth day of Shaban"
  (p. 58, folio 45), and Mulchand "performed the mehndi ceremony of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in Sehwan until
  his death" (p. 59, folio 46).
- **Two death anniversaries merged into one festival in India.** **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**: "For a few
  years, two distinct urs were celebrated: Lal Shahbaz's urs in the Islamic calendar, 18, 19 and 20
  Shaban, and Rochaldas's versi." (p. 146, folio 133) "In the late 1950s, they were celebrated separately,
  but now they are celebrated together." (p. 146, folio 133) **The merge is dated only as "One year":
  (not stated).**
- **A three-day programme, tabulated and dated.** "Table 5.1 Daily programme of Rochaldas's versi in 2012"
  sets three columns, "First day", "Second day", "Third day", against clock times (p. 146, folio 133).
  First-day entries as printed: "4.00–4.30 dhamal"; "4.30–8.30 kirtan"; "9.00 breakfast"; "10.00 Pooja and
  flag sahib dhamal"; "10.30–1.00 pm Kalam and satsang in hall"; "1.00–2.30 Bandharo sahib"; "3.30 Tea";
  "4.00 Shehnai and dhamal near flag sahib"; "4.30–6.45 Kalam and satsang in hall"; "6.45–7.00 Dhamal in
  darbar sahib"; "7.30–9.30 Bandharo sahib"; "10.00–4.00 am (Full night) Kalam in hall". Second-day
  entries mostly read "idem", with these exceptions: "9.45: dhamal, shehnai and mehndi sahib"; "Shehnai,
  mehndi sahib and ceremony dastar bandi in darbar sahib" at 4.00; "4.15: dhamal near flag sahib"; "4.30
  custom of embracing dastar bandi with garland in hall"; "5.00–5.30 kalam in darbar sahib"; "6.00–6.45
  kalam and satsang in hall". Third-day entries as extracted: "idem" at 4.00–4.30 and 4.30–8.30; "9.30
  idem"; "10.30 idem"; "idem" at 1.00–2.30; "3.00" and "3.30" with no activity; "4.00–4.45 satsang in
  darbar"; "4.45" with no activity. **The blank and activity-less cells are a text-layer problem, not a
  finding.** The accompanying prose: "the daily programme lasting three days is mostly similar every day…
  Furthermore, dhamal is performed several times a day, especially on the first day when it is performed
  three times. The dhamal is played in different places inside the darbar. Most of the time, dhamal is
  only performed with drums and only on one occasion, the drumming is accompanied with the shahnais, the
  flutes in the shrill sound that are very common in any religious ritual performed in Sindh." (p. 145,
  folio 132) **The sentence introducing the parenthesis "(and interestingly, it is not stated in the
  programme)" breaks off with the chunk** (p. 145, folio 132).
- **The festival calendar of one darbar, in full.** Besides the merged ʿurs/versi: "Two other versis are
  celebrated: that of Hari, the son and successor of Rochaldas, on 21 March, and that of Gobindram, a
  renunciant who was close to Rochaldas when he was in Sindh, on the first day of Holi. A last opportunity
  is found in the celebration of the new moon (chand). A hundred disciples then gather to sing kalams.
  Other versis are celebrated for the most outstanding followers of Rochaldas, like for example Dadi Dhan."
  (p. 148, folio 135) **"A hundred disciples" is the only regular attendance figure in these extracts.**
- **A second path's calendar, itemised by date and rite.** "In Mulchand's Sufi path, the versi was
  performed on three main occasions: 5 April (birthday of Mulchand and Gehimal), 20 November (Mulcand's
  [text-layer? Mulchand's] death) and 29 October (Gehimal's death). The function started with the raising
  of Mulchand's alam, now known as jhando. Mulchand's jhando was navy blue. The function was carried on
  with the murti, which was made virajman, a ritual when the murti is sat on the throne. Then it was the
  time of invocation: a spiritual person covers his face and invites the murshid, and his tears start
  flowing in this part known as sufiyya ji ibadat, or the 'Sufi worship'. After the arti, prasad was
  distributed among the followers and, finally, flowers were put in flowing water, usually a river. The
  function ended with the singing of Sufi kalams, and with the performance of dhamal. The dhamal was
  nevertheless informal. A devotee was playing the drum and other followers were dancing on the Mast
  qalandar rhythm." (p. 149, folio 136) In the Delhi puja rooms, "functions were performed for the birth
  (6 April) and death (20 November) anniversaries of the Sufi sant" (p. 144, folio 131) — **6 April there
  against 5 April at p. 149; both as printed.**
- **A versi begun while its subject was still alive, and the first one after his death held in the open.**
  "In 1953, Gehimal started the first celebration of Mulchand's versi, when he was still alive." (p. 82,
  folio 69) "when the first versi of Mulchand was celebrated in 1972 in the Kuthiyya, it was in an open
  air since no building had been constructed yet." (p. 83, folio 70) "It was decided that the versi of
  Mulchand and that of Gehimal would be celebrated every year. Satsangs would be performed every Friday
  evening." (p. 83, folio 70)
- **A date turned into a calendar and then into an inscription.** "one of the biggest annual celebrations
  happens on 24 March, the day after the anniversary of his death. It is said that, in March of 1963,
  while sitting in the middle of a group of disciples, Nimano asked that a devotional musical session,
  known as satsang, be organized on the 24th of each month. She died on the 23rd following that day, and
  her funeral was organized for the 24th. This figure then became sacred, and an inscription above the
  entrance of the darbar reads: 24 ware sai ki jai." (pp. 74–75, folios 61–62) **The book writes "his
  death" of Nimano here and "she" in the next two sentences; recorded as printed, not reconciled.**
- **A renaming dated and turned into a festival of its own.** "It was in 1999 that the Sakhi Kuthiyya
  became the Nimanal Sangam, thirty-six years after the death of Nimano Faqir. This change is celebrated
  on 5 December of each year under the name Puj Nimano Sain Pak Astan." (p. 142, folio 129) **The book
  states the interval but prints no death year for Nimano; none is asserted or computed here.** "It is not
  known how the devotional practice was organized during Nimano's lifetime." (p. 142, folio 129)
- **A versi dated to a solar month, with attendance unknown.** "The main performance is for Dalpat's
  versi, which is celebrated during the month of Badho, a solar month spreading from the middle of August
  to the middle of September, soon after the festival of Dasserah. It is mostly made up of the singing of
  Sufi songs in Sindhi, but I do not know how many people attend the performance. In fact, it is difficult
  to know how many people have paid visits to Dalpat's samadhi." (p. 120, folio 107) "Other versis are
  arranged for Dalpat's successors, including Dhuru's father." (p. 120, folio 107) **Dasserah is dated in
  the Notes: "the tenth day of the waning moon in the month of Asu, running from the middle of September
  to the middle of October"** (p. 213, folio 200, Ch. 4 n. 3) — **which places Dasserah after Badho, not
  before it; both statements are recorded as printed.**
- **Four versis on four sacred numerals, unmatched to their names.** "Four versis are celebrated
  throughout the year: Dada Dastgit Badhsha [text-layer? Dada Dastgir Badshah] (in other words, Abd
  al-Qadir al-Jilani), Jogi Sain, Shah Inayat (from Jhok Sharif) and Nasir Faqir. In fact, the dates of
  all of these death anniversaries are taken as sacred: the 11, the 15, the 17 and the 27." (p. 122,
  folio 109) **The month is not given for any of the four, and the numerals are not matched to the names:
  (not stated).** — **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed** for Shah Inayat of Jhok Sharif. The one
  numeral the book does attach: al-Jilani's "death anniversary, the 11th (or Yarhen) of Rabi al-Sani. For
  Molchand, the number 11 was consequently sacred. Still, Yarhen is a great festival celebrated at the
  Sufi mandir." (p. 123, folio 110) The index gives it no date and no place beyond "Sufi mandir":
  "`Yarhen, festival at Sufi mandir 110`" (p. 252, folio 239).
- **A melo founded in India on the model of a Pakistani ʿurs, with its Moharram dates.**
  **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed**: "In 1965, he was able to discover the dargah and he started to
  celebrate the melo every year from the 17th to the 19th of Moharram, as it is celebrated in Jhok
  Sharif." (p. 124, folio 111) In 2013 another observance, "at the occasion of Sadiq Muhammad's urs",
  carried a book distribution (p. 121, folio 108).
- **Two versis a year at each of two Sachal darbars in one neighbourhood, with their weekly rhythms.**
  **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast**: the Radha Krishin Lal darbar / Maqbul Manzil, founded by "Faqir Radha
  Krishin Lal Sahib Darazi 'Krishna' (d. 1980)", who "built the darbar in the 1970s"; run after him by
  Hari Ram, then by his daughter Soni, "who performed the satsang every Thursday evening"; two versis a
  year (pp. 150–151, folios 137–138). The Sachal Sain darbar / Rohani Rah: "The entrance door is decorated
  by haqq mawjud in Arabic Sindhi script. It stood for about thirty to thirty-five years in another
  building, and shifted to the present one twenty years ago. It was built by Pratap Rai Rajani, who passed
  away in 2007. His wife Mira used to sing Sachal's poetry during the satsang, which is held every Sunday
  evening. Previously, it was organized twice a week… Mira's son Jaichand daily performs the arti in the
  darbar at about 9.00 am." (p. 151, folio 138) **Both "twenty years ago" spans are relative and the year
  of writing is (not stated).**
- **A weekly rite, its day explained twice over, and its attendance counted.** "It is interesting to
  observe that the satsang is organized on Thursdays. It is well known that Thursday evening is for
  Muslims the beginning of Friday, which is itself the most sacred day of the week. In the Sufi dargahs of
  South Asia, the most important religious events are arranged on Thursday evenings, as for example the
  musical session of the qawwali. On the other hand, in a Hindu context, the arti performed on Thursday is
  also a special occasion. The arti is devoted to Vishnu, although it is called Bhraspativar arti, or the
  arti of Jupiter. It is said that this is the most beneficial sign of the planets, and that offering
  prayers on this day promotes greater learning and prosperity." (p. 143, folio 130) "About twenty people
  attend the satsangs." (p. 143, folio 130)
- **A versi held in a private flat, counted and instrumented.** "At Dadi Dhan's place, the versi in 2012
  was marked by a succession of songs sung either by men or by women, mostly a capella, with one exception
  where a singer used an ektaro, a one-string instrument that serves as a drone. About 150 people were
  involved in the versi, including Pritam, who died in 2015… The latter did not attend, but many disciples
  had travelled from Ulhasnagar." (pp. 148–149, folios 135–136) **"The latter" is the present gaddi
  nashin, who is not named on that page: (not stated).** The space was left unmarked: "the physical space
  was reduced and the sacralization of the territory was limited to the presence of a small altar: no
  inscription or any specific decorum gives information on the sacred function that this place can
  exercise." (p. 148, folio 135)
- **Where the line between private and public cult falls, posed as a question.** "It can be a private
  affair when a versi is organized in a private flat, but when more than 100 followers attend the event, is
  it still a private affair? In this case, it is at least still private if there is no institutional
  organization managing and organizing the event, as was the case for Isarlal's versi as organized by Manu
  Bhatia's parents." (p. 174, folio 161) **No attendance figure is given for any Pakistani shrine
  anywhere in these extracts: (not stated).**
- **The annual gathering as the limit of community, and a term offered for it.** "the sangat was a
  community to the extent that it gathered as a body no more than once a year, for the urs or the versi.
  In this case, it would be more relevant to call it a communitas instead of a sangat." (p. 203,
  folio 190; the same sentence at p. 203, folio 190 in the General conclusion material)
- **The annual observance is triple-indexed, and the index does not say the headwords name one thing.**
  `urs 44, 46, 110, 131–7, 165, 167–9, 190`, with seven shrine- or person-specific subentries: "in Arabic
  131"; "of Jhok Sharif 46"; "of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar 45, 68, 105, 112, 113"; "of Muhammad, Sadiq 108";
  "of Muinuddin Chishti 110"; "of Qutub Ali Shah 44"; "of Sachal Sarmast 130" (p. 251, folio 238).
  `death anniversary 69, 131–7, 157, 161`, with "Dalpat 107"; "Daulatram's wife 101"; "Hari, R. M. 60–1,
  138"; "Isarlal 161"; "Mulchand 63, 69, 70"; "Rochaldas 154, 192"; "Sachal 138" (p. 241, folio 228). And
  `versi see death anniversary` (p. 252, folio 239) — **a see-reference with no locator of its own. The
  two top-level runs overlap exactly at folios 131–7, and the index does not say the two headwords name
  the same thing: (not stated).** **`mela` / `melo` is confirmed absent from the whole index** (p. 251,
  folio 238), and there is no `langar` entry — "L runs 'landowners (zamindar) 26, 31 / Laxman (god) 94'
  with nothing between" (p. 245, folio 232); food distribution is indexed instead as "bhandaro 131" and
  "prasad 101" (pp. 240, 247, folios 227, 234).

### Ritual objects, music and text

- **Singing as the precondition of every rite.** "The continuity of the mystic space is made through the
  singing of Sufi poetry accompanying the performance of the rituals themselves. **No ritual is performed
  without the singing of a Sufi kalam.**" (p. 152, folio 139) And as the chapter's mechanism: "It draws a
  spiritual state of mind that is an indispensable condition for reaching the goal: the merging of the
  follower with the Master, and from him/her to God. The singing of the Sufi poetry puts the disciple in
  an emotional state, which can hopefully lead to ecstasy, itself understood as being the threshold of the
  meeting with God." (p. 99, folio 86) "The core of the religiosity is the expression of emotion
  throughout the singing of poetry." (p. 90, folio 77)
- **Who carried this culture, and who did not.** "This shared religious culture was not transmitted by the
  Ulemas or the pandits. It was mainly conveyed by poetry and music performed in the darbar. While some
  segments of Sindhi society had their own transmitters, this culture was primarily passed on to the
  villages by the caste musicians (manghanar or mirasi)." (p. 53, folio 40)
- **Two repertoires, and an unwritten rule of attribution.** "Every path broadly uses the classical Sindhi
  Sufi poets, as well as the modern Sufi poets, but they also refer to a repertoire which arose inside the
  community. Most of the time, the poetry has been authored by the founder of the path, or his Master…
  Since a Master is supposed to provide inspiration to his murids, even if a murid composes Sufi poetry,
  it will be attributed to the Master. This untold and unwritten rule is prevalent in many religious
  traditions all over the world." (p. 98, folio 85) And the portability distinction: "Another category of
  repertoire is made up of Sufi poetry sung only in the involved Sufi path. For example, Rochaldas's
  poetry, usually composed by Dadi Dhan or Dadi Ganga, is hardly performed in Mulchand or Nimano's paths"
  (p. 109, folio 96).
- **The classical repertoire, and what it is praised for.** "The classical Sufi corpus is praised by the
  Hindu Sufis since such poetry is made of two meaningful features: the use of Sindhi local figures and
  themes, and the importance given to music. But above all, they all claim the unity of God." (p. 94,
  folio 81) The kalams of **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed**'s Jhok Sharif "are still a part of the
  main poetry, which is sung in the urs of Jhok Sharif" (p. 59, folio 46); **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast** —
  "kalams written by Sindhi Sufis like Sachal Sarmast are still sung every day" (p. 74, folio 61); and at
  Daulatram's versi the chanting is "of Sufi songs composed by Sachal Sarmast, Shah Abd al-Latif and Qutub
  Ali Shah" (pp. 149–150, folios 136–137) — **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast** and **bhit-bhit-shah**.
- **A poem's musical specification, in detail.** Qutub Ali Shah's `Sakhi sabhajal` "is to be sung in the
  sur asa purbi, sur being the Sindhi word for rag. It is an interesting association of two surs. The
  first one is named asa, probably borrowed from the Sikhs but seldom heard today. The sur asa is related
  to hope, inspiration and courage. The sur purbi, a vernacularized form of purvi, implies the follower to
  be quiet and serious, as well as mystical. This one, too, is not commonly heard today." (p. 96,
  folio 83) Its form: "a short poem with a refrain (thal) and four stanzas" (p. 96, folio 83), with the
  Sindhi text and an English translation "given in the annex" — printed at p. 223, folio 210, with three
  footnotes, one of which records that "**Qutub Ali Shah uses the feminine term for himself in the
  original.** This is common in Sufi verse, where the seeker sees himself as the feminine surrendering to
  the higher power that is masculine". **Footnote 2 glosses "raham" where the verse line prints "rehem".**
- **The dhamal timed to the quarter hour, three times a day.** "The dhamal lasts about fifteen minutes and
  is divided into three parts of about five minutes each. The first part plays Mast qalandar, the second
  is a rhythm of rejoicing (shadmano) and the third plays again Mast qalandar. The worship begins early in
  the morning between 4.00 and 4.30. The dhamal is again performed from 4.30 to 4.45 in the afternoon and
  again from 6.45 to 7.00 in the evening. It is closed with the song of Allah Ishwar Krishna karim, a
  succession of terms drawn from Islam and Hinduism aimed at showing the convergence of the mystical
  quest." (pp. 140–141, folios 127–128) "The arti, the first part of the puja, begins at 6.00 pm."
  (p. 141, folio 128) The percussionist may be a teenager: "It so happens that it is a girl of about
  fifteen years. For devotees, dhamal means the victory of the faqir over his self. When he masters it,
  the dhamal is played as a celebration." (p. 140, folio 127)
- **The order of the evening puja, item by item, ending on Lal Shahbaz Qalandar.**
  **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, and Guru Nanak — **gurdwara-babay-de-ber**, **gurdwara-balila-sahib-bal-lila-sahib**, **gurdwara-chakki-sahib**, **gurdwara-choa-sahib**, **gurdwara-darbar-sahib-kartarpur**, **gurdwara-khoohi-bhai-lalo-bhai-lalo-di-khooi**, **gurdwara-malji-sahib**, **gurdwara-panja-sahib**, **gurdwara-patti-sahib**, **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **gurdwara-rori-sahib**, **gurdwara-sach-khand-sahib**, **gurdwara-sacha-sauda**, **gurdwara-sahib-saidpur-guru-nanak-dev-ji**, **gurdwara-sri-kiara-sahib**, **gurdwara-sri-tilganji-sahib**, **gurdwara-tambo-sahib**, **gurudwara-janam-asthan-nankana-sahib** (person-level, all eighteen rows of which he is principal figure), and Jhulelal — **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan**: "It begins with the singing of prayers devoted to Rochaldas before a devotee removes the tissue that protects the Guru Granth Sahib, and then it is the Sufi poem of Qutub Ali Shah titled Sakhi sabajhal that is sung. The puja ends with the jai (praise) formula devoted to the gods, including Guru Nanak, Jhulelal as well as Mast qalandar, that is, Lal Shahbaz Qalandar." (p. 141, folio 128)
- **A daily satsang with its hours, its instruments and its gender arrangement.** "the satsang takes place
  every day and lasts one hour, except for on Sunday and Friday when it lasts two hours, from 5.00 to 700
  [text-layer? 7.00] pm. In the ordinary satsangs, the chants are not accompanied by musicians, but for
  those on Friday and especially Sunday, they are. The instruments are made up of an electronic keyboard
  and a dholak, and both are electronically amplified. The choice of the songs is made by the devotees,
  and no rule prevails except that they try to find themes that can be linked together." (p. 142,
  folio 129) "The songs are led by Kamla, who is assisted by a choir of eight women. The rhythm is also
  given by a few tambourines struck by the singers and through the clapping of hands… Women, who are
  separated from men, are the most numerous and active. Their heads are covered with a veil. The songs
  follow each other before being followed by prayers, and then by an offering of flowers. The ritual ends
  with a song from the Guru Granth Sahib." (p. 142, folio 129)
- **Two informal genres, one of them borrowed from the Sikhs.** "Popati Hiranandani claimed that Ram
  Panjwani introduced the Sufi chownkis. In her words, a chownki is a 'chorus singing Sufi kalams composed
  by Sindhi poets with the rhythmic beat of the gharo' (Hiranandani 1984: 55). The term chowki, or
  chownki, was maybe borrowed from the Sikh tradition, since in a dictionary published in the late
  nineteenth century, it is said that chownki is 'the reading of the scriptures of Nanak Shah' without any
  reference to Sufi songs (Shirt 1879: 353). However, it is used today among the Hindu Sindhis for the
  singing of Sufi kalams. It is not easy to distinguish between chownki and satsang. Beside the chownkis,
  another informal Sufi practice was another session, named ruha ruhani, or 'Spiritual conversations'."
  (p. 144, folio 131) The gharo is glossed "an earthenware pot" (p. 161, folio 148). **The index carries
  three spellings with two different runs: "chownki 131, 148", "Sufi chowkis 131" and "Sufistic chownkies
  131, 148"** (p. 240, folio 227; p. 250, folio 237; p. 251, folio 238).
- **Ecstatic and mourning practice named around one figure.** "he also regularly attended the dhamal"
  (p. 59, folio 46); "he was moved to tears by the melodies sung by the sharnais during the processions of
  tazias for Moharram" (p. 56, folio 43); "He would take bhang, a narcotic beverage, every day, and he
  also played ektara, a one-string guitar" (p. 59, folio 46).
- **Iconography as mechanism, not decoration — and as a genealogy and a chart of power.** "the iconography
  traces the genealogy, often pluri-confessional, in which the founder of the darbar is inscribed, and
  thus locates the darbar at the crossroads of multiple religious heritages. Beyond this function, it is
  also the iconography that informs us about the relations of power within the community of disciples, for
  example, the position of the gaddi nashin vis-a-vis [text-layer? vis-à-vis; grave accent dropped] the
  founder." (p. 132, folio 119) And as institutional proof: "since the Master is supposed to be the most
  powerful, the follower can even be bestowed with a vision through the visualization of his Master's
  image." (p. 203, folio 190) Iconography is also counted as a measure of elaboration: "The most widely
  used iconography is that of the darbar of Rochaldas, while that of the Dada Sain Kuthir has three
  elements and that of the Nimanal Sangam only two." (p. 132, folio 119)
- **didar converted into darshan — the transference named in the section title.** "The iconography is of
  special importance in the context of Hinduism. It is due to the cardinal role played by the darshan or
  visualization of God in the general economy of Hindu worship. Furthermore, it meets the process of
  didar, the visualization of the Sufi Master. It will be necessary to carefully examine how the Hindu
  Sufis converted, to some extent, the Sufi didar into the Hindu darshan, knowing that some Sufi masters
  such as Qutub Ali Shah had already paved the way – for example, in using them interchangeably." (p. 130,
  folio not stated)
- **An altar read as a hierarchy, level by level, with a Muslim shrine in the middle of it.**
  **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, and Krishna — **krishna-mandir-kabari-bazar**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road** —
  and Guru Nanak's eighteen rows: "There are three levels. The first level is that of Rochaldas and his
  son Hari, with a portrait on the left of Guru Nanak, below which stands the Guru Granth Sahib. The
  portraits of Rochaldas and Hari are the only ones to be sheltered under a kind of miniature kiosk
  (chhatri). The transition from the first level to the second level is carried out by a representation of
  the darbar of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar. The second level is that of Qutub Ali Shah and two of his
  successors: Hadi Bakhsh… and Nur Husayn Shah, the current sajjada nashin. The third level consists of a
  representation of Krishna accompanied by Radha." (p. 133, folio 120) "Moreover, the four walls of the
  samadhi are decorated with fairly numerous representations as well as calligraphy. Among the
  representations is Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani, as well as Jesus. An ecumenical orientation is therefore
  displayed, but it is not confirmed by the incantations given during the ritual." (p. 133, folio 120)
- **A Muslim saint's alam standing in a Hindu darbar in India.** **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**: figure 2.3 is
  captioned "Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's alam in Rochaldas's darbar 68" (p. 10, folio not stated). "the alam is
  the name given to a medium-sized space near the main entrance. This term of Arabic origin is used for
  metonymy and signifies the whole space." (p. 80, folio 67) The index gives `alam 67–8, 130, 136, 139,
  188, 192` at **the same opening folios, 67–8, as `samadhi`** (p. 239, folio not stated), with the
  related regalia "jhando 132, 136"; "chadors 113"; "amulets (tawiz) 113" (pp. 239, 244, folios not stated
  and 231).
- **Another altar inventoried, with the sizes of the pictures read as a hierarchy.** Shiva —
  **amb-temples-amb-sharif**, **bhagnari-mandir**, **chandragup-baba-chandragup**, **katas-raj-temples**, **shahwala-teja-singh-mandir**, **shiv-mandir-chiti-ghati**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir** (person-level, all eight rows of which Shiva is principal figure) — and Krishna and Guru Nanak as above: "The biggest are those of four predecessors of Dhuru, in black and white, and adorned with garlands of withered flowers. Two portraits of gods are in colour: Krishna with Radha, in a large size, and Guru Nanak. There is also a small statue of Shiva with the snake (nanga) and another one of Vishnu. The usual Sindhi Hindu pantheon is almost complete; only Jhulelal is absent." (p. 119, folio 106) In the gaddi nashin's own flat, "Above the pool, there are rocks with, in the middle, a small statue of Shiva in the position of the mahayogi." (p. 80, folio 67)
- **Krishna's place at the altar given three competing explanations and left open.** (i) historical:
  "Krishna's worship had already developed in late-nineteenth-century Sindh among the Amils. Narsain
  states that, in the nineteenth century, the Vallabhachari sect4 was accepted by some members of the Amil
  community (Narsain 1932: 1939 [text-layer? page number; reading not recoverable])." (p. 135, folio 122)
  (ii) identitarian: "One can also understand the painting of Krishna as an identity marker of Hinduism.
  Many Sindhi Sufi cults managed by Hindus in India put Krishna's representation above the other pictures,
  as if Krishna is supposed to both protect and encompass the murshids and their disciples placed
  underneath him." (p. 135, folio 122) (iii) mystical, from a follower: "the love between Krishna and
  Radha symbolizes the 'Sufi' love (ishq) between God (Krishna) and his passionate devotee (Radha)5."
  (p. 135, folio 122) Boivin calls the place of Krishna "another issue to be investigated" (p. 135,
  folio 122).
- **The feminization of the disciple, drawn from poetics.** "So, Mulchand was the bhagwan and Gehimal was
  his Radha. Interestingly, this implies a feminization of the disciple that follows a main literary rule
  of Sindhi Sufi poetry where the murid is often depicted as the virahini, the woman/soul longing for her
  love, namely God (Asani 1994)." (p. 135, folio 122) "The couple of Hindu mythology is a kind of an
  equivalent of Majnun and Layla,6 two characters who are frequently quoted by the Hindu Sufis." (p. 135,
  folio 122)
- **An iconographic anomaly Boivin cannot source, and a conjecture he declines to endorse.** "The most
  unexpected figure is that of Ali, the first Shia imam… Unfortunately, the origin of Ali's representation
  cannot be traced, but he is represented as a warrior wearing a leather cuirass and a helmet, while in
  the 'orthodox' Shia representation, he is wearing 'civil' clothes, a kind of long dress called abaya
  which is worn in the Middle East and a turban." (p. 122, folio 109) In the Notes he records Flaskerud,
  "who thinks the first portrait of Ali was first drawn in Sindh and then brought as a gift to Fath Ali
  Shah (d. 1824)… (Flaskerud : 25)" (p. 213, folio 200, Ch. 4 n. 5), and two pages later: "**I have been
  unable to identify the provenance of this representation of Ali.**" (p. 214, folio 201, Ch. 5 n. 2)
  **The two notes are in tension and both are recorded.**
- **Relics counted, typed and handled.** "The relics are made up of two items: the sandals (chakriyun) and
  the canes, known as bayragins. There are five pairs of chakriyuns, including those of Dalpat. In fact,
  these are the only remains from the Sufi saint." (p. 119, folio 106) "The chakriyuns are wooden sandals
  that are the symbol of renunciation in the Indic world among the Hindus, Muslims and other religious
  persuasions. The ones which are attributed to Dalpat are the most worn… In Sindh, once again
  irrespective of religious belonging, they [the bayragins] are the mark of renunciation."
  (pp. 119–120, folios 106–107) A second relic set, kept by a lay family: "It is said that he used dice in
  Sindh with other Sufis. The Bhatia family has kept the ivory dice, as well as the green crossed clothe
  [text-layer? cloth] on which Isar Lal played. During the versi, the two objects were exhibited so that
  the participants could touch them and then put their hands on their faces as a sort of blessing."
  (p. 150, folio 137) Boivin's rule about relics: "Sometimes, but rarely, relics are also exhibited, not
  for being worshipped as a kind of idolatry, but because they are believed to strengthen the
  concentration of the disciple." (p. 110, folio 97) **The index's two `chakriyun` entries give different
  runs, 106–7 and 106** (pp. 240, 248, folios 227, 235).
- **A Hindu saint's tomb dressed in a Muslim cloth, because that is what the bazaar sells.**
  **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar**: "she also put a new chador on Mulchand's
  tomb. Interestingly, the chadors which are available in Sehwan come with Quranic inscriptions."
  (p. 126, folio 113)
- **A tomb described by measurement against an ordinary grave, with nothing written on it.** "In its
  centre is a brick construction that is the qabr (tomb) of Mulchand Faqir. This construction is four or
  five times the size of an ordinary grave. No decoration or inscription appears, but it is covered, as is
  the tradition, with a chador." (p. 180, folio 167) And the same tomb from inside the kafi: "The tomb of
  Mulchand is located inside a small house. It is a pretty large square made with bricks, and its size is
  much bigger than that of other tombs. No picture or inscription can be found." (p. 125, folio 112)
- **Initiation as a single once-in-a-lifetime transaction, preceded by an examination.** "A very crucial
  element is the nam, the name. This is the main part of the Sufi initiation – to be given a name… The
  nam, sometimes called a mantra, can be a name of God, such as om, hari om or Radhye Shyam, another
  naming of Krishna and Radha, or, more rarely, some Sufi verses. The nam or mantra, also shabd in
  Rochaldas's spiritual dialogues, is given once in one's life. Beforehand, the murshid has a question and
  answer session with the disciple to see if he is ready." (p. 136, folio 123) From the Glossary: "Nam:
  literally meaning name, nam is the word given to the disciple by the Master, during the initiation. **It
  is believed to be a secret.** The regular chanting of the nam is a basic practice in Sufism. Meditation
  centres around the nam."; "Nam vathan: to take the name; to be initiated to the Sufi path." (p. 220,
  folio 207); "Murid: an officially initiated disciple."; "Murshid: a Sufi Master; one who initiates the
  disciple officially." (p. 219, folio 206); "Dam: breath… When a disciple is initiated, he is given a
  chant along with which he is told about the posture he is to be in when chanting and also about the
  manner in which he is to breathe." (p. 218, folio not stated)
- **Initiation described in Sindhi, and one dated transmission.** "Mulchand became Rakhiyyal Shah's
  disciple, his taleb, and he was initiated by him. In Sindhi, it is said that he took the name 'nam
  wanhan' from him, because during initiation, the Master gives his disciple a word on which he will
  perform meditation." (p. 59, folio 46); "the ism-e azam, meaning the expression/word on which meditation
  is performed", glossed as "the exact equivalent of the mantra of the Vedantists (Parsram 1924: 119–20)"
  (p. 49, folio 36); "Gehimal finally 'took nam' (nam vathan) from Mulchand" (p. 75, folio 62); "how he
  was initiated by him (nam vathan) (Motwani 1965: 35–45)" (p. 167, folio 154); "Daulat himself received
  the nam in 1958 when he was twenty years old from Nasir Faqir, himself. The goal is to reach ecstasy,
  named kafiyyat by Daulat Kesvaram." (p. 123, folio 110). Initiation could be delegated or licensed:
  "Nimano would have designated Nirmal Jani to give the name (nam) for initiation" (p. 74, folio 61); "In
  India, he got authorization from Mulchand, through a letter, to initiate disciples since Mulchand did
  not want him to bring them to Sehwan." (p. 82, folio 69)
- **A Brahman inside a Sufi initiation, and a puja to Krishna as its second step.** "The proper ritual of
  initiation starts with the havan ritual, which is performed by a Sindhi Brahman. It is said that this is
  a Vedic ritual when a Brahman lights a fire as a process of purification… In this step, a puja is
  performed in the name of Krishna. The puja, or pudiya in Sindhi, is the basic ritual for all of the
  Hindus, despite some variations." (p. 136, folio 123) "Most of the Sufi Hindu publications start with
  praise for Krishna." (p. 136, folio 123) The same pattern at a versi: "All the versis start with the
  havan performed by a Brahman." (p. 114, folio 101)
- **The secrecy of the practice, stated as a limit on the ethnography.** "Since the initiation has a
  secret side, one will not find a detailed description of the practice of meditation, although there is
  an obvious relationship to yoga." (p. 139, folio 126)
- **Breath as the common denominator, and the yoga question deliberately left unsettled.** "most of the
  practices emphasize breath control, a common practice in the Sufism of South Asia. The Sufi poets
  constantly refer to breath, or dam." (p. 131, folio 118) "The issue of Sufi meditation raises a very
  difficult concern: the relationship between Sufi breath exercises and that of yoga, especially the
  breathing practice known as pranayama. Of course, it is beyond the scope of this study, but the Indian
  influence is nonetheless accepted by the specialists of Sufism, especially through the Naqshbandiyya.7
  The Hindu Naqshbandis studied by Dahnhardt nonetheless do not give a crucial role to pranayama, although
  they sometimes refer to it (Dahnhardt 2002: 352, 358)." (pp. 138–139, folios 125–126) "The Sufis of
  Sindh we have been dealing with were mostly in touch with the Sohrawardiyya and the Qadiriyya, but the
  issue of breathing is addressed by all of them as a crucial matter for meditation." (p. 139, folio 126)
  A sourced borrowing: "See, for example, the study on the issue of breathing techniques as **borrowed by
  the Naqshbandis from 'the Indian Yogis'** (Paul 1996: 210)." (p. 214, folio 201, Ch. 5 n. 7)
- **Rochaldas's own doctrine of pranayama, in two parts.** "Answering the questions of a devotee,
  Rochaldas argues pranayama is but a practice among others that leads to the annihilation of Ego… He adds
  that, although pranayama is the most difficult, it is also the most beneficial. Some saints do no
  [text-layer? do not] recommend it, but rather remembrance (samaran) and concentration (dhyan): 'but one
  can not fly with these practices' he adds (Hari 1995: 78)." (p. 139, folio 126) "Rochaldas states that
  there are two kinds of pranayama: one with shabd and another without shabd… The pranayama without shabd
  can only bring physical force… he also says that pranayama is a 'primary practice and it has to be
  undertaken only at the earlier stages' (Hari 1995: 145). Interestingly, pranayama needs to be practised
  as long as the Ego is not under control, but once love (ishq) has arisen, there is no more need to
  perform pranayama." (p. 139, folio 126)
- **Initiation's first degree, stated as a general rule.** "The Sufi Master has spiritual knowledge and he
  can only initiate disciples, in giving them the nam and teaching techniques of meditation. The Sufi path
  is made of different degrees and the first one aims at breaking the Ego. It requires a total submission
  to the Master. In the first step, the follower has to clean the stable and the toilets, and carry out
  other tasks which are looked upon as being degrading according to the common standards of local
  society." (pp. 202–203, folios 189–190)
- **Kathas: hagiography as a daily genre.** "Another element of the daily ritual is the recitation of the
  kathas. The kathas are stories about the lives of the saints in whose name the puja is conducted. They
  come mainly from the life of Rochaldas as well as from sacred writings of Hinduism such as Bhagavad Gita
  in the interpretation given by his son Hari. Others are borrowed from the life of Sufi Sindhis, such as
  Nasir Muhammad Faqir (d. 1960)…" (p. 141, folio 128)
- **The pothi as an institution of the episteme.** "The pothi is a kind of codex which functions as a vade
  mecum – bringing together writings of various types, such as prayers, mystical poetry in Sindhi,
  excerpts from sacred texts, folkloric narratives and treatises on divination." (p. 52, folio 39) The
  corpora are "composed of oral traditions, or sometimes handwritten traditions, which are generally
  classified as folkloric" (p. 52, folio 39). Magic and divination are named as a component of religious
  knowledge and then dropped: "The other important element of religious knowledge includes a set of
  practices and beliefs related to magic and divination." (p. 42, folio 29) **Nothing further:
  (not stated).**
- **A liturgical text republished, given away free, and transliterated for a new generation.** "The darbar
  regularly republishes the munajat a little before the versi. Each participant may have a free copy.
  Whereas once it was published in the Arabic Sindhi alphabet, the darbar began to publish it in
  Devanagari so that the younger generations may read it." (p. 148, folio 135) **No print run, no cost, no
  date of the switch: (not stated).** The text: "The munajat is made of twenty-one verses composed in an
  Arabicized Sindhi" (p. 147, folio 134), quoting "ain wasi Mustafa iana Ali Murtaza, or 'and the heir of
  Mustafa, namely Ali al-Murtaza' (Roshan Ali Shah no date: 4)" and the Quranic ayat on authority, "The
  Muslim believers are said to obey God, the Apostle and those vested with authority, the ulul al-amr
  (Quran 4:59)" (p. 147, folio 134). Its refrain "is a request addressed in simple words to the One to
  accept the prayers of all, Muslim as well as Hindu" (p. 148, folio 135). Boivin's reading: "it is a kind
  of Muslim profession of faith, which is amazingly adopted as dua by Rochaldas's followers (Roshan Ali
  Shah no date: 5)." (p. 147, folio 134)
- **Devotees' own manuscript books, in two scripts.** "Other devotees, who are mostly women, have written
  in a notebook all the kalams. I could see one is written in Devanagari, and another in Arabic Sindhi
  scripts." (p. 143, folio 130) "Always, as in all Hindu and Sufi rituals, the session will be achieved
  through the distribution of prasad." (p. 143, folio 130)
- **The arti performed with a Muslim gesture, observed once.** "Every devotee is supposed to perform the
  arti, and usually, each one finishes by joining both hands and bowing to the altar. But I also observed
  that one woman, obviously Hindu, ends the arti like the Muslim's prayer, holding the open hands, then
  spend [text-layer? reading uncertain — possibly 'spread' or 'passes'] them on the face." (p. 142,
  folio 129)
- **A daily ritual timed to the minute at a Hindu-managed dargah, with its three sequences and its text.**
  "In Pithoro, the pir officiates each evening towards 6.45, after the sunset. The ritual is a form of
  puja pat, which is divided into three principal sequences: (1) the preliminaries (arti) during which the
  pir lights the candles, and where bhajans are sung without instrumental accompaniment, (2) the
  invocatory prayer (dua) and (3) the ritual of communion (piyalo). The pir performs this last rite only
  with his disciples (murids) and apart from the sanctuary. The text of the dua is simple: Dado bhali
  kundo! 'Dado will bring only good to you!'" (p. 191, folio 178) **"Dado" is not glossed as a name:
  (not stated).** And a daily puja at a place of disappearance rather than at a tomb: "Pithoro Pir
  disappeared under the ground and in his sanctuary, and the place where he disappeared is the subject of
  a particular veneration. A small altar is there and the puja is performed on a daily basis." (p. 189,
  folio 176)
- **Two daily rituals at a Chennai centre, and a claim Boivin flags as unverified.** "There are two main
  daily rituals. The first one is a dua (invocation or prayer) devoted to Abd al-Qadir Jilani, and the
  second is the arti performed to Mata. The Sufi Dar claimed to have itself two subsidiaries, the Darvesh
  Asthan in Delhi and the Dukh Bhanjan Darbar in Kolkatta." (p. 115, folio 102) "A thorough study of the
  Sufi Dar would be needed in order to give more details." (p. 115, folio 102)
- **The origins of rituals, mostly unrecoverable.** "It is not always known how the rituals came to exist.
  Rarely, the origin of a ritual is explicit. Most of the time, it is said to be 'tradition', which could
  mean it has been performed for centuries." (p. 110, folio 97)
- **Annex 1's five poets, printed as "Abstracts of Sufi poetry in Sindhi and English translation"**
  (p. 221, folio not stated; **"Abstracts" is printed; no reading is offered**): Dalpat Sufi (pp. 221–222,
  folio not stated and folio 209), Qutub Ali Shah (p. 223, folio 210), Dadi Ganga (p. 224, folio 211),
  Nimano Faqir (p. 225, folio 212) and Gehimal Motwani (p. 226, folio 213). Two footnotes gloss the verse:
  "In Eastern philosophy, a true saint is a swan – who can separate the milk from the water, the truth
  from the illusion." (p. 222, folio 209) and "Punhal= Punhu+al. **Punhu is a Sindhi folk hero and 'al' is
  a suffix of endearment.**" (p. 225, folio 212). **"Ranjhan" in Nimano's stanza 4 is given no footnote:
  (not stated); the Sindhi line "josh jigar t" in Gehimal Motwani's piece is truncated —
  `josh jigar t [text-layer? line truncated; no reading offered]` — and no source line is printed for
  that piece: (not stated)** (p. 226, folio 213).

### Economy, endowment and patronage

- **What is absent, recorded as a finding.** **No Auqaf department, no state shrine administration, no
  waqf, no endowment, no acreage, no revenue figure, no offering total, no price of admission and no
  visitor number appears anywhere in this book's practices material — every one is (not stated)** — with
  the small handful of exceptions itemised below. The index confirms the absence structurally: there is
  no `Auqaf` entry and no `langar` entry anywhere (p. 239, folio not stated; p. 245, folio 232; p. 251,
  folio 238), and the whole economic vocabulary of the index is "landowners (zamindar) 26, 31"; "hari
  (landless farmers) 26"; "kardar (tax collector) 31"; "mukhthyarkar (revenue officer) 62"; "quasi-feudal
  system 50"; "middle class 41–8"; "pirs — power 26"; "alms (zakat) 102" (pp. 239–247, folios not stated
  and 227–234).
- **Land grants as the currency of Baluchi rule.** "Given the political domination of the Talpurs, the
  Baluchis were at the top of local society. Since the eighteenth century, they provided the bulk of the
  troops of the Kalhoras, who had ruled Sindh before them. Once in power, they reinforced their rule by
  distributing jagir to clan chiefs. Their domination was consequently doubled in power, with their being
  both warriors and landowners." (p. 38, folio 25) And the Jat case: "the malik exercised a feudal power
  over those of the Jats who resided on his jagir" (p. 39, folio 26).
- **Land granted by rulers to a darbar, undated and unquantified.** Of Baba Sukhdev Sahib at Halani: "His
  spiritual level impressed a lot the Talpur mirs who regularly granted the darbar with lands." (p. 68,
  folio 55) **No acreage, no revenue figure, no sanad: (not stated).** A second, hedged case: "He is said
  to have attracted many followers, Muslims and Hindus, and the Talpur mirs are said to have looked after
  it." (p. 118, folio 105) **The hedge "are said to have" is the book's own; no grant, land or sum is
  named: (not stated).**
- **A saint who owned no land, and the economy of a khanaqah.** "Although he had never owned a piece of
  land, he was in charge of looking after the business affairs of the Sufi lodge, the khanaqah." (p. 93,
  folio 80) **The only land-and-money sentence in that chapter.**
- **Gifts and tribute paid to a saint by a ruler and his subjects — and Boivin's own caution about it.**
  "From this moment onwards, the subjects of the rano made consequent gifts to the pir, while the rano
  himself paid a tribute to the same pir. The topic of the confrontation of the saint with the
  representative of the temporal power is consequently a redundant trope of the regional hagiography."
  (p. 188, folio 175) **No sums, no goods, no land and no date for the arrangement: (not stated).** Boivin
  frames it as a trope, not as a record.
- **The panchayat as a temple-financing and ritual-taxing body — the hardest economic material in the
  book.** "The functions of the panchayat were social and religious. For example, the panchayat fixed the
  taxes relative to marriages and other rites of passage. On the other hand, it had to provide for the
  maintenance of the temple, pay the wages of the Brahmans and give alms to religious orders. It also had
  the power to punish the offenders for his injunctions until they were excommunicated (Thakur 1959:
  74–5)." (p. 41, folio 28) **No sums, no temple named: (not stated).** Jurisdiction: "In rural Sindh, the
  panchayat of the Lohanas could have authority over other castes which, because of their small number,
  had none" (p. 41, folio 28), with the Brahmans, Jajiks and Bhats "governed by a council known as mastan
  (Thakur 1959: 73)" (pp. 40–41, folios 27–28).
- **The only recorded offering made at a shrine, and the shrine is unnamed.** "the Lohana practised animal
  sacrifices on several occasions. One of them was the celebration of Navratri, the festival of Bhavani, a
  figure of the goddess; a kid or a he-goat was then sacrificed. The second occasion was the festival of
  Khetrapal, a disciple of Shiva: a kid or a he-goat was sacrificed to the dargah of a pir in this case
  (Thakur 1959: 158 and 175)." (p. 43, folio 30)
- **Land acquisition with a price, the only cash figure in the fieldwork material.** "Gehimal had gotten
  land in Ulhasnagar from one Shri Vasumal. A 'Sufi faqir' had been living there, but after Shri Vasumal's
  death, his family sold the land to Gehimal for 2,500 rupees." (pp. 82–83, folios 69–70) **The "gotten …
  from" and the sale for 2,500 rupees sit in consecutive sentences; both are recorded.** "Gehimal started
  to build a wall surrounding the land, and the building's foundations for the darbar named Mul Kuthiyya."
  (p. 83, folio 70)
- **Congregational funding and a duty to build.** "The building of the darbar was funded by the three
  female followers of Gehimal – namely Sonu, Gopi and Devi – and also Tirthdas, his brother who was living
  in Ulhasnagar. His own followers had the duty to build the darbar." (p. 83, folio 70)
- **A building registered as a worship place, with the title in one woman's name — the book's only
  statement about legal registration.** "The darbar of Haridwar was built thanks to the financial support
  of Basant and Sonu Jethwani, as well as that of Devi. Since the building was registered as a worship
  place, it was not possible to provide many rooms for the pilgrims. Everything had been recorded under
  Sonu's name." (p. 134, folio 121) **No sums are given: (not stated).**
- **Remittance custodianship across the border, and the border itself as passable.** "The darbars in Sindh
  were most of the time preserved with different arrangements regarding their management. But, often, the
  now Indian gaddi nashins or their followers would provide money to some keepers who were in charge of
  maintaining the initial darbar. Furthermore, despite the fluctuant diplomatic relations between Pakistan
  and India, it was always possible to travel across the border." (p. 68, folio 55) **No sums, no
  periodicity, no keeper named: (not stated).**
- **A kitchen as the irreducible institution of a shrine at its poorest.** "A very small structure was
  built, and it was consequently called kutiyya, a Sindhi word meaning a cottage or a hut. No facilities
  were provided, such as toilets or electricity. A kitchen was nevertheless built since the sharing of
  food is a primary element of religious rituals in the Indian subcontinent." (p. 83, folio 70) At
  Rochaldas's darbar the same function has a name: "The most impressive building is the multi storey gaddi
  nashin residence, which is extended by a hall that houses the communal kitchen (langar)." (p. 80,
  folio 67) **The langar is named once and never described: no scale, no funding, no schedule
  (not stated).** Elsewhere the provision is `bhandaro`: "3. bhandaro – that is, food for all the assembly
  (sangat)" (p. 144, folio 131), and the versi table's "1.00–2.30 Bandharo sahib" / "7.30–9.30 Bandharo
  sahib" (p. 146, folio 133).
- **Charity as a shared duty, referred to zakat and then specialised.** "A common duty shared by the
  darbars is related to charity. Since the origins of Sufism, charity towards the poor has been considered
  the practice of a main Quranic pillar, that of zakat or alms. But beyond the will to help the poor,
  starting with feeding them, the Sufi darbars in India developed more specialized practices, mostly
  referring to the medical field." (p. 115, folio 102)
- **Three models of medical charity, itemised, with the only price statement in the book.** (i)
  Separated: "the Halani darbar in Sion has a clinic run as a charitable institution, and the darbar
  itself stands at another location in Mumbai… it is relevant to speak of a total separation of the
  activities… One can even speak of a form of secularism in this respect. The Halani clinic is a most
  impressive clinic where researchers are working on very innovative medical programmes." (p. 116,
  folio 103) (ii) Complementary: at Rochaldas's darbar "the two spaces belong to the same cluster,
  although they are separated. The medical activities were in charge of Pritamdas, one of Rochaldas's
  sons, until he passed away recently. However, it is not possible to speak of a clinic here since the
  medical activities of Pritamdas, who himself was a medical doctor, were centred on distributing
  medications to people, mostly destitute, who came to meet him." (p. 116, folio 103) (iii) Specialised:
  "Medical activity is also at work in the Sufi Mandir in Sion, Mumbai. Here, there is another specialty,
  dentistry, as a dentist comes for a few hours almost every day, and once again it is mostly to work for
  destitute people" (p. 116, folio 103). And the pharmacy: the Nimanal Sangam in Vadodara restricts itself
  to traditional charity, but "It does not prevent the darbar from having a 'pharmacie' whose pills are
  sold at very low prices." (p. 117, folio 104) **No prices, no turnover, no numbers served: (not
  stated).**
- **A welfare economy attached to a Sufi vocation, with the refusal of private practice quoted.**
  **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple** (the book's "Rohri")
  and **bhagnari-mandir**, **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge**, **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi**, **shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi**, **shri-varun-dev-mandir**, **shrine-of-abdullah-shah-ghazi**, **shrine-of-pir-mangho**, **shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya** (the book's "Karachi"): "Rochaldas decided to set up dispensaries for the poor – first in Rohri, Karachi and then in Hyderabad" (p. 56, folio 43) — Hyderabad being place-level for **guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat**; "He specialized in eye surgery, and studied Ayurveda and homeopathy; surgical operations were also performed for free" (p. 57, folio 44); "'If I had wished him to make money, I would not have asked him to terminate his contract with the government.'" (p. 56, folio 43) Nimano Faqir's foundation is described in the same register: "the Sakhi Kutiyya place of worship and human welfare" (p. 60, folio 47).
- **Charitable and educational institutions founded out of Neo-Vedanta, one of them at a shrine town.**
  **shrine-of-pir-mangho** (the book's "Mangho Pir") and **gurdwara-dash-mesh-pita**, **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple**, **sant-baba-asudaram-darbar-panno-aqil** (the book's "Sukkur"): "A number of charitable institutions were created, for example, by Hiranand and his brothers, or by his 'disciples'. Among these were the leper asylum in Mangho Pir and an orphanage in Sukkur. Many educational institutions were also established." (p. 104, folio 91) **No dates, no names for the institutions, no funding source: (not stated). The scare quotes on 'disciples' are the book's own.**
- **Charity read as social control — Boivin's own gloss.** "in all of the local religions, the religious
  classes have taken the trouble to care for, to some extent, weaker classes of their society. Otherwise,
  the ideology working as the basis of their authority could have lost any legitimacy for the people.
  Furthermore, through this action, one can see another powerful instrument of social control." (p. 117,
  folio 104) Preceded by his qualification: "This is not to say that local charitable and medical
  activities are exclusive to the Sufi darbars." (p. 117, folio 104) And the limit he declines to pursue:
  "I shall not go further into this issue since it was already addressed by **Frédérique Pagani's thesis
  on how philanthropy is constructed as a path for salvation in a Sindhi temple located in Bhopal**
  (Pagani 2007). In this respect, **there is no major difference between a Sufi Sindhi darbar and any
  other Sindhi religious institution in India.**" (p. 213, folio 200, Ch. 4 n. 2)
- **Charity and medical help as the common activity of the smaller Sufi places.** "While the Sufi paths of
  the three figures under study here are well organized, there are other places devoted to other Hindu
  Sufis from Sindh. They share a main feature: their activities are not reduced to Sufi sessions. Often,
  they also provide medical help and educational help to the destitute." (pp. 169–170, folios 156–157)
- **The social base of the Hindu Sufis: state employment and trade.** "the Hindu Sufis belonged to two
  main social groups: the merchants and the civil servants" (p. 60, folio 47); Rochaldas "was the son of a
  civil servant working in colonial administration", his father "was a cleric in the department of
  engineering" and his brother "became chief officer of the municipality of Rohri" (p. 55, folio 42);
  Mulchand's father "was a deputy collector in the revenue department" (p. 58, folio 45); Rochaldas held
  "a five-year contract with the government" (p. 56, folio 43). Refined in the Notes: "it is obvious that
  **the Amils are more involved in Sufism than the Bhaibands**. If we want to be even more precise, it is
  relevant to claim that the '**real**' Sufis, to be distinguished from **the devotees who attend the
  rituals**, belong to this category in their majority." (p. 205, folio 192) **The sentence's own grouping
  is internally odd — the ex-Bhaibands are placed with the ex-Amils and against the shopkeepers; recorded
  as printed.**
- **The Amils as a literate patron class, dated as an administrative caste.** "The Amils were a
  **subcaste of the Lohanas**. Since the **eighteenth century onwards**, they specialized in becoming the
  **official scribes in the administration of the Kalhoras and the Talpurs**." (p. 214, folio 201,
  Ch. 4 n. 10) "During the Talpur rule (1783–1843), they reinforced their influence to the point where
  they almost monopolized the function of kardar. In a given district, the kardar exercised almost
  royalty-level powers since he was collecting taxes and dispensing justice. Only the army was not under
  his control." (p. 44, folio 31) And the Hyderabad elite — place-level for
  **guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat**: "The Khudabadi Amils were at the top of the social structure of
  Hyderabad. The Amils belonged to a high caste and were well-educated and well-versed in Persian. Most of
  them were in government service and a number of them had been diwans (prime ministers) of the Talpur
  mirs." (p. 103, folio 90)
- **Commercial capital converted into land.** "After the growth of new emporium in Sindh, especially
  Karachi, the Lohanas took benefit of the extension of the commercial networks throughout the British
  colonial empire all over the world. These benefits allowed them to invest in the agricultural lands,
  mainly cotton, and they became powerful landowners, locally known as zamindars, at the end of the
  nineteenth century." (p. 44, folio 31)
- **A shrine built by a trading caste, in a place chosen for its market — the clearest statement of a
  religious-market logic applied to a specific building decision.** "The local Hindus mostly belong to the
  Lohana caste, who are also those who built the Hindu dargah." (p. 185, folio 172) "In the small town of
  Tando Ahmad Khan, there is a rich Hindu religious market, so it is not a coincidence the Hindu dargah
  was built here." (p. 193, folio 180) **No sums, no endowment, no land grant is named: (not stated); only
  the caste of the builders is given, and the building date and cost are (not stated).**
- **"Religious market" as a chapter-level frame, and Boivin's use of it three times over.** Chapter 1 is
  `The religious market in Sindh on the eve of partition 23` (p. 8, folio not stated), and the study will
  "highlight the relationship they built with their followers, their masters and their rivals in the
  religious market" (p. 18, folio 5). "The main question involved here is why different Sindhis select a
  Sufi darbar, a Nanakpanthi darbar or a Daryapanthi darbar." (p. 38, folio 25) "this means that, first,
  the Muslim Sufi market was far more developed than the Hindu market" (p. 199, folio 186); "In this
  climate of universalism permeating the religious market, which coincides with the apex of European
  colonization across the globe, and the socio-economic upheaval left by the Industrial Revolution, the
  modalities of Sufi affiliation were renegotiated." (p. 200, folio 187) **The term is not defined at
  first use: (not stated).**
- **A nineteenth-century building and renovation boom, with a stated cause in middle-class time-use.**
  "Many new darbars were founded and the oldest ones were renovated; as such, they shared a basic set-up
  in which the personal relation between the spiritual Master and his disciple was at the core of the
  religiosity – and it was a perfect fit for the busy schedule of the emerging middle classes." (p. 38,
  folio 25) **No darbar is named and no number is given: (not stated).** And a wave of shrine-building
  dated to a period: "Sindh was submitted to a religious recomposition in the late nineteenth century and
  early twentieth century, as is attested by the building of many new Nanakpanthi darbars all over Sindh."
  (p. 199, folio 186) **No count, no named darbar, no funder: (not stated).**
- **A poem's brevity read as an economic accommodation to a trading class.** "Above all, it summarized
  many of the current middle-class expectations, and is simultaneously well adapted to the modern life
  because of its brevity – making it fit especially well the very busy traders' way of life, as they
  cannot spent too much time in praying." (p. 96, folio 83) **`spent` is as printed.**
- **Publishing as the principal economic institution of transmission, and a dated bibliography of it.**
  "The main tool used by the Hindu Sindhis of India is publishing… In independent India, the Sindhi Hindus
  published books related to Sufism from the 1950s onwards. This means that, as soon as they had found a
  place to settle, they undertook the duty to publish Sufi-related books." (p. 173, folio 160) The
  programme, all dated: the Jiwan Charitra, 1965 (p. 167, folio 154); Manzil-e illahi, 1969 (p. 167,
  folio 154); Vaswani's "Darvishan jo dawr", "published in 1955 in Ajmer. In a thick book of almost 600
  pages" (p. 156, folio 143); Panjwani's 1945 book on Shah Abd al-Latif "republished in India in 1981"
  (p. 157, folio 144) — **bhit-bhit-shah**; "In 1959, he published an anthology he named Sik ji soghat…
  republished in 2000 by Thakur Chawla. About 200 poets of Sindh are mentioned, with abstracts of their
  poetry. No biographical sketch is provided, no date, no reference of any kind" (p. 158, folio 145);
  Gajwani's sixty-eight-page introduction, 1997 (p. 169, folio 156); Gajwani's Sufi Galaxy, 2000 (p. 166,
  folio 153); Hiro Thakur's Qazi Qazan, 1978 (p. 171, folio 158); Ladkani's Siraiki kalam al-Sufiya, 200
  pages, 2000 (p. 171, folio 158); "Sindhi ain Sindhiyaat", "published in 1987, the year of his death…
  while the title is in Sindhi, the book of about 100 pages was published in English" (p. 159, folio 146);
  Paru Chawla's anthology, "Probably in 1992" (p. 161, folio 148); Venti ain prarthana, 2009 (p. 172,
  folio 159); Nimanal Maan, December 2011 (p. 172, folio 159); Roohani Choond Sindhi kalaam, two volumes
  of 108 kalams each, 2008, re-edited 2009 and 2012 (pp. 172–173, folios 159–160); the Bhatias' Isarlal,
  2016 (p. 172, folio 159); Sobhraj's kalam, India, 1997 (p. 179, folio 166); Dalpat Sufi's kalam,
  published by Gidumal Khatanmal Harjani, 1965 (p. 179, folio 166); the Hindi Mulchand, 2012 (p. 173,
  folio 160); Hari's four-volume dialogues, "First published in Sindhi between 1959 and 1965… Hari
  prepared an English translation that was edited in 1998 by his own son and successor, H. M. Damodar"
  (p. 162, folio 149), and "later on translated into English and published in 1995, and republished in
  1998, with the title Some Moments with the Master. Spiritual Dialogues with the Sufi Saint" (p. 164,
  folio 151); Hari's 1962 Gita commentary, "It would be translated by his son and published in English in
  1982" (p. 163, folio 150); and "In 1992, Damodar also published a translation from Sanskrit made by 'a
  devotee' of the Shri Yoga Vaisishtha" (p. 163, folio 150). **No print runs, no prices, no sales figures
  anywhere: (not stated).** Selling has moved online: "**Most of them have a website, and they can sell
  through it different items, including books.**" (p. 205, folio 192)
- **A publishing asymmetry across the border, stated twice.** "it is interesting and important to observe
  that a number of Sufi kalams in Sindhi which have been published in India are still unpublished in
  Pakistan." (p. 169, folio 156) "From partition onwards, much Sufi poetry in Sindhi was published in
  India. Some of the most important kalams are still unpublished in Pakistan – for example, Dalpat Sufi's
  poetry." (p. 153, folio 140) And a poet published against his own wish: "Among his kalams, the most
  popular is probably the one in Sindhi that was published in India, although he did not wish his poetical
  work to be published." (p. 118, folio 105)
- **Print as the engine of Sufism's objectification, with three dated phases and a named infrastructure.**
  **bhagnari-mandir**, **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge**, **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi**, **shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi**, **shri-varun-dev-mandir**, **shrine-of-abdullah-shah-ghazi**, **shrine-of-pir-mangho**, **shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya** (the book's "Karachi") and **guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat** (the book's "Hyderabad"): "In Sindh, a printing press was brought to Karachi in the 1850s, but the main event in relation to Sufism came with the printing in 1866 of the Shah jo Risalo… The second phase was that of the rationalization of the poetry through the implementation of an analysis of the technical lexicon, initiated by Hindu literati in the 1890s, and the third phase came when the Sindhi literati took interest in the Sufi poet's life rather than the poetry alone." (p. 48, folio 35) **The third phase is not dated: (not stated).** The infrastructure: the Sindh Sahitiyya Society, established 1914; Blavatsky Printing, founded in Hyderabad (p. 48, folio 35); the Sindh Times, "the official journal of the Sindh Sabha", edited by Hiranand Shahani (p. 46, folio 33). Further dated tools: first glossary **1890**, Lilaram Watanmal Lalwani's *The Life, Religion, and Poetry of Shah Latif*, in English; Mirza Qalich Beg's Sindhi lexicon **1913**, whose "final aim was to 'democratize' the reading of Shah jo Risalo among Sindhis of all classes" (p. 100, folio 87); a Sindhi-English dictionary of **1879** (Shirt 1879: 865) (p. 99, folio 86); Sindilo's glossary of Sachal **1984**; a Karachi edition of Sami **2002** with Sindilo's glossary (p. 94, folio 81) — **bhit-bhit-shah** and **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast**.
- **Named imprints and editions from the Notes.** "the two lithographed editions of the same treatise in
  its Sindhi version in **Bombay at the beginning of the twentieth century** (Baloch 1992: 102–3)"
  (p. 209, folio 196, Ch. 1 n. 4); "This book was **reprinted in 1963 by the Sindhi Sahit Sangat,
  Bombay**." (p. 209, folio 196, Ch. 1 n. 8) — **which book is meant is not restated in the note:
  (not stated)**; "A **double CD was published in 2006**, with the title **Sufyani Chownki ain pallaw**,
  **with no listed location or publisher**." (p. 215, folio 202, Ch. 6 n. 3); the Matai thesis "submitted
  in 1972, recently reprinted by the **Institute of Sindhology** (Matai 2003)" (p. 210, folio 197,
  Ch. 1 n. 15). A cultural centre founded, and its later stewards: "In 1969, he had founded the Sita
  Sindhu Bhavan, a culture centre for the promotion of the different artistic sides of the Sindhi
  culture." (p. 157, folio 144) "After his death, the Sita Sindhu Bhagwan [text-layer? Sita Sindhu Bhavan]
  was run by a number of Sindhi 'cultural activists'." (p. 161, folio 148)
- **Publishing as an institutional activity of one path, with dates.** "Molchand was also very active in
  terms of publications. He could have played a leading role in the translation of the Diwan Dastgir, the
  poetry of Abd al-Qadir Jilani, that was officially translated from Persian into Sindhi by Kalyan Advani
  and published in 1979… he published a translation into Sindhi of Farid al-Din Attar's famous Tazkira
  al-awliyya. Furthermore, Kripalani translated Ghazzali's Ilhya ulum al-din [text-layer? Ihya ulum
  al-din], 'The Revival of Religious Sciences'. Both books were translated from an English translation
  (Gajwani 1997: 40–1)." (p. 124, folio 111) The Dil kusha was "translated into Sindhi by Professor D. K.
  Mansharamani and published in Mumbai in 1996. It was distributed for Jogi Sain's birthday, on 15 August
  1996. Later on, in 2013, at the occasion of Sadiq Muhammad's urs, Daulat Keswani distributed an English
  translation from the Sindhi version, which is available online (http://www.mysufi.in)." (p. 121,
  folio 108) "Dil Kusha is totally devoted to the issue of zikr, the Sufi mediation. It is made of 283
  sentences related to zikr." (p. 121, folio 108)
- **A pilgrimage centre rebuilt and extended with found funding, dated across sixteen years.**
  **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed** as the model: "since the dargah was found in a dilapidated
  state, he succeeded in finding funding to rebuild it, including a dome, in 1972. Later on, in 1978,
  Molchand started to build a place for pilgrims to stay close to the dargah. Known as the Sufi Asthan, it
  was completed after his death in 1981." (p. 124, folio 111) **The source of the funding is (not
  stated).**
- **Madrasa provision, quantified — the one dated count of religious institutions in pre-colonial Sindh.**
  "Arabic was taught in the madrasats, which were to form different categories of professional religious
  agents such as qazi, mufti and faqih. Teaching was divided into religious sciences (manqullat) and
  rational sciences (maqallat). At the end of the seventeenth century, there were 400 madrasats in just
  the city of Thattah, which was comparable to the major cities of Iraq (Burton 1851: 341)." (p. 41,
  folio 28) **The book's spellings `madrasats`, `manqullat`, `maqallat` are reproduced as printed.**
- **The 1872 census of Sindh, with its own disclaimer attached and its arithmetic unreconciled.** "Sindh's
  population statistics are not available in the first census of 1872. The population was 2,333,527
  million. Sindh had 1,718,688 Muslims, 393,092 Hindus, 5,034 Christians and 86,663 persons put in a
  category titled 'Other castes such as Sikhs, Parsis, Jews, etc.' (Hughes 1876: 878)." (p. 38, folio 25)
  **The disclaimer and the figures sit in consecutive sentences, the components do not sum to the total,
  and "2,333,527 million" is printed as such; none of the three is emended here.** A second, undated
  share: "Sindh was a largely Muslim-dominated country, making up about 75 per cent of the whole
  population, where the power had been in the hands of Muslims for centuries." (p. 199, folio 186) **No
  date is attached to the 75 per cent and no source is given: (not stated).**
- **The social map of Hindu Sindh, as the British drew it.** "The most numerous Brahmans belonged to the
  caste of Saraswat Brahmans. They accepted food from the Lohanas, for whom they were the spiritual
  guides. The Saraswat ate meat and drank alcohol (Aitken 1907: 182–3). The Lohanas, who represented half
  of the total Hindu population, were specialized in wholesale and retail trade. The British sources
  traditionally divided them into two groups: the Amils, who finally created a new caste… and the
  Bhaibands." (p. 40, folio 27) Traders: "Although trade was dominated by the Hindus, three Muslim groups
  carried out this activity: the Memon, the Khojah and the Bohrah." (p. 39, folio 26). Shikarpur, place-level for **gurdwara-sach-khand-sahib**, **khatwari-darbar-shikarpur**, **sant-bhagat-kanwar-ram-temple-chak**: its baniyas "tended to belong to non-literate groups, and their religious inclination was mostly towards the Nanakpanth" (pp. 30–31, folios 17–18).
- **Untouchable groups, their migration, their trades and where they settled.**
  **umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir** (the book's "Umerkot"): Bhils, Kohlis and "the Menghwar, mostly settled
  around Umerkot. Part of their devotional literature was and still is in Sindhi." "The first census
  reports that many would have migrated from Gujarat or Rajasthan between 1843 and 1872… the majority of
  these untouchables worked in agriculture. There is hardly any trace of untouchables in records of
  crafts. Rumour has it that the Hindu untouchable artisans all converted to Islam, and it is true that
  crafts were largely in the hands of Muslims." (p. 40, folio 27) **The word "Rumour" is the book's own
  hedge.** Boivin also corrects a received view flatly: "Contrary to popular belief, there were many
  untouchables in Sindh." (p. 40, folio 27) **"Menghwars" is spelt so in the General conclusion and
  "Meghwars" in chapter 7; both are reproduced as printed.**
- **Ritual exclusion enforced by caste.** "The Shikaris, for example, although Muslims, were not allowed
  to enter a mosque without submitting to a purification ritual (Burton 1851: 308)." (p. 39, folio 26) And
  among Hindus, untouchables "lived outside of the villages and their members were never permitted to
  enter the houses of the village (Thakur 1959: 70)" (p. 40, folio 27).
- **A pre-partition Hindu ritual office grounded in a caste monopoly on a dye.** **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**:
  "The Khuhnbatis, as saffron specialists, were attributed a priestly function in the ceremony of the
  mystical nuptials of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar." (p. 181, folio 168) "In the traditional marriage of the
  Daryapanthis, one of the preparatory ceremonies of marriage is known as the khuhnbo ceremony (Thakur
  1959: 176). A few days before marriage, members of the caste as well as seven women who are happily
  married (sohagan, which means they are not widows) prepare the saffron colour to dye ceremonial wedding
  clothes." (pp. 181–182, folios 168–169)
- **Renunciation as a pre-partition practice that stopped.** "While in pre-partition Sindh, there are many
  stories where businessmen decided to give up all of their worldly goods and activities to become
  renunciants, and not only in relation with Sufism, **I have never heard such a story in independent
  India.**" (p. 205, folio 192)
- **The pir's economy of followers, stated as a goal that is not conversion.** "Be they Ismaili or
  Sohrawardi, the agents' goals were similar: to gain followers of a pir rather than to convert
  individuals to Islam." (p. 23, folio 10) **The spelling `Sohrawardi` is the book's.**

### The state and communal politics

- **State control of a shrine, dated to the 1960s, with the department named seven pages later and never
  joined to it.** **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar**: "the shrine itself came under
  government control in the 1960s" (p. 180, folio 167); and "It proceeds according to the document of the
  Awqaf manager in relation to the procession of the matamis." (p. 183, folio 170) **The two statements
  are never joined by the book, and no department structure, officer, revenue or property is named:
  (not stated).**
- **A ritual protocol imposed by the State, and unchanged by Partition but for one custom.**
  **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**: "These positions, be they honorary or real, can be watched during the
  implementation of a protocol imposed by the State. This protocol, as well as the distribution of tasks
  between confessions, has not undergone fundamental change since the partition, with one exception."
  (p. 181, folio 168)
- **The only named piece of shrine-management legislation in the book, and it is Indian.** "On the Sikh
