  Suvorova 1999; Green 2012; Briggs 1938 (the Nathpanthis); Khan 1997 (Dominique-Sila Khan, on the Ismaili
  Shias in the "encountering" of Islam and Hinduism); Baloch 1992: 102–3; Jatt 2016; Gajwani 2000; Khuhro
  (2000); Daswani 2017 (**Raj Daswani's *Shattered Sindh Scattered Sindhis***); Ajwani 1970: 52; Allana
  1983; Matlani 2001; Raghavan 1966; Ramwani 2000; Schimmel 1986 and Schimmel 1986: 96–149 and Schimmel
  1974: 21; Shackle 1981 (twice); Kalhoro 2014: 238–44; Beck 2006; Baloch 1959, 1: 41–66; Baloch 1966:
  455–7; Baloch 1959 1: 487–90; Baloch 1959 1: 355; Baloch 1966: 320–3; Baloch 2 1959: 902; Memon 2005:
  450–5; Thakur 1959: 140; Flaskerud : 25; Bouillier and Khan 2009: 571 passim; Eaton 1978: 298–9; Ramey
  2008 and Ramey 2008: 90–4; Paul 1996: 210; Srinivas 2012; Pandia 2014; Khan 2001; Sikand 2003; Al-Huda
  2003; Bruce and Lawrence 2002; Buehler 1999; Dadi Ganga 1988: 22; Ladkani 2000 and Ladkani 2000: 58;
  Faruqi 2002: 35, 94, 120–1 (p. 208–216, folios not stated and 196–203).
- **His own prior work, cited nine times in the Notes**: Boivin 2005 (Muslims of Sindh and their colonial
  classification), Boivin (2013) (de-hinduization and islamization among the Ismaili Shia Khojas), Boivin
  2014 (the glossaries and lexicons on Shah Abdul Latif), Boivin 2015 (the silsila chart), Boivin 2012
  (Sehwan Sharif), Boivin 2016: 12–29 (phases of vernacularization), **Boivin and Delage 2016** (shrines
  and spaces in devotional Islam), **Boivin and Rajpal 2018** (the darbar of Udero Lal) (pp. 208–216,
  folios not stated and 196–203). **Boivin and Rajpal 2018 is dated after the book's own stated present of
  2017.** The Shah Abdul Latif and Sehwan and Udero Lal items attach to
  **bhit-bhit-shah**, **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar**, **shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan** respectively.
- **The index as a concordance to his own analytical vocabulary** (locators are the index's own folio
  runs): "mystical space 73–4, 117–18, 141"; "physical mystical space 73, 117–18, 141"; "equivalence, in
  encounter of religions 9, 151"; "indigenization 15"; "Islamicate 3, 37, 195 n.3"; "Sindhicate area 3, 6,
  11, 13"; "episteme of Sindh 39"; "objectification of Sufism 30, 34–7, 142"; "hinduization process 11,
  13, 20, 147, 157, 160, 162, 163, 188, 189, 191, 202–3 n.6"; "syncretism 11, 16"; "symbolic mystical
  space 73"; "sociotextual community 5"; "vernacularization, process of 4, 10, 15, 24, 86, 87, 88, 96, 195
  n.5"; "translation 15"; "worship, categories of 37–8"; and "**legacy, Sufi** 2, 17, 18–21, 30, 85, 108,
  116, 140, 141–61, 162, 164, 166, 173–4, 180–1, 185, 188, 190–1, 202 n.4" — **the longest single
  subentry run in the index** — with "diaspora role in transmission 157–60, 195 n.2"; "non-poetic chains
  for transmission 149–56"; "other networks of transmission 156–7"; "Panjwani, Ram and 142–9";
  "post-partition transmission, challenge of 142–9" (pp. 239–252, folios not stated and 227–239). The
  index's fourfold division of `Sufi corpus` is "Bhakti 74–7"; "classical 77–82, 152"; "modern 78, 82–6";
  "Vedanta 74–7" (p. 250, folio 237) — **"Bhakti" and "Vedanta" carry the identical run 74–7**, and
  `Sufism` separately carries "Bhakti corpus of 74–7" and "Vedanta corpus of 74–7" (pp. 250–251, folios
  237–238), four entries on one pair of folios.

## Figures and sites not in the archive

Compact list of what ruling (a) and the person rule did **not** map, with page references exactly as the
Pass-1 notes give them. Useful for future archive rows. Nothing mapped above is repeated here.

### The three Hindu Sufi protagonists and their households

- **Rai Rochaldas / Rochaldas Mansharamani / Sain Rochaldas / Dr Rochaldas** — the Hindu Sufi whose
  Ulhasnagar darbar is the book's principal Indian site; dates printed "(1879–1957)", born in Rohri,
  died "In November of 1957… He died on 10 December" — **the two statements do not agree and are not
  reconciled** — pp. 14–15, 55–58, 70, 78, 133–135, 140–141, 145, 165–166, 176, 201, 209, 214, 227
  (folios 2, 42–45, 57, 65, 120–122, 127–128, 132, 152–153, 196, 201, 214, and not stated).
- **Mulchand Faqir / Mulchand / Mulan Faqir / Mulchand Navani** — the Sehwan Hindu Sufi who never
  migrated; dates printed "(1883–1962)"; born to Parumal Navani; studied at the Hiranand Academy,
  Hyderabad; married "a woman named Suratbhai"; birth anniversary "5 April" and "6 April" on different
  pages, death anniversary "20 November", both undated by year — pp. 55, 58–59, 64, 71, 75, 77, 79, 82,
  113, 125–128, 131, 134–135, 144, 168, 180, 183, 201 (folios 42, 45–46, 51, 58, 62, 64, 66, 69, 100,
  112–115, 118, 121–122, 131, 155, 167, 170, 188).
- **Nimano Faqir / Ruqi Bhai** — the one woman among the three, of Shikarpur; dates printed
  "(1888–1963)" and "(1880–1963)"; **death given as "23 March 1963" and as "1962" on the same page**;
  founder of the Sakhi Kutiyya, Baroda, in 1947; **the book uses masculine pronouns at pp. 131–132 and
  feminine at p. 135 for the same person** — pp. 20, 55, 60, 64, 71–72, 74, 101, 109, 114–115, 131–132,
  135, 152, 169, 172, 201–202, 204–205, 220, 225, 227 (folios 7, 42, 47, 51, 58–59, 61, 88, 96, 101–102,
  118–119, 122, 139, 156, 159, 188–189, 191–192, 207, 212, 214).
- **Gehimal Motwani / Gehimal Ainani (1904–71)** — Mulchand's main disciple and the actual founder of
  the Mulchand shrines; "deputy collector" at p. 71 and "mukhthyarkar (revenue officer)" at p. 75, **the
  two offices not reconciled**; death anniversary 29 October — pp. 65, 71, 75, 82–83, 98, 113–114, 128,
  134–135, 149, 167, 201, 226 (folios 52, 58, 62, 69–70, 85, 100–101, 115, 121–122, 136, 154, 188, 213).
- **Hari / R. M. Hari / Dada Sai (Dr) R. M. Hari (1912–80)** — Rochaldas's son and successor, compiler
  of the malfuzat; **the book uses the name "Hari" for at least two men** — pp. 57–58, 70, 73, 115, 137,
  139, 147–148, 162–164, 165–166, 174 (folios 44–45, 57, 60, 102, 124, 126, 134–135, 149–151, 152–153,
  161).
- **H. M. Damodar** — Rochaldas's grandson and present gaddi nashin; led the mehndi after Gandaram —
  pp. 73, 147, 149, 162–163 (folios 60, 134, 136, 149–150).
- **Pritam / Pritam Das / Pritamdas (d. 2015)** — Rochaldas's younger son, ran the medical wing —
  pp. 11, 116, 148–149 (folios 103, 135–136, and not stated).
- **Dadi Dhan Samtani (1922–2014)** — co-author of Rochaldas's poetry, born in Sukkur, M.A. Philosophy,
  taught at the National College, Bandra; **her flat is "Mahim" at p. 148 and "Mulund" at p. 127** —
  pp. 11, 98, 109, 115, 127–128, 148–149, 163, 224 (folios 85, 96, 102, 114–115, 135–136, 150, 211, and
  not stated).
- **Dadi Ganga / Dadi Ganga Devi Jaghtiyyani** — co-author of Rochaldas's poetry; mother-in-law of
  L. K. Advani; believed "**Dr Rochaldas… visited her in sleep and made her write these poems**" —
  pp. 13, 98, 109, 163, 224 (folios 85, 96, 150, 211, and not stated).
- **Kanungos: Gandaram (d. 2008), Murlidhar, Lachhman**, and their father, who died in 1948 — pp. 58,
  147, 183, 201 (folios 45, 134, 170, 188).
- **Basant Jethwani and Sonu Jethwani** — funders of the Haridwar darbar, "Everything had been recorded
  under Sonu's name" — pp. 11, 77, 79, 84, 134–135, 143 (folios 64, 66, 71, 121–122, 130, and not
  stated).
- **Daulatram Motwani** — **"Gehimal Motwani's nephew" at p. 128 against "Gehimal Motwani, Daulatram's
  father" at p. 114; the two are not reconciled** — pp. 11, 114, 128, 144, 149–150 (folios 101, 115,
  131, 136–137).
- **Soni Kataria, Kamla, Murli, Adi Ama, Devi Naik, Gopi Gurbuxani, Tirthdas/Tirathdas Motwani,
  Daulatram Chaudhry, Shri Vasumal, Champra (Ramchand's sister), Nirmal Jani, Pushpa Tilly Jindani,
  Jivat Bhai (d. 1979), Kiku Motwani Bhalla, Manu Bhatia, Lachhman Bhatia, Mukund Bhatia, Manju and
  Kamal Mirchandani, Shoba Vaswani** — the lay personnel of the Indian paths — pp. 11, 69, 74, 79, 84,
  114, 126, 135, 142–143, 152, 156, 169, 172–174, 201, 217 (folios 61, 66, 71, 101, 113, 122, 129–130,
  139, 156, 159–161, 188, 204, and not stated).

### Muslim Sufi figures of Sindh with no archive row

- **Qutub Ali Shah / Sayyid Qutub Ali Shah / "Kutub Shah" (1810–1910)** — the book's fullest modern Sufi
  with a named shrine: "**His dargah is located in Jahaniyya, a neighbourhood in Hyderabad**"; a wujudi,
  Rochaldas's murshid, considered "the pole (qutub) of his time"; he has an ʿurs and a sajjada nashin who
  travelled to India in 1957 and 1980. **A named shrine with a named principal figure matching no row —
  the strongest candidate in this book for a new archive row.** pp. 50, 56–58, 95–96, 98, 106, 120, 130,
  133–138, 141, 149–150, 155, 158, 167, 172, 185, 187, 198, 200, 210, 223 (folios 37, 43–45, 82–83, 85,
  93, 107, 120, 124–126, 128, 136–137, 142, 145, 154, 159, 185, 187, 197, 210, and not stated).
- **Roshan Ali Shah (1852–1932)** — Qutub Ali Shah's son; composer of the dua/munajat and of "Sakhi
  sabajhal" — pp. 147, 149–150, 155, 158, 167, 172, 215 (folios 134, 136–137, 142, 145, 154, 159, 202).
- **Hadi Bakhsh** — "the son and successor of Qutub", author of a highly prized kalam — pp. 57, 98, 133,
  166–167 (folios 44, 85, 120, 153–154).
- **Nur Husayn Shah** — sajjada nashin of Jahaniyya, "still the Master of the silsilo"; performed the
  dastarbandi for Hari in 1957 and Damodar in 1980 — pp. 73–74, 133 (folios 60–61, 120).
- **Nur al-Din, sajjada nashin of Tando Jahaniyyan** — named at p. 145 (folio 132), unnamed at p. 112
  (folio not stated).
- **Bedil / Faqir Qadir Bakhsh (1814–72)** — "a Sufi born in Rohri, belonging to the **Qadiriyya**";
  eighteen works; author of the Persian *Dil kusha*; advocate of zikr khafi — pp. 68, 82, 97–98, 121,
  135, 142–143, 145, 150, 153, 155–156, 158, 166, 168, 185, 199, 212 (folios 55, 69, 84–85, 108, 122,
  129–130, 132, 137, 140, 142–143, 145, 153, 155, 185, 199).
- **Rakhiyyal Shah** — the Muslim murshid of Mulchand; **his identity with the archive's "Syed Rakhyal
  Shah" is treated as established in the Pass-1 mapping and set out in that subsection above; the four
  further mentions at pp. 75, 101, 137–138 and 227 (folios 62, 88, 124–125, 214) are recorded there.**
- **Nasir Muhammad Faqir al-Qadiri / "Nasir Faqir" (1835–1960), also "(d. 1960)"** — born and buried in
  **Jalalani Sharif**, "a small dargah located in the village where he was born"; initiated at Jhok
  Sharif; a Qadiri; **the printed span 1835–1960 is reproduced exactly** — pp. 27, 101, 120–122, 141,
  149 (folios 14, 88, 107–109, 128, 136).
- **Sain Oshaq Ali Faqir** — "the Pakistani sajjada nashin" of the Jalalani tariqa, who toured India in
  2012 — p. 122 (folio 109).
- **Rohal Faqir (1734–1804)** — founder of the **Kandri school**; resigned as Kalhora Minister of
  Finances; disciple of Shah Inayat of Jhok Sharif; **Schimmel "wrongly claimed" the Kandri poets were
  all his sons** — pp. 107–108, 213 (folios 94–95, 200).
- **Murad Faqir (1743–96)**, **Darya Khan (1775–1850)**, **Ghulam Ali**, **Shahu Faqir**, **Kamal Faqir**
  (of Nawsharo Firoz) — the rest of the Kandri school — pp. 107–108, 155, 168, 213, 216 (folios 94–95,
  142, 155, 200, 203).
- **Dalpat Sufi (d. 1841)** — born in Sehwan Sharif in the Mirani family; Talpur officer turned
  renunciant; his samadhi is in Colaba, Mumbai; author of *Sufi so jo sachu pachhani* / *Sufi so jo
  panchhani*; **the Annex 2 chart places him under both "Sehwan Sharif (Qalandariyya)" and "Jhok Sharif
  (Qadiriyya)"** — pp. 13, 20, 55, 77, 96, 98, 105, 117–119, 128, 137, 155, 209, 221–222, 227 (folios 7,
  42, 54, 64, 83, 85, 92, 104–106, 115, 124, 142, 196, 209, 214, and not stated).
- **Sakhi Qabul Muhammad II / "Sakhi Darazi" (1842–1925)**, **Sakhi Qabul Muhammad VI**, **Sakhi Qabul
  VIII**, **Sakhi Qabul Muhammad Faruqi**, **Khwaja Abd al-Haqq II (1915–78)**, **Khwaja Muḥammad Ḥafiẓ
  (Sahiḅ Dino I)**, **Khwaja Abd al-Ḥaqq** — the masters of Daraza; **recorded here as persons because
  the book gives none of them a shrine of his own; their offices are in the `shrine-of-sachal-sarmast`
  subsection above.**
- **Shah Inat (1623–1712) / Sayyid Inayat Allah Rizvi** — "probably a **Shia Muslim, initiated in the
  Qadiriyya**", G. Allana's fourth classical poet; **a third Shah Inayat, distinct in the book's own
  treatment from Sufi Shah Inayat of Jhok Sharif** — pp. 105, 211 (folios 92, 198).
- **Sayyid Shah Mehmud Shah al-Qadiri** — Shah Inayat's Master, buried at Bijapur; his dargah rebuilt
  "including a dome, **in 1972**" — p. 124 (folio 111).
- **Abd al-Sattar Shah / Sufi Ataullah Sattari / Sufi Hazrat Abdul Sattar Shah** — **recorded in the
  `dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed` subsection above; the three forms are not reconciled by the
  book.**
- **Mohsin Shah** — gaddi nashin of Tando Saindad near Tando Muhammad Khan, "affiliated to the
  **Qalandariyya**" — p. 123 (folio 110).
- **Makhdum Muhammad Hashim (1692–1762)** — qazi in Thatta, Naqshbandi of Lowari Sharif, author of the
  *Fara'iz al-islam* (translated into Sindhi 1731) — pp. 22, 41 (folios 9, 28); indexed "Hashim, Makhdum
  Muhammad 9, 28". **Distinct from the archive's three Makhdum rows.**
- **Mian Abu'l Hasan (1711)** — Naqshbandi, author of the *Muqaddimat al-Salat*; **the bare "(1711)" is
  not labelled as a birth, death or composition date: (not stated)** — p. 41 (folio 28).
- **Kojhi / Muhammad Bakhsh Shaikh (1891–1959)**, **Ajiz or Aijaz / Akhund Haji Faqir Muhammad Aijaz
  (1846–1918)**, **Qazi Qazan** (whose is "the oldest piece of Sindhi literature"), **Bekas**, **Bewas**,
  **Shaykh Ayaz**, **Rohal Shah**, **Sami / Chainrai Bachomal Dattaramani (1743–1850)** — Sindhi poets
  with no shrine attached — pp. 68, 82, 94–95, 104, 155–156, 161, 166, 171, 179, 212, 216 (folios 55, 69,
  81–82, 91, 142–143, 148, 153, 158, 166, 199, 203).

### Sindhi and Pakistani sites with no archive row

- **Tando Jahaniya / Tando Jahaniyya / Tando Jahaniyyan, Hyderabad** — one of the three Sindh seats with
  a sajjada nashin and an attached poet; the seat of the Jahaniyya silsila; **must not be confused with
  the archive's Uch Sharif Jahaniyan row** — pp. 27, 60, 112, 145, 251 (folios 14, 47, 132, 238, and not
  stated).
- **The 'Hindu dargah' of Tando Ahmad Khan / Tando Ahmed Khan** — a Lohana-built Sufi shrine with a
  golden dome hosting Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's tomb; **no founding date, no builder's name, no custodian**
  — pp. 9–10, 123, 179, 184–186, 193–194, 241, 251 (folios 110, 166, 171–174, 180–181, 228, 238, and not
  stated).
- **The temples and the gurdwara at Tando Ahmad Khan** — "a compound with several Shivaite temples…
  another temple devoted to **Raja Vir Vikramaditya**… about 200 metres from it, there is a
  **gurdwara**"; **none named, dated or given a custodian** — pp. 184–185 (folios 171–172).
- **Thano Bula Khan** — in the Kohistan, Jamshoro district, seat of a taluka, "rich in minerals", with
  "beautiful **Burgat tombs** in the vicinity"; **"Burgat" is not glossed, not dated, and the spelling is
  unfamiliar** — p. 184 (folio 171).
- **Khwaja Khizr / Zinda Pir, "on an island"** — a sanctuary of the Rohri–Sukkur triangle from which the
  Hindus "had been expelled"; **the island sanctuary is not named and the expelling party is (not
  stated)** — pp. 31, 54, 244 (folios 18, 41, 231).
- **The Halani darbar, between Nausharo Firuz and Khairpur** — "about 40 kilometres south of Daraza",
  created by **Baba Sukhdev Sahib** in **1787**, four years after the Talpurs defeated the Kalhoras in
  1783; reconstructed with a dome in the second half of the nineteenth century; **Baba Sadhuram** is "the
  eighth gaddi nashin", managing three darbars, two in India and one in Pakistan — pp. 54, 68 (folios 41,
  55).
- **Sain Vasan Shah's darbar, Rohri** — "Vasan Shah's darbar is located in Ulhasnagar, while another one
  is in Rohri, on the bank of the Indus River"; Vasan Shah (1847–1928 at p. 68, **1847–1927 at p. 209 —
  the two spans do not agree**), a follower of **Sai Parul Shah (1820–90)** and of **Paru Shah**; his
  present gaddi nashin is "the fourth descendant… 'Hazra Hazoor Darvish Sai Parmanand Sahab'" — pp. 55,
  68, 70, 78, 133, 143, 150, 209 (folios 42, 55, 57, 65, 120, 130, 137, 196).
- **Isarlal's shrine, Rohri** — Isarlal / Isar Lal / Ishwar Lal (1835–95), a disciple of Sakhi Qabul
  Muhammad II; his ivory dice and green cloth kept as touch-relics by the Bhatia family; **"there was one
  [a place of worship], but after the departure of the main followers, nobody was able to take care of
  it" at p. 169 against "Bhagwan Advani and Isar Lal have no samadhi" at p. 170** — pp. 150, 169–170,
  172, 174 (folios 137, 156–157, 159, 161).
- **Dalpat Sufi's astano, Sipahimalani Lane, Hyderabad, later "Guru Sehwani's place"** — **recorded in
  the Hyderabad place-level bullet above; named here because it is a site matching no row and a candidate
  for one.**
- **Pithoro Pir's darbar, the hamlet of Pithoro** — a Sufi tomb "surmounted by a huge Om", managed by
  Hindu Rajputs claiming descent from Pithoro's brother **Bhanwarji**, whose tomb is in a small temple
  below; an **altar devoted to Shiva in the courtyard**; an **annual fair in September**; the twenty-second
  pir in line is **Hathesingh**, **whose caste is given three incompatible ways (Thakur/Sodha Rajput,
  Meghwar per the Bhuj pujari, and Burton's version)**; the priestly **Pithoro Pota Pirs** officiate in
  village altars — pp. 10, 43, 51, 178–179, 187–192, 233, 247 (folios 30, 38, 165–166, 174–179, 220,
  234, and not stated). **A strong candidate for a future archive row; the near-miss with
  `shrine-of-makhdoom-abdul-rahim-girhori` is a location-string match only and is flagged above.**
- **Bodlo Bahar's shrine, Sehwan** — "the main follower of Lal Shahbaz who has a shrine nearby"; his
  faqirs perform a monthly whirling dance in red skirts; **no row in the 169 carries him** — pp. 140, 240
  (folios 127, 227). **A candidate for a future archive row.**
- **The kafi of Lal Das, Sehwan** — "another Hindu Sufi lodge… the gaddi nashin's spiritual duty is
  similar to any other sajjada nashins in Sehwan" — pp. 127, 180 (folios 114, 167).
- **The kafi of Doda Mard Haqani, Sehwan** — a lodge devoted to an alleged disciple of Lal Shahbaz
  Qalandar, adjoining Mulchand Kafi — pp. 122, 180, 242 (folios 109, 167, 229).
- **Mulchand's qabr and Mulchand Kafi, Sehwan** — "a cubic building composed of a single room. In its
  centre is a brick construction that is the **qabr** (tomb) of Mulchand Faqir. This construction is four
  or five times the size of an ordinary grave. No decoration or inscription appears, but it is covered,
  as is the tradition, with a **chador**. Additionally, **the kafi shelters the cremation ground of the
  Hindus of Sehwan**." — pp. 75, 79, 126, 180, 193 (folios 62, 66, 113, 167, 180).
- **Jhulelal's temple, north-western Sehwan**, and **the Shiva temple, north-eastern Sehwan** — the two
  pre-partition Hindu temples of the town; **neither named, dated or said to survive** — pp. 71, 179–180
  (folios 58, 166–167).
- **Kandri / Kandra, Rohri taluqa, Khairpur district** — **two spellings in two sentences**; Rohal
  Faqir's place of renunciation — p. 107 (folio 94).
- **Jalalani / Jalalani Sharif** — Nasir Muhammad Faqir's birth and burial village, with its small dargah
  — pp. 120–121 (folios 107–108).
- **Bubak and the Manchhar Lake** — "a village near the Manchhar Lake and not far from Sehwan", where
  Dalpat met the guru **Asardas** — p. 118 (folio 105).
- **Jhangara** — "a village located south-west of Sehwan, very close to the Manchhar Lake" — p. 75
  (folio 62).
- **Sri Chand Darbar, Thatta** — named only inside a study title, "Jatt, Zahida Rehman, 'Heterodox
  Traditions of Udasipanth in Sindh: Case Study of Sri Chand Darbar, Thatta'… 2016, pp. 975–97" (p. 235,
  folio 222). **Thatta / Thatha / Thattah has no archive row at all**; the book also gives "At the end of
  the seventeenth century, there were 400 madrasats in just the city of **Thattah**" (p. 41, folio 28)
  and the 1367 siege of "**Thatha**" (p. 189, folio 176).
- **Bharchundi** — "the pir of Bharchundi" and his accused ancestor — p. 216 (folio 203).
- **Toponyms with no archive row, recorded so the check is visibly complete** — Jacobabad (pp. 59, 244;
  folios 46, 231), Mirpur Khas, Nawab Shah, Nausharo Firuz / Nawsharo Firoz, Sonda, Kohistan,
  Baluchistan, the Kachhi plain, Halim Shah and Tehsil Mirpur, Tehsil Khandwa, Tando Saindad, Tando Adam,
  Hingorja, the Indus delta, the Thar Desert, Surat, Bhuj, Kutch/Kuch, and Hyderabad Deccan (pp. 22, 33,
  39–40, 42–43, 46–47, 59, 68, 104, 122, 169, 178–179, 184, 189, 210, 216).

### Indian and overseas sites — the whole diaspora half of the book, none of it in the archive

- **The darbar of Rochaldas, Shantinagar / "Shantinargar [text-layer? — possibly `Shantinagar`]",
  Ulhasnagar** — founded "**In August of 1949**" on emptied Second World War barracks in "a suburb of
  Kalyan, located around 60 kilometres from Bombay"; undecorated but for Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's pole and
  alam; the **only darbar in the book where the charge of gaddi nashin is hereditary**; **presently
  perhaps the only place where a dhamal is performed and an alam exhibited** — pp. 14–15, 57–58, 80–81,
  133–135, 140–141, 145, 165, 176, 201, 205, 209 (folios 2, 44–45, 67–68, 120–122, 127–128, 132, 152,
  188, 192, 196, and not stated).
- **Ulhasnagar (Maharashtra)** and its **camp 5**, **Maqbul Manzil** (the Radha Krishin Lal darbar, built
  in the 1970s), **Rohani Rah / Sachal Sain darbar**, **Mul Kuthiyya / Mul Kutiyya** (closed and then
  destroyed), **Daulatram Motwani's basement samadhi** — pp. 68, 83, 114, 128, 142–143, 150–152, 204,
  209–210 (folios 55, 70, 101, 115, 129–130, 137–139, 191, 196–197).
- **The Sakhi Kuthiyya, Baroda / Vadodara, renamed the Nimanal Sangam in 1999**, and its **Mumbai
  extension**, with overseas extensions in **Hong Kong** and **"Malaga, Spain"** — pp. 60, 72, 74, 78–79,
  114, 117, 132, 135, 142, 172, 204 (folios 47, 59, 61, 65–66, 101, 104, 119, 122, 129, 159, 191).
- **The Dada Sain Kuthir / Dada Sain Kuthur, Haridwar** — Mulchand's and Gehimal's darbar, "**the
  classic Hindu pilgrimage city**", funded by the Jethwanis — pp. 76–79, 113–114, 132, 134, 143, 176, 201
  (folios 63–66, 100–101, 119, 121, 130, 188, and not stated).
- **The Sufi mandir, Sion, Mumbai**, and **Molchand Kripalani's samadhi** — Molchand Kripalani
  (1887–1980), born in Hyderabad, follower of Mohsin Shah, attended the ʿurs of **Muinuddin Chishti
  (1142–1236) at Ajmer Sharif** every year; **the book states its own convention: "I pronounce his name
  as Molchand, for him not to be confused with Mulchand Faqir", yet the Annex 2 chart prints "Mulchand
  Kripalani" and "Molchand Kripalani" on the same page** — pp. 123, 128, 132, 186, 202, 214, 227, 250

- **The Sufi Dar, Chennai**, with the **Darvesh Asthan, Delhi** and the **Dukh Bhanjan Darbar,

- **Jogi Sain's samadhi, Powai, Mumbai** — of **Parsuram Keswani, "Jogi Sain" (1898–1998)**, disciple of
  Nasir Muhammad Faqir; his son **Daulat Keswani** received the nam in 1958 — pp. 120–123, 132 (folios
  107–110, 119).
- **Dalpat Sufi's samadhi, Colaba, Mumbai** — kept by **Dhuru Sipahimalani ("Dhuru Sain")**, "the fourth

- **The Halani clinic, Sion**, **Dadi Dhan's flat (Mahim / Mulund)**, **puja rooms in Ulhasnagar, Bombay

- **The Sufi Asthan / Abd al-Malik's dargah, Bijapur, Karnataka** — claimed by Jhok Sharif's website as

### Scholars, colonial figures, Theosophists and reformers

- **Jethmal Parsram (1886–1947)** — the Sindhi literatus whose *Sind and its Sufis* (1924) was "the first
  book devoted to Sufism of Sindh ever published in English"; founder of the Sindh Sahitiyya Society
  (1914) and of Blavatsky Printing; co-author with **Lilaram Premchand** of *Sufi sagora* (1921) —
  pp. 48–50, 155, 170, 249 (folios 35–37, 142, 157, 236).
- **Ram Panjwani (1911–87)** — born in Larkana, D. J. Sindh College Karachi, Jai Hind College and Bombay
  University, founder of the Sita Sindhu Bhavan (1969), twenty-five books; **"he was very successful in
  imposing Jhulelal as the Sindhi god of India par excellence"** — pp. 9, 33, 68, 144, 155–161, 170,
  173–174, 215 (folios 20, 55, 131, 142–148, 157, 160–161, 202, and not stated).
- **N. J. Narsain / S. J. Narsain** — the 1932 Bombay dissertation, **the earliest study the book names
  on its own topic**; the book calls Narsain "her" — pp. 27, 44–45, 54–55, 63, 135, 198 (folios 14,
  31–32, 41–42, 50, 122, 185).
- **Helena Blavatsky (1831–91)** and **Annie Besant (1847–1933)**, known in Sindh as **Basanti Devi** —

- **Sadhu T. L. Vaswani (1879–1966)**, **J. P. Vaswani**, **Ranjit Premchand Vaswani**, **Gurdial Mallik

### Classical Sufi, Bhakti and religious figures with no archive row

- **Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani / Pir Data Dastgir (d. 1192)** — founder of the Qadiriyya, "the most venerated
  Sufi all over South Asia"; his death anniversary **Yarhen, the 11th of Rabi al-Sani** — pp. 97, 115,
  121–124, 129, 132–133, 157, 170, 218, 239, 247 (folios 84, 102, 108–111, 116, 119–120, 144, 157, 234,
  and not stated). **Indexed with the cross-reference "Pir Data Dastgir *see* al-Jilani, Abd al-Qadir";
  he is NOT the archive's Data Ganj Bakhsh / Hujwiri, who is never named in this book.**
- **Mansur Hallaj (857–922)** — **"beheaded in Baghdad in the year 922. He visited Sindh in 905" in the
  glossary against "finally hanged in Bagdad [text-layer? Baghdad]" in the endnote; the two accounts do
  not agree** — pp. 94, 102, 136, 166, 212, 220 (folios 81, 89, 123, 153, 199, 207).
- **Jalal al-Din Rumi (1207–73), also "(d. 1275)"** — **two death years in one book** — pp. 48, 92, 102,
  140, 159, 170, 211 (folios 35, 79, 89, 127, 146, 157, 198).
- **Ibn al-Arabi (born Murcia 1165, died Damascus 1240)** and **Sadr al-Din al-Qonyawi**; **al-Kindi

  **Nasir-e Khusraw (1004–74)**; **Prince Dara Shikoh (1615–59)**; **Husayn Va'iz Kashefi (d. 1505)**;
  **Shah Waliullah**; **Shah Karim al-Husayni (Aga Khan IV)**; **Mahatma Ramchandraji Maharaj
  (1873–1931)** and **Pandit Brahmashankar Mishra (1861–1907)**, the Hindu Naqshbandis — pp. 24–26, 41–42,
  89, 99–102, 108, 111, 121, 124, 157, 199, 212 (folios 11–13, 28–29, 76, 86–89, 95, 108, 111, 144, 186,
  199).
- **Kabir (1440–1518)**, **Mirabai (1498–1547)**, **Tulsidas**, **Surdas**, **Dadu Dayal**, **Jaya Deva
  (twelfth century)**, **Sankara**, **Vallabhacharya (1479–1531)**, **Ramakrishna / Ramakhrishna
  (1836–86)** — **the book spells the last name two ways on the same pages** — **Vivekananda (1863–1902
  at p. 45, 1863–1903 at p. 102 — the two spans do not agree)**, **Keshub Chandra Sen (1838–84)**,
  **Patanjali (second century BC)**, **Shirdi Sai Baba (1838–1918) / "Baba Sain of Shirdi"**, **Shri
  Sathya Sai Baba (1926–2011)**, **Swami Meghraj**, **Shri Chand** — pp. 45, 53, 88–91, 102–103, 134,

- **Pabuji**, **Guga Pir / Goga**, **Khetrapal**, **Bhavani**, **Sitala**, **Durga / Devi**, **"Mata"**
  (unidentified), **Puran Bhagat**, **Raja Vir Vikramaditya**, **Rai Pithora / Prithvi Raj**, **Muin
  al-Din Tchishti**, **Amir Khwurd**, **Hamir Sodho / "Hamir Rano"**, **Parmar Sodha**, **Madan**,
  **Bijal and Rao Diach**, **the Shidi general at the battle of Dabbo** (unnamed), **Muhammad
  al-Hanafiyya**, **Akbar (1542–1605)**, **Aurangzeb**, **Fath Ali Shah (d. 1824)**, **Muhammad Shah
  Tughluq**, **Firuz Shah Tughluq**, **Muhammad bin Tughluq**, **Jam Juna I and II Samma**, **Pir

## Citable passages

*Fifteen, chosen from the sixty-five candidates the thirteen chunks nominated. Page references are
exactly as the source notes give them; no flag has been removed and none added. **The book prints no
Sindhi, Urdu, Arabic, Persian, Devanagari or Gurmukhi script anywhere in the running text** — chunk
008 states this outright for its range and chunks 002, 003 and 005 measure the same negative — so the
protocol's "original script plus gloss" requirement is **(not stated)** for every quotation below.
The single page of Sindhi script in the whole book is p. 6, which is damaged; it is under Doubts and
is not quotable.*

- "the saint is denominated Lal Shahbaz Qalandar by Muslims and Raja Bharthari (or Raja Vir) by
  Hindus" (p. 50, folio 37) — the book's clearest single statement of the saint's double identity,
  and the one an entry should quote rather than paraphrase.
- "Very grateful to them, Lal Shahbaz decided they would lead the mehndi ritual after his death, when
  his own urs would be celebrated." (p. 58, folio 45) — the origin legend the two Hindu mehndi
  families cite for their hereditary office at the Sehwan ʿurs.
- "the shrine itself came under government control in the 1960s" (p. 180, folio 167) — the book's
  only dating of state control at Sehwan, given in passing as the reason two Sayyid lineages now
  share symbolic power.
- "It proceeds according to the document of the Awqaf manager in relation to the procession of the
  matamis." (p. 183, folio 170) — the book's only naming of the Awqaf and its only statement of what
  governs the mehndi timetable.
- "the Hindus do not exercise this function as Hindus, but as Hindu Sufis" (p. 183, folio 170) — the
  author's own explanation of Hindu ritual office inside a Muslim shrine, in one clause.
- "Sehwan Sharif hosts about twenty kafis, or Sufi lodges, of which two are Hindu." (p. 193,
  folio 180) — the only institutional count for Sehwan anywhere in the book.
- "a huge terrorist attack took place in the darbar of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in Sehwan Sharif on 16
  February 2017" (p. 217, folio 204) — the attack's only dated statement; the book names no target,
  no toll and no perpetrator, so quote it and stop.
- "'Every year, a daughter of the Khonbati caste (saffron flower dyers) was married to the grave'"
  (Burton 1851: 211) (p. 181, folio 168) — the Lal Kunwar custom as Burton reports it; the book adds
  only that it "disappeared during the partition in 1947". Burton's spelling `Khonbati` must be kept
  inside his quotation; the author's own prose on the same page writes `Khuhnbatis`.
- "the inscription written on the main gate of Sachal's dargah is haqq mawjud" (p. 102, folio 89) —
  the only epigraphic fact the book gives for Daraza.
- "Daraza: the name of a town in Northern Sindh where Sachal's dargah is." (p. 218, folio not stated)
  — the glossary's own siting of Sachal's dargah, useful because the body text never says it.
- "Jhok Sharif: a village with an important Sufi Centre in Taluqa Mirpur Bathoro in Southern Sindh
  where Sufi Shah Inayat's dargah is." (p. 219, folio 206) — the **only** place in the book that
  attaches Sufi Shah Inayat to Jhok Sharif; every other Jhok mapping rests on the place alone.
- "the main centre of the Daryapanth is in Udero Lal, the name given to the village which houses a
  vast sanctuary dating back to the seventeenth century" (pp. 42–43, folios 29–30) — the book's only
  dating of the Udero Lal sanctuary.
- "the famous Udasi temple in Sadh Bela which was created thanks to a gift of land" (p. 31, folio 18)
  — the only endowment statement for Sadh Belo, and one of the very few for any site in the book.
- "while Shah Abd al-Latif's poetry was highly praised, his darbar at Bhit Shah is not mentioned as a
  reference point" (pp. 60–61, folios 47–48) — a negative finding about the Bhit Shah darbar's place
  in Hindu Sufi practice, and the one sentence in the book that names both the poet and his site.
- "In India, it is actually the samadhi that replaces the tomb." (p. 131, folio 118) — the book's
  thesis about Partition and ritual space compressed into one line.

## Doubts and review points

### The damage-class inventory, and what the brief's flag counts actually count

**Ruling (b) requires the damage classes to be inventoried once as well as marked inline. This is
that inventory for the whole book.** The route is `text_layer` and the transcription is clean by this
corpus's standards.

**The brief's three counts — 29 `[text-layer?]`, 8 `[OCR?]`, 9 `[illegible…]` — do not count flagged
words, and a human must correct the table before it is used to audit coverage.**

1. **`[OCR?]` and `[illegible]`: the notes say there are none in the source.** Ten of the thirteen
   chunks state, in their own Doubts sections and in as many words, that **no `[OCR?]` and no
   `[illegible]` transcriber flag exists anywhere in this book's source**, the route being
   `text_layer` — chunks 001, 002, 003, 004, 005, 006, 008 ("No transcriber flag was present anywhere
   in chunk_007.txt to carry over"), 009 ("the chunk carries no `[OCR?]`, `[illegible]` or
   `[text-layer?]` marks of its own"), 010 and 011. **The brief's 8 and 9 are therefore occurrences
   of the two strings inside the notes, and most of those occurrences are the negatives themselves.**
   No `[OCR?]` or `[illegible]` flag has been carried into this file, because the notes report none
   to carry.
2. **The 29 does not count `[text-layer?]` flags either.** The per-chunk inventories name far more
   than twenty-nine individual instances between them — chunk 001 names four, chunk 002 three,
   chunk 003 six, chunk 004 five, chunk 005 four, chunk 006 five, chunk 007 seven plus eight fused
   endnote digits, chunk 008 ten, chunk 009 nine, chunk 010 one, chunk 011 about sixteen, chunk 012
   two named plus the numeral class, chunk 013 one. **Every one of those flags is the note-taker's
   own, offered as a reversible reading with the printed form first, not as a correction.** A human
   reconciling the brief's table should count the inline marks in the chunk notes, not the strings.

**What the flags attach to, by class:**

- **Class A — one or two letters lost, dropped or transposed inside a proper name or a technical
  term.** This is the dominant class and it is where the risk sits, because every instance falls on a
  name: `Simon Dibgy` [Digby] (p. 20, folio 7); `'Persaniate'` [Persianate] (p. 16, folio 3);
  `Blavatsly` [Blavatsky] (p. 50, folio 37); `insal kamil` [insan kamil] (p. 56, folio 43);
  `Shantinargar` [Shantinagar] (p. 58, folio 45); `ShahAbd al-Latif` [Shah Abd al-Latif] (p. 91,
  folio 78); `Qtub Ali Shah 1985: 38` [Qutub Ali Shah] (p. 101, folio 88); `Khaipur` [Khairpur]
  (p. 107, folio 94); `Ilhya ulum al-din` [Ihya ulum al-din] (p. 124, folio 111); `Dada Dastgit
  Badhsha` [Dada Dastgir Badshah] (p. 122, folio 109); `Ulhasnagari` [Ulhasnagar] (p. 85, folio 72);
  `Jhulelal Lal` [Jhulelal] (p. 83, folio 70); `dastarband` [dastarbandi] (p. 74, folio 61); `gadi
  nashin` (p. 74, folio 61); `Norther` [Northern] and `clothe` [cloth] (p. 150, folio 137); `ovels`
  [novels] (p. 162, folio 149); `Ulhaasnagar` [Ulhasnagar] (p. 147, folio 134); `Mulcand's`
  [Mulchand's] (p. 149, folio 136); `Panjawni` [Panjwani] (p. 159, folio 146; p. 162, folio 149);
  `Sita Sindhu Bhagwan` [Sita Sindhu Bhavan] (p. 161, folio 148, against "Sita Sindhu Bhavan" at
  p. 157, folio 144); `Paru Chala` [Paru Chawla] (p. 161, folio 148); `ishq majuzi` [ishq majazi]
  (p. 166, folio 153); `Rochaldar's` [Rochaldas's] (p. 176, folio not stated); `The Marchandanis`
  [Mirchandanis] (p. 175, folio 162); `Sindh` [Sindhi] (p. 170, folio 157); `form` [from] (p. 199,
  folio 186); `Shah Ab al-Latif` [Shah Abd al-Latif] (p. 220, folio 207); `Bagdad` [Baghdad] (p. 212,
  folio 199); `technic` [technique] and `Satchidanandra` — **marked with no reading offered** —
  (p. 220, folio 207); `unmentionned` [unmentioned] (p. 214, folio 201); `réplicas` [replicas]
  (p. 210, folio 197, where the acute may be the French-speaking author's and not damage);
  `Lowari Shari` [Lowari Sharif] (p. 228, folio 215); `baniyas, cast` [caste] (p. 240, folio 227).
- **Class B — detached and displaced Arabic/Sindhi diacritics.** The structural case is p. 6, below.
  Elsewhere: `Khwaja Salah ̣al-Din Ḥafiẓ Abd al-Wahhab` [`Khwaja Salaḥ al-Din Ḥafiẓ Abd al-Wahhab`]
  (p. 93, folio 80), where the combining dot-below has detached from the `h` of `Salah` and floats
  before `al-Din` — **this is Sachal Sarmast's birth name, so the flag is load-bearing**;
  `Miyyan Nur Maḥmad Shah` (p. 59, folio 46), **the only dotted character in chunk 003**, with
  `Muḥammad` and `Maḥmūd` both possible and **neither chosen**; `Suf̄i sagura` with a combining macron
  on the `f` (p. 231, folio 218, bibliography; also p. 11, folio not stated as `Suf̄i`). Chunk 013
  records that **no dotted or underdotted characters appear at all** on pp. 250–253, where chunk 012's
  range carried several — whether the printed page carries diacritics there is undetermined and none
  was supplied.
- **Class C — lost or stray word-spaces, hyphens and punctuation.** `Acknowledgementsxi` (p. 12,
  folio not stated), running head and roman folio glued; `wahdat-e- wujud` (p. 94, folio 81);
  `Shah Abd al-Latif 's` / `Latif ’s`, a space before the possessive apostrophe after a descender-less
  `f` (p. 14, folio not stated; p. 48, folio 35; p. 49, folio 36; p. 61, folio 48; p. 160, folio 147);
  `the masters –the ones in India`, a missing space after a dash (p. 32, folio 19); one straight ASCII
  apostrophe among curly ones, "upon his father's death in 1948" (p. 147, folio 134);
  `dukhda kateen tun paan hi .`, a space before the stop (p. 223, folio 210); a lost opening quotation
  mark, `'Tasawwuf or Theosophy'` (p. 251, folio 238); `Kali (goddess ) 91`, a stray space before the
  closing parenthesis, and `Khetrapal, (festival of disciple of Shiva) 30` and `Navratri, (festival of
  goddess Bhavani) 30`, stray commas before the glosses (pp. 244, 246, folios 231, 233).
- **Class D — end-of-line hyphenation and one bare soft hyphen, preserved rather than normalised.**
  `Bibli-ography`, `sacro-saint` (p. 11, folio not stated); `oral-based` (p. 18, folio 5);
  `non-literate` (p. 27, folio 14); `being-in-translation` (p. 28, folio 15); `late-nineteenth-century`
  (p. 88, folio 75); `much-acclaimed` (p. 92, folio 79); `pan-Sindhi` and the one bare soft hyphen,
  `autono­mous` (p. 98, folio 85). Chunk 004 records the opposite finding for pp. 68–87 — **no
  soft-hyphen artefact at all and no word broken across a line** — so the class is not book-wide.
- **Class E — endnote reference numerals fused to the preceding character**, book-wide, with no space
  and no superscript marking. Its dangerous instances have their own sub-heading below.
- **Class F — table, column, chart and figure-caption reflow**, confined to Table 5.1, the Annexes and
  the Index. See the folio and finding-aid sub-headings below.
- **Class G — URLs with inserted intra-URL break characters**: Ch. 4 n. 11 (p. 214, folio 201),
  Ch. 6 n. 1 (p. 215, folio 202) and Ch. 7 n. 3 (p. 216, folio 203) show
  `http:​//www​.dawn​.com/​2011/​…`; the URLs in Ch. 4 n. 1 and Ch. 6 n. 2 are clean. **None was
  repaired.**
- **Class H — italics lost entirely in the Index** (pp. 250–253, folios 237–240), which sets book
  titles and *see* / *see also* in italic; the title entries in the chunk-013 record are reconstructed
  as titles **from context, not from the page's own marking**.

### The Sindhi-script page at p. 6, reproduced verbatim

**This is the one substantial damage locus in the book, and it must never be reconstructed,
transliterated or paraphrased.** Reproduced here exactly as chunk 001 records it:

- **`[text-layer?]` — the Sindhi page, p. 6 (folio not stated).** The page carries only this, reproduced
  exactly as the text layer returns it, over three lines:
  ```
  ُ
  آوڌوت                   ڀاونا ڀ َڳ ُ
        ت ِگيَني ،آچا َر ُج
  ```
  The defect is structural, not a single bad character: **a damma (`ُ`) sits alone on the first line with
  no letter under it; `ڀ َڳ ُ` is broken from the `ت` that opens the third line; and the diacritics inside
  `ِگيَني` and `آچا َر ُج` are detached and displaced.** `[text-layer?]` **[reading of the individual tokens
  with the floating marks set aside: `آوڌوت`, `ڀاونا`, `ڀڳت`, `گياني`, `آچارج`]**. **No reading of the
  line as a whole is offered, no word order is asserted and no translation is given** — the printed
  sequence cannot be recovered from this extraction. **Whether the page is a dedication, an epigraph, a
  Sindhi half-title or something else is (not stated); the page carries no label and no attribution.**
  A human with the PDF page image must settle this; the consolidator must not paraphrase it.

### The series statement contradicts itself between two consecutive pages

Kept side by side, unresolved:

| page | what is printed |
|---|---|
| p. 3 (folio not stated), the series page | `Islamic South Asia Series` |
| p. 5 (folio not stated), the copyright page | `Series: Library of Islamic South Asia` |

**Neither is preferred here and neither is corrected.** Chunk 001 records that the book "gives its own
series two different names, one page apart, and neither is corrected". The assembling coordinator's
`*Source: …*` line must either choose or print both, and a human should check the printed spine or the
publisher's record before it does. Take every other bibliographic detail from the book's own title and
copyright pages, never from general knowledge.

### Flagged names a human must verify

**Transliterated Sindhi and Sindhi-script names are this book's specific risk.** The author states his
refusal of academic transliteration (p. 12, folio not stated), and the result is that the same person,
place, institution or title is printed several ways, sometimes on one page, with no way to tell the
book's inconsistency from an extraction slip. Every form below is reproduced as printed; nothing has
been harmonised.

**Persons — the dangerous pairs first.**

- **`Mulchand` / `Molchand` — the single most dangerous ambiguity in the book.** *Mulchand Faqir of
  Sehwan* is printed "Molchand's tomb (maqbaro)" and "the Mulchand kafi" in consecutive sentences
  (p. 113, folio 100). *Molchand Kripalani (1887–1980)* of the Sion Sufi mandir is a different man
  (p. 123, folio 110), and the book attaches Ch. 4 n. 7 to his name at exactly that point:
  "**I pronounce his name as Molchand, for him not to be confused with Mulchand Faqir**" (p. 214,
  folio 201). **The book then breaks its own rule on one page**: the Annex 2 chart prints "Mulchand
  Kripalani" under `Saindad (Qalandariyya)` and "Molchand Kripalani" under `Jhok Sharif (Qadiriyya)`
  (p. 227, folio 214). **Do not pool "Mulchand Kripalani" with "Mulchand Faqir", and do not read the
  Saindad and Jhok Sharif lines as two people.** Also `Mulcand's` [Mulchand's] (p. 149, folio 136).
- **`Rakhiyyal Shah` / `Rakhyal Shah`.** The book prints "Rakhiyyal Shah" (pp. 75, 101, 137–138,
  folios 62, 88, 124–125), "Sayyid Rakhiyyal Shah" (p. 249, folio 236), "Rakhiyyal Shah al-Qadiri"
  (p. 231, folio 218), "al-Qadiri, Rakhiyyal Shah" (p. 239, folio not stated) and "(via Rakhiyyal
  Shah)" in the silsila chart (p. 227, folio 214); the list of illustrations gives only `(c) Rakhiyyal
  Shah` (p. 10, folio not stated). The archive's `dargah-fateh-pur-sharif` names "Syed Rakhyal Shah",
  d. 1940, Qadiri, at Gandawah, Balochistan. The book prints **`Rakhiyyal Shah (1842–1940)`** at
  p. 101 (folio 88) — the death year agrees — and the chart's line is itself labelled `(Qadiriyya)`,
  so the order agrees too; but the book gives him **no place, no title and no gloss anywhere**, and
  his position in the chain sits with Dalpat Sufi. **A human must settle whether the book's Rakhiyyal
  Shah is the archive's Syed Rakhyal Shah before any Fatehpur fact is entered.** The bibliography's
  second entry for him is also malformed as printed — "Rakhiyyal Shah al-Qadiri, Chandiyo, Hakim Eijaz
  Hasan (Compiled by), *Rakhiyyal Shah Jo Kalam*" — with "Chandiyo" in a position that could be a
  second surname or a place (p. 231, folio 218).
- **`Nimano Faqir` / `Nimano Faqeer` / `Nimala`.** "Nimanal Maan. Puj Nimala Sai jin ja Kalaam"
  (p. 172, folio 159) where the surrounding text has "Nimano" and "Nimanal"; "Nimano Faqeer" and
  "Nimano Faqir" in consecutive bibliography entries (p. 231, folio 218); the annexe's pen-name
  "nimani"/"Nimani" (p. 225, folio 212). **And the book's own pronouns for her contradict each other**
  — see Contradictions below.
- **`Daulat Keswani` / `Daulat Kesvani` / `Daulat Kesvaram`** — three spellings of one man in three
  pages (pp. 121–123, folios 108–110).
- **`Daulatram` names at least two and possibly three men.** "Daulatram Chaudhry", one of Mulchand's
  three disciples (p. 75, folio 62); "Daulatram", Tirathdas's own son, building the basement Mul
  Kutiyya (pp. 75–76, folios 62–63) — **whether they are the same person is (not stated) and the two
  must not be merged**; and "Daulatram Motwani" (p. 128, folio 115; p. 144, folio 131; p. 114,
  folio 101).
- **`Tirthdas` / `Tirathdas`** (p. 75, folio 62; p. 83, folio 70 / pp. 75–76, folios 62–63).
- **`Sain Vasan Shah` / `Wasan Shah` / `Vasan Shah`**, all three within three pages (pp. 68, 70;
  folios 55, 57).
- **`Isarlal` / `Isar Lal` / `Ishwar Lal`** (p. 150, folio 137; p. 169, folio 156; pp. 172, 174,
  folios 159, 161).
- **`Paru` names two different people. Do not merge.** "Paru Shah", a saint at Rohri (p. 150,
  folio 137; p. 156, folio 143), and "Paru" / "Paru Chawla" / `Paru Chala` [Paru Chawla] / "Paru
  Thakur Chawla", the Sindhi cultural activist (pp. 161–162, folios 148–149).
- **`Hari` names at least two people, and one non-person. Do not merge.** "R. M. Hari (1912–80)", son
  and successor of Rochaldas (p. 147, folio 134; p. 148, folio 135; p. 162, folio 149) — printed
  **`H. M. Hari`** two pages earlier in the same discussion (p. 96, folio 83, against "R. M. Hari" at
  p. 97, folio 84, **no reading offered**); "Hari Ram" / "Dada Hari Ram Kataria" of the Radha Krishin
  Lal darbar (p. 151, folio 138; p. 152, folio 139); and "Hari Om Jhulelal", a chant (p. 147,
  folio 134).
- **`Sakhi Qabul Muhammad` — a regnal numeral that may or may not be damage.** "the sixth murshid of
  Daraza, Sachal's dargah, Sakhi Qabul Muhammad **II** (1842–1925)" (p. 150, folio 137) against
  "Sakhi Qabul Muhammad **VI**", "Sakhi Qabul **VI**" and "Sakhi Qabul **VIII**" (p. 152, folio 139).
  **"II" was deliberately not marked as damage**, because "the second of that name" and "the sixth
  murshid in sequence" are compatible. **Only the "II" form carries the dates (1842–1925).** The index
  adds a third and fourth form: "Faruqi, Sakhi Qabul Muhammad" (p. 242, folio 229), "Faruqi, Dr. Sakhi
  Qabool Muhammad" (p. 234, folio 221) and the inverted "Muhammad II, Sakhi Qabul 45, 47, 119, 122,
  137" (p. 246, folio 233), **which may or may not be the same man**. A human must settle this before
  either numeral or those dates enter the `shrine-of-sachal-sarmast` entry.
- **`Faruqi` is a shared surname and must not identify two men.** The archive gives Sachal Sarmast's
  name as "Sachal Sarmast (Abdul Wahab Faruqi)"; the book's "Sakhi Qabool Muhammad Faruqi" (p. 212,
  folio 199), "Faruqi 2002" (p. 217, folio 204) and "Sakhi Qabool Muhammad II of Daraza Sharif"
  (p. 220, folio 207) are **not** the saint.
- **`Shah Abd al-Latif` — five forms.** `Shah Abd al-Latif` (p. 213, folio 200 and throughout),
  `Shah Abdul Latif` (p. 212, folio 199), `Shah Ab al-Latif` [text-layer? Shah Abd al-Latif] (p. 220,
  folio 207), `ShahAbd al-Latif` [text-layer? Shah Abd al-Latif] (p. 91, folio 78), the short form
  "Shah Latif" (p. 68, folio 55; p. 171, folio 158) and, inside the Sachal paragraph only, `Shah Laṭif`
  with a dot below (p. 93, folio 80). The index files him under A — "al-Latif, Shah Abd" (p. 245,
  folio 232).
- **`Shah Abd al-Karim` / `Shah Karim`** (pp. 91, 100, 105, folios 78, 87, 92; p. 171, folio 158), and
  in the index under two letters, "al-Karim, Shah Abd 78" and "Karim, Shah 92". The book **never
  prints "Bulri"** in the body. Do not confuse with **`Abd al-Karim Sindilo`**, a twentieth-century
  glossary compiler (p. 94, folio 81).
- **`Sarmast` is shared by three men.** Sachal Sarmast; "Sayyid Ali Sarmast" (p. 56, folio 43; index
  "Sarmast, Sayyid Ali 43", p. 248, folio 235), whom the book makes Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's successor
  and Qutub Ali Shah's maternal ancestor; and the bare "Sarmast" in the glossary's `Siraiki` entry
  (p. 220, folio 207), **which the glossary does not expand: (not stated)**.
- **`Dalpat Sufi, or Sachal Sarmast` (p. 20, folio 7)** — an apposition the book makes and its own
  Table of annexes appears not to support: `Dalpat Sufi 208` is the first of five separately abstracted
  poets, alongside `Qutub Ali Shah 210`, `Dadi Ganga 211`, `Nimano Faqir 212` and `Gehimal Motwani 213`
  (p. 13, folio not stated), and Sachal Sarmast is not among them. **Neither read the two as aliases
  nor silently split them: the chunk says "or" and says nothing more.** Chunk 001 calls this "the
  single item most likely to corrupt the `shrine-of-sachal-sarmast` entry".
- **`Shah Bahu` / `Sultan Bahu`.** "Sultan Bahu" at p. 121 (folio 108); "Shah Bahu" in a three-name
  list of "Non-Sindhi poets" at p. 171 (folio 158); the index prints "Shah Bahu 158" (p. 249,
  folio 236) — **and folio 108 does not appear in that entry's run, which is evidence against the
  identification rather than for it.**
- **`Bulhe Shah` / `Bullhe Shah`** (p. 78-run in the index, p. 149, folio 136; p. 158, folio 145;
  p. 171, folio 158) against the archive's `Bulleh Shah`.
- **`Ghulam Farid` is not `Ghulam Ali` and is not `Fariduddin`.** "Ghulam Farid" (p. 161, folio 148;
  p. 171, folio 158; index "Farid, Ghulam 78, 148, 158", p. 242, folio 229); "Ghulam Ali", a different
  man, Murad's son (p. 168, folio 155); "Attar, Farid al-Din 108, 111, 147, 157" (p. 239, folio not
  stated), a third Farid. The book gives Ghulam Farid **no honorific, no dates and no place**.
- **`Shah Inayat` / `Shah Inat` — three different men.** "Shah Inayat" bare (p. 49, folio 36);
  "Shah Inayat from / of / (from) Jhok Sharif", qualified every time (pp. 107, 122, 124, folios 94,
  109, 111); "Shah Inat, a Sindhi Sufi poet of the seventeenth century" (p. 105, folio 92); and
  **"Shah Inat (1623–1712)", complete name "Sayyid Inayat Allah Rizvi", "probably a Shia Muslim,
  initiated in the Qadiriyya"** (p. 211, folio 198), whom the book keeps separate from the Jhok figure
  and never links to him. The index adds "Inayat, Shah 36, 94, 111" and, separately, "Inat, Shah 92,
  198 n.3" (p. 243, folio 230), and the bibliography "Minyoon Shah Inat" (p. 229, folio not stated).
- **`Nur Husayn Shah`** (p. 73, folio 60; p. 133, folio 120) and **`Miyyan Nur Maḥmad Shah`** (p. 59,
  folio 46; index "Shah, Nur Maḥmad 46", p. 242, folio 229) are two different names and must not be
  joined.
- **`Hazra Hazoor Darvish Sai Parmanand Sahab`** (p. 70, folio 57) — `Hazra` may be the book's own
  spelling; **no reading is offered.**
- **`Muin al-Din Tchishti`** (pp. 187, 188, folios 174, 175), a French-influenced transliteration used
  consistently there, against "Muinuddin Chishti" (p. 123, folio 110).
- **`Trump, Ernst` / `Ernst Trump`** (p. 63, folio 50; index p. 251, folio 238) — reproduced exactly;
  **RULE 2 forbids supplying an alternative spelling from outside the book.**
- **`Thapan` vs `Thapar`, and `Ramey` vs `Ramsey`.** The citations read `(Thapan 2005)`,
  `(Thapan 2005: 210–11)`, `(Thapan 2005: 213)`, `(Thapan 2005: 219)`, `(Thapan 2005: 220)` and the
  running text reads `Thapar proposed a tentative typology`, `Thapar's categories deserve discussion`
  (p. 27, folio 14), `In the wake of Thapar's culturalist approach` (p. 28, folio 15); the citation
  `(Ramey 2008: 7)` against `In fact, Ramsey is more interested in labelling…` (p. 28, folio 15).
  **A `[text-layer?]` flag was deliberately NOT applied**, because whole-word substitutions of that
  kind are not what a text layer produces; they read as the book's own inconsistency. Also
  `Mahatma Ramchandraji Maharaj (1873–1931)` (p. 25, folio 12) against `Ramachandra's family` and
  `the technical lexicon built by Ramachandra` (p. 26, folio 13) — **recorded as printed, probably the
  book's short form, but not asserted as such.**
- **`Jotwani` cited as `Motwani`.** "As coined by Motilal Jotwani in his book on the Sufis of Sindh,
  they were the 'Great Integrators' (Motwani 1986: 150)", followed by "(Motwani 1986: 155)" (p. 91,
  folio 78), against `Jotwani 1996` and `Jotwani 1975 (1996)` elsewhere (pp. 88, 101, folios 75, 88).
  **No reading offered.** This matters because both the "Great Integrators" coinage and the "late
  participaters" quotation are attached to Shah Latif and Sachal.
- **Further author-name pairs, all as printed:** `Mallik` / `Mallick 1949` (p. 155, folio 142; index
  p. 245, folio 232 against bibliography p. 230, folio 217); `Paniker` / `Panikker` in one sentence
  (p. 171, folio 158); `Narsain, N. J.` and `Narsain, S. J.` printed consecutively (p. 246, folio 233)
  against the bibliography's `Narsain, S. J.` (p. 236, folio 223); `Dibgy, Simon 7–8` (p. 242,
  folio 229) against `Digby, Simon` (p. 234, folio 221); `Ladkani, Sugnaram Mangadas` (p. 245,
  folio 232) against `Ladkani, Sognaram Mangaldas` (p. 230, folio 217); `Matlani, Mohanlal` (p. 245,
  folio 232) against `Matlani, Mohan` (p. 236, folio 223); `Pollock, Sheldom` and `Pollock, Sheldon`
  in consecutive entries (p. 237, folio 224); `Blavatsly, Tibet 37` printed two lines below
  `Blavatsky, Helena 33–4, 35, 142` (p. 240, folio 227) — **read as a misprint for Blavatsky, but the
  entry as printed is a place filed under a personal name and cannot be resolved.**
- **`Bhagwan K. Ajwani` and `Bhagwan Advani`** — same first name, same argument, one page apart
  (pp. 169–170, folios 156–157). **The book never says they are the same man and they are not merged.**
- **`Ramakrishna` / `Ramakhrishna`** (pp. 102–103, folios 89–90); **`Mira Bai` / `Mirabai`** (p. 89,
  folio 76; p. 91, folio 78); **`Raja Barthari` / `Raja Bharthari` / `Raja Vir`** (p. 50, folio 37;
  p. 200, folio 187; index entries "Raja Barthari 165" and "Raja Bharthari 37" printed consecutively,
  p. 247, folio 234); **`Hamir Sodho` / `Hamir Rano`** (p. 188, folio 175); **`Shobha` / `Shoba`**
  (p. 114, folio 101 against the Figure 4.1 caption, p. 116, folio 103) — and chunk 001 separately
  records that whether "Shoba Vaswani" and "Shoba, the gaddi nashin's sister" are the same person is
  **(not stated)**.
- **Custodial names across chunks:** "Sufi Ataullah Sattari, the sajjada nashin of Jhok" (p. 128,
  folio 115) against "the sajjada nashin, Sufi Abd al-Sattar Shah" (p. 120, folio 107) and "Sufi
  Hazrat Abdul Sattar Shah of Jhok Sharif" (p. 101, folio 88). **One man at two dates, two successive
  incumbents, or a transcription difference — not resolvable; all forms stand.**
- **`Asan Gul`** (fig. 7.1 caption, p. 10, folio not stated; p. 182, folio 169), "a Hindu sajjada
  nashin in Sehwan Sharif" — **the chunk's single most quotable institutional fact and its least
  supported.** He appears in no body sentence, no shrine is named for him, and the body text's Hindu
  office-holders are Lal Gul, Ramchand and Murad Shah. **He may be Lal Gul under another name, a third
  man, or a caption error; the book does not say.**

**Places, institutions and titles.**

- **`Sadh Bela` (book) / `Sadh Belo` (archive)**, printed as two separate index entries one line apart,
  "Sadh Bela (sanctuary) 18" and "Sadh Belo 48, 55, 56" (p. 248, folio 235); plus "Udais temple, Sadh
  Bela 18" against "Udasis 40, 196 n.10" on the next line (p. 251, folio 238). **Baba Bankhandi
  Maharaj is never named anywhere in the book: (not stated).**
- **`Udero Lal` (book) / `Odero Lal (Udero Lal Teerath Asthan)` (archive)**, plus `Jhulelal`,
  `Uderolal`, `Daryalal`, `Darya Shah` (p. 156, folio 143) and `Shaykh Tahir` (p. 184, folio 171)
  against the archive's `Sheikh Tahir`. The book hedges Shaykh Tahir's order — "could have been a
  Sohrawardi" — which **must not harden into a silsila**.
- **`Jinda Pir` / `Zinda Pir`**, printed in one sentence "of two different things" (p. 54, folio 41),
  and carried in the index as **two separate entries at the identical folio 41** — "Jinda Pir 41"
  (p. 244, folio 231) and "Zinda Pir 41" (p. 252, folio 239). **The index does not say they are one
  thing and nobody has assumed it.**
- **`Kathwaro Darbar` / `Katwara Sahib` / `the Khatwara Sahib`** (p. 68, folio 55; p. 114, folio 101;
  index p. 244, folio 231) against the archive's `Khatwari Darbar`. **The book never locates it, never
  names a figure for it, and prints three spellings**; chunk 004 mapped it, chunk 012 refused it — see
  Placement below.
- **`Lowari` (book) / `Luari` (archive)**, plus `Lowari Shari` [text-layer? Lowari Sharif] (p. 228,
  folio 215) and the index's "Lowari Sharif (Badin, Sindh) 9".
- **`Fatehpur` / `Fathepur`, on the same page** (p. 59, folio 46), and in the index "of Fatehpur 46"
  (p. 241, folio 228) against "Fathepur 46" (p. 242, folio 229).
- **`Lakkiyaris` / `Lakkiyyaris` / `Lakiyyaris` / `Lakkiyyari, Gul Muhammad`** — four forms in three
  pages (pp. 180–183, folios 167–170) against the archive's `Lakyari`. **No form was chosen and none
  was marked as damage**, because a consistent "Lakkiyyari" is not obviously the intended form either.
- **`Meranis` / `Mirani` and `Nevanis` / `Navanis`** — four spellings for the two Hindu mehndi families
  of Sehwan across one book (p. 183, folio 170 against p. 118, folio 105).
- **`Khonbati` (inside Burton's quotation) / `Khuhnbatis` (the author's own prose), same page**
  (p. 181, folio 168). **Burton's spelling must not be normalised.**
- **`Tando Jahaniya` / `Tando Jahaniyya` / `Tando Jahaniyyan`** (p. 27, folio 14; p. 60, folio 47;
  p. 112, folio not stated); **`Tando Ahmad Khan` / `Tando Ahmed Khan`** (p. 123, folio 110; p. 179,
  folio 166); `Tando Saindad near Tando Muhammad Khan`; `Tando Allahyar`. **Four different Tandos.**
- **`Sohrawardi` / `Sohrawardiyya`** (pp. 51, 56, 178, 187, 194, 200, folios 38, 43, 165, 174, 181,
  187; index p. 250, folio 237) against the archive's `Suhrawardi`.
- **`Meghwars` / `Menghwars`** (pp. 191, 192, 197; folios 178, 179, 184); **`Kutch` / `Kuch`**, all

  matters because "Kandri" is also the name the author gives a whole school of Sufism;
  **`Lakhshmi Narayan`** (p. 192, folio 179) against the archive's `Laxmi Narayan`.
- **Institutions and terms:** `kuthiyya` / `kutiyya`, `Mul Kuthiyya` / `Mul Kutiyya`, used
  interchangeably and sometimes in adjacent sentences (pp. 74, 75, 76, 79, 83, 114, 143; folios 61,

### Flagged dates, years and numerals

- **`2,333,527 million` (p. 38, folio 25)** — flagged `[text-layer? — the figure alone, "2,333,527",
  is the reading the component numbers support]`, **written reversibly, not substituted.** Three
  problems in one paragraph: the sentence before it says "Sindh's population statistics are not
  available in the first census of 1872"; the figure and the scale word cannot both be intended; and
  the named components — **1,718,688 Muslims, 393,092 Hindus, 5,034 Christians, 86,663 "Other
  castes"** — sum to **2,203,477**, which is **130,050 short** of the stated total. All five figures
  are quoted exactly as printed; the arithmetic is reported, not resolved.
- **`(Bellamy 201: 19)` (p. 50, folio 37)** — a **truncated year**; the same sentence says "In her book
  published in 2011", so the reading is `2011`. **The only defect in chunk 003 on which a citable fact
  depends.**
- **`700` in "from 5.00 to 700 pm" → `700 [text-layer? 7.00]` (p. 142, folio 129)** — a lost decimal
  point in a timetable.
- **`(Narsain 1932: 1939)` (p. 135, folio 122)** — a 1939 page in a 1932 work; marked, **no reading
  offered.**
- **`(Motwani 1965: 2012)` (p. 173, folio 160)** — a year where a page range belongs; the sense is the
  1965 book in its 2012 Hindi translation, but the form is anomalous.
- **`(Flaskerud : 25)` (p. 213, folio 200)** — a space before the colon and **no year at all**, where
  every other citation in the chunk carries one. **No year is supplied.** A human must recover it
  before the Flaskerud claim about the first portrait of Ali is cited.
- **`Baloch 2 1959: 902` (p. 216, folio 203)** — the volume number before the year, against
  "Baloch 1959, 1: 41–66" (p. 213, folio 200), "Baloch 1959 1: 487–90" (p. 215, folio 202) and
  "Baloch 1959 1: 355" (p. 216, folio 203). **Four orderings of one author's volume and year inside
  eleven pages; whether "Baloch 2 1959" is a transposition or the book's own error is (not stated).**
- **`(1711)` after "a Sufi Naqshbandi, Mian Abu'l Hasan" (p. 41, folio 28)** — a lone year with **no
  `d.` and no dash**, where every other parenthesis of that shape in the chunk is a life span or an
  explicit death year, and the sentence is about a manuscript. **No reading offered.**
- **Life spans reproduced exactly and not corrected because they are implausible on their face:**
  `Nasir Muhammad Faqir al-Qadiri (1835–1960)` — **125 years** (p. 120, folio 107), printed elsewhere
  as "(d. 1960)" with no birth year (p. 141, folio 128); `Sami (1743–1850)` — **107 years**; `Qutub
  Ali Shah (1810–1910)` — **100 years**; `Rakhiyyal Shah (1842–1940)` — **98 years** (p. 101, folio 88;
  the notes give a page only for the last of the three, the others falling within pp. 88–105,
  folios 75–92). **No emendation is proposed for any of them.**
- **Abbreviated second years, reproduced as printed:** `(1308–84)` (p. 56, folio 43) and `(1901–81)`,
  among nine en-dashed life dates in chunk 003.
- **Endnote numerals that can be read as part of a date, a page or a count** — see the next
  sub-heading; **`most of them Hindu (105).20` (p. 61, folio 48)** is the worst of them: the Hindu
  death count is **105** and **20** is the endnote number. It must not be read as `105.20` or as a
  total of 120 plus 20.
- **Index locator runs that are defective as printed:** `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar … 67–8, 67–9, …`, **two
  overlapping runs in sequence** (p. 245, folio 232) — the folio range 67–69 is certain but the printed
  form is defective; `nam 61, 62, 66, 107–8, 110, 123–7, 124, 154, 189`, with **124 printed after the
  run 123–7 that contains it** (p. 246, folio 233); `Karachi 13, 31, 33, 34, 43, 44, 49–51 54–55, …`,
  with a **missing comma between two runs** and `54–55` set with two digits where the index elsewhere
  uses `54–5` (p. 244, folio 231).
- **A source line that is a bare number, twice:** the second Dalpat annexe is sourced **`(53)`**
  (p. 222, folio 209) and the Nimano Faqir annexe **`(174)`** (p. 225, folio 212). **Whether these are
  poem numbers, page numbers or entries in a numbered edition is (not stated)**, and no edition is
  named for either. The Gehimal Motwani and Qutub Ali Shah annexes carry **no source line at all**.
- **Quantities that carry no date, no source, or both, and must not enter an entry bare:** the
  **75-per-cent** figure (p. 199, folio 186), with no date and no source; **140,000 Hindus** crossing
  the border "in December" (p. 63, folio 50), **the year of that December being (not stated)**, the
  sentence sitting between events dated 6 and 13 January 1948 and followed by "on 14 January";
  **2,500 rupees** for the Ulhasnagar plot (p. 83, folio 70) — the book's only cash figure in that
  chunk, against "a pretty large field" (p. 80, folio 67) as its only land description; **"about twenty
  kafis, of which two are Hindu"** (p. 193, folio 180); **"the twenty-second pir of the line"** for
  Pithoro (p. 190, folio 177) against a fifteenth-century dating by association ("In 1439", p. 188,
  folio 175) — **the book draws no arithmetic and neither did the note-taker.**
- **Calendar dates with no year, and a calendar the book will not join to names.** "the nineteenth day
  of Shaban" (p. 58, folio 45), **no year**; "the 18th of Shaban" (p. 181, folio 168) — the only ʿurs
  dating in chunk 009, **no year, no Gregorian equivalent**, and the book does not say whether the
  three-day sequence is the whole ʿurs or only the mehndi cycle; "Sachal's versi on the 14th of
  Ramazan" nine lines before "Sachal's birthday is on Ramazan in the Islamic calendar" (p. 151,
  folio 138) — **both reported, neither preferred**; "the dates of all of these death anniversaries
  are taken as sacred: the 11, the 15, the 17 and the 27" (p. 122, folio 109), following a list of four
  names **in a different order, with no month given and no pairing stated**. The glossary settles one
  of the four — the **11** is al-Jilani's "Yarhen, the 11th… the 11th of Rabi al-Sani" (p. 218, folio
  not stated; p. 220, folio 207) — and **15, 17 and 27 remain unassigned: (not stated).** **Nothing
  assigns a date to Shah Inayat, and `dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed` must not receive one from
  this book.** Separately, the Bijapur melo runs "from the 17th to the 19th of Moharram, as it is
  celebrated in Jhok Sharif" (p. 124, folio 111), and **whether that 17 is the same 17 as the versi
  list's is (not stated).**
- **Era style, measured as a negative across three chunks: the book prints no era marker at all** — no
  `ce`, no `AD`, no `AH`, no `BCE`, no `CE` (pp. 29–48, 49–67 and throughout). Every year is bare.
  **A consolidator must not add an era the book does not print.**

### Endnote numerals fused to the preceding character — the instances on which a fact depends

Book-wide, with no space and no superscript marking. The class is inventoried once here; the instances
below are listed individually **because a name, a date, a page number or a count depends on each**:

`the migrants,10` (p. 33, folio 20); `(Blumhardt, 1905: 35).4` — the page is 35, the note is 4 (p. 41,
folio 28); `another book on Sachal Sarmast in 1922,8` — the year is 1922, the note is 8 (p. 48,
folio 35); `most of them Hindu (105).20` (p. 61, folio 48); `in Sehwan,4` — following a comma inside a
list of three places, so it can be misread as part of the enumeration (p. 79, folio 66);
`(Motwani 1986: 150).3` (p. 91, folio 78); `(Bedil 2012).10` (p. 97, folio 84);
`Muhammad Iqbal (1877–1938)14` — the dates are 1877–1938, the note is 14 (p. 102, folio 89);
`somewhere Ahmed,16` — **endnote 16 falls inside a closing verse quotation, so anyone quoting that line
must drop the 16** (p. 105, folio 92); `(Ajwani 1970: 116).21` — the page is 116, the note is 21
(p. 107, folio 94); `Molchand7 Kripalani` — the note is 7 and the name is Molchand (p. 123, folio 110);
`...no date: 5).8` (p. 147, folio 134); `(1838–1918).10` (p. 148, folio 135);
`(Hiranandani 1984: 55).3` (p. 161, folio 148); `the non-migrant Sindhis4 in India` and
`a more hinduized6 version` — **both sit mid-sentence with no preceding full stop, so a naive reader
takes "Sindhis4" and "hinduized6" for words** (pp. 162–163, folios 149–150).

Chunks 001 and 004 record the same class as harmless in their own ranges, none of their instances
following a numeral. **The texts of the notes themselves are at pp. 208–217 (folios not stated and
196–204) and were transcribed in chunk 011; the note texts for chunks 008 and 010 are outside their
own ranges and are (not stated) there.**

### The folio situation

**What the notes establish.** The book prints a folio on most pages and the notes read it off that
page's own running head, every time. A **PDF − 13 = folio** relation was measured independently in
chunks 002, 005, 008, 010, 011, 012 and 013, held on every folio-printing page tested, and was
**contradicted nowhere** — and it was **never used to supply a folio**. Where a page prints no folio,
the notes write `folio not stated` rather than computing one. Chunk 008 states the rule at its
sharpest: p. 154 prints no folio, the relation would give 141, and **folio 141 therefore appears
nowhere in these notes and is used in no citation**. Chunk 010 records the same for p. 196 (would be
183, "recorded here as a cross-check only").

**Folio position and running heads (measured in chunk 005 and confirmed in chunk 011).** The folio sits
**first on odd PDF pages and last on even PDF pages**. The two running heads are the **chapter title**
on folio-last pages and the **book title** `The Hindu Sufis of South Asia` on folio-first pages —
**never a `Chapter N` form**, so a scanner keyed to "Chapter" finds nothing. The run of spaces between
title and numeral is not constant (`Space            77` against `Space          79` and
`Space          89`, pp. 90, 92, 102, folios 77, 79, 89), so the header must be parsed on the numeral,
not on column position. Chunk 005 also notes that its folio-first pages all carry **even** folios and
its folio-last pages **odd** ones — the opposite pairing from the briefing's worked examples, which are
format illustrations rather than paired data.

**No folio anywhere in this book sits in a footer.** Chunks 011 and 012 checked the foot of every page
in their ranges explicitly: General Conclusion, Notes, Glossary, Annexes, Bibliography and Index all
print the folio in a running head at the top. **The feet of Annexes pp. 222, 223, 224, 225 and 227
carry the Annexes' own numbered footnotes 1–8, which are not folios.**

**Pages that print a bare numeral and nothing else.** p. 35 prints `22` alone — read as folio 22 by
inference from the relation and from position, with the alternative (a part-title or section numeral,
as the `1` on p. 36 is a chapter numeral) **recorded and refused**; **no fact is cited to p. 35**, so
nothing depends on the reading. p. 111 prints `98` alone and **is** cited as folio 98; p. 195 prints
`182` alone and is cited as folio 182; p. 207 prints `194` alone at the top of an otherwise empty page
and is cited as folio 194; **p. 253 carries the single line `240` and no other content whatever** and
is the volume's final printed page. **p. 253 is the one uncertain case**: the extracted text gives no
positional evidence whether the `240` sits in a running head or a footer, and no section name
accompanies it; chunk 013 cites it as folio 240 **because it is printed, not because −13 predicts it**.

**Pages cited `folio not stated` rather than computed.** p. 6 (the Sindhi page); p. 12 and the other
front-matter pages of chunk 001 (pp. 3, 5, 10, 11, 13, 14); p. 36, the only such page in chunk 002,
carrying substantive material (the three masters, the chapter's aim, the Amil thesis, the caste
question — on the relation it would be 23, **used nowhere**); p. 66, the chapter-2 opener; p. 86, the
chapter-3 display opener; p. 112, the chapter-4 opener; p. 154; p. 176; p. 196; **pp. 208, 218 and
221**, the Notes, Glossary and Annexes display openers, checked at top **and** bottom, so that **folios
195, 205 and 208 are never printed anywhere in the book**; p. 229, the Bibliography opener, which
prints neither head nor foot folio; p. 239, the Index opener, likewise. Chunk 003 adds that **the
number of the chapter that ends on p. 65 (folio 52) is itself (not stated)**, because this book's
running heads carry the chapter *title*, never `Chapter two`.

**The section-and-folio map (chunk 011, PDF pp. 204–227)** gives, per page, the printed folio, the head
position, the section and the contents, and establishes the back-matter order **General Conclusion
(ends p. 206) → Notes (p. 208 opener, ends p. 217) → Glossary (p. 218 opener, ends p. 220) → Annexes
(p. 221 opener, running past p. 227, where the chunk breaks off mid-sentence)**. Chunk 012 establishes
the remainder: **Annexes → Bibliography (1 Corpus and references, from p. 229; 2 Studies, from the
lower half of p. 232 to p. 238, folio 225) → Index (p. 239 opener to p. 252, folio 239)**. Chunk 013
closes it: **the Index's last entry is "ziyarat 37, 191" at the foot of the right column of p. 252
(folio 239)** — no colophon, no closing rule, nothing after it — and **the book runs to printed folio
240 across 253 PDF pages.**

**A display-title case wrinkle that a header scanner must be told about.** The display title is set
lower case where the running head over the same section is title case: `The transmission of the Sufi
legacy in India` (p. 154) against `The Transmission of the Sufi Legacy in India`; `General conclusion`
(p. 196; and the notes heading at p. 217, folio 204) against `General Conclusion` (pp. 198, 200, 202,
204, 206; folios 185, 187, 189, 191, 193). Chunk 006 recorded the same at chapter 4. **A tool matching
note-headings to section-headings by string equality would miss it.** Separately, the section heading
on p. 187 (folio 174) is **broken across two printed lines with a hyphen** — `The Sufi-` /
`related cults of the Sindhi Dalits` — and that hyphen is a line-break hyphen, not part of the title.

**The column reconstruction (chunks 012 and 013), and exactly how far it can be trusted.** In the
Index, **roughly 542 physical lines across the eleven pages of chunk 012 — all but about a dozen — set
two unrelated columns side by side**, and in chunk 013 **112 of 119 content lines (94%)** do the same.
Worse, **the right column continues the left in reading order**, so a right-column line can be a
subentry of an entry printed lower in the left column (`hindu references in 91–5` belongs to
`poetry (Sufi)`, p. 247, folio 234; `as pilgrimage 12` belongs to `darbars (shrine)`, p. 241,
folio 228). Every one of the twenty-two columns and ten page breaks in chunk 012, and all six column
and page breaks in chunk 013, were checked for alphabetical continuity and **all checked out**; no
fragment was genuinely unassignable, so **no column `[text-layer?]` flags were raised**. Three worked
examples show what a naive read-across produces: `Sindhiyyat 140, 142–3, 146, 148–9,` … `legacy 2, 17,
18–21, 30, 85, 108, 116,` are two unrelated entries, the second a subentry of `Sufis` twelve lines
higher (p. 250, folio 237); `Tahir, Shaykh 38, 165` … `of Sachal Sarmast 130` would produce "Shaykh
Tahir of Sachal Sarmast", a relationship the book does not assert (p. 251, folio 238); `wahdat-e wujud
(unity of being) 74,` … `Zakariyya, Baha al-Din 38, 165,` would attribute *wahdat al-wujud* to
Bahauddin Zakariya (p. 252, folio 239). **Two limits stand.** First, **alphabetical continuity proves
only that the order is non-decreasing; it cannot prove that no entry was dropped at a break**, and that
bites hardest at the chunk 012 → 013 break (`Sindhi Hindus` on p. 249 → `Sindhis` on p. 250), where any
entry falling between the two sides would be invisible to both workers. Second, **where two adjacent
entries happen to be alphabetically compatible across a column boundary the check cannot distinguish
them**; chunk 012 found no such case but could not rule one out. **A further hazard:** the headword
`Sufism` sits on p. 250 and **is not repeated at the top of p. 251**, so p. 251's first fifteen indented
lines have their headword on the previous page (pp. 250–251, folios 237–238).

**Other reflow that the folio map does not cover.** Table 5.1 (p. 146, folio 133) comes through as a
single stream: its **third-day column gives a bare time with no activity three times — "3.00", "3.30"
and "4.45" — so the activity at those hours is (not stated) in the text layer, not absent from the
printed table**, and several second- and third-day cells come through empty (10.00; 6.45–7.00;
7.30–9.30; the full-night row) with **no way to tell a blank cell from a dropped one**. The Annex 2
chart's cell boundaries are lost (p. 227, folio 214): `Nimano Faqir Radha Kishin  Pratap Rajani` has
two gaps of different widths and `Dalpat Sufi Mulchand Faqir (via Rakhiyyal Shah) Molchand Kripalani`
has **three names with no separator at all**; only `Rochaldas\t\t\tDalpat Sufi` retains a tab, so the
reading "three names in three cells" **is inferred and is not certain**, and whether the chart carried
vertical rules, dates or arrows is **(not stated)**. Figure captions are interleaved into the body:
the sentence at p. 149 (folio 136) is interrupted by the whole of the Figure 5.2 caption and resumes at
the head of p. 150 (folio 137); "Figure 4.2 Mulchand's tomb in Sehwan Sharif." is extracted at the head
of p. 126 (folio 113) before the sentence continuing from the previous chunk. **No caption's placement
in the extraction should be read as its placement on the printed page; the running heads, not the
captions, fixed the folios.**

### The finding aids' limits — and the absolute bar on turning a locator into a `(p. N)`

**No index locator and no endnote locator from this book may ever become a `(p. N)`.** Cite the PDF
page the entry is printed on. Two features of this book make the rule sharper than usual:

1. **Every locator the Index prints is a PRINTED FOLIO, not a PDF page** — chunk 012 states it in as
   many words ("All locators are **printed folios**"). So converting "Lal Shahbaz Qalandar 7, 36, 37,
   43, 45–7, 58, 62, 67–8, 67–9, 105, 108, …" into page references would be wrong **twice over**: they
   are locators, and they are folios. The entry itself is printed on **p. 245 (folio 232)**; the `urs`
   entry on **p. 251 (folio 238)**; the Hinglaj run on **p. 243 (folio 230)**; `Sachal Sarmast` on
   **p. 248 (folio 235)**; `al-Latif, Shah Abd` and `Shah jo risalo` on **p. 245 (folio 232)** and
   within pp. 240–249 (folios 227–236).
2. **Index entries of the form `198 n.5`, `199 n.13`, `200 n.4`, `201 n.6`, `203 n.5`, `196 n.13`,
   `204 n.1` fuse a folio with an endnote number.** Neither half is a page reference. `204 n.1` is
   pointed at by three separate index entries (`Chishtiyya`, `sajjada nashin — of Daraza`,
   `Sohrawardiyya`), **and what that note says is in neither index chunk: (not stated).**

**The Notes as a finding aid, and its failure mode.** Note numbering restarts at 1 in every chapter.
The running head on all ten Notes pages is the **bare word `Notes`** and names no chapter, so a
first-line header scan of pp. 208–217 returns **no chapter attribution at all** — which a tool could
mistake for "notes unattributable". Every chapter attribution in the chunk-011 index was taken from the
**printed heading inside the notes**: `Introduction` p. 208 (folio not stated), notes 1–10;
`Chapter 1` pp. 209–210 (folios 196–197), notes 1–21; `Chapter 2` pp. 210–211 (folios 197–198),
notes 1–6; `Chapter 3` pp. 211–213 (folios 198–200), notes 1–21; `Chapter 4` pp. 213–214 (folios
200–201), notes 1–11; `Chapter 5` pp. 214–215 (folios 201–202), notes 1–11; `Chapter 6` pp. 215–216
(folios 202–203), notes 1–12; `Chapter 7` pp. 216–217 (folios 203–204), notes 1–7; `General conclusion`
p. 217 (folio 204), notes 1–2. **The headings give chapter numbers, not chapter titles**, and matching
a note to a chapter *title* needs the table of contents, which is **(not stated)** in that chunk.

**Four notes cannot be cited alone, because the note does not restate its own subject:** Ch. 7 n. 2,
"According to some Hindu informants, the Muslims are forbidden to visit" — **the site is not named**
(p. 216, folio 203); Ch. 7 n. 1, "About this controversial issue" — **the issue is not named** (p. 216,
folio 203); Ch. 1 n. 8, "This book was reprinted in 1963" — **the book is not named** (p. 209,
folio 196); Ch. 1 n. 20, "About the Manzilgah Affair" — **the affair is not described** (p. 210,
folio 197). Each depends on a body sentence outside chunk 011 and must be stitched.

**One note-to-numeral pairing is unverified and must be checked on the printed page:** chunk 006
records the numeral 18 after `Sita.18` (p. 106, folio 93), whereas note 18 in chunk 011 is about
Ayodhya (p. 212, folio 199). Plausible, since the sentence concerns Ram's return to Ayodhya, **but not
verified** — do not cite note 18 as the source of a fact about Sita until someone has looked.

**The Index's own structure adds two traps.** Entries are alphabetised letter by letter with the Arabic
article filed under `al-`, **so the same person appears under two letters** (`al-Latif, Shah Abd` under
A; `al-Karim, Shah Abd` under A and `Karim, Shah` under K; `Alam, Shah Rukn-e` inverted without the
