article). And **`urs` is absent from chunk 012's range** (it falls after the S-cut, in chunk 013), as
are `mela`/`melo`, `langar` and `Auqaf` from the M, L and A ranges printed there — **absence from a
range is not absence from the book.**

**The Bibliography as a finding aid.** **178 entries in all** on the note-taker's own page-by-page
count — 71 in `1 Corpus and references` and 107 in `2 Studies` — and **the book gives no total**. The
71 is itself uncertain: **two entries are cross-references that also carry the full entry** ("Dadi
Dhan, *see* Samtani…" and "Dadi Ganga, *see* Jaghtiyyani…", p. 229, folio not stated), so a count
treating cross-references as pointers only gives **69**. Ten of the 178 entries are Boivin's own, nine
of them on p. 233 (folio 220) — **Boivin is the most-cited author in his own bibliography by a factor
of three** — and **four of the ten are in French**. **Five entries print "no date" or "no publisher, no
place"; six more give a place but no publisher**, including "*Venti ain prarthna*, 2009 (no publisher,
no place)" (p. 232, folio 219), the book's own parenthesis. A whole class of misprints is reproduced
rather than corrected: "The **CHishti** Order" (p. 234, folio 221); "The Indian **Nasqhbandiyya**" and
"University of South **Carolin** Press" (p. 234, folio 221); "The **Campor** Flame… **Soceity**"
(p. 235, folio 222); "Ministry of **Inforation** and Broadcasting" (p. 237, folio 224); "Sarah
**Diamonf**" and "The **Leventh** to Fifteenth Centuries" and "**Hindism**" and "Walker, **Benjmin**"
(p. 238, folio 225); "**Routedge**" and "Palgrave **Mcmillan**" and "Churnjeet **Main**" (p. 233,
folio 220); "**Departemnt** of Sindhi" and "**Sindi** Kitab Ghar" (p. 236, folio 223); "**Balckwell**"
(p. 235, folio 222); "**Londres** and New York" — French for London in an English bibliography (p. 235,
folio 222). **"Palgrave Macmillan" appears in three forms** across pp. 233, 234 and 237 (folios 220,
221, 224), and **the academy's name in four**: "Sahitiya Academy", "Sahitya Akademi", "Sahitiya
Akedemi", "Sindhi Akademy" (pp. 230–232, folios 217–219). Two entries a human must chase: **the index
carries "*Dictionary of Sindhi Literature* (Jotwani) 94" (p. 242, folio 229) and the bibliography has
no such work** — its only Jotwani study is "Jotwani, Motilal, *Sufis of Sindh*… 1975 (1996)" (p. 235,
folio 222); and **Hughes's gazetteer appears twice with conflicting dates and titles** — "*Gazeeteer of
the Province of Sind*, London, George Bell and Sons, **1872**" (p. 229, folio not stated) against
"*Gazetter of the Province of Sindh*, Karachi, Indus Publications, 1996 (**1st edn, 1876**)" (p. 235,
folio 222).

### Contradictions inside the book

Kept side by side; none is reconciled.

- **The chapter count.** "The book is divided into **seven** chapters" (p. 29, folio 16) against the
  same walkthrough describing only six and closing "**The sixth and last chapter** will move the reader
  from India to Pakistan" (p. 32, folio 19). **Check the printed table of contents before any claim
  about the book's shape enters an entry.**
- **"five groups", four listed.** Digby's anecdote typology is announced as five and the sentence
  carries four semicolon-separated items (p. 21, folio 8). **Either a group was dropped in the
  printing, or the text layer lost a clause, or one item stands for two. No reading offered.**
- **"two main events" followed by a third** (p. 126, folio 113).
- **The Literary Society graduates.** "six Indians, five Hindus and one Muslim graduated from a
  university. The majority of the graduated Indians were Hindu, and then Parsi and Muslim." (p. 46,
  folio 33) — five plus one exhausts the six, leaving no Parsi.
- **Sachal Sarmast's death year.** `(1739–1825)` (p. 93, folio 80) against `(1739–1829)` (p. 101,
  folio 88), eight pages apart in one chapter; the birth year agrees. **The archive row carries a third
  value, 1827, which appears nowhere in the book and must not be back-filled.**
- **Nimano Faqir's death year.** "After Nimano's death in **1962**" against "Nimano Faqir died on
  23 March **1963**" and "She was ten years old when Nimano died in **1963**", all on p. 74 (folio 61),
  with "in March of 1963… She died on the 23rd following that day" (pp. 74–75, folios 61–62). **Two of
  four say 1963 and one says 1962.** Mulchand's death year is 1962 (p. 71, folio 58), a plausible
  source of the slip, **but that is not offered as a reading.** Separately, "It was in 1999 that the
  Sakhi Kuthiyya became the Nimanal Sangam, **thirty-six years after** the death of Nimano Faqir"
  (p. 142, folio 129) **would yield a year the book does not print; no year has been derived and none
  should be.**
- **Nimano Faqir's gender, inside one chunk.** "Nimano Faqir was cremated, but **his** ashes were
  deposited in a tomb" (p. 131, folio 118) and "a large portrait of Nimano Faqir, who is in bust and
  with **his** hair unfurled" (p. 132, folio 119) against "Interestingly Nimano is represented from
  when **she** was young. **Her** brown hair is down." (p. 135, folio 122) — and chunk 006 reads her as
  a woman ("A woman Sufi master", p. 115, folio 102). Chunk 004 records a further instance: "one of the
  biggest annual celebrations happens on 24 March, the day after the anniversary of **his** death"
  (p. 74, folio 61), where every other reference on the page is "she" and "her". **Nothing is
  harmonised**, and it is unclear whether the celebration described is hers or a successor's; the two
  portraits may also be two different pictures in two different rooms.
- **Nimano Faqir's migration year.** **1947** (p. 60, folio 47) against **1948** (p. 64, folio 51).
- **Rochaldas's return from Aden.** "Soon after attaining his medical doctorate in **1904**… he stayed
  there for **two years**" and, in the very next sentence, "Back in Sindh in **1905**" (p. 56,
  folio 43). **1904 + two ≠ 1905; no year is computed.**
- **Rochaldas's birth year is not printed.** "born in Rohri, like Bedil, but seven years after his
  demise" (p. 98, folio 85), Bedil's dates being `(1814–72)` (p. 97, folio 84). **Not computed: (not
  stated).**
- **Gehimal Motwani's office.** "he was **deputy collector**… posted at different places across Sindh,
  including Sehwan Sharif" (p. 71, folio 58) against "He was posted twice in Sehwan as
  **mukhthyarkar (revenue officer)**" (p. 75, folio 62).
- **Ramchand's kinship to Mulchand — and this bears directly on the Sehwan mehndi custodianship.**
  "the sajjada nashin is Ramchand, Mulchand's **nephew**" (p. 193, folio 180) against chapter 4's
  "Mohan Lal, who was Mulchand's nephew… Mohan Lal passed away in 1981 and his son Ramchand began to
  perform the mehndi" (p. 125, folio 112), on which Ramchand is Mulchand's **great-nephew**. **The two
  pages cannot both be right; both must stand with their pages.**
- **Mohan Lal: cousin or nephew, and what happened in 1981.** "the duty of the mehndi was transmitted
  to his **cousin**, Mohan Lal" (p. 183, folio 170) against "Mohan Lal, who was Mulchand's **nephew**"
  (p. 125, folio 112); and Mohan Lal "passed it on to his son, Ramchand, **in 1981**" (p. 183,
  folio 170) — a transfer *inter vivos* — against "Mohan Lal **passed away in 1981** and his son
  Ramchand began to perform the mehndi" (p. 125, folio 112). **And chunk 006 inserts two figures
  chunk 009 omits entirely**: "Chandmal Chandrani" as Mulchand's "first successor", who migrated in
  1971, and his son "Kanya Lal", who refused the duty (p. 125, folio 112), where p. 183 (folio 170)
  runs straight from Mulchand's death in 1962 to Mohan Lal.
- **Daulatram Motwani: nephew or son of Gehimal?** "Daulatram Motwani, Gehimal Motwani's **nephew**"
  (p. 128, folio 115) against "the Khatwara Sahib and Gehimal Motwani, Daulatram's **father**" (p. 114,
  folio 101).
- **Daulatram's home: Ulhasnagar or Bombay?** "a samadhi in the basement of his building in
  **Ulhasnagar**" (p. 128, folio 115) against "Such puja rooms were in Ulhasnagar, at Daulatram
  Motwani's home in **Bombay**, and in Delhi" (p. 144, folio 131), and chunk 006's "Daulatram Motwani's
  place in Ulhasnagar" (p. 114, folio 101). **The list's punctuation may be the whole of the problem.**
- **Dadi Dhan's flat: Mulund or Mahim?** "Dadi Dhan in **Mulund**, in Mumbai" (p. 127, folio 114)
  against "Dadi Dhan's flat in **Mahim**, Mumbai" (p. 115, folio 102).
- **The Ulhasnagar land: gift or sale?** "Gehimal had **gotten** land in Ulhasnagar from one Shri
  Vasumal" and, next sentence, "his family **sold** the land to Gehimal for 2,500 rupees" (pp. 82–83,
  folios 69–70), against "Gehimal was **given** a piece of land almost facing a temple" (p. 83,
  folio 70). **All three formulations recorded.**
- **The Mul Kutiyya: closed or sold?** "After the **closure** of Mul Kutiyya in Ulhasnagar" (p. 76,
  folio 63) against the three main followers who "decided to **sell** the darbar" (p. 84, folio 71).
  **Probably one event told twice, but the book does not say so and no date is given for either:
  (not stated).**
- **"Before partition… after 1947", in one sentence.** "Before partition, it was settled in Sehwan
  Sharif in Pakistan after 1947." (p. 114, folio 101) — and it calls Sehwan "in Pakistan" for a period
  the same clause places before partition. **This is the only sentence in that chunk dating the seat of
  Mulchand's path before its move to Ulhasnagar.**
- **Two authors for "the dua".** "The dua is the devotional supplication (munajat) composed by the son
  of Qutub Ali Shah, Roshan Ali Shah (1852–1932)" (p. 147, folio 134) against "the final dua whose
  author was Nasir Faqir" (p. 149, folio 136). **They may be different duas at different occasions, but
  the book does not say so.**
- **Two lists of the stations of the path, on one page.** "wahadaniyyat… marifat… hirarat… fana" and
  then "tariqa… marifa… fana" (p. 160, folio 147). **They match in neither number nor terms.**
- **Two dates for one English translation.** "edited in **1998** by his own son and successor,
  H. M. Damodar" (p. 162, folio 149) against "later on translated into English and published in
  **1995**, and republished in **1998**" (p. 164, folio 151). **Reconcilable, but do not quote one
  without the other.**
- **An ashram built after its founder's death.** "a trip to Puttaparthi… Sai Baba's native town, where
  his ashram was built in **2012**", of a man the same page dates "**(1926–2011)**" (p. 148, folio 135).
  **The date may belong to the trip rather than the building.**
- **How Mansur Hallaj died, Glossary against Notes.** "he was **beheaded** in Baghdad in the year 922"
  (p. 220, folio 207) against "condemned by ulemas to death, and was finally **hanged** in Bagdad
  [text-layer? Baghdad]" (p. 212, folio 199). **The year 922 agrees in both.** The glossary's further
  claim that "He visited Sindh in 905" appears nowhere in the notes.
- **The first portrait of Ali, across two chapters' notes.** Ch. 4 n. 5 reports Flaskerud's view that
  "the first portrait of Ali was first drawn in Sindh and then brought as a gift to Fath Ali Shah
  (d. 1824)" (p. 213, folio 200) against Ch. 5 n. 2's flat "**I have been unable to identify the
  provenance of this representation of Ali.**" (p. 214, folio 201). **They may concern different
  images; the book does not say so: (not stated).**
- **A citation dated after the book's own present.** The book dates itself to 2017 ("is this statement
  still relevant in 2017", p. 204, folio 191; "the mail we exchanged in September of 2017", p. 215,
  folio 202) yet Ch. 2 n. 5 cites "**Boivin and Rajpal 2018**" (p. 210, folio 197) as the source for
  the Udero Lal domes. **The only citation in that chunk later than 2017; whether it is forthcoming,
  mis-dated or simply later than the fieldwork is (not stated).**
- **Lal Shahbaz Qalandar given two different Hindu identifications in one chunk** — worshipped "as
  **Jhulelal**" at Tando Ahmad Khan (p. 184, folio 171) and "by the local Hindus as **Raja Barthari**"
  at Sehwan (p. 200, folio 187). **Both recorded; the book reconciles neither.**
- **Caste in Sindh, the book against itself.** The chapter opening entertains "the claim… that there
  was no caste system in Sindh, or at least not so rigid a caste system as seen in other parts of
  India, because of the very small number of Brahmans" (p. 36, folio not stated); five pages later it
  states flatly "Contrary to popular belief, there were many untouchables in Sindh" (p. 40, folio 27)
  and describes a functioning panchayat order (p. 41, folio 28). **Both are the book's own.**
- **Three incompatible caste ascriptions for the pir of Pithoro, on one page.** "Hathesingh claims
  being a **Thakur**… the pir belongs to the subgroup of **Sodhas**. This assertion is surprising when
  it is known that Pithoro Pir was **Muslim**. The priest (pujari) of the temple of Bhuj affirms… that
  the pir of Pithoro is a **Meghwar**. Burton made the same thing clear." (p. 191, folio 178) **The
  book records the disagreement without adjudicating it.**
- **The Pithoro tradition: oral or textual.** Burton reports pothis; "According to the current pir, the
  tradition of Pithoro Pir is mainly oral"; "Nonetheless, the Pirpanthis could have pothis." (p. 190,
  folio 177)
- **Isar Lal's place of worship.** "In the case of Isar Lal (1835–95), there **was** one, but after the
  departure of the main followers, nobody was able to take care of it" (p. 169, folio 156) against
  "Bhagwan Advani and Isar Lal **have no samadhi**" (p. 170, folio 157). **Whether these are consistent
  depends on tense and the book does not resolve it.**
- **The Amil/Bhaiband sentence.** "two main groups among the Hindu Sindhis: the shopkeepers and
  businessmen on one side, **the ex-Bhaibands**, the civil servants working in Indian administration…
  and the ex-Amils on the other side" (p. 205, folio 192), three sentences before "the Amils are more
  involved in Sufism than the Bhaibands". **Whether "ex-Bhaibands" is an error for the businessmen's
  group or a deliberate distinction is (not stated)**, and this is the book's only statement of the
  social basis of Hindu Sufi practice.
- **A block quotation printed twice.** The Qutub Ali Shah quatrain beginning "Waking up at midnight,
  those that chant the name (ganhan nam)" appears complete on p. 137 (folio 124) with its source
  parenthesised, "(Qutub Ali Shah 1985: 54)", and complete again on p. 138 (folio 125) with the same
  source unparenthesised. **Probably a typesetting or extraction duplication; the argument does not
  require it twice.**
- **A duplicated sentence, probably editorial.** "These issues will be addressed in the fourth chapter"
  and, two sentences later, "They will be studied in the fourth chapter" (p. 32, folio 19).
- **"Hamida Khuhro suggests that the police were more or less complacent, and that **his** father,
  Sindh's chief minister Muhammad Ayyub Khuhro…"** (p. 62, folio 49) — an internal inconsistency in the
  book's own sentence.
- **A forward reference that lands twice.** Chunk 006 quotes "there are even special Sufi sessions
  organized under the name of 'Darazi satsangs' (See last section of chapter 5)" (p. 109, folio 96).
  Chunk 007 finds the Darazi satsang described at p. 142 (folio 129), **in the third section of chapter
  5, not its last**; chunk 008 finds the section `The 'Darazi satsangs'` at p. 150 (folio 137). **The
  book's own forward reference may point at more than one passage.**
- **A man is placed in two silsilas three times over, in the Annex 2 chart** (p. 227, folio 214):
  **Rochaldas** under both `Jahaniyya (Sohrawardiyya)` and `Sehwan Sharif (Qalandariyya)`; **Dalpat
  Sufi** under both `Sehwan Sharif (Qalandariyya)` and `Jhok Sharif (Qadiriyya)`; **Molchand/Mulchand
  Kripalani** under both `Saindad (Qalandariyya)` and `Jhok Sharif (Qadiriyya)`. **Whether these are
  successive initiations, dual affiliations, or an artefact of the chart's layout is (not stated).**
- **The Darazi silsila bullet pairs two poets with two silsilas without matching them.** "Their two
  major poets, Sachal Sarmast and Qutub Ali Shah" (p. 200, folio 187) — **which poet belongs to which
  silsila is (not stated)**, and no assignment has been made.
- **Two Sindhi geographies for one section.** "a shrine located in the Kohistan, in Sindh" (p. 184,
  folio 171) refers to the Hindu dargah, but the paragraph that gives the district — "It is in the
  Jamshoro district and the seat of a taluka" (p. 184, folio 171) — has **Thano Bula Khan** as its
  subject, not Tando Ahmad Khan. **Whether Tando Ahmad Khan is in Jamshoro district is therefore not
  stated. Do not let an entry assert it.**
- **The state at Sehwan is named twice and never joined.** "government control in the 1960s" (p. 180,
  folio 167) and "the Awqaf manager" (p. 183, folio 170). **The book does not say the Awqaf is the
  department that took control, and no inference was drawn.**
- **The Mirchandani chronology.** Two volumes "have already been published in 2008, and re-edited in
  2009 and in 2012" (p. 172, folio 159) while the script they use follows an alphabet "adopted… in
  2011"; the book resolves it two sentences later, but the first sentence read alone is wrong.
- **The referent of "He" at p. 179 (folio 166).** "Mukund Bhatia was close to the Sufi poet, Kojhi… He
  became one of the closest followers of the sajjada nashin of Daraza at the point when he became his
  khalifa." **"He" may be Mukund Bhatia or Kojhi, and this decides whether the Hindu Sufi or the Muslim
  poet was the Daraza khalifa** — which matters for the Sachal row.
- **The pronoun in the chunk's main legend.** "When he was about seven, it is said that Shah Laṭif came
  to pay homage to the local murshid. After seeing Sachal, he is said to have exclaimed…" (p. 93,
  folio 80). Grammatically the seven-year-old is Shah Latif; the exclamation ("This God-gifted child")
  makes it Sachal. **No reading imposed, no arithmetic performed on the printed dates, and "the local
  murshid" is not named: (not stated).**
- **The gaddi nashin who gives the Shah Rukn-e Alam variant is not joined to Hathesingh.** p. 187
  (folio 174) attributes the variant to "the gaddi nashin"; Hathesingh is named as "the current pir of
  Pithoro" only at p. 190 (folio 177), and the book never writes "gaddi nashin Hathesingh". **A natural
  reading, not a stated one.**
- **Two undated "currents".** "Nur Husayn Shah, the current sajjada nashin" (p. 133, folio 120) and
  "still the Master of the silsilo" (p. 73, folio 60) — **the year of "current" is (not stated)**; "the
  sajjada nashin of Daraza" who times the ʿurs by telephone (p. 143, folio 130) is **unnamed** and is
  not stated to hold the same office as Sakhi Qabul Muhammad II.
- **A relative chronology with no anchor.** "It stood for about thirty to thirty-five years in another
  building, and shifted to the present one twenty years ago" and "since twenty years, they have stopped
  meeting" (p. 151, folio 138). **The year the author counts from is (not stated)**; the fieldwork dates
  in that chunk are 2011 and 2012 (p. 149, folio 136) but the book does not anchor the spans to them.
- **Pen-names against attributions in the Annexes.** Dalpat's piece closes on "Dalpat", Qutub Ali
  Shah's on "Qutub", Nimano Faqir's on "nimani"/"Nimani"; **Dadi Ganga's closes on "rochal"/"Rochal"
  and the book explains why in footnote 6** (p. 224, folio 211); **Gehimal Motwani's closes on
  "balka"/"Balka" and the book explains nothing: (not stated).** Do not attribute that piece firmly on
  the heading alone.
- **The facing-translation pairing in the first Dalpat annexe cannot be trusted line for line**
  (p. 221, folio not stated): "Deh abhman jai jalawi" faces "Transforms with word and thought"; "Bir
  akar men sada samawi" faces "Transforms Ego into Self"; "Apna ap, ap men lahi" faces "Speaks of the
  Unseen"; "Har bandi de vach tyani eko malik" faces "Happy unhappy he never is" while "In everyone
  sees one God" sits a line earlier. **Whether the displacement is the extraction's or the printed
  book's cannot be settled from the text layer. Nothing was retranslated or moved.** The inter-column
  gap is **not constant within a single poem**, so alignment cannot be trusted anywhere in the Annexes.
- **Index defects, recorded and unresolved.** `urs` at folio 130 against `death anniversary — Sachal
  138` for the same man; `urs … 131–7` and `death anniversary 69, 131–7, 157, 161` sharing the block
  131–7 exactly, with `versi` a *see*-reference to the second and not the first, and **the index
  nowhere saying the three terms name one thing**; `Sufis — rituals 18–19, 118` against top-level
  `rituals 18–19, 117`; `tomb (maqbaro) 62` against `maqbaro (tomb) 62, 66, 100`; `Sufis — murshids 59`
  against two identical seventeen-folio murshid runs; `Tando Ahmad Khan dargah 110, 166, 171–4` against
  the `dargah(s)` subentry `of Tando Ahmad Khan 110, 171–4` — **folio 166 in one, absent from the
  other**; `chakriyun (sandals) 106–7` against `sandals (chakriyun) 106`; `Vedas 81, 92` against the
  form `Vedas 92` reported from the other index chunk; `Tilak, Home Rule League 34`, a personal name
  and an organisation on one line with one locator, **printed as one entry and not interleaving**;
  "Slaves of African *see* Shidi", the book's own English, reproduced as printed.
- **`Sachal Sarmast` and `Dalpat Sufi` share a locator run.** The two index entries are **identical for
  their first seventeen locators and identical again after Dalpat's 104–8 insert** (p. 248, folio 235;
  p. 241, folio 228). **Either the two men are discussed together throughout, or one entry was copied
  from the other in compiling the index — and a Dalpat folio must not be treated as a Sachal folio on
  this evidence.** This compounds the "Dalpat Sufi, or Sachal Sarmast" apposition at p. 20 (folio 7).
- **`dargah(s) — of Sachal Sarmast 18` and, under `Daraza`, `dargah of 54`** are printed as **two
  separate things at two different folios, without being equated** (p. 241, folio 228). Both were
  recorded under the Sachal row and neither claimed as the other; **a human should check folios 18 and
  54 in the book.**

### Contradictions against sources the book names, and against `shrine_index.tsv`

- **Against Burton.** The Lal Kunwar custom is Burton's (Burton 1851: 211, p. 181, folio 168), and the
  book adds that it "disappeared during the partition in 1947"; Burton is also the authority the book
  cites against the current pir on the Pithoro Pir's caste (p. 191, folio 178). **Burton's spellings
  stay inside his quotations.**
- **Against Bouillier and Khan 2009.** The "dargah mandir" devoted to **Pir Baba Ratannath in Delhi**
  (p. 214, folio 201; index "Pir Baba Ratannath, Delhi 201 n.6", p. 247, folio 234) — **the claim is
  not Boivin's own observation**, and the same authors' title in the bibliography, "Hajji Ratan or Baba
  Ratan's Multiple Identities" (p. 233, folio 220), **is the book's own signal that the figure is
  contested.**
- **Against `(Bruce and Lawrence 2002)` (p. 227, folio 214)**, the authority for the claim that the
  Chishtiyya used the samaʿ to spread Sufi ideology and Islam in the Middle Ages — a load-bearing
  citation in Annex 2's argument. **Recorded exactly as printed; no substitute first name is proposed,
  but note that the same book cites "Ernst 1999" at p. 208 (folio not stated) and the bibliography
  prints "Ernst, Carl W. and Bruce B. Lawrence" (p. 234, folio 221).** A human must check the printed
  page before this is repeated.
- **Against `shrine_index.tsv`, recorded and not resolved.**
  - `shrine-of-makhdoom-jahaniyan-jahangasht`: the book prints **"(d. 1383)"** and calls him **"a saint
    of Multan"** (p. 189, folio 176); the archive row carries **1384** and locates the shrine at **Uch
    Sharif, Bahawalpur District**. The book also prints **"(1308–84)"** for the same man at p. 56
    (folio 43), and the id's own spelling is *jahaniyan*, one `y`, where the book prints "Jahaniyyan".
  - `shrine-of-sachal-sarmast`: the book gives 1825 and 1829; the archive row carries **1827**.
  - `dargah-pir-ratan-nath-jee`: the book puts the figure's dargah mandir in **Delhi**; the archive row
    is at **Jhanda Bazar, Peshawar**. **Three readings are open and the book settles none**: a separate
    Delhi dargah mandir of the same figure, a different figure, or a mislocated archive row.
  - `shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan`: the book gives "the darbar of Udero Lal, the huge
    sanctuary **close to Hyderabad**" and "Jhulelal/Shaykh Tahir" (p. 184, folio 171); the archive gives
    **Odero Lal Village (near Tando Adam), Matiari District** and "Sheikh Tahir / Udero Lal (Jhulelal)".
    **Divergent but not contradictory.**
  - `shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya` (1267) and `shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam` (1335): **the death years
    agree with the archive**; only the order's spelling differs, "Sohrawardi" for "Suhrawardi" (p. 187,
    folio 174).
  - `dargah-of-khwaja-muhammad-zaman-luari-sharif`: the archive's silsila **Naqshbandi** matches the
    Annexes passage on Lowari Sharif, **but the row's principal figure, Khwaja Muhammad Zaman, is not
    named anywhere in the book: (not stated).**

### Placement — material that attaches at place level

**Ruling (a) is in force and these attachments are place-level, not site-specific.** The conversion
into multi-id bullets sits in the archive sections; what is recorded here is the attachment level and
the review point that follows from it. **A place-level attachment gives a row the place's material, not
a statement about that row's site**, and an entry must not read it as one.

- **Sehwan Sharif** is the heaviest. It is named with no shrine, tomb, saint, ʿurs or custodian
  attached at p. 30 (folio 17), p. 45 (folio 32), p. 97 (folio 84), p. 113 and p. 125 (folios 100,
  112), p. 128 (folio 115), p. 161 (folio 148), pp. 186 and 193 (folios 173, 180), and pp. 210–211
  (folios 197–198). **Two of these are already carried on the saint's own row** (the February 2017
  attack, p. 186, folio 173; "the growing fame of the saint of Sehwan Sharif", p. 193, folio 180) **on
  the ground that the surrounding argument is entirely about his shrine — a stricter reader could treat
  both as place-level, and the row would be the same either way.** Note that the town is named with the
  honorific `Sharif`, and that the archive also holds `shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar` at "Laki (Lakhi),
  near Sehwan Sharif" — **"near" is not "in", and a human should decide whether proximity counts.**
  Chunk 005's "In his teens, he went to Sehwan Sharif, where he is reported to have been vouchsafed
  spiritual visions" (p. 97, folio 84) is exactly the sentence a Lal Shahbaz entry would want and
  exactly the sentence that says nothing about him.
- **Jhok Sharif.** "He also visited Jhok Sharif and Daraza" (p. 97, folio 84); "Sufi Hazrat Abdul
  Sattar Shah of Jhok Sharif" (p. 101, folio 88), which attaches a person but not the row's principal
  figure; the index entries "Jhok Sharif 46, 47, 105, 106, 111, 128" (p. 244, folio 231) and "Sharif,
  Jhok 14" (p. 249, folio 236). **The row is also carried on the site itself elsewhere — "the dargah of
  Jhok Sharif", "the sajadah nashin of Jhok Sharif", "the urs of Jhok Sharif", "the Sufi centre of Jhok
  Sharif" (pp. 59–60, folios 46–47) and "a great Sufi centre" with a named sajjada nashin (pp. 128,
  141, folios 115, 128) — but in every one of those the book still never says who is buried there.**
  The saint is named at exactly one place in the book: the glossary (p. 219, folio 206).
- **Daraza / Khairpur.** Daraza is **not** bare where it carries a named sajjada nashin twice and a
  dargah with an inscribed gate (pp. 97, 101, 135, 143, 179; folios 84, 88, 122, 130, 166); Khairpur is
  bare at p. 26 (folio 13), p. 93 (folio 80), pp. 107 and 122 (folios 94, 109), p. 150 (folio 137) and
  in the index run "Khairpur 13, 18, 55, 80, 109, 137, 156". **Khairpur District holds a second row,
  `dargah-of-pir-muhammad-rashid-roze-dhani-pir-jo-goth`, which nothing in the book touches**, so the
  Khairpur material reaches it at place level only. **"Hingorja near Khairpur" (p. 169, folio 156), a
  birthplace with no shrine, is a place-level attachment to the district, not to either site.**
- **Karachi — eleven rows in the supplied index**, none distinguished by any of the book's mentions
  (pp. 26, 56, 57, 62, 66, 67, 94, 103, 115, 156, 209; folios 13, 43, 44, 49, not stated, 54, 81, 90,
  102, 143, 196). Note that the chunk notes variously count the Karachi rows as nine, ten and eleven;
  **the supplied `shrine_index.tsv` gives eleven** (`bhagnari-mandir`,
  `darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir`, `jhollay-lal-mandir`,
  `shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi`, `shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge`,
  `shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi`, `shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi`, `shri-varun-dev-mandir`,
  `shrine-of-abdullah-shah-ghazi`, `shrine-of-pir-mangho`, `shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya`).
  **A human should settle the count before any coverage audit.**
- **Multan — eight rows**, from two bare clauses: "in 1397, Pir Muhammad was in Multan" (p. 189,
  folio 176), "with Multan as capital" (p. 216, folio 203), "settled in Multan" (p. 227, folio 214) and
  p. 98 (folio 85). **Three further Multan occurrences are not bare** — they carry Baha al-Din
  Zakariyya, Shah Rukn-e Alam and Makhdum Jahaniyyan Jahangasht by name.
- **Hyderabad (Sindh).** Bare at pp. 30, 54, 56, 59, 60, 95, 103, 118, 119, 123, 184, 189, 209 (folios
  17, 41, 43, 46, 47, 82, 90, 105, 106, 110, 171, 176, 196). **The archive's only Hyderabad row is
  `guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat`, and the book names two Hyderabad sites that are not it** —
  Dalpat's astano on Sipahimalani Lane (pp. 107, 209; folios 94, 196) and Qutub Ali Shah's dargah in
  the Jahaniyya neighbourhood (pp. 95, 167; folios 82, 154). **So a place-level attachment here lands
  on the wrong institution, and an entry must say so.** Note also "Hyderabad Deccan" (p. 210,
  folio 197), a different city entirely.
- **Sukkur and Rohri.** Sukkur bare at p. 71 (folio 58), p. 104 (folio 91), p. 115 (folio 102); Rohri
  bare at pp. 55, 56, 57, 60, 61, 97–98, 107, 209 (folios 42, 43, 44, 47, 48, 84–85, 94, 196), and
  **not** bare at p. 150 (folio 137), where "Isarlal, who had a shrine in Rohri" attaches a shrine to a
  figure who is **no archive row's principal figure**, and at p. 68 (folio 55) and p. 78 (folio 65),
  where Sain Vasan Shah's "original sanctuary" and "Rohri dham" likewise attach to a non-archive
  figure. **The only row whose location names Rohri is `sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple` ("near
  Sukkur/Rohri"), which is a different site, so a Rohri attachment reaches it at place level and says
  nothing about Sadh Belo.**
- **Shikarpur** (pp. 60, 94, 214, and the index run "Shikarpur 17, 47, 55, 81"; folios 47, 81, 201,
  and pp. 240–249) — three rows, of which `sant-bhagat-kanwar-ram-temple-chak` is separately carried
  **on Bhagat Kanwar Ram's own name**, not on the toponym.
- **Uch / Utch, Bahawalpur** — "Utch near Bahawalpur" (p. 56, folio 43) and the index's "Utch
  (Bahawalpur, Punjab) 43" (p. 251, folio 238) — **five Uch Sharif rows, none named.**
- **Lahore** — "born in Lahore" of Iqbal (p. 212, folio 199). **`mazar-e-iqbal` is carried on the
  person already, so a place-level attachment here adds nothing but the rest of the Lahore rows.**
- **Lakki / Laki** (p. 61, folio 48, in a riot list; index "Lakki 48"), **Hala** (p. 91, folio 78),
  **Mangho Pir** (p. 104, folio 91 — **and note the word order: the book writes the place `Mangho Pir`
  where the archive writes the saint `Pir Mangho`, so a name-matching script misses it**),
  **Larkana** (p. 156, folio 143), **Umarkot / Umerkot** (pp. 47, 188, 189, 191; folios 34, 175, 176,
  178; index "Umerkot 27"), **Lowari / Luari Sharif** (p. 22, folio 9; p. 228, folio 215; index
  "Lowari Sharif (Badin, Sindh) 9" — the book's "close to Badin" corroborates the row's district),
  **Rohri taluqa** (p. 107, folio 94), **Jamshoro district / Kohistan** (p. 184, folio 171),
  **Thar Parkar** (p. 190, folio 177 — three rows), **Thatta / Thattah** (index p. 251, folio 238).
- **Toponyms with no archive row at all**, so place-level attachment yields nothing and the material
  belongs under "Figures and sites not in the archive": Tando Ahmad Khan, Tando Jahaniya/Jahaniyyan,
  Thano Bula Khan, Mirpur Khas, Kohistan, Sonda, Nausharo Firuz/Nawsharo Firoz, Jalalani Sharif, Bubak,
  Champra, Meru, Nao, Kandri, Kashmir, Punjab and Bihar and Bengal as provinces, Baluchistan, Bhuj,
  Kutch/Kuch, Ujjain, Malwa, Ghazni, Ghor, Tarain, Amritsar, Delhi, Ajmer, Shirdi, Ayodhya, Ulhasnagar,
  Haridwar, Vadodara, Sion/Mumbai, Kalyan, Lucknow, Madras, Kolkatta, Chennai, Barmer, Jodhpur,
  Rajasthan, Gujarat, Maharashtra, Calcutta, Junagarh, Dwarka, Baghdad, Karbala, Iraq.
- **Two place-level attachments that would land on the wrong site and must be marked as such.**
  "Shahi Bazar in Sehwan" (p. 125, folio 112) resembles the locations of
  `sant-baba-bhagat-ram-darbar-mandir` ("Shahi Bazar, Dadu") and `bhai-sant-thawan-das-mandir` ("Old
  Shahi Bazar, Mehar") but **names a different town**. "Pithoro", the hamlet where Pithoro Pir's tomb
  stands (p. 189, folio 176), appears inside the location string of
  `shrine-of-makhdoom-abdul-rahim-girhori` ("Girhor Sharif, near Pithoro, Umarkot"), but **the book
  names neither Makhdoom Abdul Rahim Girhori nor Girhor Sharif**, and its Pithoro Pir is plainly a
  different site.

### Placement — material that attaches at person level

**These attachments are person-level rather than site-specific, and an entry must present them that
way: the book says something about the person, not about that row's shrine.**

- **Guru Nanak — eighteen rows, and the chunk notes disagree with themselves about the count.** Chunks
  006 and 011 say seventeen; chunks 007, 009, 012 and 013 say eighteen and flag the discrepancy.
  **Counted against the supplied `shrine_index.tsv`, the answer is eighteen** — the seventeen-item list
  omits `gurdwara-patti-sahib`. (`gurdwara-babay-nanki`, principal figure "Bebe Nanaki", is **not** one
  of them.) The evidence in this book is thin everywhere: "reciting the banis of Shri Guru Nanak"
  (p. 68, folio 55); a colour portrait on a Mumbai altar (p. 119, folio 106); a portrait on Rochaldas's
  altar and pictures on a devotee's wall (pp. 133–134, folios 120–121); the closing jai formula
  (p. 141, folio 128); "Nanak Shah" in a nineteenth-century dictionary's definition of chownki (p. 144,
  folio 131); "Guru Nanak's poetry has been kept in this new edition" (p. 172, folio 159); "Guru
  Nanak's banis" again (p. 209, folio 196); a liturgical prayer (p. 216, folio 203); the index run
  "Guru Nanak 40, 41, 55, 76, 77, 78, 90, 120, 121, 128, 138, 143, 147, 159, 196 n.13, 203 n.7"
  (p. 242, folio 229). **Not one of these names a gurdwara, a place, a date or one of Guru Nanak's own
  acts.** The **Nanakpanth** is a different handle altogether and takes nothing — "a panth's name is
  not its founder's name" (pp. 26, 31, 38, 42, 43, 53, 158; folios 13, 18, 25, 29, 30, 40, 145) — and
  the "many new Nanakpanthi darbars all over Sindh" (p. 199, folio 186) and the "several important
  Nanakpanthi darbars throughout Sindh" (p. 53, folio 40) **name no darbar**. The one darbar named, the
  **Halani darbar** (p. 54, folio 41), matches no row. The **Guru Granth Sahib** (p. 121, folio 108;
  p. 218, folio not stated) is a scripture, not a person.
- **Gorakhnath — two rows, `gorakhnath-goraknath-temple` and `tilla-jogian`, sharing one principal
  figure.** The evidence is four clauses, none naming a site or a date: "the Nathpanthis, a Shivaite
  school of renunciants who were the followers of Gorakhnath" (p. 21, folio 8); "They are the followers
  of Gorakhnath, who is believed to be an avatar of Shiva" (p. 77, folio 64); "an ascetic order founded
  by Gorakhnath" (p. 120, folio 107); "Gorakhnath would have sent an invisible army to help Goga Pir
  against Rai Pithora" (p. 188, folio 175); the index run "Gorakhnath 8, 64, 107, 175" (p. 242,
  folio 229). **Note that the book calls him an avatar of a deity, which sits on the boundary of the
  deity class below**, and that the chunk-011 range does **not** name him at all — the Nathpanth is
  named without its founder there (p. 208, folio not stated; p. 219, folio 206).
- **Jhulelal / Udero Lal — three rows** (`shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan`,
  `jhollay-lal-mandir`, `darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir`). **The site-level material
  is separate and sits on the first row alone**, because the book names the darbar and gives it an
  architectural fact ("hosts many domes", p. 210, folio 197) and a date ("dating back to the
  seventeenth century", pp. 42–43, folios 29–30). **Everything else is person-level or deity-level:**
  "the isht devta of the Hindu Sindhis of India" (p. 70, folio 57); "isht devta, or community God"
  (p. 156, folio 143); the jai formula (p. 141, folio 128); named **as absent** from an altar (p. 119,
  folio 106); the long index run (p. 244, folio 231). **Two Jhulelal temples the book describes are
  neither of the Karachi rows** — one "in the northwestern part" of Sehwan (p. 71, folio 58; p. 180,
  folio 167) and one in Ulhasnagar (p. 83, folio 70) — and **the Thakur material (p. 210, folio 197;
  p. 217, folio 204) is about the priests of Jhulelal generally, not of Odero Lal**, the book saying
  the Thakurs "came from Sehwan Sharif". **The variant "Darya Shah" (p. 156, folio 143) points toward
  the Daryalal row, and "Zinda Pir in the caption of the image" (p. 156, folio 143) must not be allowed
  to bridge Jhulelal to `darbar-ghamkol-sharif-zinda-pir`, whose principal figure is Khwaja Muhammad
  Qasim, d. 1999, a different person entirely.**
- **Deity names used as comparanda or as objects of private devotion.** The chunk notes refused these
  as mapping handles throughout, consistently, and flagged the question as unruled. **Recorded here as
  the attachment level they would have if it is ruled on**: **Shiva — eight rows**
  (`amb-temples-amb-sharif`, `bhagnari-mandir`, `chandragup-baba-chandragup`, `katas-raj-temples`,
  `shahwala-teja-singh-mandir`, `shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi`, `shiv-mandir-chiti-ghati`,
  `umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir`), from pp. 21, 43, 77, 80, 119, 179, 180, 185, 186, 191, 192 (folios 8,
  30, 64, 67, 106, 166, 167, 172, 173, 178, 179) and the index's "Shiva (god) 30" and "avatar — of
  Shiva 64, 166"; **Krishna — two rows** (`krishna-mandir-kabari-bazar`, `krishna-mandir-ravi-road`);
  **Valmiki — three rows** (`lal-kurti-temple-balmiki-mandir-rawalpindi`, `valmik-mandir-naqi-road`,
  `valmiki-swamiji-mandir-gracy-lines-rawalpindi`), from "the legendary author of the Ramayana"
  (p. 163, folio 150) and the index's "Valmiki 150"; **Kali — three rows** (`kalat-kali-temple`,
  `kali-bari-mandir`, `kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi`); **Rama — one row**
  (`ram-mandir-saidpur-ram-kund-mandir`), and note that the book's forms are "Ramachandra" (p. 134,
  folio 121) and "Sri Ramchandra" (pp. 139, 163; folios 126, 150), **so equating them with the
  archive's "Rama" is itself an identification the book does not print**; **Hanuman — one row**
  (`shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi`); **Varun — one row** (`shri-varun-dev-mandir`), the index
  printing the god's name twice with identical runs, "Varun (Vedic god) 41, 165" and "Vedic god Varun
  41, 165" (pp. 251–252, folios 238–239). **Vishnu, Brahma, Radha, Laxman, Sita, Sitala, Devi, Mata,
  Goga and Ravan name no archive row's principal figure.** The nearest to Vishnu are
  `prahladpuri-temple` ("Narasimha") and `shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge`
  ("Lakshmi-Narayan"), **neither of which the book names as a form of Vishnu.**
- **Two person-level mappings already made that rest on a bare name and must be weighed as such.**
  `mazar-e-iqbal` — one sentence on Iqbal's reading of Hallaj (p. 102, folio 89) and one explanatory
  endnote (p. 212, folio 199); **the row's 1938 must not be back-filled: the book gives no birth year,
  no death year and no tomb.** `sant-bhagat-kanwar-ram-temple-chak` — a parenthesis inside a note about
  somebody else, the pir of Bharchundi, where Bhagat Kanwar Ram appears only as the man whose
  assassination an ancestor was accused of ordering (p. 216, folio 203); **the printed "(1885–1939)"
  agrees with the archive's 1939 and adds a birth year, which is the one piece of new data — no place,
  no date of the assassination, no temple: (not stated).** Likewise `garh-maharaja-shorkot` on "Sultan
  Bahu's poetry, he being an important seventeenth-century Sufi from Punjab" (p. 121, folio 108), **a
  literary parallel drawn by a third party, not the book's own account — the row's 1691 and Sarwari
  Qadiri must not be back-filled**; and `shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya` on one clause in which the saint
  acts only as the initiator of Pithoro Pir (p. 178, folio 165), **no Multan, no death year, no
  shrine.**

### Placement — names that could be two archive rows

- **`Shah Inayat` / `Shah Inat`.** `dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed` (Jhok Sharif, d. 1718) and
  `shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri` (Mozang Chungi, Lahore, c. 1728). **The book settles it wherever it
  qualifies the name** — "Shah Inayat from / of / (from) Jhok Sharif", pp. 107, 122, 124 (folios 94,
  109, 111) — **and leaves it open wherever it does not**: the bare "Shah Inayat" in "'three great Sufi
  teachers'" (p. 49, folio 36) and the index's "Inayat, Shah 36, 94, 111" (p. 243, folio 230), whose
  folio 111 is shared with "dargah(s) — of Jhok Sharif 46, 111, 115" — **suggestive, not an assertion.**
  **A third man, "Shah Inat (1623–1712)", "Sayyid Inayat Allah Rizvi"** (p. 211, folio 198), and "Shah
  Inat, a Sindhi Sufi poet of the seventeenth century" (p. 105, folio 92), is neither row: the book
  keeps him separate and **the 1712/1718 proximity must not be read as a match.**
- **`Jinda Pir` / `Zinda Pir`** — `gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur` and
  `darbar-ghamkol-sharif-zinda-pir`. The index carries them as two entries **at the identical folio
  41**, with no gloss and no location (pp. 244, 252; folios 231, 239).
- **`Udero Lal` bare** (index, p. 251, folio 238) — the name is inside the principal figure of both
  `shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan` and `jhollay-lal-mandir`, and **it is also unresolved
  whether the entry names the town or the figure.**
- **`Sayyid Jalal al-Din Bukhari`** — `shrine-of-jalaluddin-surkh-posh-bukhari-jalaluddin-bukhari`
  (d. 1291) and `shrine-of-makhdoom-jahaniyan-jahangasht` (d. 1384), both at Uch Sharif. **Settled on
  the book's own gloss** — "better known under the name of Makhdum Jahaniyyan Jahangasht (d. 1383)"
  (p. 189, folio 176), and "Jalal al-Din Bukhari Makhdum-i Jahaniyyan Jahangasht (1308–84)" (p. 56,
  folio 43), whose `–84` matches 1384 and not 1291. **Recorded so the fork is not re-run.**
- **`Shah Karim`.** Chunk 005 takes `shrine-of-shah-abdul-karim-bulri` on the name plus the
  genealogical relation to Shah Abd al-Latif (pp. 91, 100, 105; folios 78, 87, 92); chunk 009
  **refuses** the same name at p. 171 (folio 158) for want of dates, tomb, toponym or an "Abdul".
  **The two chunks disagree; a human must settle it.** The book never prints "Bulri" in the body; the
  bibliography does name Bulri outright.
- **`Ghulam Farid`.** Chunk 008 **refuses** `mithankot-kot-mithan` (p. 161, folio 148); chunks 009 and
  012 **take** it (p. 171, folio 158; index p. 242, folio 229). **The chunks disagree.** The archive
  row's Khwaja Ghulam Farid, d. 1901, Chishti, must not be back-filled: the book gives no honorific, no
  date and no place.
- **`Shah Bahu` / `Sultan Bahu`.** Chunk 006 takes `garh-maharaja-shorkot` on "Sultan Bahu" (p. 121,
  folio 108); chunk 009 takes it on "Shah Bahu" (p. 171, folio 158) **and flags it as the weakest of
  its eight**; chunk 012 **refuses** "Shah Bahu 158" (p. 249, folio 236) on the ground that **folio 108
  is absent from that index entry's run, which is evidence against the identification.** Unsettled.
- **`Kathwaro Darbar` / `Katwara Sahib` / `Khatwara Sahib`.** Chunk 004 **takes**
  `khatwari-darbar-shikarpur` on "the Kathwaro Darbar, which was transferred from Shikarpur to Nagpur
  (Madhya Pradesh)" (p. 68, folio 55) — noting that the book prints Nagpur's state as "(Madhya
  Pradesh)", reproduced and not adjudicated; chunk 006 **refuses** "the Khatwara Sahib" (p. 114,
  folio 101), where the grammar does not even settle whether it names a person or a place; chunk 012
  **refuses** all three index forms (p. 244, folio 231), the book never locating the darbar and never
  naming a figure for it. **A lead offered without being claimed: folio 55 carries both "Kathwaro
  Darbar 55" and "Shikarpur 17, 47, 55, 81".**
- **`Rakhiyyal Shah` — flagged in five separate chunks and taken in none.** Chunk 001 flags
  `dargah-fateh-pur-sharif` "for reversal" on the strength of a list-of-illustrations string alone
  (p. 10, folio not stated); chunks 004, 005, 007 and 011 each record it as the strongest unmade
  mapping in their range (p. 75, folio 62; p. 101, folio 88; pp. 137–138, folios 124–125; p. 227,
  folio 214). **The death year 1940 and the Qadiri order both agree with the archive row; the place
  does not appear in the book at all. This is the single mapping most in need of a human decision.**
- **`Pir Baba Ratannath` / `Baba Pir Ratan Nath`** — `dargah-pir-ratan-nath-jee`, taken on the person
  under the 18 September 2026 ruling, with **the book putting the dargah mandir in Delhi and the row in
  Peshawar.**
- **`shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge`, taken on a deity-name plus a city.** "In Karachi,
  for example, a painting of Ramdeo Pir stands in the temple of Lakhshmi Narayan." (p. 192, folio 179)
  **The book gives no address, no custodian and no description beyond the painting**, and the archive
  holds exactly one Lakshmi-Narayan row in Karachi. **If the book means some other Karachi temple of
  that name, the material moves to "Figures and sites not in the archive" and nothing else changes.**
- **`gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur`, taken on the site and named as the weakest of its
  chunk.** "A new darbar… built on the bank of the Indus River in Sukkur, this coming to be known as
  Jinda Pir" (p. 54, folio 41), founded in "the last decade of the nineteenth century" by Hindus
  wanting "a distinct Hindu cult of Khwaja Khizr, also known as Zinda Pir". **For: name + city +
  riverbank. Against: the book spells `Jinda Pir` and the row `Jind Pir`, the book never calls the
  place a gurdwara, and Guru Nanak is never named in connection with it: (not stated).**
- **`sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple`.** The identification rests on three converging details in one
  sentence — an island site, the Rohri–Sukkur stretch of the Indus, and the label "Udasi temple"
  matching the row's Nanakpanthi/Udasi category (p. 31, folio 18) — **but the book gives no district,
  no founder and no date, and Baba Bankhandi Maharaj is never named: (not stated).**
- **`ramapir-temple-tando-allahyar`, thin twice over.** "Rama Pir (Ramdeo or Ramdev)" among four
  deified heroes (p. 43, folio 30); "Ramdeo Pir, still known in Sindh as Rama Pir" with his cult
  described (p. 192, folio 179); the index's "Rama Pir 30". **The book never names Tando Allahyar,
  never names any temple of his, and gives him no dates**; and it calls him both a "saint" and, by
  implication, one of the "divinized heroes" (p. 194, folio 181), **so a reader applying the deity
  refusal rather than the person ruling would move him out.** Note too that the book classifies him as
  a Hindu deified hero venerated by untouchables where the archive row is a Hindu Temple — compatible,
  but the book says nothing about a temple.
- **`bhit-bhit-shah` is carried in six chunks and the book never once prints "Bhittai" or "Bhit"
  outside two phrases.** The row rests on the name "Shah Abd al-Latif" / "Shah Abdul Latif" / "Shah
  Latif" alone in chunks 001, 004, 006, 009, 010 and 011, with **no dates, no silsila and no shrine**;
  the exceptions are "his darbar at Bhit Shah" (pp. 60–61, folios 47–48) and "Bhit Shah" with a named
  darbar (p. 178, folio 165). **Do not import the archive row's 1752 from the row** — though the book
  does print "(d. 1752)" independently at p. 48 (folio 35) and p. 155 (folio 142), and "(1689–1752)" at
  p. 93 (folio 80). Two further cautions: the p. 50 (folio 37) sentence "This is the case of the visit
  to the dargah by the great mystic poet Shah Abd al-Latif" **can be read as a visit to his dargah or
  by him to another's, and should be quoted whole**; and "Sufi poetry – Shah jo Risalo – first"
  (p. 170, folio 157) **does not say whose work it is** — the attribution there is the note-taker's
  from the book's usage, not a statement on the page. The same applies to "Ernst Trump's printing of
  the Shah jo Risalo" (p. 63, folio 50). **The archive row `bari-imam` ("Sayyid Abdul Latif Kazmi") was
  considered and refused twice** — this book's Shah Abd al-Latif is unambiguously a Sindhi poet.

### Near-misses considered and refused — do not re-run these

Recorded so the checks are not repeated and so a string-matcher's false positives are on the record.

- **`Tando Jahaniya` / `Tando Jahaniyya` / `Tando Jahaniyyan` ≠ `shrine-of-makhdoom-jahaniyan-jahangasht`**
  (p. 27, folio 14; p. 60, folio 47; p. 112, folio not stated). The book locates Tando Jahaniyya "in
  Hyderabad" and Jahaniyyan Jahangasht at "Utch near Bahawalpur in Punjab". **The resemblance is not
  evidence.**
- **"the Jahaniyya silsila" / "the Jahaniyyan lineage" ≠ that row** (pp. 73, 98, 166–167, 200; folios
  60, 85, 153–154, 187). **A silsila name is not a mapping handle**, and no person is named. Note that
  the Annex 2 chart strengthens the near-miss without closing it: it labels its Jahaniyya silsila
  **`(Sohrawardiyya)`**, which now agrees with the row's Suhrawardi order, **but still names no person**
  (p. 227, folio 214). **If a human confirms the link, the id is
  `shrine-of-makhdoom-jahaniyan-jahangasht` and the facts are at p. 200 (folio 187) and p. 227
  (folio 214).**
- **"the Jalalaniyya (Qadiriyya)" ≠ `shrine-of-jalaluddin-surkh-posh-bukhari-jalaluddin-bukhari`**
  (p. 227, folio 214): **the order and the province both disagree**, and no person is named.
- **"Ali Akbar" ≠ `shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi`** (p. 41, folio 28): the
  book's Ali Akbar is a member of Husayn's family.
- **"Makhdum Muhammad Hashim" is none of the archive's three Makhdum rows** (p. 41, folio 28).
- **"Shams Tabrizi" ≠ `shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari`, `shamsabad` or
  `shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi`** (p. 121, folio 108; index "Tabrizi, Shams
  108", p. 251, folio 238): identifying them would be an import from general knowledge (RULE 2).
- **"Pir Data Dastgir" / "Abd al-Qadir Jilani (d. 1192)" ≠ `data-darbar`** (pp. 115, 121, 122, 123,
  218; folios 102, 108, 109, 110, not stated). The book is explicit that Pir Data Dastgir is the South
  Asian name of al-Jilani of Baghdad; the archive row's figure is **Hazrat Ali ibn Usman al-Hujwiri
  (Data Ganj Bakhsh)**, who is **not named anywhere in the book: (not stated)**. **The shared element
  "Data" is exactly what a later merge could get wrong.**
- **The Sehwan lineages are not the Multan and Laki rows.** "the Sabzwaris also share the symbolic
  power" (p. 180, folio 167; index "Sabzwaris 167") ≠ `shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari`; the
  "Lakkiyaris" / "Lakiyyaris" / "Lakkiyyaris" and "Gul Muhammad Lakkiyyari" (pp. 180–183, folios
  167–170; index "Lakkiyyaris 168", "Lakkiyyari, Gul Muhammad 170") ≠ `shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar`:
  **the book never names Lakhi Shah Saddar, never names a shrine at Laki, and never says the
  Lakkiyaris descend from or serve him. A lineage name is not a principal figure.** This is the closest
  call in chunk 009; **if a reviewer rules the lineage is that row's family, the material at
  pp. 180–181 and 183 (folios 167–168, 170) transfers mechanically.**
- **"Shah Sadr" ≠ `shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar`** (p. 185, folio 172), "said to have been the Sufi
  Master of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in Sindh": **no patronymic, no nisba, no place, no dates, and the
  shrine described is one of three subsidiary shrines inside the Tando Ahmad Khan compound. If a human
  confirms it, the id is `shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar` and the facts are at p. 185, folio 172.**
- **"Pithoro Pir" ≠ `shrine-of-makhdoom-abdul-rahim-girhori`** (p. 10, folio not stated; p. 51,
  folio 38; pp. 178–179, folios 165–166; pp. 189–191, folios 176–178), **despite "near Pithoro" in the
  row's location string.**
- **"the Singh Sabha" ≠ `gurdwara-singh-sabha`** (p. 199, folio 186): a late-nineteenth-century reform
  movement, not the Quetta building. **A trap for a string-matcher.**
- **"Sakhi" is an honorific and bridges nothing** — "Sakhi Qabul Muhammad II", "Sakhi Kutiyya", "Sakhi
  sabhajal", "Sakhi Darazi", "Sakhi 59" ≠ `sakhi-sarwar`, `darbar-sakhi-shah-chan-charagh`,
  `purana-bhalwal` ("Sakhi Badshah") or `sant-baba-asudaram-darbar-panno-aqil` ("Sakhi Baba"). **The
  book never prints "Sarwar".**
- **"ziyarat 37, 191" ≠ `ziarat-kaka-sahib`** (p. 252, folio 239) — the practice term, not the place.
  **Chunk 013 calls it "the chunk's most seductive false match".**
- **"Sohrawardiyya 38, 43, 85, 126, 187, 204 n.1" ≠ the archive's thirteen Suhrawardi rows** (p. 250,
  folio 237) — an order, not a site. **Silsila names are not mapping handles**, which is also why the
  Naqshbandi mentions (pp. 41, 42, folios 28, 29) claim none of the archive's Naqshbandi rows.
- **"of Muinuddin Chishti 110" ≠ `khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz`**; **"of Qutub Ali Shah 44" ≠
  `tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak`**; **"Singh, Guru Gobind 40" ≠ the six Guru Hargobind rows** (only
  `gurdwara-dash-mesh-pita` names Guru Gobind Singh); **"Swami Meghraj 81" ≠
  `swami-dharmdas-darbar-larkana-kennedy-market` or `shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi`**;
  **"Waliullah, Shah 29" ≠ `bhai-waliram-darbar`**; **"Baba Sadhuram 55" ≠
  `sant-baba-asudaram-darbar-panno-aqil`**; **Khan's *The Pranami Faith* ≠ `parnami-mandir`** (all
  pp. 235, 250–252; folios 222, 237–239).
- **The Ghurid and Ghaznavid sultans are not the Lahore Aibak row** — "Muhammad Shihab al-Din of Ghor"
  (p. 187, folio 174), "the sultan of Ghazni, Muizz al-Din" (p. 188, folio 175); **"Qutbuddin" and
  "Aibak" are printed nowhere in that chunk**, so `tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak` is not engaged.
- **"Sufi tomb 165", "tomb (maqbaro) 62"** (p. 251, folio 238) — generic, no saint, no place; refused
  for every tomb row. **"Jainism 36" names no Tirthankara**, so the three Jain rows take nothing.
- **"ranjhan" and "Punhal"/"Punhu" in Nimano Faqir's verse ≠ `mausoleum-of-waris-shah`** (p. 225,
  folio 212): **Waris Shah is not named anywhere in that chunk, and a beloved's name inside a verse is
  not a handle on his row.**
- **"the Khatwara Sahib", "Bodlo Bahar", "Isarlal's Rohri shrine", "Tando Ahmad Khan's Hindu dargah",
  "Pithoro Pir", "Qutub Ali Shah's Hyderabad dargah", "Dalpat's Hyderabad astano", "the Halani darbar",
  "the unnamed gurdwara 200 metres from the Tando Ahmad Khan dargah" and "the Jhulelal temple of
  Sehwan" are named sites or named saints that match no archive row.** **Bodlo Bahar** (p. 140,
  folio 127), "the main follower of Lal Shahbaz who has a shrine nearby", with a monthly rite, is
  **exactly the profile that would take an id and has no row**; **Tando Ahmad Khan's Hindu dargah** is
  described, mapped and photographed (pp. 9–10, folios not stated; pp. 184–185, folios 171–172). **Both
  are strong candidates for future archive rows.**

### The Hinglaj row

**The chunk notes record, in five separate chunks (003, 005, 006, 012, 013), that the archive row
`Shaktipeeth Shri Hinglaj Mata Mandir` exists in `shrine_index.tsv` with an EMPTY id cell**, that the
Hinglaj material is therefore filed unmapped under "Other saints, sites and events", and that **no id
has been invented for it anywhere.** Chunk 005 calls fixing the cell "blocking for this book", because
the defect costs a real cross-reference: the book names Hinglaj as the object of a **Hindu pilgrimage
undertaken by Shah Latif with Nathpanthi jogis** (p. 92, folio 79), which links `bhit-bhit-shah` to the
Hinglaj row.

**The `shrine_index.tsv` supplied with this Pass 2 carries the id `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir`
for that row** — with the category, principal-figure, figure-died and silsila cells still empty, and a
stray non-ASCII character inside the location string. **A human must confirm whether the defect the
notes describe has been repaired, and if so, re-run the Hinglaj material against the row.** Until that
is confirmed, nothing has been mapped on it here.

The book's Hinglaj material, with its pages: "Parsram claimed that the universal wisdom is kept in
Sindh, in Hinglaj to be exact, which is a pilgrimage centre located in the Sindhi-speaking area of
Baluchistan." (p. 50, folio 37) — **note that the book calls Hinglaj a pilgrimage centre in Baluchistan
while saying the wisdom is kept "in Sindh" in the same sentence; the reconciliation is the book's own
phrase and is left as printed**; "He probably travelled with them on a number of Hindu pilgrimages,
including Dwarka and Hinglaj." (p. 92, folio 79); "a number of sacred Hindu places, such as Hinglaj,
Kasi, or Kashi (Banaras), Sri Lanka and the Ganga" (p. 106, folio 93); and the index run **"Hinglaj 37,
79, 93"** printed on p. 243 (folio 230) — **locators, not pages.** Hinglaj is explicitly absent from
chunks 001, 002, 004, 007, 008, 009, 010, 011 and 013.

### What the Pass-1 workers could not place

- **"Burgat tombs"** — "there are beautiful Burgat tombs in the vicinity" of Thano Bula Khan (p. 184,
  folio 171). **Not glossed, not dated, not attributed to any community, and the spelling is
  unfamiliar. Possibly a text-layer corruption of a different word, but with no second occurrence to
  test against it is left as printed and unflagged.**
- **"Dado"** in the Pithoro dua — "Dado bhali kundo! 'Dado will bring only good to you!'" (p. 191,
  folio 178). **Whether "Dado" names Pithoro Pir, a grandfather-title, or another figure is (not
  stated).**
- **"Raja Barthari"** — "a main character of the Nathpanth" (p. 200, folio 187). **Not dated, not
  located, not identified beyond that clause.**
- **"the Kanungos"** — "at the time of the Kanungos" (p. 201, folio 188). **Neither a date nor a gloss.**
- **"Panjwani"** — "as we saw, Panjwani's trajectory is exemplary, and influential" (p. 201, folio 188),
  a back-reference to material outside that chunk; **no first name, no dates, no site there.**
- **"the Pirpanthis"** (p. 190, folio 177) — **not glossed.**
- **"the three main Sufi paths"** — "The data collected reflects the situation of three main Sufi paths
  among the Hindu Sindhis of India" (p. 198, folio 185). **The three are not named on that page**, and
  whether they are a subset of the four named Hindu Sufi figures elsewhere **cannot be settled.**
  Chunk 001 records the same gap for "the three 'main Sufi-related paths' the author became familiar
  with" and for the undefined "the Sehwan system" and "the religious market".
- **"hirarat"** (p. 160, folio 147) and **"a dholak (cattle drum)"** (p. 150, folio 137) — reproduced
  exactly; **no reading proposed, no emendation.**
- **"The network built by around darbar is not always easy to understand."** (p. 115, folio 102) —
  **two different repairs are possible and they change the sense, so no reading is offered.**
- **`Satchidanandra`** (p. 220, folio 207) and **`josh jigar t`** (p. 226, folio 213), a Sindhi verse
  line that **stops mid-word against a complete English line** — **marked, with no reading offered; a
  human must read the printed page.** The truncated verse line is the only place in the Annexes where a
  line of primary text is lost.
- **Index entries nobody could place**, recorded so a later reader does not think they were missed:
  "Champra 113"; "Meru 45"; "Nao 45"; "Kohji 148"; "Blavatsly, Tibet 37"; "Gobindram 135"; "hindu momin
  nahyan 7"; "hekal 88"; "pohtal 46"; "shahanshah 57"; "mastan 27"; "chang 79"; "charan 79"; "kamach
  79"; "kishti 80"; "Sakhi 59"; "Dhunjishaw 34"; "attendants 1" and "shewadaris (attendants) 1" as two
  entries; "saints 54"; "God 83"; "culture 4"; "influences 8"; "ideology 8, 74" (pp. 239–249, folios
  not stated and 227–236). **None bears on an archive mapping.**
- **A Pass-1 process limit, stated by the worker who hit it.** Chunk 013 records that **its Read of
  chunk 012's notes was truncated at line 496 of 1029**, so it could not check the rest of chunk 012's
  index record; the immediate casualty is the `Vedas 81, 92` / `Vedas 92` disagreement, **which remains
  unreconciled.** Anything else the two index chunks disagree about may be unchecked for the same
  reason.
- **The book's own non-idiomatic English is the author's, not damage, and none of it has been
  smoothed** — "Their domination was consequently doubled in power" (p. 38, folio 25); "even this point
  is not to underrate another main actor" (p. 36, folio not stated); "such a situation was already
  experimented upon in the Western diaspora" and "highly productive in illustrating out the numerous
  factors involved" (p. 33, folio 20); "the sacro-saint 'social networks' were not always unuseful"
  (p. 11, folio not stated); "the episteme of Sindh **before on the eve of** British colonization"
  (p. 52, folio 39); "used to perform mehndi **since centuries** during the urs of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar
  **since centuries**" — printed twice in one sentence (p. 58, folio 45); an **unmatched closing
  parenthesis** in "you must be a kafir [non-Muslim] just from your carnal envelope, but in your heart
  you are a momin [a Muslim])!" (p. 56, folio 43); "the world hek" for "the word hek" (p. 101,
  folio 88); "they cannot spent too much time" (p. 96, folio 83); "participaters" inside a quotation
  (p. 91, folio 78); "he is probably **the one of** the Sufi poets of Sindh to use the most numerous
  references to Hindu gods" (p. 150, folio 137); "the Sufis of Sindhis" (p. 149, folio 136);
  **"the death birthday"** (p. 127, folio 114); "Gidumal 1932 : 152" with a space before the colon
  against "Gidumal 1932: 150" without (p. 103, folio 90). **A human with the printed page should decide
  which of these are the book's and which the extraction's; none may be silently normalised in a
  quotation.**

### Chunk stitch points, and material outside the text layer

- **Every chunk boundary except the first and last is mid-sentence, and each must be stitched before a
  sentence spanning one is quoted.** 001 ends p. 28 (folio 15) at "In fact, Ramsey is more interested
  in labelling the 'syncretism' of the Sindhi Hindus"; 002 opens p. 29 (folio 16) at "instead of
  identifying the role played by Sufism…" and ends p. 48 (folio 35) at "the second is on Sufi culture
  in"; 003 opens p. 49 (folio 36) mid-sentence and ends p. 67 (folio 54) at "as was the"; 004 opens
  p. 68 (folio 55) at "case for Rai Rochaldas." and ends p. 87 (folio 74) at "Such a formulation
  implies that Sufism was influenced by one or the other Hindu"; 005 opens p. 88 (folio 75) at
  "movement." and ends at the foot of p. 105 (folio 92) after a displayed four-line verse credited
  "(Sachal Sarmast 1997: 75)"; 006 opens p. 106 (folio 93) inside a discussion whose beginning is in
  005 and ends p. 125 (folio 112) at "He lives in a huge haveli from the eighteenth century with his
  joint"; 007 runs pp. 126–145 and both its ends are mid-sentence; 008 opens on "urs of Lal Shahbaz
  Qalandar", **whose subject is not in its pages**, and closes mid-sentence into 009; 010 opens p. 184
  (folio 171) at "interaction between Muslims and Hindus through Sufism can be observed / through other
  perspectives, as well." and **ends at a clean paragraph break** at the foot of p. 203 (folio 190),
  **with whether the general conclusion continues being (not stated) from that chunk**; 011 opens
  p. 204 (folio 191) and ends p. 227 (folio 214) at "Maybe because of the Sohrawardiyya's hold on
  Sindh, the Chishtiyya could not spread in Sindh as it" — **so Annex 2's commentary is incomplete and
  whether a third annexe follows is (not stated)**; 012 opens mid-sentence at p. 228 (folio 215) and
  breaks off mid-entry at p. 249 (folio 236) inside `Sindhi Hindus` at "syncretism of 15–16"; 013 runs
  pp. 250–253 to the end of the book. **Chunk 002's account of Parsram's *Sind and its Sufis* (1924) is
  incomplete by exactly the width of its own break.**
- **Two figures are not in the text layer and are the most likely place in the book for further archive
  mappings.** **Figure 2.1, "Sufi seats from Sindh in India." (p. 69, folio 56)** — a map or chart whose
  content is **lost to these notes**, so whatever seats it names beyond the prose is **(not stated)**;
  and **Figure 2.2, "Hindu Sufi figures and their Sufi connections in Sindh." (p. 76, folio 63)**, which
  by its title may name Sufi masters and shrines that never appear in the running text. **A human with
  the PDF should read both.** Figures 2.3 (p. 81, folio 68), 2.4 (p. 83, folio 70), 7.2 and 7.3 are
  photographs; only their captions survive.
- **Figure 1.1** sits at printed page 45 and its caption is quoted in the list of illustrations at p. 10
  (folio not stated). **Which of its six names are the three "Hindu Sufis" and which the three "Muslim
  murshids" is (not stated)** — and this is the context the `dargah-fateh-pur-sharif` mapping needs.
- **Gaps every chunk left open, stated so nobody fills them.** Across all thirteen chunks the book gives
  **no death year, no birth year and no silsila in its own voice** for Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, Sufi Shah
  Inayat, Shah Abd al-Karim, Rochaldas, Mulchand Faqir, Nimano Faqir, Dalpat Sufi, Bhagat Kanwar Ram
  beyond "(1885–1939)", Rama Pir, Sheikh Tahir, Baba Bankhandi Maharaj or Khwaja Muhammad Zaman; **no
  ʿurs date with a year for any saint except al-Jilani's Yarhen**; and **no endowment, revenue, land
  area, budget, offering, attendance or visitor figure for any shrine in Sindh or India** — the only
  quantities anywhere are the 2,500 rupees for the Ulhasnagar plot (p. 83, folio 70), the 105 Hindu
  deaths (p. 61, folio 48), the 140,000 border crossings (p. 63, folio 50), the undated 75 per cent
  (p. 199, folio 186) and the "about twenty kafis" of Sehwan (p. 193, folio 180). **Every one of these
  is (not stated) and none may be supplied from an archive row.**
